### graph-rep
--idea
A graph is a set of nodes (also called vertices) joined by edges. It models anything made of things and links: cities and roads, people and friendships, tasks and dependencies. The first decision in any graph problem is how to store it in memory, because every algorithm after this reads the graph through that storage.

The usual answer is an adjacency list: for each node, a list of the nodes it connects to. It stores only the edges that exist, so it takes space proportional to nodes plus edges, and walking the neighbours of one node costs only as many steps as that node has neighbours. An edge can be directed (one way, like a one-way street) or undirected (two way). An undirected edge is stored as two directed ones.
--build
1. Number the nodes `0` to `n - 1`. If the input uses names or large ids, map each to a number first.
2. For an undirected graph, call `buildAdj(n, edges)` (code piece `u-adj`). It makes `n` empty lists and, for every edge `(u, v)`, appends `v` to `adj[u]` and `u` to `adj[v]`. Forget the reverse edge and half your edges are missing.
3. For a directed graph (one-way edges), make `n` empty lists yourself and call `addEdge(adj, u, v, true)` (code piece `u-edge`) for each edge. With the last argument false it also stores the reverse, so the same call serves both kinds.
4. For a weighted graph, store the pair `(v, w)` instead of just `v`. The weight is the cost of the edge.
5. To visit all neighbours of `u`, loop over `adj[u]`. The total work over all nodes is the number of edges (counted twice if undirected).
--think
- Signal: any statement about connections, "can I get from A to B", dependencies, networks, grids of cells, or states with moves between them. Decide first what a node is and what an edge means. This choice is the real modelling step.
- Adjacency list is the default. Use an adjacency matrix (see `adjmat`) only when `n` is small (a few thousand at most) or you need to ask "is there an edge from u to v" in constant time.
- An edge list (just an array of `(u, v, w)`) is the best form for algorithms that sort edges, such as Kruskal's minimum spanning tree, and for Bellman-Ford.
- Multi-edges (two edges between the same pair) and self-loops (an edge from a node to itself) may appear. Decide whether they matter before you code. Cycle detection and counting problems are the usual victims.
- Pitfalls: ids that start at 1 while arrays start at 0, forgetting the reverse edge, and building a list of lists in a language where the inner lists are shared (in Python, `[[]] * n` makes one list repeated `n` times, use a comprehension).
--cost
Building takes O(V + E) time and space. Listing the neighbours of one node costs O(its degree), where degree is the number of edges at that node.
--uses
u-adj
u-edge
--java
// edges[i] = {u, v}; returns adjacency lists
static List<List<Integer>> graphFromEdges(int n, int[][] edges, boolean directed) {
    if (!directed) return buildAdj(n, edges);          // piece: stores both directions
    List<List<Integer>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int[] e : edges) addEdge(adj, e[0], e[1], true);   // piece: one direction only
    return adj;
}
--py
def graph_from_edges(n, edges, directed=False):
    """edges is a list of (u, v). Returns adj where adj[u] lists the neighbours of u."""
    if not directed:
        return build_adj(n, edges)             # piece: stores both directions
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n)]               # not [[]] * n, that shares one list
    for u, v in edges:
        add_edge(adj, u, v, True)              # piece: one direction only
    return adj
--cpp
// edges[i] = {u, v}
vector<vector<int>> graphFromEdges(int n, const vector<pair<int,int>>& edges, bool directed) {
    if (!directed) return buildAdj(n, edges);          // piece: stores both directions
    vector<vector<int>> adj(n);
    for (auto [u, v] : edges) addEdge(adj, u, v, true);    // piece: one direction only
    return adj;
}
--go
// edges[i] = {u, v}; returns adjacency lists
func graphFromEdges(n int, edges [][2]int, directed bool) [][]int {
    if !directed {
        return buildAdj(n, edges) // piece: stores both directions
    }
    adj := make([][]int, n) // nil slices are fine as empty lists
    for _, e := range edges {
        addEdge(adj, e[0], e[1], true) // piece: one direction only
    }
    return adj
}
### adjmat
--idea
An adjacency matrix is a square table with one row and one column per node. The cell in row `u`, column `v` says whether there is an edge from `u` to `v` (1 or 0), or holds the edge cost. You can check any single edge in one step, just by looking at the cell.

The price is space: the table has `n` times `n` cells even if the graph has few edges. The matrix also has a neat algebraic property. Multiply it by itself and cell `(u, v)` becomes the number of walks of length 2 from `u` to `v`; the k-th power counts walks of length k. Floyd-Warshall (all-pairs shortest paths) also works directly on this table.
--build
1. Make an `n` by `n` table `m` of zeros: one row per node, each made with `filled(n, 0)` (code piece `u-fill`). For shortest paths use infinity for "no edge" and 0 on the diagonal instead.
2. For each edge `(u, v)`, set `m[u][v] = 1`. If undirected, also set `m[v][u] = 1`.
3. To test an edge, read `m[u][v]`. To list neighbours of `u`, scan the whole row `u`, which costs `n`.
4. To count walks of length `k`, compute the matrix power `m^k` by repeated squaring: multiply the result by `m` for each set bit of `k`, squaring `m` each step.
5. Matrix multiply: `c[i][j] = sum over t of a[i][t] * b[t][j]`. Use `long` and watch for overflow, or take results modulo a prime.
--think
- Signal: `n` is at most a couple of thousand and the question is "is there a direct edge", or the problem is dense (almost every pair is joined), or it asks for walks of exactly length k.
- If `n` is large and edges are few, a matrix wastes memory: 100,000 nodes would need ten billion cells. Use an adjacency list.
- A walk may repeat nodes and edges, a path may not. Matrix powers count walks. Do not confuse them.
- For reachability within k steps, use boolean multiplication (OR of ANDs) instead of counting, so numbers never overflow.
- Pitfalls: the diagonal (a node reaching itself), forgetting the symmetric cell for undirected edges, and integer overflow in counts. Walk counts grow exponentially.
--cost
Space O(V squared). Edge test O(1). Matrix multiply O(V cubed), so counting walks of length k costs O(V cubed log k).
--uses
u-fill
--java
static int[][] buildMatrix(int n, int[][] edges, boolean directed) {
    int[][] m = new int[n][];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) m[i] = filled(n, 0);      // piece: a row of n zeros
    for (int[] e : edges) {
        m[e[0]][e[1]] = 1;
        if (!directed) m[e[1]][e[0]] = 1;
    }
    return m;
}

static long[][] mul(long[][] a, long[][] b) {
    int n = a.length;
    long[][] c = new long[n][n];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        for (int t = 0; t < n; t++) {
            if (a[i][t] == 0) continue;
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) c[i][j] += a[i][t] * b[t][j];
        }
    return c;
}

// number of walks of exactly k edges between every pair
static long[][] walks(int[][] m, int k) {
    int n = m.length;
    long[][] base = new long[n][n], res = new long[n][n];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { res[i][i] = 1; for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) base[i][j] = m[i][j]; }
    for (; k > 0; k >>= 1) {                 // repeated squaring
        if ((k & 1) == 1) res = mul(res, base);
        base = mul(base, base);
    }
    return res;
}
--py
def build_matrix(n, edges, directed=False):
    m = [filled(n, 0) for _ in range(n)]       # piece: a row of n zeros
    for u, v in edges:
        m[u][v] = 1
        if not directed:
            m[v][u] = 1
    return m

def mat_mul(a, b):
    n = len(a)
    c = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        for t in range(n):
            if a[i][t]:
                for j in range(n):
                    c[i][j] += a[i][t] * b[t][j]
    return c

def walks(m, k):
    """number of walks with exactly k edges between every pair"""
    n = len(m)
    res = [[int(i == j) for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]   # identity
    base = [row[:] for row in m]
    while k:                                                    # repeated squaring
        if k & 1:
            res = mat_mul(res, base)
        base = mat_mul(base, base)
        k >>= 1
    return res
--cpp
typedef vector<vector<long long>> Mat;

vector<vector<int>> buildMatrix(int n, const vector<pair<int,int>>& edges, bool directed) {
    vector<vector<int>> m(n);
    for (auto& row : m) row = filled(n, 0);              // piece: a row of n zeros
    for (auto [u, v] : edges) {
        m[u][v] = 1;
        if (!directed) m[v][u] = 1;
    }
    return m;
}

Mat matMul(const Mat& a, const Mat& b) {
    int n = a.size();
    Mat c(n, vector<long long>(n, 0));
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        for (int t = 0; t < n; t++) {
            if (!a[i][t]) continue;
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) c[i][j] += a[i][t] * b[t][j];
        }
    return c;
}

// number of walks with exactly k edges between every pair
Mat walks(const vector<vector<int>>& m, int k) {
    int n = m.size();
    Mat base(n, vector<long long>(n)), res(n, vector<long long>(n, 0));
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { res[i][i] = 1; for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) base[i][j] = m[i][j]; }
    for (; k > 0; k >>= 1) {                  // repeated squaring
        if (k & 1) res = matMul(res, base);
        base = matMul(base, base);
    }
    return res;
}
--go
func buildMatrix(n int, edges [][2]int, directed bool) [][]int {
    m := make([][]int, n)
    for i := range m {
        m[i] = filled(n, 0) // piece: a row of n zeros
    }
    for _, e := range edges {
        m[e[0]][e[1]] = 1
        if !directed {
            m[e[1]][e[0]] = 1
        }
    }
    return m
}

type Mat [][]int64

func mul(a, b Mat) Mat {
    n := len(a)
    c := make(Mat, n)
    for i := range c {
        c[i] = make([]int64, n)
        for t := 0; t < n; t++ {
            if a[i][t] == 0 {
                continue
            }
            for j := 0; j < n; j++ {
                c[i][j] += a[i][t] * b[t][j]
            }
        }
    }
    return c
}

// number of walks of exactly k edges between every pair
func walks(m [][]int, k int) Mat {
    n := len(m)
    base, res := make(Mat, n), make(Mat, n)
    for i := 0; i < n; i++ {
        base[i], res[i] = make([]int64, n), make([]int64, n)
        res[i][i] = 1 // identity
        for j := 0; j < n; j++ {
            base[i][j] = int64(m[i][j])
        }
    }
    for ; k > 0; k >>= 1 { // repeated squaring
        if k&1 == 1 {
            res = mul(res, base)
        }
        base = mul(base, base)
    }
    return res
}
### bfs
--idea
Breadth-first search (BFS) explores a graph in rings around the start: first the start itself, then everything one edge away, then everything two edges away, and so on. Picture a stone dropped in a pond: the ripple reaches nearby points first. Because of this order, the first time BFS reaches a node it has found the route with the fewest edges.

The tool that makes the rings is a queue (first in, first out). Nodes found earlier are processed earlier, so every node at distance `d` is handled before any node at distance `d + 1`. Each node is marked the moment it is put in the queue, so it is queued at most once.
--build
1. Make `dist` of size `n`, filled with `-1` meaning "not reached yet". Set `dist[src] = 0`.
2. Put `src` in a queue.
3. While the queue is not empty, take `v` from the front.
4. For each neighbour `w` of `v`: if `dist[w] == -1`, set `dist[w] = dist[v] + 1`, set `parent[w] = v`, and push `w`.
5. When the queue is empty, `dist[w]` is the fewest edges from `src` to `w`, or `-1` if unreachable.
6. Invariant: the queue always holds nodes of distance `d` followed by nodes of distance `d + 1`, never more than two levels. That is why distances come out right.
7. To get the actual path, walk from the target back through `parent` until you reach `src`, then reverse.
--think
- Signal: "minimum number of steps/moves/edges", "nearest", "level by level", an unweighted graph, or a puzzle where each move costs the same.
- Decide what the nodes are. For a puzzle, a node is a whole configuration of the puzzle and an edge is one legal move.
- If edges have different costs, BFS is wrong. Use Dijkstra (positive costs) or 0-1 BFS (costs 0 or 1).
- If you need to start from many places at once, use multi-source BFS. If you know both ends, bidirectional search is faster.
- Mark nodes when you push them, not when you pop them. Marking late lets the same node enter the queue many times and can blow up the running time.
- Pitfalls: not handling disconnected graphs (nodes stay at `-1`), and forgetting the start in a graph given 1-indexed.
--cost
Time O(V + E), space O(V). Each node is queued once and each edge looked at once (twice if undirected).
--java
// returns dist[]: fewest edges from src, -1 if unreachable
static int[] bfs(List<List<Integer>> adj, int src) {
    int n = adj.size();
    int[] dist = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(dist, -1);
    ArrayDeque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    dist[src] = 0;
    q.add(src);
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int v = q.poll();
        for (int w : adj.get(v)) {
            if (dist[w] == -1) {              // first visit is the shortest
                dist[w] = dist[v] + 1;
                q.add(w);
            }
        }
    }
    return dist;
}
--py
from collections import deque

def bfs(adj, src):
    """Fewest edges from src to every node, -1 if unreachable."""
    dist = [-1] * len(adj)
    dist[src] = 0
    q = deque([src])
    while q:
        v = q.popleft()
        for w in adj[v]:
            if dist[w] == -1:             # first visit is the shortest
                dist[w] = dist[v] + 1
                q.append(w)
    return dist
--cpp
// returns dist: fewest edges from src, -1 if unreachable
vector<int> bfs(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int src) {
    vector<int> dist(adj.size(), -1);
    queue<int> q;
    dist[src] = 0;
    q.push(src);
    while (!q.empty()) {
        int v = q.front(); q.pop();
        for (int w : adj[v]) {
            if (dist[w] == -1) {              // first visit is the shortest
                dist[w] = dist[v] + 1;
                q.push(w);
            }
        }
    }
    return dist;
}
--go
// returns dist[]: fewest edges from src, -1 if unreachable
func bfsDist(adj [][]int, src int) []int {
    dist := make([]int, len(adj))
    for i := range dist {
        dist[i] = -1
    }
    dist[src] = 0
    q := []int{src}
    for len(q) > 0 {
        v := q[0]
        q = q[1:] // pop from the front
        for _, w := range adj[v] {
            if dist[w] == -1 { // first visit is the shortest
                dist[w] = dist[v] + 1
                q = append(q, w)
            }
        }
    }
    return dist
}
### dfs
--idea
Depth-first search (DFS) explores a graph by going as deep as it can: from the current node, step to an unvisited neighbour, and keep going. When a node has no unvisited neighbours left, it backs up to the previous node and tries the next neighbour there. It is like exploring a maze with a ball of string: you walk down one corridor to the end, then return to the last junction and try another.

The insight is that DFS keeps a stack of "where I came from" (the call stack if recursive). Because it finishes a node only after everything reachable from it is finished, the order in which nodes start and finish carries structure: it powers cycle detection, topological sort, strongly connected components and bridges.
--build
1. Make `visited` of size `n`, all false.
2. Define `visit(v)`: mark `v` visited, record it in the order list (this is the preorder, the moment of entering), then for each neighbour `w` of `v` that is not visited, call `visit(w)`.
3. Call `visit(src)`. For a whole graph that may be disconnected, loop over every node and call `visit` on each unvisited one.
4. The moment `visit(v)` returns is the finish time of `v`. Record it too if you need postorder.
5. The code below avoids recursion: it keeps an explicit stack of nodes and a pointer `it[v]` saying which neighbour to try next. This gives exactly the same order as recursion and cannot overflow the call stack on a long chain of 100,000 nodes.
6. Invariant: the stack always holds a path from the start to the current node, and every node on it is visited but not finished.
--think
- Signal: "can I reach", "is there a cycle", "all connected pieces", "any order that respects dependencies", "explore every possibility" (backtracking is DFS on a tree of choices).
- DFS does not find shortest paths in an unweighted graph. Use BFS for that.
- Know the edge types in a directed graph: tree edges (to a new node), back edges (to a node still on the stack, which means a cycle), forward and cross edges (to an already finished node). Many algorithms are just "look at the back edges".
- In a language with a small recursion limit (Python, or Java with default stack size), a deep graph crashes recursion. Use the explicit stack version, or raise the limit and run in a big thread.
- Pitfalls: forgetting to mark visited before recursing (infinite loop on cycles), and in undirected graphs treating the edge back to the parent as a cycle.
--cost
Time O(V + E), space O(V) for the visited array and stack.
--java
// returns nodes in the order DFS first enters them
static List<Integer> dfsOrder(List<List<Integer>> adj, int src) {
    int n = adj.size();
    boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
    int[] it = new int[n];                    // next neighbour index to try per node
    List<Integer> order = new ArrayList<>();
    ArrayDeque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
    seen[src] = true; order.add(src); st.push(src);
    while (!st.isEmpty()) {
        int v = st.peek();
        if (it[v] < adj.get(v).size()) {
            int w = adj.get(v).get(it[v]++);
            if (!seen[w]) { seen[w] = true; order.add(w); st.push(w); }
        } else {
            st.pop();                          // v is finished
        }
    }
    return order;
}
--py
def dfs_order(adj, src):
    """Nodes in the order DFS first enters them (iterative, no recursion limit)."""
    seen = [False] * len(adj)
    it = [0] * len(adj)                   # next neighbour index to try per node
    order = [src]
    seen[src] = True
    stack = [src]
    while stack:
        v = stack[-1]
        if it[v] < len(adj[v]):
            w = adj[v][it[v]]
            it[v] += 1
            if not seen[w]:
                seen[w] = True
                order.append(w)
                stack.append(w)
        else:
            stack.pop()                   # v is finished
    return order
--cpp
// nodes in the order DFS first enters them
vector<int> dfsOrder(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int src) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<char> seen(n, 0);
    vector<int> it(n, 0), order, st;          // it[v]: next neighbour index to try
    seen[src] = 1; order.push_back(src); st.push_back(src);
    while (!st.empty()) {
        int v = st.back();
        if (it[v] < (int)adj[v].size()) {
            int w = adj[v][it[v]++];
            if (!seen[w]) { seen[w] = 1; order.push_back(w); st.push_back(w); }
        } else {
            st.pop_back();                    // v is finished
        }
    }
    return order;
}
--go
// returns nodes in the order DFS first enters them
func dfsOrder(adj [][]int, src int) []int {
    seen := make([]bool, len(adj))
    it := make([]int, len(adj)) // next neighbour index to try per node
    order := []int{src}
    seen[src] = true
    st := []int{src}
    for len(st) > 0 {
        v := st[len(st)-1]
        if it[v] < len(adj[v]) {
            w := adj[v][it[v]]
            it[v]++
            if !seen[w] {
                seen[w] = true
                order = append(order, w)
                st = append(st, w)
            }
        } else {
            st = st[:len(st)-1] // v is finished
        }
    }
    return order
}
### gridbfs
--idea
Many problems give a map instead of a graph: a grid of cells, some open and some walls. You can treat every open cell as a node, and the edges join a cell to its open neighbours up, down, left and right (sometimes also diagonals). You never build the graph; you compute the neighbours on the fly from the row and column.

BFS on this implicit graph gives the fewest moves from one cell to another. Think of water poured at the start cell, spreading one step per tick.
--build
1. The four moves come from the piece `neighbors(r, c, rows, cols)` (code piece `u-nbr`): it returns the in-bounds cells up, down, left and right of `(r, c)`. For eight directions you would add the diagonals to that list.
2. Make `dist[r][c] = -1` for every cell. Set the start cell to 0 and push it.
3. Pop `(r, c)`. Loop over `neighbors(r, c, R, C)`. Because that call already throws away cells outside the grid, only two more checks remain.
4. Skip a neighbour if it is a wall or already has a distance.
5. Otherwise set `dist[nr][nc] = dist[r][c] + 1` and push it.
6. Stop early when you pop the target, or read `dist[target]` at the end.
--think
- Signal: a map of characters, "shortest path in a maze", "minimum moves", "spread" or "infect" per time step.
- Decide the node. If only position matters, node = cell. If extra state matters (keys collected, one wall-break left, direction), node = (cell, state) and `dist` gains a dimension.
- If different cells cost different amounts, use Dijkstra. If costs are only 0 or 1, use 0-1 BFS.
- If many cells start at once (several fires), use multi-source BFS.
- Pitfalls: swapping row and column, checking the bounds after indexing, forgetting the start may itself be a wall, and treating the target equal to the start (answer 0).
- Encode a cell as one integer `r * cols + c` when you need it as a map key or in a flat array.
--cost
Time O(R times C) for an R by C grid, space O(R times C).
--uses
u-nbr
--java
// grid[r] is a string of '.' (open) and '#' (wall); returns fewest moves or -1
static int gridShortest(String[] grid, int sr, int sc, int tr, int tc) {
    int R = grid.length, C = grid[0].length();
    int[][] dist = new int[R][C];
    for (int[] row : dist) Arrays.fill(row, -1);
    ArrayDeque<int[]> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    if (grid[sr].charAt(sc) == '#') return -1;
    dist[sr][sc] = 0;
    q.add(new int[]{sr, sc});
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int[] cur = q.poll();
        if (cur[0] == tr && cur[1] == tc) return dist[tr][tc];
        for (int[] nb : neighbors(cur[0], cur[1], R, C)) {          // piece: in-bounds cells
            int nr = nb[0], nc = nb[1];
            if (grid[nr].charAt(nc) == '#' || dist[nr][nc] != -1) continue;
            dist[nr][nc] = dist[cur[0]][cur[1]] + 1;
            q.add(new int[]{nr, nc});
        }
    }
    return -1;
}
--py
from collections import deque

def grid_shortest(grid, sr, sc, tr, tc):
    """grid is a list of strings with '.' open and '#' wall. Fewest moves or -1."""
    R, C = len(grid), len(grid[0])
    if grid[sr][sc] == '#':
        return -1
    dist = [[-1] * C for _ in range(R)]
    dist[sr][sc] = 0
    q = deque([(sr, sc)])
    while q:
        r, c = q.popleft()
        if (r, c) == (tr, tc):
            return dist[r][c]
        for nr, nc in neighbors(r, c, R, C):          # piece: in-bounds cells
            if grid[nr][nc] != '#' and dist[nr][nc] == -1:
                dist[nr][nc] = dist[r][c] + 1
                q.append((nr, nc))
    return -1
--cpp
// grid[r] is a string of '.' (open) and '#' (wall); returns fewest moves or -1
int gridShortest(const vector<string>& grid, int sr, int sc, int tr, int tc) {
    int R = grid.size(), C = grid[0].size();
    if (grid[sr][sc] == '#') return -1;
    vector<vector<int>> dist(R, vector<int>(C, -1));
    queue<pair<int,int>> q;
    dist[sr][sc] = 0;
    q.push({sr, sc});
    while (!q.empty()) {
        auto [r, c] = q.front(); q.pop();
        if (r == tr && c == tc) return dist[r][c];
        for (auto [nr, nc] : neighbors(r, c, R, C)) {          // piece: in-bounds cells
            if (grid[nr][nc] == '#' || dist[nr][nc] != -1) continue;
            dist[nr][nc] = dist[r][c] + 1;
            q.push({nr, nc});
        }
    }
    return -1;
}
--go
// grid[r] is a string of '.' (open) and '#' (wall); returns fewest moves or -1
func gridShortest(grid []string, sr, sc, tr, tc int) int {
    R, C := len(grid), len(grid[0])
    dist := make([][]int, R)
    for r := range dist {
        dist[r] = make([]int, C)
        for c := range dist[r] {
            dist[r][c] = -1
        }
    }
    if grid[sr][sc] == '#' {
        return -1
    }
    dist[sr][sc] = 0
    q := [][2]int{{sr, sc}}
    for len(q) > 0 {
        cur := q[0]
        q = q[1:]
        if cur[0] == tr && cur[1] == tc {
            return dist[tr][tc]
        }
        for _, nb := range neighbors(cur[0], cur[1], R, C) { // piece: in-bounds cells
            nr, nc := nb[0], nb[1]
            if grid[nr][nc] == '#' || dist[nr][nc] != -1 {
                continue
            }
            dist[nr][nc] = dist[cur[0]][cur[1]] + 1
            q = append(q, nb)
        }
    }
    return -1
}
### floodfill
--idea
Flood fill takes one cell of a grid and spreads to every connected cell with the same value, repainting them. It is the paint-bucket tool in a drawing program. It also counts islands, measures the size of a region, and finds out whether two cells are in the same region.

It is just a graph search (DFS or BFS) on a grid where two neighbouring cells are joined when they have the same value. The only twist is that painting a cell with a new colour also acts as the "visited" mark.
--build
1. Read `old = grid[r][c]`. If `old == newColor`, return at once, otherwise the fill would never stop (the cells never look "new").
2. Push `(r, c)` on a stack and paint it `newColor`.
3. Pop a cell. Loop over `neighbors(x, y, rows, cols)` (code piece `u-nbr`, the in-bounds cells up, down, left and right). For each one that still has colour `old`, paint it `newColor` and push it.
4. Repeat until the stack is empty.
5. Invariant: every cell on the stack is already painted, and every cell that still has colour `old` and touches a painted cell is either on the stack or will be reached from one.
6. To count islands, scan every cell and run a fill from each unpainted land cell, adding one each time.
--think
- Signal: "connected region of same value", "number of islands", "enclosed area", "paint", "largest blob".
- Use a stack or queue instead of recursion on big grids: a 1000 by 1000 grid can be a million cells deep.
- Choose BFS or DFS freely when you only need the region. Choose BFS when you need distances.
- Neighbour rule is part of the problem: 4-way or 8-way. Check which one the statement means.
- Regions touching the border (like "surrounded regions") are solved backwards: fill from the border first, then everything not reached is enclosed.
- Pitfall: `old == newColor` infinite loop, and modifying the input when you should not (copy first).
--cost
Time O(R times C), space O(R times C) in the worst case for the stack.
--uses
u-nbr
--java
// repaints the region of (r, c) with newColor, returns number of cells repainted
static int floodFill(int[][] g, int r, int c, int newColor) {
    int old = g[r][c];
    if (old == newColor) return 0;                  // otherwise it never ends
    int R = g.length, C = g[0].length, count = 1;
    ArrayDeque<int[]> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
    g[r][c] = newColor;
    st.push(new int[]{r, c});
    while (!st.isEmpty()) {
        int[] cur = st.pop();
        for (int[] nb : neighbors(cur[0], cur[1], R, C)) {      // piece: in-bounds cells
            if (g[nb[0]][nb[1]] == old) {
                g[nb[0]][nb[1]] = newColor;         // painting also marks it visited
                count++;
                st.push(nb);
            }
        }
    }
    return count;
}
--py
def flood_fill(g, r, c, new_color):
    """Repaints the region of (r, c) in place; returns number of cells repainted."""
    old = g[r][c]
    if old == new_color:                  # otherwise it never ends
        return 0
    R, C = len(g), len(g[0])
    g[r][c] = new_color
    count = 1
    stack = [(r, c)]
    while stack:
        x, y = stack.pop()
        for nx, ny in neighbors(x, y, R, C):      # piece: in-bounds cells
            if g[nx][ny] == old:
                g[nx][ny] = new_color     # painting also marks it visited
                count += 1
                stack.append((nx, ny))
    return count
--cpp
// repaints the region of (r, c) with newColor, returns number of cells repainted
int floodFill(vector<vector<int>>& g, int r, int c, int newColor) {
    int old = g[r][c];
    if (old == newColor) return 0;                  // otherwise it never ends
    int R = g.size(), C = g[0].size(), count = 1;
    vector<pair<int,int>> st;
    g[r][c] = newColor;
    st.push_back({r, c});
    while (!st.empty()) {
        auto [x, y] = st.back(); st.pop_back();
        for (auto [nx, ny] : neighbors(x, y, R, C)) {          // piece: in-bounds cells
            if (g[nx][ny] == old) {
                g[nx][ny] = newColor;               // painting also marks it visited
                count++;
                st.push_back({nx, ny});
            }
        }
    }
    return count;
}
--go
// repaints the region of (r, c) with newColor, returns number of cells repainted
func floodFill(g [][]int, r, c, newColor int) int {
    old := g[r][c]
    if old == newColor { // otherwise it never ends
        return 0
    }
    R, C, count := len(g), len(g[0]), 1
    g[r][c] = newColor
    st := [][2]int{{r, c}}
    for len(st) > 0 {
        cur := st[len(st)-1]
        st = st[:len(st)-1]
        for _, nb := range neighbors(cur[0], cur[1], R, C) { // piece: in-bounds cells
            if g[nb[0]][nb[1]] == old {
                g[nb[0]][nb[1]] = newColor // painting also marks it visited
                count++
                st = append(st, nb)
            }
        }
    }
    return count
}
### components
--idea
A connected component of an undirected graph is a maximal group of nodes where any two can reach each other. A graph that looks like several separate islands has several components. Finding them tells you which nodes belong together, how many groups there are, and how big each is.

The method: scan the nodes in order. When you meet a node that has no label yet, it starts a new component, and a DFS or BFS from it labels everything reachable. Nothing reachable can have been labelled earlier by a different component, because reachability is symmetric in an undirected graph.
--build
1. Make `comp` of size `n` filled with `-1` (no label). Set `count = 0`.
2. For each node `s` from 0 to `n - 1`: if `comp[s] == -1`, this is a new component.
3. Label `comp[s] = count`, then run a search from `s` (the code uses an explicit stack) that labels every reachable unlabelled node with `count`.
4. Increase `count` by one after the search ends.
5. At the end `comp[v]` is the component id of `v` and `count` is the number of components. Two nodes are connected exactly when their ids are equal.
6. Invariant: before the search from `s`, every labelled node has all its neighbours labelled too, so the labelled part is a union of whole components.
--think
- Signal: "groups", "clusters", "are A and B connected", "how many separate networks", "minimum edges to join everything" (that is components minus one).
- For a static graph, DFS or BFS is enough. If edges are added over time and you must answer connectivity as they come, use a disjoint set union (`dsu`). If edges are also removed, see `dynconn`.
- For directed graphs "connected" has two meanings. Weak components ignore direction (use this same code on the undirected version). Strong components need `scc`.
- Component sizes are often what the problem wants: keep a counter per label.
- Pitfalls: isolated nodes are components of size one, so do not skip them, and the answer for "edges to connect all" is `count - 1`, not `count`.
--cost
Time O(V + E), space O(V).
--java
// returns comp[v], component ids 0..count-1 (count = max id + 1)
static int[] components(List<List<Integer>> adj) {
    int n = adj.size(), count = 0;
    int[] comp = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(comp, -1);
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        if (comp[s] != -1) continue;                // already labelled
        ArrayDeque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
        comp[s] = count; st.push(s);
        while (!st.isEmpty()) {
            int v = st.pop();
            for (int w : adj.get(v))
                if (comp[w] == -1) { comp[w] = count; st.push(w); }
        }
        count++;                                    // next unlabelled node starts a new one
    }
    return comp;
}
--py
def components(adj):
    """Returns (comp, count): comp[v] is the component id of v."""
    n = len(adj)
    comp = [-1] * n
    count = 0
    for s in range(n):
        if comp[s] != -1:                 # already labelled
            continue
        comp[s] = count
        stack = [s]
        while stack:
            v = stack.pop()
            for w in adj[v]:
                if comp[w] == -1:
                    comp[w] = count
                    stack.append(w)
        count += 1                        # next unlabelled node starts a new one
    return comp, count
--cpp
// returns comp[v]; ids are 0..count-1, count = max id + 1
vector<int> components(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
    int n = adj.size(), count = 0;
    vector<int> comp(n, -1);
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        if (comp[s] != -1) continue;                // already labelled
        vector<int> st = {s};
        comp[s] = count;
        while (!st.empty()) {
            int v = st.back(); st.pop_back();
            for (int w : adj[v])
                if (comp[w] == -1) { comp[w] = count; st.push_back(w); }
        }
        count++;                                    // next unlabelled node starts a new one
    }
    return comp;
}
--go
// returns comp[v], component ids 0..count-1 (count = max id + 1)
func components(adj [][]int) []int {
    comp := make([]int, len(adj))
    for i := range comp {
        comp[i] = -1
    }
    count := 0
    for s := range adj {
        if comp[s] != -1 { // already labelled
            continue
        }
        comp[s] = count
        st := []int{s}
        for len(st) > 0 {
            v := st[len(st)-1]
            st = st[:len(st)-1]
            for _, w := range adj[v] {
                if comp[w] == -1 {
                    comp[w] = count
                    st = append(st, w)
                }
            }
        }
        count++ // next unlabelled node starts a new one
    }
    return comp
}
### treetrav
--idea
A binary tree node has at most two children, left and right. A traversal is a rule for visiting every node exactly once. The three classic depth-first orders differ only in when the node itself is visited compared to its two subtrees: preorder is node, left, right; inorder is left, node, right; postorder is left, right, node.

Each order has a use. Inorder on a binary search tree gives the keys in sorted order. Preorder lets you copy or serialize a tree, since the parent comes before its children. Postorder processes children before parents, which is what you want when a node's answer depends on its subtrees (sizes, heights, deleting a tree).
--build
1. Store the tree as arrays `left[v]` and `right[v]`, with `-1` for "no child", and a `root`.
2. Write `go(v)`: if `v == -1`, return.
3. Preorder: record `v`, then `go(left)`, then `go(right)`.
4. Inorder: `go(left)`, record `v`, then `go(right)`.
5. Postorder: `go(left)`, `go(right)`, record `v`.
6. The three are the same function with the record line moved. The code below runs all three in one walk by recording `v` at three points: on entering, between children, and on leaving.
7. Level order (row by row) is different: it is BFS with a queue, see `bfs`.
--think
- Signal: a rooted tree where each node's result depends on its children (use postorder), or where you need a sorted listing of a search tree (inorder), or where you copy, print or serialize the tree (preorder).
- A node's depth comes from preorder thinking (pass the depth down). A node's height or subtree size comes from postorder thinking (return the value up).
- For a general tree with many children, the same idea holds: preorder visits the node before all its children, postorder after.
- Recursion depth equals tree height. A degenerate tree (a chain) of 100,000 nodes overflows the call stack in many languages. Use an explicit stack, or Morris traversal if you need O(1) extra space.
- Pitfalls: forgetting the empty-child case, and mixing up which of the three the problem wants. Given preorder and inorder you can rebuild the tree uniquely, but preorder and postorder alone cannot always do so.
--cost
Time O(n), space O(h) where h is the height of the tree.
--java
// returns {preorder, inorder, postorder} lists of node ids
static List<List<Integer>> treeOrders(int[] left, int[] right, int root) {
    List<List<Integer>> res = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) res.add(new ArrayList<>());
    walk(left, right, root, res);
    return res;
}

static void walk(int[] left, int[] right, int v, List<List<Integer>> res) {
    if (v == -1) return;
    res.get(0).add(v);                       // preorder: before children
    walk(left, right, left[v], res);
    res.get(1).add(v);                       // inorder: between children
    walk(left, right, right[v], res);
    res.get(2).add(v);                       // postorder: after children
}
--py
def tree_orders(left, right, root):
    """left[v] and right[v] are child ids or -1. Returns (pre, ino, post)."""
    pre, ino, post = [], [], []

    def walk(v):
        if v == -1:
            return
        pre.append(v)                     # before children
        walk(left[v])
        ino.append(v)                     # between children
        walk(right[v])
        post.append(v)                    # after children

    walk(root)
    return pre, ino, post
--cpp
// results are returned in pre, in, post (node ids)
void walkTree(const vector<int>& left, const vector<int>& right, int v,
              vector<int>& pre, vector<int>& in, vector<int>& post) {
    if (v == -1) return;
    pre.push_back(v);                         // before children
    walkTree(left, right, left[v], pre, in, post);
    in.push_back(v);                          // between children
    walkTree(left, right, right[v], pre, in, post);
    post.push_back(v);                        // after children
}

array<vector<int>, 3> treeOrders(const vector<int>& left, const vector<int>& right, int root) {
    array<vector<int>, 3> res;
    walkTree(left, right, root, res[0], res[1], res[2]);
    return res;
}
--go
// returns {preorder, inorder, postorder} lists of node ids
func treeOrders(left, right []int, root int) [3][]int {
    var res [3][]int
    var walk func(v int)
    walk = func(v int) {
        if v == -1 {
            return
        }
        res[0] = append(res[0], v) // preorder: before children
        walk(left[v])
        res[1] = append(res[1], v) // inorder: between children
        walk(right[v])
        res[2] = append(res[2], v) // postorder: after children
    }
    walk(root)
    return res
}
### cycleund
--idea
A cycle is a path that starts and ends at the same node without reusing an edge. In an undirected graph, a connected piece with no cycle is a tree, and it has exactly one fewer edge than nodes. So "does this graph have a cycle" is the same as "is it a forest (a set of trees)".

DFS finds a cycle like this: while exploring, if you meet a neighbour that is already visited and it is not the edge you just came along, you have found a second route to a node you already reached, which closes a loop.
--build
1. Build adjacency lists where each entry holds the neighbour and the id of the edge. Edge ids matter because two parallel edges between the same pair form a cycle, and node-based parent checks cannot see that.
2. Make `seen` all false. For each unvisited node, start a DFS with `parentEdge = -1`.
3. In the DFS at `v`: for each `(w, id)` in `adj[v]`, skip it if `id == parentEdge` (that is the edge we arrived by).
4. If `w` is already seen, a cycle exists: return true.
5. Otherwise mark `w` and recurse into `w` with `parentEdge = id`.
6. If every start finishes with no hit, there is no cycle.
7. Shortcut with counting: a graph with `n` nodes and `c` components is a forest exactly when `E = n - c`. Any extra edge means a cycle. A disjoint set union (`dsu`) gives the same answer: an edge whose two ends are already in one set closes a cycle.
--think
- Signal: "is it a tree", "redundant edge", "does adding this edge create a loop", "can this be a valid network without loops".
- Undirected and directed cycle detection are different problems. Here, the edge back to the parent is not a cycle. In a directed graph you need the three-state check, see `cycledir`.
- If edges arrive one at a time and you must report the first that creates a cycle, use DSU, not repeated DFS.
- Use edge ids rather than the parent node, or you will miss a cycle made of two parallel edges.
- Pitfalls: self-loops (an edge from `v` to `v`) are cycles of length one, and recursion depth on long chains.
--cost
Time O(V + E), space O(V + E).
--java
// edges[i] = {u, v}. True if the undirected graph contains a cycle.
static boolean hasCycleUndirected(int n, int[][] edges) {
    List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int i = 0; i < edges.length; i++) {
        adj.get(edges[i][0]).add(new int[]{edges[i][1], i});
        adj.get(edges[i][1]).add(new int[]{edges[i][0], i});
    }
    boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++)
        if (!seen[s] && cycleFrom(adj, seen, s, -1)) return true;
    return false;
}

static boolean cycleFrom(List<List<int[]>> adj, boolean[] seen, int v, int parentEdge) {
    seen[v] = true;
    for (int[] e : adj.get(v)) {
        if (e[1] == parentEdge) continue;           // the edge we came along
        if (seen[e[0]]) return true;                // second route to a known node
        if (cycleFrom(adj, seen, e[0], e[1])) return true;
    }
    return false;
}
--py
import sys

def has_cycle_undirected(n, edges):
    """edges is a list of (u, v). True if the undirected graph has a cycle."""
    sys.setrecursionlimit(10000 + 2 * n)
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    for i, (u, v) in enumerate(edges):
        adj[u].append((v, i))
        adj[v].append((u, i))
    seen = [False] * n

    def go(v, parent_edge):
        seen[v] = True
        for w, eid in adj[v]:
            if eid == parent_edge:        # the edge we came along
                continue
            if seen[w] or go(w, eid):     # second route to a known node
                return True
        return False

    return any(not seen[s] and go(s, -1) for s in range(n))
--cpp
bool cycleFrom(const vector<vector<pair<int,int>>>& adj, vector<char>& seen, int v, int parentEdge) {
    seen[v] = 1;
    for (auto [w, id] : adj[v]) {
        if (id == parentEdge) continue;             // the edge we came along
        if (seen[w]) return true;                   // second route to a known node
        if (cycleFrom(adj, seen, w, id)) return true;
    }
    return false;
}

// edges[i] = {u, v}. True if the undirected graph contains a cycle.
bool hasCycleUndirected(int n, const vector<pair<int,int>>& edges) {
    vector<vector<pair<int,int>>> adj(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < (int)edges.size(); i++) {
        adj[edges[i].first].push_back({edges[i].second, i});
        adj[edges[i].second].push_back({edges[i].first, i});
    }
    vector<char> seen(n, 0);
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++)
        if (!seen[s] && cycleFrom(adj, seen, s, -1)) return true;
    return false;
}
--go
// edges[i] = {u, v}. True if the undirected graph contains a cycle.
func hasCycleUndirected(n int, edges [][2]int) bool {
    type arc struct{ to, id int }
    adj := make([][]arc, n)
    for i, e := range edges {
        adj[e[0]] = append(adj[e[0]], arc{e[1], i})
        adj[e[1]] = append(adj[e[1]], arc{e[0], i})
    }
    seen := make([]bool, n)
    var from func(v, parentEdge int) bool
    from = func(v, parentEdge int) bool {
        seen[v] = true
        for _, a := range adj[v] {
            if a.id == parentEdge { // the edge we came along
                continue
            }
            if seen[a.to] || from(a.to, a.id) { // second route to a known node
                return true
            }
        }
        return false
    }
    for s := 0; s < n; s++ {
        if !seen[s] && from(s, -1) {
            return true
        }
    }
    return false
}
### cycledir
--idea
In a directed graph, edges have a direction, and a cycle means you can follow arrows and come back to where you started. This matters for dependencies: if task A needs B, B needs C, and C needs A, none of them can ever start. Finding the cycle tells you the plan is impossible.

DFS gives each node one of three colours. White means not seen yet. Grey means it is on the current path (we entered it but have not finished). Black means finished, everything reachable from it was explored. If DFS ever steps onto a grey node, it has found a way back to its own path, which is a cycle.
--build
1. Make `state` of size `n`, all 0 (white). Use 1 for grey and 2 for black.
2. For each node `s` with `state[s] == 0`, call `visit(s)`.
3. `visit(v)`: set `state[v] = 1`. For each neighbour `w`: if `state[w] == 1`, return true (cycle). If `state[w] == 0` and `visit(w)` returns true, return true. Skip black nodes: they cannot lead back.
4. After all neighbours, set `state[v] = 2` and return false.
5. Invariant: the grey nodes form exactly the current DFS path. An edge into a grey node therefore points back up the path, closing a loop.
6. Why black is safe to skip: a black node finished without finding a cycle among everything reachable from it, and the graph is fixed, so entering it again cannot give a new answer.
7. An alternative is Kahn's algorithm (`kahn`): if a topological order cannot include all nodes, a cycle exists. It avoids recursion.
--think
- Signal: "dependencies", "prerequisites", "deadlock", "is this a valid order", "does the process ever loop". Directed edges.
- Two colours (visited or not) are not enough here. A node reached twice via different paths is not a cycle, so you must separate "on the current path" from "finished".
- If you also need the cycle itself, store `parent` pointers and, on hitting a grey `w` from `v`, walk back from `v` to `w`.
- On a functional graph (every node has one outgoing edge) there is a simpler method, see `funcgraph`.
- To find all nodes that lie on some cycle, use strongly connected components (`scc`): a component with more than one node, or a self-loop, is a cycle.
- Pitfalls: self-loops count (neighbour is the node itself, which is grey), and deep recursion on long chains.
--cost
Time O(V + E), space O(V).
--java
// adj is a directed graph. True if it has a cycle.
static boolean hasCycleDirected(List<List<Integer>> adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    int[] state = new int[n];                  // 0 white, 1 grey (on path), 2 black (done)
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++)
        if (state[s] == 0 && visitCycle(adj, state, s)) return true;
    return false;
}

static boolean visitCycle(List<List<Integer>> adj, int[] state, int v) {
    state[v] = 1;
    for (int w : adj.get(v)) {
        if (state[w] == 1) return true;        // back edge to the current path
        if (state[w] == 0 && visitCycle(adj, state, w)) return true;
    }
    state[v] = 2;
    return false;
}
--py
import sys

def has_cycle_directed(adj):
    """adj is a directed graph. True if it has a cycle."""
    sys.setrecursionlimit(10000 + 2 * len(adj))
    state = [0] * len(adj)                # 0 white, 1 grey (on path), 2 black (done)

    def visit(v):
        state[v] = 1
        for w in adj[v]:
            if state[w] == 1:             # back edge to the current path
                return True
            if state[w] == 0 and visit(w):
                return True
        state[v] = 2
        return False

    return any(state[s] == 0 and visit(s) for s in range(len(adj)))
--cpp
bool visitCycle(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, vector<int>& state, int v) {
    state[v] = 1;                                  // grey: on the current path
    for (int w : adj[v]) {
        if (state[w] == 1) return true;            // back edge to the current path
        if (state[w] == 0 && visitCycle(adj, state, w)) return true;
    }
    state[v] = 2;                                  // black: finished
    return false;
}

// adj is a directed graph. True if it has a cycle.
bool hasCycleDirected(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
    vector<int> state(adj.size(), 0);
    for (int s = 0; s < (int)adj.size(); s++)
        if (state[s] == 0 && visitCycle(adj, state, s)) return true;
    return false;
}
--go
// adj is a directed graph. True if it has a cycle.
func hasCycleDirected(adj [][]int) bool {
    state := make([]int, len(adj)) // 0 white, 1 grey (on path), 2 black (done)
    var visit func(v int) bool
    visit = func(v int) bool {
        state[v] = 1
        for _, w := range adj[v] {
            if state[w] == 1 { // back edge to the current path
                return true
            }
            if state[w] == 0 && visit(w) {
                return true
            }
        }
        state[v] = 2
        return false
    }
    for s := range adj {
        if state[s] == 0 && visit(s) {
            return true
        }
    }
    return false
}
### bipartite
--idea
A graph is bipartite if you can paint every node one of two colours so that no edge joins two nodes of the same colour. Think of splitting people into two teams so that every pair of rivals ends up on opposite teams. It models "two sides" situations: workers and jobs, students and clubs, left and right.

The test is a colouring game. Colour the start node 0. Everything next to it must be 1. Everything next to those must be 0, and so on. The colours are forced, so the only way to fail is to find an edge whose ends are forced to the same colour. That happens exactly when the graph has an odd cycle (a loop with an odd number of edges).
--build
1. Make `color` of size `n`, all `-1` (uncoloured).
2. For each uncoloured node `s` (the graph may be in several pieces), set `color[s] = 0` and push it on a queue.
3. Pop `v`. For each neighbour `w`: if `color[w] == -1`, set `color[w] = 1 - color[v]` and push `w`.
4. If `color[w] == color[v]`, the edge is inside one colour: the graph is not bipartite, stop.
5. If all pieces finish, `color` is a valid two-colouring.
6. Invariant: every coloured node has a colour forced by its distance from its start, even distance gets 0, odd gets 1. A clash means two nodes joined by an edge have the same parity of distance, which closes an odd cycle.
--think
- Signal: "split into two groups so that conflicting pairs are apart", "can this be two-coloured", "odd cycle", "assign two sides", or the setup of a matching problem (see `matching`).
- Check every component separately: the colouring of one piece does not constrain another.
- Bipartite is the same as "no odd cycle". A tree is always bipartite. A triangle never is.
- For more than two colours, it becomes graph colouring (`graphcolor`) which is hard in general.
- You can also do it with DFS or with a disjoint set union that stores parity. The BFS version is the simplest.
- Pitfalls: forgetting disconnected parts, and self-loops (a node joined to itself is a one-edge odd cycle, and the check `color[w] == color[v]` catches it).
--cost
Time O(V + E), space O(V).
--java
// returns color[] (0 or 1 per node), or null if the graph is not bipartite
static int[] bipartite(List<List<Integer>> adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    int[] color = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(color, -1);
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        if (color[s] != -1) continue;
        color[s] = 0;
        ArrayDeque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
        q.add(s);
        while (!q.isEmpty()) {
            int v = q.poll();
            for (int w : adj.get(v)) {
                if (color[w] == -1) { color[w] = 1 - color[v]; q.add(w); }
                else if (color[w] == color[v]) return null;   // odd cycle
            }
        }
    }
    return color;
}
--py
from collections import deque

def bipartite(adj):
    """Returns a list of 0/1 colours per node, or None if not bipartite."""
    color = [-1] * len(adj)
    for s in range(len(adj)):
        if color[s] != -1:
            continue
        color[s] = 0
        q = deque([s])
        while q:
            v = q.popleft()
            for w in adj[v]:
                if color[w] == -1:
                    color[w] = 1 - color[v]
                    q.append(w)
                elif color[w] == color[v]:        # odd cycle
                    return None
    return color
--cpp
// returns color per node (0 or 1), or an empty vector if the graph is not bipartite
vector<int> bipartite(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<int> color(n, -1);
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        if (color[s] != -1) continue;
        color[s] = 0;
        queue<int> q;
        q.push(s);
        while (!q.empty()) {
            int v = q.front(); q.pop();
            for (int w : adj[v]) {
                if (color[w] == -1) { color[w] = 1 - color[v]; q.push(w); }
                else if (color[w] == color[v]) return {};       // odd cycle
            }
        }
    }
    return color;
}
--go
// returns color[] (0 or 1 per node), or nil if the graph is not bipartite
func bipartite(adj [][]int) []int {
    color := make([]int, len(adj))
    for i := range color {
        color[i] = -1
    }
    for s := range adj {
        if color[s] != -1 {
            continue
        }
        color[s] = 0
        q := []int{s}
        for len(q) > 0 {
            v := q[0]
            q = q[1:]
            for _, w := range adj[v] {
                if color[w] == -1 {
                    color[w] = 1 - color[v]
                    q = append(q, w)
                } else if color[w] == color[v] { // odd cycle
                    return nil
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return color
}
### topo
--idea
A topological order of a directed graph lists every node so that for every edge `u -> v`, `u` appears before `v`. It is a valid schedule for tasks with prerequisites: build the foundation before the walls. It exists exactly when the graph has no directed cycle (such a graph is called a DAG, a directed acyclic graph).

The DFS method rests on one fact: in DFS, a node finishes only after everything reachable from it has finished. So if you write nodes down in the order they finish, then every node appears after all the nodes it points to. Reverse that list and every node comes before the nodes it points to.
--build
1. Make `state` of size `n` (0 not seen, 1 on the current path, 2 finished) and an empty list `order`.
2. For each node `s` with `state[s] == 0`, call `visit(s)`.
3. `visit(v)`: set `state[v] = 1`. For each neighbour `w`: if `state[w] == 1` there is a cycle, so no topological order exists, report failure. If `state[w] == 0`, call `visit(w)`.
4. After all neighbours, set `state[v] = 2` and append `v` to `order`.
5. When every start is done, reverse `order`. That is a topological order.
6. Invariant: when `v` is appended, every node reachable from `v` is already in `order`. So reversed, `v` precedes all of them.
7. Many valid orders usually exist. If a problem needs the smallest one in some sense, use Kahn's algorithm with a heap (`kahn`).
--think
- Signal: "order the tasks", "prerequisites", "build order", "dependency", "who goes before whom", or any DP that must be computed over a DAG in dependency order.
- First decide the direction of the edges. "A requires B" can be `B -> A` (B before A). A wrong direction gives the reverse order.
- If there is a cycle, no order exists. Detect it in the same pass, as above.
- Topological order is also the order in which to do DP on a DAG: longest path, number of paths, and shortest path in a DAG all take one pass in this order.
- DFS-based gives an order where one chain runs deep. Kahn's version runs level by level, easy to extend to "lexicographically smallest order" or "count the waves".
- Pitfalls: not handling disconnected pieces, and forgetting to reverse the finish order.
--cost
Time O(V + E), space O(V).
--java
// returns a topological order, or an empty list if there is a cycle
static List<Integer> topoSort(List<List<Integer>> adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    int[] state = new int[n];
    List<Integer> order = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++)
        if (state[s] == 0 && !topoVisit(adj, state, order, s)) return new ArrayList<>();
    Collections.reverse(order);                 // finish order reversed
    return order;
}

static boolean topoVisit(List<List<Integer>> adj, int[] state, List<Integer> order, int v) {
    state[v] = 1;
    for (int w : adj.get(v)) {
        if (state[w] == 1) return false;        // cycle
        if (state[w] == 0 && !topoVisit(adj, state, order, w)) return false;
    }
    state[v] = 2;
    order.add(v);                               // v finishes after all it points to
    return true;
}
--py
import sys

def topo_sort(adj):
    """Returns a topological order, or [] if the graph has a cycle."""
    sys.setrecursionlimit(10000 + 2 * len(adj))
    n = len(adj)
    state = [0] * n
    order = []

    def visit(v):
        state[v] = 1
        for w in adj[v]:
            if state[w] == 1:             # cycle
                return False
            if state[w] == 0 and not visit(w):
                return False
        state[v] = 2
        order.append(v)                   # v finishes after all it points to
        return True

    for s in range(n):
        if state[s] == 0 and not visit(s):
            return []
    return order[::-1]                    # finish order reversed
--cpp
bool topoVisit(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, vector<int>& state, vector<int>& order, int v) {
    state[v] = 1;
    for (int w : adj[v]) {
        if (state[w] == 1) return false;            // cycle
        if (state[w] == 0 && !topoVisit(adj, state, order, w)) return false;
    }
    state[v] = 2;
    order.push_back(v);                             // v finishes after all it points to
    return true;
}

// returns a topological order, or an empty vector if there is a cycle
vector<int> topoSort(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<int> state(n, 0), order;
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++)
        if (state[s] == 0 && !topoVisit(adj, state, order, s)) return {};
    reverse(order.begin(), order.end());            // finish order reversed
    return order;
}
--go
// returns a topological order, or nil if there is a cycle
func topoSort(adj [][]int) []int {
    state := make([]int, len(adj))
    var order []int
    var visit func(v int) bool
    visit = func(v int) bool {
        state[v] = 1
        for _, w := range adj[v] {
            if state[w] == 1 { // cycle
                return false
            }
            if state[w] == 0 && !visit(w) {
                return false
            }
        }
        state[v] = 2
        order = append(order, v) // v finishes after all it points to
        return true
    }
    for s := range adj {
        if state[s] == 0 && !visit(s) {
            return nil
        }
    }
    for i, j := 0, len(order)-1; i < j; i, j = i+1, j-1 { // finish order reversed
        order[i], order[j] = order[j], order[i]
    }
    return order
}
### kahn
--idea
Kahn's algorithm builds a topological order by repeatedly removing nodes that have no remaining prerequisites. The in-degree of a node is the number of edges pointing into it. A node with in-degree 0 has nothing it must wait for, so it can go next in the order. Take it out, and the nodes it pointed to lose one prerequisite each.

This is exactly how you would schedule a project: do everything that is ready, then see what became ready. If you run out of ready nodes while some remain, the remaining ones wait on each other in a cycle.
--build
1. Compute `indeg[v]` for every node by counting incoming edges.
2. Put every node with `indeg[v] == 0` in a queue, using `enqueue(q, v)` (code piece `u-queueop`).
3. While the queue is not empty: `v = dequeue(q)` and append `v` to `order`.
4. For each neighbour `w` of `v`, decrease `indeg[w]` by one. If it becomes 0, `enqueue(q, w)`.
5. At the end, if `order` has `n` nodes it is a valid topological order. If it has fewer, the leftover nodes are on or behind a cycle.
6. Invariant: a node enters the queue only when all its prerequisites are already in `order`, so every edge goes from an earlier position to a later one.
7. To get the smallest order by node number, replace the queue with a min-heap. To count waves (the number of rounds if everything ready runs in parallel), process the queue one level at a time.
--think
- Signal: same as `topo`: dependencies, prerequisites. Prefer Kahn when you need no recursion, the lexicographically smallest order, level-by-level rounds, or a clean cycle test (`len(order) < n`).
- Use DFS-based topological sort when you are already doing a DFS for other reasons, or you want the finish times.
- The longest chain of dependencies (minimum number of rounds) is the number of levels in Kahn.
- For DP over a DAG, process in this order and push values forward along edges.
- Pitfalls: in-degree counted wrongly when there are duplicate edges (count them consistently on both increment and decrement), and reporting a partial order as if it were complete.
--cost
Time O(V + E), space O(V). With a heap for the smallest order, O((V + E) log V).
--uses
u-queueop
--java
// returns a topological order, or an empty list if there is a cycle
static List<Integer> kahn(List<List<Integer>> adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    int[] indeg = new int[n];
    for (List<Integer> out : adj) for (int w : out) indeg[w]++;
    Deque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (indeg[v] == 0) enqueue(q, v);
    List<Integer> order = new ArrayList<>();
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int v = dequeue(q);
        order.add(v);
        for (int w : adj.get(v))
            if (--indeg[w] == 0) enqueue(q, w);       // last prerequisite done
    }
    return order.size() == n ? order : new ArrayList<>();   // fewer means a cycle
}
--py
def kahn(adj):
    """Topological order, or [] if there is a cycle."""
    n = len(adj)
    indeg = [0] * n
    for out in adj:
        for w in out:
            indeg[w] += 1
    q = deque()
    for v in range(n):
        if indeg[v] == 0:
            enqueue(q, v)
    order = []
    while q:
        v = dequeue(q)
        order.append(v)
        for w in adj[v]:
            indeg[w] -= 1
            if indeg[w] == 0:             # last prerequisite done
                enqueue(q, w)
    return order if len(order) == n else []     # fewer means a cycle
--cpp
// returns a topological order, or an empty vector if there is a cycle
vector<int> kahn(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<int> indeg(n, 0), order;
    for (auto& out : adj) for (int w : out) indeg[w]++;
    queue<int> q;
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (indeg[v] == 0) enqueue(q, v);
    while (!q.empty()) {
        int v = dequeue(q);
        order.push_back(v);
        for (int w : adj[v])
            if (--indeg[w] == 0) enqueue(q, w);           // last prerequisite done
    }
    return (int)order.size() == n ? order : vector<int>();   // fewer means a cycle
}
--go
// returns a topological order, or nil if there is a cycle
func kahn(adj [][]int) []int {
    n := len(adj)
    indeg := make([]int, n)
    for _, out := range adj {
        for _, w := range out {
            indeg[w]++
        }
    }
    var q, order []int
    for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
        if indeg[v] == 0 {
            enqueue(&q, v) // piece
        }
    }
    for len(q) > 0 {
        v := dequeue(&q) // piece
        order = append(order, v)
        for _, w := range adj[v] {
            if indeg[w]--; indeg[w] == 0 { // last prerequisite done
                enqueue(&q, w)
            }
        }
    }
    if len(order) != n { // fewer means a cycle
        return nil
    }
    return order
}
### msbfs
--idea
Multi-source BFS answers "how far is each node from its nearest source", where there are several sources at once. Instead of running BFS from each source, you put all of them in the queue at distance 0 and run a single BFS. The rings expand from all sources simultaneously, and each node gets the distance to whichever source reaches it first.

Imagine several fires starting at the same moment: the time a spot burns is the distance to the nearest fire. One pass handles any number of sources.
--build
1. Add one virtual node, number `n`, and give it an edge to every source. Call the new adjacency `g`: it is `adj` plus one extra row that lists the sources. The real edges do not change.
2. Call the piece `bfs(g, n)` (from `u-bfsloop`). It runs the usual BFS from the virtual node and returns `d`, the fewest edges from the virtual node to each node, `-1` if unreachable.
3. All sources are one edge from the virtual node, so they all get `d = 1` and enter the queue together. This is the same as pushing every source at distance 0 before the first pop.
4. The virtual edge cost one extra step for everyone, so subtract it: `dist[v] = d[v] - 1` for every real node, and keep `-1` as `-1`.
5. `dist[v]` is now the distance from `v` to its nearest source, or `-1` if no source reaches it.
6. Invariant: the queue is sorted by distance, as in normal BFS, because all the sources are at the same distance from the virtual node. The proof of correctness is identical.
7. The piece returns distances only. If you also need to know which source is nearest, write the loop yourself and copy `owner[w] = owner[v]` when you set a distance.
--think
- Signal: "distance to the nearest X", "time for everything to spread", "rotting oranges", "nearest gate for every room", "walls and gates", "for each cell, closest 1".
- Why it works: the virtual node is a super-source joined to all real sources. A normal BFS from it is this algorithm. Its edges count one step each here, so the code subtracts one at the end.
- Reversing the question helps. If asked "for each source, how far is the nearest target", run the search from the targets instead, since there are fewer of them or it becomes one pass.
- For weighted edges, the same trick works with Dijkstra: push all sources at distance 0.
- Pitfalls: pushing sources one at a time with a BFS between them (that is the slow wrong version), and forgetting that unreachable nodes stay `-1`.
--cost
Time O(V + E), the same as a single BFS no matter how many sources. Space O(V): the extra row and the distance array.
--uses
u-bfsloop
--java
// returns dist[v] = fewest edges from v to the nearest source, -1 if none reaches it
// bfs(adj, s) comes from the code piece
static int[] multiSourceBfs(List<List<Integer>> adj, int[] sources) {
    int n = adj.size();
    List<List<Integer>> g = new ArrayList<>(adj);          // same rows, plus one more
    List<Integer> start = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int s : sources) start.add(s);
    g.add(start);                                          // virtual node n points at every source
    int[] d = bfs(g, n);                                   // piece: distances from the virtual node
    int[] dist = new int[n];
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) dist[v] = d[v] > 0 ? d[v] - 1 : -1;   // drop the extra step
    return dist;
}
--py
from collections import deque     # the bfs piece uses it

def multi_source_bfs(adj, sources):
    """dist[v] = fewest edges from v to the nearest source, -1 if none reaches it."""
    n = len(adj)
    g = adj + [list(sources)]             # virtual node n points at every source
    d = bfs(g, n)                         # piece: distances from the virtual node
    return [x - 1 if x > 0 else -1 for x in d[:n]]   # drop the extra step
--cpp
// dist[v] = fewest edges from v to the nearest source, -1 if none reaches it
// bfs(adj, s) comes from the code piece
vector<int> multiSourceBfs(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, const vector<int>& sources) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<vector<int>> g = adj;
    g.push_back(sources);                                  // virtual node n points at every source
    vector<int> d = bfs(g, n);                             // piece: distances from the virtual node
    d.pop_back();                                          // forget the virtual node itself
    for (int& x : d) x = x > 0 ? x - 1 : -1;               // drop the extra step
    return d;
}
--go
// returns dist[v] = fewest edges from v to the nearest source, -1 if none reaches it
// bfs(adj, s) comes from the code piece
func multiSourceBfs(adj [][]int, sources []int) []int {
    n := len(adj)
    g := append(adj[:n:n], append([]int(nil), sources...)) // virtual node n points at every source
    d := bfs(g, n)                                         // piece: distances from the virtual node
    dist := make([]int, n)
    for v := range dist {
        dist[v] = -1
        if d[v] > 0 {
            dist[v] = d[v] - 1 // drop the extra step
        }
    }
    return dist
}
### sp01
--idea
0-1 BFS finds shortest paths when every edge costs either 0 or 1. Dijkstra would work but needs a heap. Here a double-ended queue (deque) is enough: edges of cost 0 keep you at the same distance, so you put that neighbour at the front of the line; edges of cost 1 move you one farther, so that neighbour goes to the back.

The deque always holds nodes whose distances are the current value `d` followed by nodes with `d + 1`, which is the same sorted order a heap would give, at constant cost per operation.
--build
1. Set `dist[src] = 0` and every other distance to infinity. Put `src` in a deque.
2. Pop from the front: `v`. (It may be a stale entry if `dist` improved since it was pushed. Checking is optional but cheap.)
3. For each edge `(v, w, c)` with `c` equal to 0 or 1: if `dist[v] + c < dist[w]`, set `dist[w] = dist[v] + c`.
4. If `c == 0`, push `w` at the front. If `c == 1`, push `w` at the back.
5. Repeat until the deque is empty.
6. Invariant: from front to back, the stored distances are non-decreasing and differ by at most 1. Cost-0 pushes to the front and cost-1 pushes to the back both keep that true.
7. A node may be pushed more than once (a first push at `d + 1`, a later push at `d`). That is fine; the stale copy is ignored when popped because its distance is not an improvement.
--think
- Signal: a grid or graph where some moves are free and others cost one: "minimum number of walls to break", "minimum turns", "flip the fewest cells", "minimum cost where each step is 0 or 1".
- Modelling trick: a cost-1 option is a "paid action" and a cost-0 is "go along". For "minimum direction changes in a grid", the node is the cell and the edge costs 0 if the arrow already points that way, 1 otherwise.
- Use plain BFS if all costs are 1. Use Dijkstra if costs can be other values. Costs {0, k} for small k can be done with a bucket queue (k lists), a generalization.
- It is not valid with negative costs.
- Pitfalls: stale entries (without the check you still get correct distances, only more work), and pushing to the wrong end.
--cost
Time O(V + E), space O(V).
--java
// adj.get(v) holds {neighbour, cost} with cost 0 or 1
static int[] bfs01(List<List<int[]>> adj, int src) {
    int n = adj.size();
    int[] dist = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(dist, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
    dist[src] = 0;
    ArrayDeque<Integer> dq = new ArrayDeque<>();
    dq.addFirst(src);
    while (!dq.isEmpty()) {
        int v = dq.pollFirst();
        for (int[] e : adj.get(v)) {
            int nd = dist[v] + e[1];
            if (nd < dist[e[0]]) {
                dist[e[0]] = nd;
                if (e[1] == 0) dq.addFirst(e[0]);    // free move: same distance, front
                else dq.addLast(e[0]);               // paid move: one farther, back
            }
        }
    }
    return dist;
}
--py
from collections import deque

def bfs01(adj, src):
    """adj[v] is a list of (neighbour, cost) with cost 0 or 1."""
    INF = float("inf")
    dist = [INF] * len(adj)
    dist[src] = 0
    dq = deque([src])
    while dq:
        v = dq.popleft()
        for w, c in adj[v]:
            nd = dist[v] + c
            if nd < dist[w]:
                dist[w] = nd
                if c == 0:
                    dq.appendleft(w)      # free move: same distance, front
                else:
                    dq.append(w)          # paid move: one farther, back
    return dist
--cpp
// adj[v] holds {neighbour, cost} with cost 0 or 1
vector<int> bfs01(const vector<vector<pair<int,int>>>& adj, int src) {
    vector<int> dist(adj.size(), INT_MAX);
    dist[src] = 0;
    deque<int> dq = {src};
    while (!dq.empty()) {
        int v = dq.front(); dq.pop_front();
        for (auto [w, c] : adj[v]) {
            if (dist[v] + c < dist[w]) {
                dist[w] = dist[v] + c;
                if (c == 0) dq.push_front(w);        // free move: same distance, front
                else dq.push_back(w);                // paid move: one farther, back
            }
        }
    }
    return dist;
}
--go
type edge struct{ to, cost int }

// adj[v] holds edges with cost 0 or 1; unreachable stays at 1<<60
func bfs01(adj [][]edge, src int) []int {
    dist := make([]int, len(adj))
    for i := range dist {
        dist[i] = 1 << 60
    }
    dist[src] = 0
    // deque in two parts: front is a stack (push and pop at its end), back is a queue read from index bi
    front, back := []int{src}, []int(nil)
    for bi := 0; len(front) > 0 || bi < len(back); {
        var v int
        if len(front) > 0 {
            v = front[len(front)-1]
            front = front[:len(front)-1]
        } else {
            v = back[bi]
            bi++
        }
        for _, e := range adj[v] {
            if nd := dist[v] + e.cost; nd < dist[e.to] {
                dist[e.to] = nd
                if e.cost == 0 { // free move: same distance, front
                    front = append(front, e.to)
                } else { // paid move: one farther, back
                    back = append(back, e.to)
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return dist
}
### treediam
--idea
The diameter of a tree is the length of the longest path between any two nodes. In a network of roads shaped like a tree, it is the two places that are farthest apart. Naively you would try every pair, which is far too slow for large trees.

There is a neat two-step trick. Start at any node and find the farthest node from it, call it `a`. Then find the farthest node from `a`, call it `b`. The path from `a` to `b` is a longest path. The reason: in a tree, a farthest node from any starting point is always one end of some diameter, so `a` is an endpoint and the farthest from an endpoint is the other endpoint.
--build
1. Store the tree as an adjacency list. This version measures length in edges, so a plain BFS gives the distances.
2. Write `farthestNode(src)`: run `bfs(adj, src)` (code piece `u-bfsloop`), which returns the distance from `src` to every node, then return the node with the largest distance.
3. Run `a = farthestNode(0)`.
4. Run `b = farthestNode(a)`.
5. The diameter length is `bfs(adj, a)[b]` (reuse the distances from step 4 in real code), and its two ends are `a` and `b`. Keep parent pointers in a BFS if you need the path itself.
6. For weighted edges, the same two passes work, but you must replace the BFS with a traversal that adds edge weights while walking (a DFS with a parent guard). Weights must be non-negative.
7. An alternative uses one DP pass: for every node, the longest downward path through its children; combine the best two children. That one is needed when you want the diameter of every subtree, or when node weights can be negative.
--think
- Signal: "longest path in a tree", "two farthest nodes", "maximum distance between two nodes", "farthest pair".
- The two-pass trick needs non-negative edge weights. With negative weights, use the DP pass instead.
- The center of a tree sits in the middle of this path (see `treecenter`). The eccentricity of any node (its farthest distance to another node) equals the larger of its distances to the two diameter ends.
- On a general graph with cycles this does not work. A longest path in a general graph is hard.
- Pitfalls: counting nodes versus counting edges (a path of `k` nodes has length `k - 1`), and, if you write the weighted version with recursion, a chain of a hundred thousand nodes overflowing the call stack.
--cost
Time O(V), space O(V). Two passes over the tree.
--uses
u-bfsloop
--java
// tree as adjacency lists; returns the diameter length in edges
static int treeDiameter(List<List<Integer>> adj) {
    int a = farthestNode(adj, 0);
    int b = farthestNode(adj, a);
    return bfs(adj, a)[b];                        // piece: distances from a
}

static int farthestNode(List<List<Integer>> adj, int src) {
    int[] dist = bfs(adj, src);                   // piece: distance from src to everyone
    int best = src;
    for (int v = 0; v < dist.length; v++) if (dist[v] > dist[best]) best = v;
    return best;
}
--py
def tree_diameter(adj):
    """Tree as adjacency lists. Returns the diameter length in edges."""
    def farthest(src):
        dist = bfs(adj, src)              # piece: distance from src to everyone
        return max(range(len(adj)), key=lambda v: dist[v])

    a = farthest(0)
    b = farthest(a)
    return bfs(adj, a)[b]
--cpp
int farthestNode(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int src) {
    vector<int> dist = bfs(adj, src);             // piece: distance from src to everyone
    int best = src;
    for (int v = 0; v < (int)dist.size(); v++) if (dist[v] > dist[best]) best = v;
    return best;
}

// tree as adjacency lists; returns the diameter length in edges
int treeDiameter(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
    int a = farthestNode(adj, 0);
    int b = farthestNode(adj, a);
    return bfs(adj, a)[b];
}
--go
// tree as adjacency lists; returns the diameter length in edges
func treeDiameter(adj [][]int) int {
    a := farthestNode(adj, 0)
    b := farthestNode(adj, a)
    return bfs(adj, a)[b] // piece: distances from a
}

func farthestNode(adj [][]int, src int) int {
    dist := bfs(adj, src) // piece: distance from src to everyone
    best := src
    for v := range dist {
        if dist[v] > dist[best] {
            best = v
        }
    }
    return best
}
### bellman
--idea
Bellman-Ford finds shortest paths from one start node even when some edge costs are negative. Dijkstra cannot do that, because it assumes a settled node can never be improved later, and a negative edge can break that. Bellman-Ford makes no such assumption. It simply tries to improve every edge, again and again, until nothing improves.

Why it ends: a shortest path never needs to use more than `n - 1` edges (a node would repeat otherwise, which only helps if there is a negative cycle). After round `k`, every node whose best path uses at most `k` edges has its final distance. So `n - 1` rounds are enough.
