If a further round still improves something, a negative cycle is reachable, and "shortest" has no meaning.
--build
1. Keep the graph as an edge list `(u, v, w)`. Set `dist[src] = 0` and all other distances to `INF` (from code piece `u-inf`, a big number that is still safe to add to; it is 1e9, so raise it if your path costs can reach that). Make `parent` filled with `-1`.
2. Repeat up to `n - 1` times: for every edge `(u, v, w)` whose start is reached (`dist[u]` below `INF`), call `relax(dist, parent, u, v, w)` (code piece `u-relax`). It sets `dist[v] = dist[u] + w` and `parent[v] = u` when that is shorter, and returns true if it changed anything.
3. If a whole round changes nothing, stop early. Everything is final.
4. Do one more round. If any edge still relaxes, a negative cycle is reachable from `src`: report it (the code returns `null` or an empty result).
5. Invariant: after round `k`, `dist[v]` is at most the cost of the cheapest path to `v` that uses at most `k` edges. The `dist` values are always the cost of some real walk, so they never go below the true answer.
6. To recover a path, walk `parent` back from the target (piece `u-parentpath` does this).
--think
- Signal: shortest path with negative costs, "arbitrage" (a currency loop that gains), "can the total keep dropping", or at most `k` edges allowed (run exactly `k` rounds on a copy of the old array each round).
- No negative edges: use Dijkstra, it is much faster. Negative edges but a sparse graph and speed matters: SPFA (`spfa`) is usually faster in practice, but has the same worst case. All pairs with negative edges: Johnson (`johnson`) or Floyd-Warshall.
- Only cycles reachable from `src` are caught by this version. For a negative cycle anywhere in the graph, see `negcycle`.
- Undirected edge with a negative weight is itself a negative cycle (go back and forth), so use directed edges.
- Check for infinity before relaxing, otherwise "infinity plus a negative weight" looks like an improvement.
- Pitfalls: overflow with a huge infinity, and for the "at most `k` edges" variant forgetting to read from the previous round's array.
--cost
Time O(V times E), space O(V). Early exit makes it fast on easy inputs.
--uses
u-relax
u-inf
--java
// edges[i] = {u, v, w}; returns dist (INF = unreachable), or null if a negative cycle is reachable
static long[] bellmanFord(int n, int[][] edges, int src) {
    long[] dist = new long[n];
    int[] parent = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(dist, INF);                               // piece: safe "infinity"
    Arrays.fill(parent, -1);
    dist[src] = 0;
    for (int round = 0; round < n; round++) {
        boolean changed = false;
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            if (dist[e[0]] >= INF) continue;              // start not reached yet
            if (relax(dist, parent, e[0], e[1], e[2])) changed = true;   // piece
        }
        if (!changed) return dist;                        // settled early
        if (round == n - 1) return null;                  // still improving after n-1 rounds
    }
    return dist;
}
--py
def bellman_ford(n, edges, src):
    """edges is a list of (u, v, w). Returns dist, or None if a negative cycle is reachable."""
    dist = [INF] * n                                      # piece: INF
    parent = [-1] * n
    dist[src] = 0
    for rnd in range(n):
        changed = False
        for u, v, w in edges:
            if safe_add(dist[u], w) >= INF:               # start not reached yet
                continue
            if relax(dist, parent, u, v, w):              # piece
                changed = True
        if not changed:
            return dist                                   # settled early
        if rnd == n - 1:
            return None                                   # still improving after n-1 rounds
    return dist
--cpp
// edges[i] = {u, v, w}; returns dist (INF = unreachable), or empty if a negative cycle is reachable
vector<long long> bellmanFord(int n, const vector<array<int,3>>& edges, int src) {
    vector<long long> dist(n, INF);                       // piece: safe "infinity"
    vector<int> parent(n, -1);
    dist[src] = 0;
    for (int round = 0; round < n; round++) {
        bool changed = false;
        for (auto& [u, v, w] : edges) {
            if (safeAdd((int) min<long long>(dist[u], INF), w) >= INF) continue;   // piece: start not reached yet
            if (relax(dist, parent, u, v, w)) changed = true;    // piece
        }
        if (!changed) return dist;                        // settled early
        if (round == n - 1) return {};                    // still improving after n-1 rounds
    }
    return dist;
}
--go
// edges[i] = {u, v, w}; returns dist (INF = unreachable), or nil if a negative cycle is reachable
func bellmanFord(n int, edges [][3]int, src int) []int {
    dist := make([]int, n)
    for i := range dist {
        dist[i] = INF // piece: safe "infinity"
    }
    parent := make([]int, n)
    for i := range parent {
        parent[i] = -1
    }
    dist[src] = 0
    for round := 0; round < n; round++ {
        changed := false
        for _, e := range edges {
            if safeAdd(dist[e[0]], e[2]) >= INF { // start not reached yet (piece)
                continue
            }
            if relax(dist, parent, e[0], e[1], e[2]) { // piece
                changed = true
            }
        }
        if !changed { // settled early
            return dist
        }
        if round == n-1 { // still improving after n-1 rounds
            return nil
        }
    }
    return dist
}
### spfa
--idea
SPFA (Shortest Path Faster Algorithm) is Bellman-Ford with a to-do list. Plain Bellman-Ford re-examines every edge in every round, even edges whose start node did not change. SPFA only looks at the edges leaving nodes whose distance just improved, because only those can cause a further improvement.

It keeps a queue of "nodes that changed". Take one off the queue, relax its outgoing edges, and any neighbour that improves goes on the queue (if it is not already there). It handles negative edges like Bellman-Ford, and is often much faster on sparse graphs.
--build
1. Set `dist[src] = 0`, all others infinity, `parent` filled with `-1`. `enqueue(q, src)` (code piece `u-queueop`) and set `inQ[src] = true`.
2. `v = dequeue(q)`, set `inQ[v] = false`.
3. For each edge `(v, w, c)`: call `relax(dist, parent, v, w, c)` (code piece `u-relax`). If it returns true, `dist[w]` improved. If `w` is not already in the queue, add one to `pushes[w]`, `enqueue(q, w)` and set `inQ[w] = true`.
4. If `pushes[w]` reaches `n`, node `w` has improved `n` times, which cannot happen without a negative cycle. Report it.
5. Repeat until the queue is empty.
6. Invariant: any node whose outgoing edges could still relax something is in the queue. When the queue empties, no edge can relax, so all distances are final.
--think
- Signal: shortest path with negative edges on a sparse graph, or a graph where only a few nodes change per round.
- The worst case is the same as Bellman-Ford, O(V times E), and adversarial test data exists that triggers it. On contests with hacks, prefer Dijkstra when there are no negative edges, and Bellman-Ford when worst case matters.
- The `pushes[w] >= n` test is the cycle check: with no negative cycle, a node can be improved at most `n - 1` times via successively longer paths.
- Alternative for negative edges plus all-pairs: Johnson's algorithm (`johnson`) uses Bellman-Ford once and then Dijkstra.
- Pitfalls: forgetting `inQ`, which lets the queue grow with duplicates, and forgetting that it finds only cycles reachable from `src`.
--cost
Time O(V times E) worst case, typically near O(E) on random sparse graphs. Space O(V + E).
--uses
u-relax
u-queueop
--java
// adj.get(v) holds {neighbour, cost}; returns dist or null if a negative cycle is reachable
static long[] spfa(List<List<int[]>> adj, int src) {
    int n = adj.size();
    long[] dist = new long[n];
    Arrays.fill(dist, Long.MAX_VALUE / 4);
    int[] parent = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(parent, -1);
    int[] pushes = new int[n];
    boolean[] inQ = new boolean[n];
    Deque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    dist[src] = 0; enqueue(q, src); inQ[src] = true;
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int v = dequeue(q);
        inQ[v] = false;
        for (int[] e : adj.get(v)) {
            if (relax(dist, parent, v, e[0], e[1]) && !inQ[e[0]]) {   // piece: improved?
                if (++pushes[e[0]] >= n) return null;                  // n improvements: negative cycle
                enqueue(q, e[0]); inQ[e[0]] = true;
            }
        }
    }
    return dist;
}
--py
def spfa(adj, src):
    """adj[v] is a list of (neighbour, cost). Returns dist, or None on a reachable negative cycle."""
    n = len(adj)
    dist = [float("inf")] * n
    parent = [-1] * n
    pushes = [0] * n
    in_q = [False] * n
    dist[src] = 0
    q = deque()
    enqueue(q, src)
    in_q[src] = True
    while q:
        v = dequeue(q)
        in_q[v] = False
        for w, c in adj[v]:
            if relax(dist, parent, v, w, c) and not in_q[w]:     # piece: improved?
                pushes[w] += 1
                if pushes[w] >= n:                                # n improvements: negative cycle
                    return None
                enqueue(q, w)
                in_q[w] = True
    return dist
--cpp
// adj[v] holds {neighbour, cost}; returns dist, or an empty vector on a reachable negative cycle
vector<long long> spfa(const vector<vector<pair<int,int>>>& adj, int src) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<long long> dist(n, LLONG_MAX / 4);
    vector<int> parent(n, -1), pushes(n, 0);
    vector<char> inQ(n, 0);
    queue<int> q;
    dist[src] = 0; enqueue(q, src); inQ[src] = 1;
    while (!q.empty()) {
        int v = dequeue(q);
        inQ[v] = 0;
        for (auto [w, c] : adj[v]) {
            if (relax(dist, parent, v, w, c) && !inQ[w]) {        // piece: improved?
                if (++pushes[w] >= n) return {};                  // n improvements: negative cycle
                enqueue(q, w); inQ[w] = 1;
            }
        }
    }
    return dist;
}
--go
// adj[v] holds {neighbour, cost}; returns dist or nil if a negative cycle is reachable
func spfa(adj [][][2]int, src int) []int {
    n := len(adj)
    dist := make([]int, n)
    for i := range dist {
        dist[i] = 1<<60
    }
    parent := make([]int, n)
    for i := range parent {
        parent[i] = -1
    }
    pushes := make([]int, n)
    inQ := make([]bool, n)
    var q []int
    dist[src] = 0
    enqueue(&q, src) // piece
    inQ[src] = true
    for len(q) > 0 {
        v := dequeue(&q) // piece
        inQ[v] = false
        for _, e := range adj[v] {
            if relax(dist, parent, v, e[0], e[1]) && !inQ[e[0]] { // piece: improved?
                if pushes[e[0]]++; pushes[e[0]] >= n { // n improvements: negative cycle
                    return nil
                }
                enqueue(&q, e[0])
                inQ[e[0]] = true
            }
        }
    }
    return dist
}
### negcycle
--idea
A negative cycle is a loop of edges whose costs add up to less than zero. Walking around it again and again keeps lowering your total cost forever, so shortest paths through it are undefined. Detecting one matters for real problems: currency exchange loops that make free money, or schedules whose constraints contradict each other.

Bellman-Ford does the detection. To find a negative cycle anywhere in the graph (not only one reachable from a chosen start), start every node at distance 0, as if a secret source joined to all nodes by zero-cost edges. If any edge still improves after `n` rounds, a negative cycle exists, and the parent pointers lead into it.
--build
1. Set `dist[v] = 0` for every node and `parent[v] = -1`. (Starting everything at 0 means every node is a possible start.)
2. For up to `n` rounds, relax every edge `(u, v, w)` with `relax(dist, parent, u, v, w)` (code piece `u-relax`). Whenever it returns true, it has lowered `dist[v]` and set `parent[v] = u`; remember `x = v`.
3. If in some round nothing changed (`x` stayed unset), there is no negative cycle: return empty.
4. If the `n`-th round still changed something, `x` is a node that was improved, so it is reachable from a negative cycle, or on one.
5. Walk `x = parent[x]` exactly `n` times. This must land you inside the cycle, because any chain of `n` parent steps has to repeat a node.
6. From that node `c`, follow `parent` until you return to `c`, collecting the nodes. Reverse the list to get the cycle in edge order.
7. Invariant: `dist[v]` is always the cost of some real walk ending at `v`. After `n` rounds without a cycle, no walk is better than the best simple path, so no more improvement is possible.
--think
- Signal: "arbitrage", "can the cost drop forever", "are these difference constraints consistent" (constraints of the form `x_j - x_i <= c` are edges of weight `c`, and they are solvable exactly when there is no negative cycle), "time loop".
- For currency problems, take the logarithm of each rate and negate: a product greater than 1 becomes a negative sum.
- Floyd-Warshall can also detect it: after running, any node with `dist[i][i] < 0` lies on a negative cycle.
- Use `bellman` instead when you need distances from one start and the graph has a start. Use this when the question is just "is there any loop".
- Pitfalls: starting `dist` at infinity from a single source only finds cycles reachable from it, the walk-back `n` times step is easy to forget, and with a zero-cost cycle there is nothing to report.
--cost
Time O(V times E), space O(V).
--uses
u-relax
--java
// edges[i] = {u, v, w}; returns the nodes of a negative cycle in order, or an empty list
static List<Integer> findNegativeCycle(int n, int[][] edges) {
    long[] dist = new long[n];                       // all 0: every node is a start
    int[] parent = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(parent, -1);
    int x = -1;
    for (int round = 0; round < n; round++) {
        x = -1;
        for (int[] e : edges)
            if (relax(dist, parent, e[0], e[1], e[2])) x = e[1];     // piece: lowered dist[v]
        if (x == -1) return new ArrayList<>();       // a quiet round: no negative cycle
    }
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) x = parent[x];       // n steps back lands inside the cycle
    List<Integer> cycle = new ArrayList<>();
    int v = x;
    do { cycle.add(v); v = parent[v]; } while (v != x);
    Collections.reverse(cycle);
    return cycle;
}
--py
def find_negative_cycle(n, edges):
    """edges is a list of (u, v, w). Returns the nodes of a negative cycle in order, or []."""
    dist = [0] * n                        # all 0: every node is a start
    parent = [-1] * n
    x = -1
    for _ in range(n):
        x = -1
        for u, v, w in edges:
            if relax(dist, parent, u, v, w):      # piece: lowered dist[v]
                x = v
        if x == -1:                       # a quiet round: no negative cycle
            return []
    for _ in range(n):                    # n steps back lands inside the cycle
        x = parent[x]
    cycle = [x]
    v = parent[x]
    while v != x:
        cycle.append(v)
        v = parent[v]
    return cycle[::-1]
--cpp
// edges[i] = {u, v, w}; returns the nodes of a negative cycle in order, or empty
vector<int> findNegativeCycle(int n, const vector<array<int,3>>& edges) {
    vector<long long> dist(n, 0);                    // all 0: every node is a start
    vector<int> parent(n, -1);
    int x = -1;
    for (int round = 0; round < n; round++) {
        x = -1;
        for (auto& [u, v, w] : edges)
            if (relax(dist, parent, u, v, w)) x = v;            // piece: lowered dist[v]
        if (x == -1) return {};                      // a quiet round: no negative cycle
    }
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) x = parent[x];       // n steps back lands inside the cycle
    vector<int> cycle;
    int v = x;
    do { cycle.push_back(v); v = parent[v]; } while (v != x);
    reverse(cycle.begin(), cycle.end());
    return cycle;
}
--go
// edges[i] = {u, v, w}; returns the nodes of a negative cycle in order, or nil
func findNegativeCycle(n int, edges [][3]int) []int {
    dist := make([]int, n) // all 0: every node is a start
    parent := make([]int, n)
    for i := range parent {
        parent[i] = -1
    }
    x := -1
    for round := 0; round < n; round++ {
        x = -1
        for _, e := range edges {
            if relax(dist, parent, e[0], e[1], e[2]) { // piece: lowered dist[v]
                x = e[1]
            }
        }
        if x == -1 { // a quiet round: no negative cycle
            return nil
        }
    }
    for i := 0; i < n; i++ { // n steps back lands inside the cycle
        x = parent[x]
    }
    var cycle []int
    for v := x; ; {
        cycle = append(cycle, v)
        if v = parent[v]; v == x {
            break
        }
    }
    for i, j := 0, len(cycle)-1; i < j; i, j = i+1, j-1 {
        cycle[i], cycle[j] = cycle[j], cycle[i]
    }
    return cycle
}
### floyd
--idea
Floyd-Warshall computes the shortest distance between every pair of nodes at once. It uses a table `d[i][j]` and a simple idea: allow the "middle stops" one node at a time. First ask for the best route from `i` to `j` using no middle stops (just the direct edge). Then allow node 0 as a stop, then nodes 0 and 1, and so on.

When node `k` becomes allowed, the best route from `i` to `j` either avoids `k` (the old value) or goes `i` to `k` to `j`, and both halves only use stops allowed before `k`. So the new value is the smaller of `d[i][j]` and `d[i][k] + d[k][j]`. Three nested loops do it.
--build
1. Make a table `d` with `d[i][i] = 0`, `d[i][j] = cost` of the edge if one exists (keep the smallest if there are parallel edges), otherwise infinity.
2. For `k` from 0 to `n - 1` (this must be the outermost loop):
3. For every `i`, for every `j` where `d[i][k]` and `d[k][j]` are both finite: call `chmin(d[i], j, d[i][k] + d[k][j])` (code piece `u-maxupd`). It keeps the smaller of the old and the new value.
4. When all three loops finish, `d[i][j]` is the shortest distance from `i` to `j`, or infinity if there is no route.
5. Invariant: after processing `k`, `d[i][j]` is the shortest route from `i` to `j` that uses only nodes `0..k` as middle stops.
6. Negative cycles show up as `d[i][i] < 0` for some `i`. If that happens, distances through that node are not trustworthy.
7. To rebuild routes, keep `next[i][j]` (the first step from `i` toward `j`) and update it together with `d` whenever `chmin` returns true.
--think
- Signal: "distance between all pairs", small `n` (up to about 400 to 500), queries about many different pairs, "transitive closure" (replace sums with OR to learn who can reach whom), "minimum over a bottleneck" (replace sum with max and min).
- With large sparse graphs, running Dijkstra from each node (or Johnson's algorithm, `johnson`) is better. With negative edges and a single source use `bellman`.
- Loop order matters: `k` must be outermost. Putting `k` innermost gives a wrong answer.
- Overflow: infinity plus infinity can overflow. Guard with a finite check, or choose infinity as about a quarter of the maximum.
- Works with negative edges (not negative cycles). Also computes the "minimum cost to go from `i` to `j`" for graph with unusual costs such as best-of-products by logs.
- Pitfalls: not setting `d[i][i] = 0`, using `int` when sums exceed 2 billion, and treating an undirected edge as one-way.
--cost
Time O(V cubed), space O(V squared).
--uses
u-maxupd
--java
// d[i][j]: edge cost, INF if none, d[i][i] = 0. Updated in place to all-pairs distances.
static void floydWarshall(long[][] d) {
    final long INF = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4;
    int n = d.length;
    for (int k = 0; k < n; k++)                 // k = the newest allowed middle stop
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            if (d[i][k] == INF) continue;
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
                if (d[k][j] == INF) continue;
                chmin(d[i], j, d[i][k] + d[k][j]);      // piece: keep the smaller
            }
        }
}
--py
def floyd_warshall(d):
    """d[i][j]: edge cost, INF if none, d[i][i] = 0. Updated in place to all-pairs distances."""
    inf = float("inf")
    n = len(d)
    for k in range(n):                    # k = the newest allowed middle stop
        for i in range(n):
            if d[i][k] == inf:
                continue
            for j in range(n):
                chmin(d[i], j, d[i][k] + d[k][j])       # piece: keep the smaller
    return d
--cpp
// d[i][j]: edge cost, INF if none, d[i][i] = 0. Updated in place to all-pairs distances.
void floydWarshall(vector<vector<long long>>& d) {
    const long long INF = LLONG_MAX / 4;
    int n = d.size();
    for (int k = 0; k < n; k++)                 // k = the newest allowed middle stop
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            if (d[i][k] == INF) continue;
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
                if (d[k][j] == INF) continue;
                chmin(d[i][j], d[i][k] + d[k][j]);      // piece: keep the smaller
            }
        }
}
--go
// d[i][j]: edge cost, inf if none, d[i][i] = 0. Updated in place to all-pairs distances.
func floydWarshall(d [][]int) {
    const inf = 1 << 60
    n := len(d)
    for k := 0; k < n; k++ { // k = the newest allowed middle stop
        for i := 0; i < n; i++ {
            if d[i][k] == inf {
                continue
            }
            for j := 0; j < n; j++ {
                if d[k][j] == inf {
                    continue
                }
                chmin(d[i], j, d[i][k]+d[k][j]) // piece: keep the smaller
            }
        }
    }
}
### astar
--idea
A* (pronounced "A star") is Dijkstra with a sense of direction. Dijkstra spreads out evenly in all directions, even away from the goal. A* adds a guess `h(v)` of how much cost remains from `v` to the goal, and always expands the node with the smallest `dist[v] + h(v)`: cost so far plus the guess of cost to go. It therefore leans toward the goal and expands far fewer nodes.

The guess must be admissible: never larger than the true remaining cost. Then the first time the goal is taken from the queue its distance is the true shortest one. With `h = 0` everywhere, A* is exactly Dijkstra. Straight-line distance on a map is a classic admissible guess, since no road is shorter than a straight line.
--build
1. Choose the heuristic `h[v]`: a number that never exceeds the true cost from `v` to the goal. For grids with four-way moves of cost 1, use the Manhattan distance (rows apart plus columns apart).
2. Make a min-heap with `newPq()` (code piece `u-pq`). Set `dist[src] = 0` and `pushPq(pq, dist[src] + h[src], src)`. The key is `f`, cost so far plus the guess.
3. `popPq(pq)` gives the smallest `(f, v)`. If `f` is larger than `dist[v] + h[v]`, it is a stale entry, skip it.
4. If `v` is the goal, return `dist[v]`.
5. For each edge `(v, w, c)`: if `dist[v] + c < dist[w]`, set `dist[w] = dist[v] + c` and `pushPq(pq, dist[w] + h[w], w)`.
6. If the heap empties, the goal is unreachable.
7. Invariant: with an admissible `h`, when the goal is popped no unexplored route can have `f` smaller, so its `dist` is optimal. If `h` is also consistent (`h[v] <= c + h[w]` for every edge), a node popped once is final and never needs re-opening. The code re-pushes on any improvement, so it stays correct even if `h` is only admissible.
--think
- Signal: shortest path from one start to one goal in a large implicit graph (a map, a puzzle) where you know something about the goal's direction. Sliding-tile puzzles, pathfinding in games, "fewest moves" with a good estimate.
- Choose the heuristic carefully. Too small is safe but slow (down to Dijkstra). Too large breaks optimality: you get a path, but maybe not the shortest. Stronger admissible heuristics (for example pattern databases for puzzles) expand fewer nodes.
- Not useful when you need distances to all nodes, since it stops at the goal. Use Dijkstra then.
- With many states, memory is the limit. IDA* trades time for memory by repeating depth-limited searches.
- Pitfalls: a heuristic that overestimates (for example Manhattan distance when diagonal moves are allowed), mixing units, and stale entries.
--cost
Time depends on the heuristic: at worst O((V + E) log V) like Dijkstra, usually far less. Space O(V).
--uses
u-pq
--java
// adj.get(v) holds {neighbour, cost}; h[v] is an admissible estimate of the cost from v to goal
static long astar(List<List<int[]>> adj, int src, int goal, long[] h) {
    long[] dist = new long[adj.size()];
    Arrays.fill(dist, Long.MAX_VALUE / 4);
    dist[src] = 0;
    PriorityQueue<long[]> pq = newPq();
    pushPq(pq, h[src], src);                         // key f = dist + h
    while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
        long[] cur = popPq(pq);
        int v = (int) cur[1];
        if (cur[0] > dist[v] + h[v]) continue;       // stale entry
        if (v == goal) return dist[v];
        for (int[] e : adj.get(v)) {
            long nd = dist[v] + e[1];
            if (nd < dist[e[0]]) {
                dist[e[0]] = nd;
                pushPq(pq, nd + h[e[0]], e[0]);
            }
        }
    }
    return -1;                                       // goal unreachable
}
--py
def astar(adj, src, goal, h):
    """adj[v] is a list of (neighbour, cost); h[v] never overestimates the cost from v to goal."""
    dist = [float("inf")] * len(adj)
    dist[src] = 0
    pq = []
    push_pq(pq, h[src], src)              # key f = dist + h
    while pq:
        f, v = pop_pq(pq)
        if f > dist[v] + h[v]:
            continue                      # stale entry
        if v == goal:
            return dist[v]
        for w, c in adj[v]:
            nd = dist[v] + c
            if nd < dist[w]:
                dist[w] = nd
                push_pq(pq, nd + h[w], w)
    return -1                             # goal unreachable
--cpp
// adj[v] holds {neighbour, cost}; h[v] never overestimates the cost from v to goal
long long astar(const vector<vector<pair<int,int>>>& adj, int src, int goal, const vector<long long>& h) {
    vector<long long> dist(adj.size(), LLONG_MAX / 4);
    dist[src] = 0;
    MinPq pq;
    pushPq(pq, h[src], src);                         // key f = dist + h
    while (!pq.empty()) {
        auto [f, v] = popPq(pq);
        if (f > dist[v] + h[v]) continue;            // stale entry
        if (v == goal) return dist[v];
        for (auto [w, c] : adj[v]) {
            if (dist[v] + c < dist[w]) {
                dist[w] = dist[v] + c;
                pushPq(pq, dist[w] + h[w], w);
            }
        }
    }
    return -1;                                       // goal unreachable
}
--go
// adj[v] holds {neighbour, cost}; h[v] is an admissible estimate of the cost from v to goal
func astar(adj [][][2]int, src, goal int, h []int) int {
    dist := make([]int, len(adj))
    for i := range dist {
        dist[i] = 1<<60
    }
    dist[src] = 0
    pq := newPq()
    pushPq(pq, h[src], src) // key f = dist + h
    for len(*pq) > 0 {
        cur := popPq(pq)
        v := cur[1]
        if cur[0] > dist[v]+h[v] { // stale entry
            continue
        }
        if v == goal {
            return dist[v]
        }
        for _, e := range adj[v] {
            if nd := dist[v] + e[1]; nd < dist[e[0]] {
                dist[e[0]] = nd
                pushPq(pq, nd+h[e[0]], e[0])
            }
        }
    }
    return -1 // goal unreachable
}
### bibfs
--idea
Bidirectional search runs two searches at once: one forward from the start, one backward from the target. They grow toward each other and stop when they meet. The gain is large. If every node has about `b` neighbours and the answer is `d` steps long, one search looks at about `b` to the power `d` nodes, but two searches that each go `d / 2` deep look at only about two times `b` to the power `d / 2`. That is the square root of the work.

Picture digging a tunnel from both sides of a mountain: each crew digs half the distance, and the total digging is much smaller than one crew digging all of it, because the work grows so fast with depth.
--build
1. If `src == dst`, return 0.
2. Keep two distance arrays `da` (from `src`) and `db` (from `dst`), filled with `-1`. Set `da[src] = 0`, `db[dst] = 0`. Keep a frontier list for each side: the nodes at the current outermost level.
3. Repeat while both frontiers are non-empty: pick the side with the smaller frontier (this keeps the work balanced).
4. Expand that side by one full level. For each node `v` in its frontier and each neighbour `w`: if the other side has reached `w`, record the candidate `dist_this[v] + 1 + dist_other[w]` and keep the minimum. Otherwise, if this side has not reached `w`, set its distance and add it to the next frontier.
5. After finishing the level, if any candidate was recorded, return the smallest. (Finishing the whole level, not stopping at the first meeting, is what makes it exact.)
6. If a frontier empties without a meeting, the two are not connected: return -1.
7. Invariant: before each expansion, no node has been reached from both sides and no edge joins the two explored regions, so the shortest path is at least (depth of A) + (depth of B) + 1. That is why the first level that finds a meeting gives the exact answer.
--think
- Signal: shortest path between two known endpoints in a graph that fans out a lot (word ladder, puzzle states, social graph "degrees of separation"), when the graph is too big for a one-sided BFS.
- It needs to be able to walk edges backward. In an undirected graph that is free. In a directed graph, build the reversed graph for the backward side.
- It is a BFS speed-up, so edges must be unweighted. For weighted graphs the equivalent (bidirectional Dijkstra) needs a more careful stopping rule.
- If the graph is not very branchy (a long thin corridor), you save little.
- Pitfalls: stopping at the first meeting inside a level, which can give an answer that is one too large, and forgetting the case `src == dst`.
--cost
Time about O(b to the power d/2) for branching factor b and distance d, versus O(b to the power d) for one-sided BFS. Worst case O(V + E). Space O(V).
--java
// undirected graph; fewest edges from src to dst, -1 if not connected
static int bidirectionalBfs(List<List<Integer>> adj, int src, int dst) {
    if (src == dst) return 0;
    int n = adj.size();
    int[][] dist = new int[2][n];
    for (int[] d : dist) Arrays.fill(d, -1);
    List<List<Integer>> front = new ArrayList<>();
    front.add(new ArrayList<>(List.of(src)));
    front.add(new ArrayList<>(List.of(dst)));
    dist[0][src] = 0; dist[1][dst] = 0;
    while (!front.get(0).isEmpty() && !front.get(1).isEmpty()) {
        int s = front.get(0).size() <= front.get(1).size() ? 0 : 1;   // expand the smaller side
        List<Integer> next = new ArrayList<>();
        int best = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
        for (int v : front.get(s))
            for (int w : adj.get(v)) {
                if (dist[1 - s][w] != -1) best = Math.min(best, dist[s][v] + 1 + dist[1 - s][w]);
                if (dist[s][w] == -1) { dist[s][w] = dist[s][v] + 1; next.add(w); }
            }
        if (best != Integer.MAX_VALUE) return best;      // whole level done, exact
        front.set(s, next);
    }
    return -1;
}
--py
def bidirectional_bfs(adj, src, dst):
    """Undirected graph. Fewest edges from src to dst, -1 if not connected."""
    if src == dst:
        return 0
    n = len(adj)
    dist = [[-1] * n, [-1] * n]
    dist[0][src] = 0
    dist[1][dst] = 0
    front = [[src], [dst]]
    while front[0] and front[1]:
        s = 0 if len(front[0]) <= len(front[1]) else 1      # expand the smaller side
        nxt = []
        best = None
        for v in front[s]:
            for w in adj[v]:
                if dist[1 - s][w] != -1:
                    cand = dist[s][v] + 1 + dist[1 - s][w]
                    if best is None or cand < best:
                        best = cand
                if dist[s][w] == -1:
                    dist[s][w] = dist[s][v] + 1
                    nxt.append(w)
        if best is not None:
            return best                   # whole level done, exact
        front[s] = nxt
    return -1
--cpp
// undirected graph; fewest edges from src to dst, -1 if not connected
int bidirectionalBfs(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int src, int dst) {
    if (src == dst) return 0;
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<int> dist[2] = {vector<int>(n, -1), vector<int>(n, -1)};
    vector<int> front[2] = {{src}, {dst}};
    dist[0][src] = 0; dist[1][dst] = 0;
    while (!front[0].empty() && !front[1].empty()) {
        int s = front[0].size() <= front[1].size() ? 0 : 1;   // expand the smaller side
        vector<int> next;
        int best = INT_MAX;
        for (int v : front[s])
            for (int w : adj[v]) {
                if (dist[1 - s][w] != -1) best = min(best, dist[s][v] + 1 + dist[1 - s][w]);
                if (dist[s][w] == -1) { dist[s][w] = dist[s][v] + 1; next.push_back(w); }
            }
        if (best != INT_MAX) return best;                 // whole level done, exact
        front[s] = next;
    }
    return -1;
}
--go
// undirected graph; fewest edges from src to dst, -1 if not connected
func bidirectionalBfs(adj [][]int, src, dst int) int {
    if src == dst {
        return 0
    }
    n := len(adj)
    var dist [2][]int
    for s := range dist {
        dist[s] = make([]int, n)
        for v := range dist[s] {
            dist[s][v] = -1
        }
    }
    front := [2][]int{{src}, {dst}}
    dist[0][src], dist[1][dst] = 0, 0
    for len(front[0]) > 0 && len(front[1]) > 0 {
        s := 1
        if len(front[0]) <= len(front[1]) { // expand the smaller side
            s = 0
        }
        var next []int
        best := 1 << 60
        for _, v := range front[s] {
            for _, w := range adj[v] {
                if dist[1-s][w] != -1 { // the two searches touch
                    best = min(best, dist[s][v]+1+dist[1-s][w])
                }
                if dist[s][w] == -1 {
                    dist[s][w] = dist[s][v] + 1
                    next = append(next, w)
                }
            }
        }
        if best != 1<<60 { // whole level done, exact
            return best
        }
        front[s] = next
    }
    return -1
}
### mst
--idea
A spanning tree of a connected graph uses just enough edges to join all nodes together, with no loops (so exactly `n - 1` edges). A minimum spanning tree (MST) is the one with the smallest total edge cost. It answers "what is the cheapest way to wire up all these towns, so everyone is connected".

Kruskal's algorithm is greedy: sort all edges by cost and go through them from cheapest. Take an edge if it joins two groups that are not yet connected, skip it if both ends are already connected (it would close a loop). The reason this is safe: the cheapest edge leaving any group is always part of some minimum tree (the cut property), so taking cheapest-first never ruins optimality.
--build
1. Put every edge `(u, v, w)` in a list and sort it by `w`, smallest first, with `sortEdges(edges)` (code piece `u-edgesort`).
2. Set up a disjoint set union (DSU, see `dsu`): `parent[v] = v` for every node. `find(v)` returns the leader of `v`'s group, with path halving (point nodes closer to the leader as you search). The code keeps a tiny inline version so the lesson stands alone.
3. Go through the sorted edges. For each, compute `ru = find(u)`, `rv = find(v)`.
4. If `ru != rv`, the edge connects two different groups: take it. Add `w` to the total, and merge the groups by setting `parent[ru] = rv`.
5. If `ru == rv`, skip it.
6. Stop after `n - 1` taken edges. If fewer than `n - 1` were taken at the end, the graph is disconnected and has no spanning tree (a spanning forest instead).
7. Invariant: the taken edges always form a forest (no cycles) that is contained in some minimum spanning tree.
--think
- Signal: "connect everything at minimum total cost", "cheapest network", "minimum cost to join all", "clusters by removing the heaviest links" (stop Kruskal early at `k` groups, or drop the largest MST edges).
- Kruskal works from an edge list and sorting, best for sparse graphs. Prim (`prim`) grows from one node with a heap, good for dense graphs. Boruvka (`boruvka`) merges cheapest edges in rounds and is good for parallel work.
- If edge weights are distinct, the MST is unique. Otherwise there can be several with the same total, and the problem usually asks only for the total.
- Maximum spanning tree: sort descending. Bottleneck question (minimize the largest edge on the path between two nodes): the MST path is the answer.
- The MST gives the path between any two nodes that minimises the maximum edge, the "minimax" path. Combine with `lca` to answer many such queries.
- Pitfalls: summing in `int` (use `long`), an input that may be disconnected, and forgetting that it is for undirected graphs. The directed version is `arbor`.
--cost
Time O(E log E) for sorting, plus near-constant union-find work. Space O(V + E).
--uses
u-edgesort
--java
// edges[i] = {u, v, w}; returns the MST total weight, or -1 if the graph is not connected
static long kruskal(int n, int[][] edges) {
    int[][] sorted = edges.clone();
    sortEdges(sorted);                           // piece: cheapest first
    int[] parent = new int[n];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) parent[i] = i;
    long total = 0;
    int taken = 0;
    for (int[] e : sorted) {
        int ru = findRoot(parent, e[0]), rv = findRoot(parent, e[1]);
        if (ru == rv) continue;                  // would close a loop
        parent[ru] = rv;                         // join the two groups
        total += e[2];
        if (++taken == n - 1) break;
    }
    return taken == n - 1 ? total : -1;
}

static int findRoot(int[] parent, int x) {
    while (parent[x] != x) { parent[x] = parent[parent[x]]; x = parent[x]; }   // path halving
    return x;
}
--py
def kruskal(n, edges):
    """edges is a list of (u, v, w). Returns the MST total weight, or -1 if not connected."""
    edges = list(edges)
    sort_edges(edges)                             # piece: cheapest first
    parent = list(range(n))

    def find(x):
        while parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = parent[parent[x]]         # path halving
            x = parent[x]
        return x

    total = taken = 0
    for u, v, w in edges:
        ru, rv = find(u), find(v)
        if ru == rv:
            continue                              # would close a loop
        parent[ru] = rv                           # join the two groups
        total += w
        taken += 1
        if taken == n - 1:
            break
    return total if taken == n - 1 else -1
--cpp
int findRoot(vector<int>& parent, int x) {
    while (parent[x] != x) { parent[x] = parent[parent[x]]; x = parent[x]; }   // path halving
    return x;
}

// edges[i] = {u, v, w}; returns the MST total weight, or -1 if the graph is not connected
long long kruskal(int n, vector<array<int,3>> edges) {
    sortEdges(edges);                            // piece: cheapest first
    vector<int> parent(n);
    iota(parent.begin(), parent.end(), 0);
    long long total = 0;
    int taken = 0;
    for (auto& [u, v, w] : edges) {
        int ru = findRoot(parent, u), rv = findRoot(parent, v);
        if (ru == rv) continue;                  // would close a loop
        parent[ru] = rv;                         // join the two groups
        total += w;
        if (++taken == n - 1) break;
    }
    return taken == n - 1 ? total : -1;
}
--go
// edges[i] = {u, v, w}; returns the MST total weight, or -1 if the graph is not connected
func kruskal(n int, edges [][3]int) int {
    sorted := append([][3]int(nil), edges...)
    sortEdges(sorted) // piece: cheapest first
    parent := make([]int, n)
    for i := range parent {
        parent[i] = i
    }
    total, taken := 0, 0
    for _, e := range sorted {
        ru, rv := findRoot(parent, e[0]), findRoot(parent, e[1])
        if ru == rv { // would close a loop
            continue
        }
        parent[ru] = rv // join the two groups
        total += e[2]
        if taken++; taken == n-1 {
            break
        }
    }
    if taken != n-1 {
        return -1
    }
    return total
}

func findRoot(parent []int, x int) int {
    for parent[x] != x {
        parent[x] = parent[parent[x]] // path halving
        x = parent[x]
    }
    return x
}
### prim
--idea
Prim's algorithm builds a minimum spanning tree by growing it from one node, like a crystal. At each step it looks at every edge that leaves the current tree and takes the cheapest one, adding the new node on its far side. It stops when all nodes are in.

It is correct for the same reason as Kruskal: for any set of nodes already chosen, the cheapest edge leaving that set belongs to some minimum spanning tree (the cut property). A min-heap holds the candidate edges so the cheapest one is found quickly. The picture is very close to Dijkstra, but the heap key is the single edge cost, not the total distance from the start.
--build
1. Pick any start node, say 0. Make a min-heap with `newPq()` (code piece `u-pq`) and `pushPq(pq, 0, 0)`: key is the edge cost, value is the node.
2. `popPq(pq)` gives the cheapest `(w, v)`. If `v` is already in the tree, skip it (a stale candidate).
3. Otherwise add `v` to the tree: set `inTree[v] = true`, add `w` to the total, count one more node, and `pushPq(pq, c, u)` for every edge `(v, u, c)` to a node not yet in the tree.
4. Repeat until the heap is empty.
5. If fewer than `n` nodes were added, the graph is disconnected and no spanning tree exists.
6. Invariant: the tree built so far is a subtree of some minimum spanning tree, and every edge crossing from the tree to outside is in the heap (or was already skipped because its far end joined later).
--think
- Signal: same as `mst`: cheapest way to connect all nodes. Choose Prim when you have an adjacency list or a dense graph (an O(V squared) version with no heap is excellent for complete graphs given as a matrix), and Kruskal when you have a sorted or sortable edge list.
- Prim is not Dijkstra. Dijkstra's key is `dist[v] + w` (total path cost); Prim's key is just `w`. Mixing them up is the classic error.
- On a complete graph of points in the plane, compute edge weights on the fly with the O(V squared) array version: keep `best[v]`, the cheapest edge from the tree to `v`, and scan for the minimum each round.
- It works on connected undirected graphs only. For several components, run from each unvisited node.
- Pitfalls: pushing duplicates is fine, but you must skip nodes already in the tree when popping, and using `int` for the total.
--cost
Time O(E log V) with a binary heap. Space O(V + E).
--uses
u-pq
--java
// adj.get(v) holds {neighbour, weight}; returns the MST total weight, or -1 if disconnected
static long prim(List<List<int[]>> adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    boolean[] inTree = new boolean[n];
    PriorityQueue<long[]> pq = newPq();
    pushPq(pq, 0, 0);                                // key = edge cost, value = node: start at node 0
    long total = 0;
    int count = 0;
    while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
        long[] cur = popPq(pq);
        int v = (int) cur[1];
        if (inTree[v]) continue;                     // stale candidate
        inTree[v] = true;
        total += cur[0];
        count++;
        for (int[] e : adj.get(v))
            if (!inTree[e[0]]) pushPq(pq, e[1], e[0]);
    }
    return count == n ? total : -1;
}
--py
def prim(adj):
    """adj[v] is a list of (neighbour, weight). Returns the MST total weight, or -1 if disconnected."""
    n = len(adj)
    in_tree = [False] * n
    pq = []
    push_pq(pq, 0, 0)                     # key = edge cost, value = node: start at node 0
    total = count = 0
    while pq:
        w, v = pop_pq(pq)
        if in_tree[v]:
            continue                      # stale candidate
        in_tree[v] = True
        total += w
        count += 1
        for u, c in adj[v]:
            if not in_tree[u]:
                push_pq(pq, c, u)
    return total if count == n else -1
--cpp
// adj[v] holds {neighbour, weight}; returns the MST total weight, or -1 if disconnected
long long prim(const vector<vector<pair<int,int>>>& adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<char> inTree(n, 0);
    MinPq pq;
    pushPq(pq, 0, 0);                                // key = edge cost, value = node: start at node 0
    long long total = 0;
    int count = 0;
    while (!pq.empty()) {
        auto [w, v] = popPq(pq);
        if (inTree[v]) continue;                     // stale candidate
        inTree[v] = 1;
        total += w;
        count++;
        for (auto [u, c] : adj[v])
            if (!inTree[u]) pushPq(pq, c, u);
    }
    return count == n ? total : -1;
}
--go
// adj[v] holds {neighbour, weight}; returns the MST total weight, or -1 if disconnected
func prim(adj [][][2]int) int {
    n := len(adj)
    inTree := make([]bool, n)
    pq := newPq()
    pushPq(pq, 0, 0) // key = edge cost, value = node: start at node 0
    total, count := 0, 0
    for len(*pq) > 0 {
        cur := popPq(pq)
        v := cur[1]
        if inTree[v] { // stale candidate
            continue
        }
        inTree[v] = true
        total += cur[0]
        count++
        for _, e := range adj[v] {
            if !inTree[e[0]] {
                pushPq(pq, e[1], e[0])
            }
        }
    }
    if count != n {
        return -1
    }
    return total
}
### euler
--idea
An Euler tour of a tree flattens it into an array. Walk the tree depth-first and give every node a timestamp when you first enter it (`tin`) and the last timestamp used inside its subtree (`tout`). All nodes in the subtree of `v` get timestamps from `tin[v]` to `tout[v]`, with no gaps and nothing else mixed in.

That one fact turns tree questions into array questions. "Add a value to every node in the subtree of v" becomes "add to the range `[tin[v], tout[v]]`". "Is `a` an ancestor of `b`" becomes "is `tin[a] <= tin[b] <= tout[a]`". Pair the array with a Fenwick tree or segment tree for fast updates and sums.
--build
1. Pick a root. Set `timer = 0`. Make `tin`, `tout`, and `order` (the node at each timestamp).
2. Visit the root: set `tin[v] = timer`, `order[timer] = v`, increase `timer`.
3. For each child (neighbour except the parent), visit it the same way, depth first.
4. After all children are done, set `tout[v] = timer - 1`, the last timestamp handed out so far.
5. The code uses an explicit stack with a pointer per node (the next neighbour to try), so a chain of 100,000 nodes will not overflow.
6. Use: subtree of `v` is positions `tin[v]` to `tout[v]` in `order`. Node `a` is an ancestor of `b` exactly when `tin[a] <= tin[b]` and `tout[b] <= tout[a]`.
7. Invariant: a node's subtree occupies one unbroken block that starts at the node itself, because DFS finishes a whole subtree before leaving it.
--think
- Signal: queries on subtrees ("sum over the subtree", "add to the whole subtree", "count nodes with property in the subtree"), ancestor tests, or turning a tree into a line so a segment tree or Fenwick tree (`fenwick`) can serve it.
- For queries along a path between two nodes (not whole subtrees), you need something stronger: heavy-light decomposition (`hld`), which also uses this same numbering.
- The "tour that writes a node every time you pass it" (2n - 1 entries) is a different variant used for LCA by range minimum (`lcaeuler`). This lesson is the compact form with one entry per node.
- Subtree sums can also be done by plain DP in post order when nothing changes. Use the tour when there are updates mixed with queries.
- Pitfalls: mixing 0 and 1 indexing, using `tout` as exclusive in one place and inclusive in another (pick one and stay with it), and recursion depth.
--cost
Time O(V), space O(V).
--java
// returns {tin, tout, order}; subtree of v is order[tin[v]..tout[v]]
static int[][] eulerTour(List<List<Integer>> adj, int root) {
    int n = adj.size();
    int[] tin = new int[n], tout = new int[n], order = new int[n], parent = new int[n], it = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(parent, -1);
    int timer = 0;
    ArrayDeque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
    tin[root] = timer; order[timer++] = root; st.push(root);
    while (!st.isEmpty()) {
        int v = st.peek();
        if (it[v] < adj.get(v).size()) {
            int w = adj.get(v).get(it[v]++);
            if (w == parent[v]) continue;
            parent[w] = v;
            tin[w] = timer; order[timer++] = w;      // enter w
            st.push(w);
        } else {
            tout[v] = timer - 1;                     // leave v: last timestamp in its subtree
            st.pop();
        }
    }
    return new int[][]{tin, tout, order};
}
--py
def euler_tour(adj, root=0):
    """Returns (tin, tout, order). The subtree of v is order[tin[v] .. tout[v]]."""
    n = len(adj)
    tin = [0] * n
    tout = [0] * n
    order = []
    parent = [-1] * n
    it = [0] * n
    tin[root] = 0
    order.append(root)
    stack = [root]
    while stack:
        v = stack[-1]
        if it[v] < len(adj[v]):
            w = adj[v][it[v]]
            it[v] += 1
            if w == parent[v]:
                continue
            parent[w] = v
            tin[w] = len(order)           # enter w
            order.append(w)
            stack.append(w)
        else:
            tout[v] = len(order) - 1      # leave v: last timestamp in its subtree
            stack.pop()
    return tin, tout, order
--cpp
// returns tin, tout, order through the arguments; subtree of v is order[tin[v]..tout[v]]
void eulerTour(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int root,
               vector<int>& tin, vector<int>& tout, vector<int>& order) {
    int n = adj.size();
    tin.assign(n, 0); tout.assign(n, 0); order.clear();
    vector<int> parent(n, -1), it(n, 0), st;
    tin[root] = 0; order.push_back(root); st.push_back(root);
    while (!st.empty()) {
        int v = st.back();
        if (it[v] < (int)adj[v].size()) {
            int w = adj[v][it[v]++];
            if (w == parent[v]) continue;
            parent[w] = v;
            tin[w] = order.size();                   // enter w
            order.push_back(w);
            st.push_back(w);
        } else {
            tout[v] = (int)order.size() - 1;         // leave v: last timestamp in its subtree
            st.pop_back();
        }
    }
}
--go
// returns tin, tout, order; subtree of v is order[tin[v]..tout[v]]
func eulerTour(adj [][]int, root int) (tin, tout, order []int) {
    n := len(adj)
    tin, tout, order = make([]int, n), make([]int, n), make([]int, 0, n)
    parent, it := make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
    for i := range parent {
        parent[i] = -1
    }
    tin[root] = 0
    order = append(order, root)
    st := []int{root}
    for len(st) > 0 {
        v := st[len(st)-1]
        if it[v] < len(adj[v]) {
            w := adj[v][it[v]]
            it[v]++
            if w == parent[v] {
                continue
            }
            parent[w] = v
            tin[w] = len(order) // enter w
            order = append(order, w)
            st = append(st, w)
        } else {
            tout[v] = len(order) - 1 // leave v: last timestamp in its subtree
            st = st[:len(st)-1]
        }
    }
    return
}
### treecenter
--idea
The center of a tree is the node (or the two adjacent nodes) in the middle of its longest path. It is the best place for a hub: it minimises the largest distance to any other node. If the longest path has an even number of nodes there are two centers, if odd there is exactly one.

A clean way to find it is to peel the tree like an onion. Remove all the leaves (nodes with one neighbour) at once. New leaves appear. Remove those. Keep going. The last one or two nodes left are the center. Each peel removes exactly the outermost layer, so the middle is what survives.
--build
1. Compute `deg[v]`, the number of neighbours of each node. Put every node with `deg == 1` in `leaves`. Set `remaining = n`.
2. While `remaining > 2`: set `remaining` to `remaining - len(leaves)`.
3. Build `next` (the new leaves): for each `leaf` in `leaves`, for each neighbour `w` whose degree is still positive, decrease `deg[w]`. If `deg[w]` becomes 1, add `w` to `next`. Set `deg[leaf] = 0`.
4. Set `leaves = next`.
5. When the loop ends, `leaves` holds the center: one node or two.
6. Special cases: `n == 1` gives the node itself; `n == 2` gives both nodes. The loop test handles them, but `n == 1` must not look for leaves with degree 1 (it has degree 0), so return `[0]` first.
7. Invariant: after each peel, the remaining nodes form a tree whose center is the same as the original tree's, because every longest path loses both ends at once.
--think
- Signal: "best meeting point on a tree", "minimise the maximum distance to all nodes", "tree radius", "root the tree to make it as shallow as possible" (rooting at the center gives the smallest height).
- The radius equals half the diameter rounded up. Once you have the diameter path (`treediam`), the center is its middle node(s). The peeling method needs no weights and no path recovery.
- For weighted trees, peeling by hop count is wrong. Use the diameter path with weights and take the node nearest to half its length.
- The same layer-by-layer idea is used to hash a tree shape for isomorphism (`treeiso`), since two trees have the same shape only if their centers match.
- Pitfalls: `n = 1` and `n = 2`, and forgetting that a path graph of even node count has two centers.
--cost
Time O(V), space O(V).
--java
// tree given as adjacency lists; returns the 1 or 2 center nodes
static List<Integer> treeCenter(List<List<Integer>> adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    if (n == 1) return new ArrayList<>(List.of(0));
    int[] deg = new int[n];
    List<Integer> leaves = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
        deg[v] = adj.get(v).size();
        if (deg[v] == 1) leaves.add(v);
    }
    int remaining = n;
    while (remaining > 2) {
        remaining -= leaves.size();
        List<Integer> next = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int leaf : leaves) {
            deg[leaf] = 0;
            for (int w : adj.get(leaf))
                if (deg[w] > 0 && --deg[w] == 1) next.add(w);   // w became a leaf
        }
        leaves = next;                                           // peel one layer
    }
    return leaves;
}
--py
def tree_center(adj):
    """Returns the 1 or 2 center nodes of a tree."""
    n = len(adj)
    if n == 1:
        return [0]
    deg = [len(a) for a in adj]
    leaves = [v for v in range(n) if deg[v] == 1]
    remaining = n
    while remaining > 2:
        remaining -= len(leaves)
        nxt = []
        for leaf in leaves:
            deg[leaf] = 0
            for w in adj[leaf]:
                if deg[w] > 0:
                    deg[w] -= 1
                    if deg[w] == 1:       # w became a leaf
                        nxt.append(w)
        leaves = nxt                      # peel one layer
    return leaves
--cpp
// tree given as adjacency lists; returns the 1 or 2 center nodes
vector<int> treeCenter(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    if (n == 1) return {0};
    vector<int> deg(n), leaves;
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
        deg[v] = adj[v].size();
        if (deg[v] == 1) leaves.push_back(v);
    }
    int remaining = n;
    while (remaining > 2) {
        remaining -= leaves.size();
        vector<int> next;
        for (int leaf : leaves) {
            deg[leaf] = 0;
            for (int w : adj[leaf])
                if (deg[w] > 0 && --deg[w] == 1) next.push_back(w);   // w became a leaf
        }
        leaves = next;                                                 // peel one layer
    }
    return leaves;
}
--go
// tree given as adjacency lists; returns the 1 or 2 center nodes
func treeCenter(adj [][]int) []int {
    n := len(adj)
    if n == 1 {
        return []int{0}
    }
    deg := make([]int, n)
    var leaves []int
    for v := range adj {
        deg[v] = len(adj[v])
        if deg[v] == 1 {
            leaves = append(leaves, v)
        }
    }
    for remaining := n; remaining > 2; {
        remaining -= len(leaves)
        var next []int
        for _, leaf := range leaves {
            deg[leaf] = 0
            for _, w := range adj[leaf] {
                if deg[w] > 0 {
                    if deg[w]--; deg[w] == 1 { // w became a leaf
                        next = append(next, w)
                    }
                }
            }
        }
        leaves = next // peel one layer
    }
    return leaves
}
### lca
--idea
In a rooted tree, the lowest common ancestor (LCA) of two nodes `u` and `v` is the deepest node that is an ancestor of both. In a family tree it is the closest shared ancestor. Many tree questions reduce to it: the distance between `u` and `v` is `depth[u] + depth[v] - 2 * depth[lca]`.

Binary lifting makes each query fast. For each node store its ancestors at distances 1, 2, 4, 8, and so on: `up[j][v]` is the ancestor `2^j` steps above `v`. Any jump distance is a sum of powers of two, so you can climb any number of steps in about `log n` hops. To find the LCA, first lift the deeper node to the depth of the other, then lift both together as high as possible while they stay different. One more step up reaches the LCA.
--build
1. Root the tree. Compute `depth[v]` and `parent[v]` with a BFS from the root (set the root's parent to itself, so jumps past the root stay on the root).
2. Let `LOG` be the smallest number with `2^LOG >= n`. Build the lifting table with `buildLift(parent, LOG)` (code piece `u-binlift`): `up[0]` is the parent array, and `up[j][v] = up[j-1][ up[j-1][v] ]`, because jumping `2^j` is two jumps of `2^(j-1)`.
3. Query `lca(u, v)`. If `depth[u] < depth[v]`, swap them so `u` is the deeper one.
4. Lift `u` by `depth[u] - depth[v]`: for each bit `j` set in that difference, do `u = up[j][u]`.
5. If `u == v`, return `u` (one was the ancestor of the other).
6. For `j` from `LOG - 1` down to 0: if `up[j][u] != up[j][v]`, set `u = up[j][u]` and `v = up[j][v]`. This jumps both as high as possible without meeting.
7. Return `up[0][u]`, the parent of the pair now just below the LCA. (Steps 3 to 7 are the code piece `u-lcaq`, written here as the `lca` method.)
8. Invariant for step 6: after each step, `u` and `v` are still at equal depth and still different, and their LCA is a strict ancestor of both. The largest safe jumps first mean at the end they sit directly below the LCA.
--think
- Signal: "closest common ancestor", "distance between two nodes in a tree", "path between two nodes" (the path goes up to the LCA and down), "is the ancestor of", many queries on a static tree.
- Alternatives: Euler tour plus a sparse table (`lcaeuler`) answers in O(1) per query after O(n log n) build. Tarjan's offline method (`lcatarjan`) answers all queries in one DFS with a disjoint set. Heavy-light decomposition (`hld`) also gives LCA as a by-product. Binary lifting is the easiest to write and also supports k-th ancestor (`kthanc`) and "max edge on the path" by storing extra data in the `up` table.
- Walk it iteratively. A deep tree breaks recursive DFS.
- Pitfalls: not equalising depths first, a root whose parent is not itself (which makes jumps fall off), and for a forest forgetting that nodes in different trees have no LCA.
--cost
Build O(n log n) time and space, query O(log n).
--uses
u-binlift
--java
static int LOG;
static int[][] up;
static int[] depth;

static void buildLca(List<List<Integer>> adj, int root) {
    int n = adj.size();
    LOG = 1;
    while ((1 << LOG) < n) LOG++;
    int[] parent = new int[n];
    depth = new int[n];
    boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
    ArrayDeque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    q.add(root); seen[root] = true; parent[root] = root;      // root is its own parent
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int v = q.poll();
        for (int w : adj.get(v))
            if (!seen[w]) { seen[w] = true; parent[w] = v; depth[w] = depth[v] + 1; q.add(w); }
    }
    up = buildLift(parent, LOG);                              // piece: 2^j-th ancestors
}

static int lca(int u, int v) {
    if (depth[u] < depth[v]) { int t = u; u = v; v = t; }
    int diff = depth[u] - depth[v];
    for (int j = 0; j < LOG; j++) if ((diff >> j & 1) == 1) u = up[j][u];   // equalise depth
    if (u == v) return u;
    for (int j = LOG - 1; j >= 0; j--)
        if (up[j][u] != up[j][v]) { u = up[j][u]; v = up[j][v]; }           // jump while different
    return up[0][u];
}
--py
class Lca:
    def __init__(self, adj, root=0):
        n = len(adj)
        self.LOG = max(1, (n - 1).bit_length())
        self.depth = [0] * n
        parent = [root] * n               # root is its own parent
        seen = [False] * n
        seen[root] = True
        order = [root]
        for v in order:                   # BFS, appending while iterating
            for w in adj[v]:
                if not seen[w]:
                    seen[w] = True
                    parent[w] = v
                    self.depth[w] = self.depth[v] + 1
                    order.append(w)
        self.up = build_lift(parent, self.LOG)        # piece: 2^j-th ancestors

    def query(self, u, v):
        if self.depth[u] < self.depth[v]:
            u, v = v, u
        diff = self.depth[u] - self.depth[v]
        for j in range(self.LOG):         # equalise depth
            if diff >> j & 1:
                u = self.up[j][u]
        if u == v:
            return u
        for j in range(self.LOG - 1, -1, -1):
            if self.up[j][u] != self.up[j][v]:    # jump while different
                u, v = self.up[j][u], self.up[j][v]
        return self.up[0][u]
--cpp
struct Lca {
    int LOG;
    vector<vector<int>> up;               // up[j][v]: ancestor 2^j steps above v
    vector<int> depth;

    Lca(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int root = 0) {
        int n = adj.size();
        LOG = 1;
        while ((1 << LOG) < n) LOG++;
        vector<int> parent(n, root);                 // root is its own parent
        depth.assign(n, 0);
        vector<char> seen(n, 0);
        queue<int> q;
        q.push(root); seen[root] = 1;
        while (!q.empty()) {
            int v = q.front(); q.pop();
            for (int w : adj[v])
                if (!seen[w]) { seen[w] = 1; parent[w] = v; depth[w] = depth[v] + 1; q.push(w); }
        }
        up = buildLift(parent, LOG);                 // piece: 2^j-th ancestors
    }

    int query(int u, int v) const {
        if (depth[u] < depth[v]) swap(u, v);
        int diff = depth[u] - depth[v];
        for (int j = 0; j < LOG; j++) if (diff >> j & 1) u = up[j][u];       // equalise depth
        if (u == v) return u;
        for (int j = LOG - 1; j >= 0; j--)
            if (up[j][u] != up[j][v]) { u = up[j][u]; v = up[j][v]; }         // jump while different
        return up[0][u];
    }
};
--go
type Lca struct {
    log   int
    up    [][]int // up[j][v] = 2^j-th ancestor of v
    depth []int
}

func buildLca(adj [][]int, root int) *Lca {
    n := len(adj)
    t := &Lca{log: 1, depth: make([]int, n)}
    for 1<<t.log < n {
        t.log++
    }
    parent := make([]int, n)
    seen := make([]bool, n)
    parent[root], seen[root] = root, true // root is its own parent
    for q := []int{root}; len(q) > 0; q = q[1:] {
        v := q[0]
        for _, w := range adj[v] {
            if !seen[w] {
                seen[w], parent[w], t.depth[w] = true, v, t.depth[v]+1
                q = append(q, w)
            }
        }
    }
    t.up = buildLift(parent, t.log) // piece: 2^j-th ancestors
    return t
}

func (t *Lca) lca(u, v int) int {
    if t.depth[u] < t.depth[v] {
        u, v = v, u
    }
    diff := t.depth[u] - t.depth[v]
    for j := 0; j < t.log; j++ {
        if diff>>j&1 == 1 { // equalise depth
            u = t.up[j][u]
        }
    }
    if u == v {
        return u
    }
    for j := t.log - 1; j >= 0; j-- {
        if t.up[j][u] != t.up[j][v] { // jump while different
            u, v = t.up[j][u], t.up[j][v]
        }
    }
    return t.up[0][u]
}
### kthanc
--idea
The k-th ancestor of a node `v` is the node you reach by walking `k` steps up toward the root: the parent is the 1st ancestor, the grandparent the 2nd, and so on. Walking one step at a time costs up to `n` per query, too slow when there are many queries on a deep tree.

Binary lifting jumps by powers of two. Store `up[j][v]`, the node `2^j` steps above `v`. Any `k` is a sum of distinct powers of two (its binary digits), so you reach the answer by making one jump for each 1-bit of `k`. For `k = 13 = 8 + 4 + 1` you jump 8, then 4, then 1.
--build
1. Root the tree. With a BFS from the root, compute `parent[v]` (the root is its own parent) and `depth[v]`.
2. Choose `LOG` so that `2^LOG` is at least `n`. Build the table with `buildLift(parent, LOG)` (code piece `u-binlift`): `up[j][v]` is the node `2^j` steps above `v`, and each row is two jumps of the row before.
3. To answer `kth(v, k)`: if `k > depth[v]`, the tree is not that tall above `v`, return `-1`. This check is needed because the root points to itself, so jumping past it would silently return the root.
4. Otherwise for each bit `j` of `k` that is set, set `v = up[j][v]`.
5. Return `v`.
6. Invariant: after handling bits `0..j`, `v` is the ancestor exactly `(k mod 2^(j+1))` steps above the original node.
--think
- Signal: "go up `k` levels", "the ancestor at depth `d`" (that is `k = depth[v] - d`), "the `k`-th node on the path from `u` to `v`" (use the LCA, then jump on whichever side holds the `k`-th node), and problems where each node points to a parent-like next node and you need "after `k` steps" (such as a function `f` applied `k` times).
- The same table works on any functional graph (`funcgraph`) to find where you are after `10^18` steps: jump by the bits of `k`.
- Alternative: an offline DFS with a stack of the current path lets you answer all queries for one node when you are at it (the `k`-th ancestor is `path[depth - k]`). Another is the ladder algorithm for O(1) queries, rarely needed.
- Pitfalls: `k` larger than the depth (must return "none"), using a sentinel node instead of `-1` and forgetting it points to itself, and too small a `LOG`.
--cost
Build O(n log n) time and space, each query O(log n).
--uses
u-binlift
--java
static int LOG;

// parent[v] = parent of v, the root is its own parent; returns up[j][v]
static int[][] buildAncestors(int[] parent) {
    LOG = 1;
    while ((1 << LOG) < parent.length) LOG++;
    return buildLift(parent, LOG);                    // piece: 2^j-th ancestors
}

// k-th ancestor of v, or -1 if the tree is not that tall (depth[v] < k)
static int kthAncestor(int[][] up, int[] depth, int v, int k) {
    if (k > depth[v]) return -1;                      // the root points to itself: check first
    for (int j = 0; j < LOG; j++)
        if ((k >> j & 1) == 1) v = up[j][v];          // one jump per set bit
    return v;
}
--py
def build_ancestors(parent):
    """parent[v] is the parent of v, the root is its own parent. Returns (up, LOG)."""
    log = max(1, (len(parent) - 1).bit_length())
    return build_lift(parent, log), log               # piece: 2^j-th ancestors

def kth_ancestor(up, log, depth, v, k):
    """k-th ancestor of v, or -1 if the tree is not that tall (depth[v] < k)."""
    if k > depth[v]:
        return -1                         # the root points to itself: check first
    for j in range(log):
        if k >> j & 1:
            v = up[j][v]                  # one jump per set bit
    return v
--cpp
// parent[v] = parent of v, the root is its own parent; returns up[j][v]
vector<vector<int>> buildAncestors(const vector<int>& parent) {
    int LOG = 1;
    while ((1 << LOG) < (int)parent.size()) LOG++;
    return buildLift(parent, LOG);                    // piece: 2^j-th ancestors
}

// k-th ancestor of v, or -1 if the tree is not that tall (depth[v] < k)
int kthAncestor(const vector<vector<int>>& up, const vector<int>& depth, int v, int k) {
    if (k > depth[v]) return -1;                      // the root points to itself: check first
    for (int j = 0; j < (int)up.size(); j++)
        if (k >> j & 1) v = up[j][v];                 // one jump per set bit
    return v;
}
--go
// parent[v] = parent of v, the root is its own parent; returns up[j][v]
func buildAncestors(parent []int) [][]int {
    log := 1
    for 1<<log < len(parent) {
        log++
    }
    return buildLift(parent, log) // piece: 2^j-th ancestors
}

// k-th ancestor of v, or -1 if the tree is not that tall (depth[v] < k)
func kthAncestor(up [][]int, depth []int, v, k int) int {
    if k > depth[v] { // the root points to itself: check first
        return -1
    }
    for j := range up {
        if k>>j&1 == 1 { // one jump per set bit
            v = up[j][v]
        }
    }
    return v
}
### lcaeuler
--idea
This is another way to answer LCA queries (closest shared ancestor in a rooted tree) by turning the problem into a range minimum query. Walk the tree depth first and write down a node every time you enter it or come back to it after finishing a child. The list has `2n - 1` entries. For two nodes `u` and `v`, look at the stretch of the list between their first appearances. The shallowest node in that stretch is their LCA.

The reason: walking from `u` to `v` in that tour goes up from `u` toward the LCA and then down toward `v`, and the highest point it reaches is exactly the LCA. A sparse table (a precomputed table of range minimums for every power-of-two length) then finds the shallowest entry in constant time.
--build
1. Run an iterative DFS from the root. Append the node to `euler` when first entering it, and again each time you return to it from a child. Record `first[v]`, the index of its first appearance, and `depth[v]`.
2. The `euler` list now has `2n - 1` entries.
3. Build a sparse table `sp` over `euler`: `sp[0][i] = euler[i]`, and `sp[j][i]` is the shallower of `sp[j-1][i]` and `sp[j-1][i + 2^(j-1)]`, where shallower means smaller `depth`.
4. To query `lca(u, v)`: let `l = min(first[u], first[v])` and `r = max(first[u], first[v])`. Let `k = floor(log2(r - l + 1))`.
5. Return the shallower of `sp[k][l]` and `sp[k][r - 2^k + 1]`. The two blocks of length `2^k` overlap and together cover `[l, r]`. Overlap is harmless for a minimum.
6. Invariant: between two consecutive entries of `euler`, depth changes by exactly one. That is why the shallowest node between the first appearances of `u` and `v` must be a common ancestor, and the lowest one possible.
--think
- Signal: very many LCA queries (hundreds of thousands) on a static tree, where O(log n) each is too slow, or you also need the Euler tour for other reasons.
- Compare: binary lifting (`lca`) is simpler and supports k-th ancestor and path aggregates; this is faster per query (constant time) but uses a bigger table (`2n` times log) and does nothing else.
- Sparse tables work for any idempotent operation (min, max, gcd), because overlapping blocks are allowed. They do not work for sums.
- This also gives the distance between two nodes with the depth formula, and the reduction in the other direction (range minimum as an LCA in a Cartesian tree) is a classic.
- Pitfalls: using `first` with the wrong index when `u == v`, building the table over nodes but comparing by index instead of depth, and the iterative DFS must re-append the parent after each child returns.
--cost
Build O(n log n) time and space, query O(1).
--java
static int[] first, depthOf, lg;
static int[][] sp;                                    // sp[j][i]: shallowest node in euler[i .. i + 2^j - 1]

static void buildLcaEuler(List<List<Integer>> adj, int root) {
    int n = adj.size();
    first = new int[n]; depthOf = new int[n];
    int[] it = new int[n], parent = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(parent, -1);
    List<Integer> euler = new ArrayList<>();
    ArrayDeque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
    st.push(root); first[root] = 0; euler.add(root);
    while (!st.isEmpty()) {
        int v = st.peek();
        if (it[v] < adj.get(v).size()) {
            int w = adj.get(v).get(it[v]++);
            if (w == parent[v]) continue;
            parent[w] = v; depthOf[w] = depthOf[v] + 1;
            first[w] = euler.size(); euler.add(w);    // enter w
            st.push(w);
        } else {
            st.pop();
            if (!st.isEmpty()) euler.add(st.peek());   // return to the parent
        }
    }
    int m = euler.size();
    lg = new int[m + 1];
    for (int i = 2; i <= m; i++) lg[i] = lg[i / 2] + 1;
    sp = new int[lg[m] + 1][];
    sp[0] = new int[m];
    for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) sp[0][i] = euler.get(i);
    for (int j = 1; j <= lg[m]; j++) {
        sp[j] = new int[m - (1 << j) + 1];
        for (int i = 0; i + (1 << j) <= m; i++) {
            int a = sp[j - 1][i], b = sp[j - 1][i + (1 << (j - 1))];
            sp[j][i] = depthOf[a] <= depthOf[b] ? a : b;
        }
    }
}

static int lcaEuler(int u, int v) {
    int l = Math.min(first[u], first[v]), r = Math.max(first[u], first[v]);
    int k = lg[r - l + 1];
    int a = sp[k][l], b = sp[k][r - (1 << k) + 1];
    return depthOf[a] <= depthOf[b] ? a : b;
}
--py
class LcaEuler:
    def __init__(self, adj, root=0):
        n = len(adj)
        self.first = [0] * n
        self.depth = [0] * n
        euler = [root]
        it = [0] * n
        parent = [-1] * n
        stack = [root]
        while stack:
            v = stack[-1]
            if it[v] < len(adj[v]):
                w = adj[v][it[v]]
                it[v] += 1
                if w == parent[v]:
                    continue
                parent[w] = v
                self.depth[w] = self.depth[v] + 1
                self.first[w] = len(euler)
                euler.append(w)           # enter w
                stack.append(w)
            else:
                stack.pop()
                if stack:
                    euler.append(stack[-1])   # return to the parent
        m = len(euler)
        d = self.depth
        self.sp = [euler]                 # sp[j][i]: shallowest node in euler[i .. i + 2^j - 1]
        j = 1
        while (1 << j) <= m:
            prev, half = self.sp[-1], 1 << (j - 1)
            self.sp.append([prev[i] if d[prev[i]] <= d[prev[i + half]] else prev[i + half]
                            for i in range(m - (1 << j) + 1)])
            j += 1

    def query(self, u, v):
        l, r = sorted((self.first[u], self.first[v]))
        k = (r - l + 1).bit_length() - 1
        a, b = self.sp[k][l], self.sp[k][r - (1 << k) + 1]
        return a if self.depth[a] <= self.depth[b] else b
--cpp
struct LcaEuler {
    vector<int> first, depth, lg;
    vector<vector<int>> sp;               // sp[j][i]: shallowest node in euler[i .. i + 2^j - 1]

    LcaEuler(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int root = 0) {
        int n = adj.size();
        first.assign(n, 0); depth.assign(n, 0);
        vector<int> euler = {root}, it(n, 0), parent(n, -1), st = {root};
        while (!st.empty()) {
            int v = st.back();
            if (it[v] < (int)adj[v].size()) {
                int w = adj[v][it[v]++];
                if (w == parent[v]) continue;
                parent[w] = v; depth[w] = depth[v] + 1;
                first[w] = euler.size(); euler.push_back(w);   // enter w
                st.push_back(w);
            } else {
                st.pop_back();
                if (!st.empty()) euler.push_back(st.back());    // return to the parent
            }
        }
        int m = euler.size();
        lg.assign(m + 1, 0);
        for (int i = 2; i <= m; i++) lg[i] = lg[i / 2] + 1;
        sp.assign(lg[m] + 1, {});
        sp[0] = euler;
        for (int j = 1; j <= lg[m]; j++) {
            sp[j].resize(m - (1 << j) + 1);
            for (int i = 0; i + (1 << j) <= m; i++) {
                int a = sp[j - 1][i], b = sp[j - 1][i + (1 << (j - 1))];
                sp[j][i] = depth[a] <= depth[b] ? a : b;
            }
        }
    }

    int query(int u, int v) const {
        int l = min(first[u], first[v]), r = max(first[u], first[v]);
        int k = lg[r - l + 1];
        int a = sp[k][l], b = sp[k][r - (1 << k) + 1];
        return depth[a] <= depth[b] ? a : b;
    }
};
--go
type LcaEuler struct {
    first, depth, lg []int
    sp               [][]int // sp[j][i]: shallowest node in euler[i .. i + 2^j - 1]
}

func buildLcaEuler(adj [][]int, root int) *LcaEuler {
    n := len(adj)
    t := &LcaEuler{first: make([]int, n), depth: make([]int, n)}
    it, parent := make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
    for i := range parent {
        parent[i] = -1
    }
    euler := []int{root}
    st := []int{root}
    for len(st) > 0 {
        v := st[len(st)-1]
        if it[v] < len(adj[v]) {
            w := adj[v][it[v]]
            it[v]++
            if w == parent[v] {
                continue
            }
            parent[w], t.depth[w] = v, t.depth[v]+1
            t.first[w] = len(euler) // enter w
            euler = append(euler, w)
            st = append(st, w)
        } else {
            st = st[:len(st)-1]
            if len(st) > 0 {
                euler = append(euler, st[len(st)-1]) // return to the parent
            }
        }
    }
    m := len(euler)
    t.lg = make([]int, m+1)
    for i := 2; i <= m; i++ {
        t.lg[i] = t.lg[i/2] + 1
    }
    t.sp = [][]int{euler}
    for j := 1; j <= t.lg[m]; j++ {
        row := make([]int, m-(1<<j)+1)
        for i := range row {
            a, b := t.sp[j-1][i], t.sp[j-1][i+(1<<(j-1))]
            row[i] = a
            if t.depth[b] < t.depth[a] {
                row[i] = b
            }
        }
        t.sp = append(t.sp, row)
    }
    return t
}

func (t *LcaEuler) lca(u, v int) int {
    l, r := min(t.first[u], t.first[v]), max(t.first[u], t.first[v])
    k := t.lg[r-l+1]
    a, b := t.sp[k][l], t.sp[k][r-(1<<k)+1]
    if t.depth[a] <= t.depth[b] {
        return a
    }
    return b
}
### treematch
--idea
On a tree you can pick a set of edges so that no two share a node (a matching), or pick a set of nodes so that every edge has at least one end chosen (a vertex cover), or pick nodes so that no two are neighbours (an independent set). On general graphs these are hard. On a tree they are easy, and they are linked: the maximum matching size equals the minimum vertex cover size (this is König's theorem, true for all bipartite graphs, and every tree is bipartite). The maximum independent set has `n` minus that many nodes.

A greedy from the bottom works. A leaf has only one edge, so the best use of a leaf is to match it with its parent. Take the deepest unmatched node and, if its parent is also free, match them. Taking the leaf edge is never worse than skipping it, since the parent could have at most one match anyway.
--build
1. Root the tree at node 0. Find an order in which every node comes after its parent (BFS order) and record `parent[v]`.
2. Make `matched[v] = false` for all nodes and set `count = 0`.
3. Process the nodes in reverse of that order, so children are handled before parents.
4. For each node `v`, if `v` is not matched and `v` has a parent `p` that is not matched, match them: set both `matched` flags and add one to `count`.
5. `count` is the size of a maximum matching, and also the minimum vertex cover size. The maximum independent set size is `n - count`.
6. Invariant: when `v` is processed, all nodes below it are already decided and none of them can use the edge `(v, parent)` anymore, so matching `v` upward is the only way `v` can still contribute.
