7. For weighted versions (maximum weight matching, maximum weight independent set, minimum weight vertex cover) use a DP with two states per node, `take[v]` and `skip[v]`, computed from children in post order. The greedy no longer applies.
--think
- Signal: "choose edges on a tree with no shared endpoint", "guard every road with the fewest guards" (vertex cover), "largest set of people with no two friends" on a tree-shaped friendship graph (independent set), "place cameras".
- Unweighted: the greedy above is enough and runs in one pass. Weighted or with extra rules: tree DP with states "this node is used" or "not used".
- Dominating set (cameras that watch neighbours too) is a separate tree DP with three states, or a greedy that places a camera at the parent of an uncovered leaf.
- Do not use this on graphs with cycles. For bipartite graphs use real matching (`matching`), for general graphs `blossom`, and for independent sets in general graphs see `clique`.
- Pitfalls: processing in the wrong order (parents before children breaks the greedy), a forest (run from every root), and mixing up "cover edges" with "cover nodes".
--cost
Time O(n), space O(n).
--java
// tree as adjacency lists; returns the size of a maximum matching (= minimum vertex cover)
static int treeMatching(List<List<Integer>> adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    int[] parent = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(parent, -2);                          // -2 = not reached, -1 = root
    int[] order = new int[n];
    int head = 0, tail = 0;
    parent[0] = -1; order[tail++] = 0;
    while (head < tail) {
        int v = order[head++];
        for (int w : adj.get(v))
            if (parent[w] == -2) { parent[w] = v; order[tail++] = w; }
    }
    boolean[] matched = new boolean[n];
    int count = 0;
    for (int i = tail - 1; i >= 0; i--) {             // children before parents
        int v = order[i], p = parent[v];
        if (p >= 0 && !matched[v] && !matched[p]) {
            matched[v] = matched[p] = true;
            count++;
        }
    }
    return count;
}
--py
def tree_matching(adj):
    """Size of a maximum matching on a tree (= minimum vertex cover size)."""
    n = len(adj)
    parent = [-2] * n                     # -2 = not reached, -1 = root
    parent[0] = -1
    order = [0]
    for v in order:                       # BFS order, parents before children
        for w in adj[v]:
            if parent[w] == -2:
                parent[w] = v
                order.append(w)
    matched = [False] * n
    count = 0
    for v in reversed(order):             # children before parents
        p = parent[v]
        if p >= 0 and not matched[v] and not matched[p]:
            matched[v] = matched[p] = True
            count += 1
    return count
--cpp
// tree as adjacency lists; returns the size of a maximum matching (= minimum vertex cover)
int treeMatching(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<int> parent(n, -2), order = {0};           // -2 = not reached, -1 = root
    parent[0] = -1;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < order.size(); i++) {        // BFS order, parents before children
        int v = order[i];
        for (int w : adj[v])
            if (parent[w] == -2) { parent[w] = v; order.push_back(w); }
    }
    vector<char> matched(n, 0);
    int count = 0;
    for (int i = (int)order.size() - 1; i >= 0; i--) { // children before parents
        int v = order[i], p = parent[v];
        if (p >= 0 && !matched[v] && !matched[p]) {
            matched[v] = matched[p] = 1;
            count++;
        }
    }
    return count;
}
--go
// tree as adjacency lists; returns the size of a maximum matching (= minimum vertex cover)
func treeMatching(adj [][]int) int {
    n := len(adj)
    parent := make([]int, n) // -2 = not reached, -1 = root
    for i := range parent {
        parent[i] = -2
    }
    parent[0] = -1
    order := []int{0} // BFS order doubles as the visit queue
    for head := 0; head < len(order); head++ {
        for _, w := range adj[order[head]] {
            if parent[w] == -2 {
                parent[w] = order[head]
                order = append(order, w)
            }
        }
    }
    matched := make([]bool, n)
    count := 0
    for i := len(order) - 1; i >= 0; i-- { // children before parents
        v := order[i]
        if p := parent[v]; p >= 0 && !matched[v] && !matched[p] {
            matched[v], matched[p] = true, true
            count++
        }
    }
    return count
}
### scc
--idea
In a directed graph, two nodes `u` and `v` are strongly connected if you can get from `u` to `v` and also from `v` back to `u` by following arrows. A strongly connected component (SCC) is a maximal group of nodes that are all strongly connected to each other. Think of a road network with one-way streets: an SCC is a part of town where you can drive from any spot to any other spot and back.

Every node belongs to exactly one SCC (a lone node is an SCC of size one), because "can reach and be reached" is an equivalence: if `a` and `b` are strongly connected and `b` and `c` are, then so are `a` and `c`. If you shrink each SCC to a single node, the result has no cycles (see `condense`), which is why SCCs are the key to cutting a messy directed graph into an ordered structure. This lesson shows the definition as code, the slow and clear way. Kosaraju (`kosaraju`) and Tarjan (`tarjan`) compute the same answer in one linear pass.
--build
1. For every node `s`, find the set `reach[s]` of nodes reachable from `s`. Call `dfsIter(adj, s)` (code piece `u-iterdfs`), a depth-first search with an explicit stack that returns a true/false flag per node.
2. Make `comp` filled with `-1` (no component yet) and `count = 0`.
3. For each node `v` in order, if `comp[v]` is still `-1`, it starts a new component. Look at every node `w` with no label yet: if `reach[v][w]` and `reach[w][v]` are both true, `w` is strongly connected to `v`, so set `comp[w] = count`. (`v` itself qualifies, since a node reaches itself.)
4. Add one to `count`.
5. Invariant: when `v` starts a component, none of its mutual-reach partners were labelled yet, since a partner would have been labelled when the earlier member of the same group started. So each group is labelled once, as a whole.
6. This costs a search from every node, which is too slow for big graphs. The linear algorithms get the same labels from only two passes.
--think
- Signal: a directed graph and a question of mutual reachability: "groups of cities that can all reach each other", "which web pages form a cluster of links back and forth", "minimum edges to add to make the graph strongly connected", "is there a cycle through this node" (it lies on one exactly when its SCC has more than one node, or it has a self-loop).
- The slow version here is fine for a few thousand nodes and is the easiest way to be sure of the definition. For more, use `kosaraju` (two DFS passes) or `tarjan` (one pass with low-link values).
- After you have the SCCs, almost every problem continues with `condense`: shrink each SCC to a node and work on the resulting DAG (directed acyclic graph, no cycles) with DP or topological order.
- 2-SAT (`twosat`) is a direct use: a formula is unsatisfiable exactly when a variable and its negation land in the same SCC.
- Do not confuse with undirected connected components (`components`). In an undirected graph, reachability is symmetric, so one search is enough. In a directed graph it is not.
- Pitfalls: treating "reachable from `v`" as "same component" (only mutual reachability counts), and recursion depth in the recursive linear versions on a long chain.
--cost
Time O(V times (V + E)) for this version, space O(V squared) for the reach table. The linear algorithms run in O(V + E).
--uses
u-iterdfs
--java
// returns comp[v]: ids 0..k-1, two nodes share an id exactly when they reach each other
static int[] sccByReachability(List<List<Integer>> adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    boolean[][] reach = new boolean[n][];
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) reach[s] = dfsIter(adj, s);       // piece: what s can reach
    int[] comp = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(comp, -1);
    int count = 0;
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
        if (comp[v] != -1) continue;
        for (int w = 0; w < n; w++)
            if (comp[w] == -1 && reach[v][w] && reach[w][v]) comp[w] = count;   // mutual reach
        count++;
    }
    return comp;
}
--py
def scc_by_reachability(adj):
    """Returns (comp, count). Two nodes share a comp id exactly when they reach each other."""
    n = len(adj)
    reach = [dfs_iter(adj, s) for s in range(n)]      # piece: what s can reach
    comp = [-1] * n
    count = 0
    for v in range(n):
        if comp[v] != -1:
            continue
        for w in range(n):
            if comp[w] == -1 and reach[v][w] and reach[w][v]:    # mutual reach
                comp[w] = count
        count += 1
    return comp, count
--cpp
// returns comp[v]: ids 0..k-1, two nodes share an id exactly when they reach each other
vector<int> sccByReachability(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<vector<bool>> reach(n);
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) reach[s] = dfsIter(adj, s);       // piece: what s can reach
    vector<int> comp(n, -1);
    int count = 0;
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
        if (comp[v] != -1) continue;
        for (int w = 0; w < n; w++)
            if (comp[w] == -1 && reach[v][w] && reach[w][v]) comp[w] = count;   // mutual reach
        count++;
    }
    return comp;
}
--go
// returns comp[v]: ids 0..k-1, two nodes share an id exactly when they reach each other
func sccByReachability(adj [][]int) []int {
    n := len(adj)
    reach := make([][]bool, n)
    for s := range reach {
        reach[s] = dfsIter(adj, s) // piece: what s can reach
    }
    comp := make([]int, n)
    for i := range comp {
        comp[i] = -1
    }
    count := 0
    for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
        if comp[v] != -1 {
            continue
        }
        for w := 0; w < n; w++ {
            if comp[w] == -1 && reach[v][w] && reach[w][v] { // mutual reach
                comp[w] = count
            }
        }
        count++
    }
    return comp
}
### kosaraju
--idea
Kosaraju's algorithm finds all strongly connected components (groups of nodes that can reach each other along arrows) with two depth-first passes. It rests on a neat fact: if you reverse every edge, the strongly connected components stay exactly the same, but the arrows between different components flip direction.

Pass one runs DFS on the original graph and records the order in which nodes finish. The node that finishes last lies in a "source" component (one with no arrows coming in from other components). Pass two walks through nodes from last finished to first, and runs a search on the reversed graph. From a source component, the reversed graph lets the search escape only inside its own component (the arrows leaving it in the reversed graph were the ones entering it, and there are none), so it collects exactly that component.
--build
1. Pass one on `adj`: for every unvisited node, run an iterative DFS (explicit stack of node and next-neighbour index). When a node has no more neighbours to try, append it to `order`. That is the finish order.
2. Build `radj`, the reversed graph: for every edge `v -> w` add `w -> v`.
3. Make `comp` filled with `-1` and `count = 0`.
4. Pass two: go through `order` from the end to the start. For each node `s` with `comp[s] == -1`, label it `count`, then search `radj` from `s` and label every unlabelled node reached with `count`. Then add one to `count`.
5. Return `comp` and `count`.
6. Why pass two stays inside one component: take the unlabelled node `s` that finished last. Its component `C` has no incoming arrow from another unlabelled component, because such a component would hold a node that finished later. In `radj`, arrows leaving `C` toward unlabelled nodes would be arrows that entered `C` in the original, and there are none. Labelled nodes are blocked by the label check.
7. Bonus: components come out numbered in topological order of the condensation (`condense`): component 0 is a source, and every arrow between components goes from a lower number to a higher one.
--think
- Signal: same as `scc`. Choose Kosaraju when you want the easiest correct linear SCC code to remember: two plain DFS passes and a reversed graph. Choose Tarjan (`tarjan`) when memory matters, since Tarjan needs no reversed copy and one pass.
- The component numbers are already in topological order, so DP over the condensation can just loop `0..count-1` (or backwards).
- If the graph is given as an edge list, building both `adj` and `radj` is cheap, so the extra copy costs little.
- Recursion is the usual way to write DFS. On a chain of 100,000 nodes it overflows the call stack in Python, Java and sometimes C++. The code here uses an explicit stack so it does not.
- Pitfalls: running pass two in the wrong order (it must go from the latest finisher), forgetting to reverse the edges, and running pass two on the original graph by mistake (it then leaks into other components).
--cost
Time O(V + E), space O(V + E) for the reversed graph.
--java
// returns comp[v]; components are numbered 0..k-1 in topological order of the condensation
static int[] kosaraju(List<List<Integer>> adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
    int[] it = new int[n];
    int[] order = new int[n];
    int top = 0;
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {                       // pass one: finish order
        if (seen[s]) continue;
        ArrayDeque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
        seen[s] = true; st.push(s);
        while (!st.isEmpty()) {
            int v = st.peek();
            if (it[v] < adj.get(v).size()) {
                int w = adj.get(v).get(it[v]++);
                if (!seen[w]) { seen[w] = true; st.push(w); }
            } else {
                order[top++] = v;                       // v is finished
                st.pop();
            }
        }
    }
    List<List<Integer>> radj = new ArrayList<>();       // reversed graph
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) radj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) for (int w : adj.get(v)) radj.get(w).add(v);
    int[] comp = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(comp, -1);
    int count = 0;
    for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) {                  // pass two: latest finisher first
        int s = order[i];
        if (comp[s] != -1) continue;
        ArrayDeque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
        comp[s] = count; st.push(s);
        while (!st.isEmpty()) {
            int v = st.pop();
            for (int w : radj.get(v)) if (comp[w] == -1) { comp[w] = count; st.push(w); }
        }
        count++;
    }
    return comp;
}
--py
def kosaraju(adj):
    """Returns (comp, count). Components are numbered in topological order of the condensation."""
    n = len(adj)
    seen = [False] * n
    order = []
    for s in range(n):                                  # pass one: finish order
        if seen[s]:
            continue
        seen[s] = True
        stack = [(s, 0)]
        while stack:
            v, i = stack[-1]
            if i < len(adj[v]):
                stack[-1] = (v, i + 1)
                w = adj[v][i]
                if not seen[w]:
                    seen[w] = True
                    stack.append((w, 0))
            else:
                stack.pop()
                order.append(v)                         # v is finished
    radj = [[] for _ in range(n)]                       # reversed graph
    for v in range(n):
        for w in adj[v]:
            radj[w].append(v)
    comp = [-1] * n
    count = 0
    for s in reversed(order):                           # pass two: latest finisher first
        if comp[s] != -1:
            continue
        comp[s] = count
        stack = [s]
        while stack:
            v = stack.pop()
            for w in radj[v]:
                if comp[w] == -1:
                    comp[w] = count
                    stack.append(w)
        count += 1
    return comp, count
--cpp
// returns comp[v]; components are numbered 0..k-1 in topological order of the condensation
vector<int> kosaraju(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<char> seen(n, 0);
    vector<int> it(n, 0), order;
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {                       // pass one: finish order
        if (seen[s]) continue;
        vector<int> st = {s};
        seen[s] = 1;
        while (!st.empty()) {
            int v = st.back();
            if (it[v] < (int)adj[v].size()) {
                int w = adj[v][it[v]++];
                if (!seen[w]) { seen[w] = 1; st.push_back(w); }
            } else {
                order.push_back(v);                     // v is finished
                st.pop_back();
            }
        }
    }
    vector<vector<int>> radj(n);                        // reversed graph
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) for (int w : adj[v]) radj[w].push_back(v);
    vector<int> comp(n, -1);
    int count = 0;
    for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) {                  // pass two: latest finisher first
        int s = order[i];
        if (comp[s] != -1) continue;
        vector<int> st = {s};
        comp[s] = count;
        while (!st.empty()) {
            int v = st.back(); st.pop_back();
            for (int w : radj[v]) if (comp[w] == -1) { comp[w] = count; st.push_back(w); }
        }
        count++;
    }
    return comp;
}
--go
// returns comp[v]; components are numbered 0..k-1 in topological order of the condensation
func kosaraju(adj [][]int) []int {
    n := len(adj)
    seen := make([]bool, n)
    it := make([]int, n)
    order := make([]int, 0, n)
    for s := 0; s < n; s++ { // pass one: finish order
        if seen[s] {
            continue
        }
        seen[s] = true
        for st := []int{s}; len(st) > 0; {
            v := st[len(st)-1]
            if it[v] < len(adj[v]) {
                w := adj[v][it[v]]
                it[v]++
                if !seen[w] {
                    seen[w] = true
                    st = append(st, w)
                }
            } else {
                order = append(order, v) // v is finished
                st = st[:len(st)-1]
            }
        }
    }
    radj := make([][]int, n) // reversed graph
    for v := range adj {
        for _, w := range adj[v] {
            radj[w] = append(radj[w], v)
        }
    }
    comp := make([]int, n)
    for i := range comp {
        comp[i] = -1
    }
    count := 0
    for i := n - 1; i >= 0; i-- { // pass two: latest finisher first
        s := order[i]
        if comp[s] != -1 {
            continue
        }
        comp[s] = count
        for st := []int{s}; len(st) > 0; {
            v := st[len(st)-1]
            st = st[:len(st)-1]
            for _, w := range radj[v] {
                if comp[w] == -1 {
                    comp[w] = count
                    st = append(st, w)
                }
            }
        }
        count++
    }
    return comp
}
### condense
--idea
The condensation of a directed graph shrinks every strongly connected component (SCC, a group of nodes that can all reach each other) into a single node, and keeps an arrow from component A to component B whenever some original arrow goes from a node in A to a node in B. The result is always a DAG (directed acyclic graph): it has no cycles, because a cycle between components would merge them into one.

That is the whole point. A tangled graph with cycles becomes an ordered one you can process with a topological order, dynamic programming, or "which components have no incoming arrows". Think of folding every loop of a road map into one roundabout node: the map of roundabouts has no loops left.
--build
1. First get a component label for every node with `kosaraju` or `tarjan`: `comp[v]` in `0..k-1`, and `k`, the number of components.
2. Make `k` empty sets, one per component. Sets remove duplicate arrows between the same two components.
3. For every original edge `v -> w`: if `comp[v] != comp[w]`, add `comp[w]` to the set of `comp[v]`. An edge inside a component is dropped.
4. Turn each set into a list. This is the condensation DAG.
5. To order the components so that every arrow goes forward, call `topoOrder(dag)` (code piece `u-toposort`). It returns every component exactly once, because a DAG has no cycle to block it.
6. Typical continuations: DP over the DAG in that order (longest path, count of paths, best value reachable), the number of source components (no incoming arrows) which is the minimum number of starting points that reach everything, and adding edges to make the graph strongly connected (that needs `max(sources, sinks)` new edges when there is more than one component).
--think
- Signal: a directed graph with cycles where the question only depends on which groups reach which. Examples: "minimum nodes to start from so everything is reachable" (count the source components), "maximum value collectable in a walk where you can revisit" (sum values per component, then longest path on the DAG), "make it strongly connected with fewest edges".
- Weights: give each component node the sum (or count) of its members, so DP on the DAG works on component totals.
- If the labels come from Kosaraju, they are already topologically ordered, and the topological sort step is optional. Tarjan numbers components in the reverse order.
- Remember to dedupe arrows when you count them; otherwise the DP double counts routes.
- Pitfalls: forgetting that a component of size one still is a node, building the DAG from edges within a component (self-loops), and treating the component ids as the original node ids.
--cost
Time O(V + E) for building the DAG and sorting it (sets add a log factor if tree sets are used), space O(V + E).
--uses
u-toposort
--java
// comp[v] in 0..k-1. Returns the condensation DAG as adjacency lists over component ids.
static List<List<Integer>> condense(List<List<Integer>> adj, int[] comp, int k) {
    List<Set<Integer>> sets = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) sets.add(new TreeSet<>());          // sets drop duplicate arrows
    for (int v = 0; v < adj.size(); v++)
        for (int w : adj.get(v))
            if (comp[v] != comp[w]) sets.get(comp[v]).add(comp[w]);  // arrow between components
    List<List<Integer>> dag = new ArrayList<>();
    for (Set<Integer> s : sets) dag.add(new ArrayList<>(s));
    return dag;
}

// the components in an order where every arrow goes forward
static List<Integer> condensedOrder(List<List<Integer>> adj, int[] comp, int k) {
    return topoOrder(condense(adj, comp, k));                        // piece: topological order
}
--py
def condense(adj, comp, k):
    """comp[v] in 0..k-1. Returns the condensation DAG as adjacency lists over component ids."""
    dag = [set() for _ in range(k)]                  # sets drop duplicate arrows
    for v in range(len(adj)):
        for w in adj[v]:
            if comp[v] != comp[w]:
                dag[comp[v]].add(comp[w])            # arrow between components
    return [sorted(s) for s in dag]

def condensed_order(adj, comp, k):
    """The components in an order where every arrow goes forward."""
    return topo_order(condense(adj, comp, k))        # piece: topological order
--cpp
// comp[v] in 0..k-1. Returns the condensation DAG as adjacency lists over component ids.
vector<vector<int>> condense(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, const vector<int>& comp, int k) {
    vector<set<int>> sets(k);                                    // sets drop duplicate arrows
    for (int v = 0; v < (int)adj.size(); v++)
        for (int w : adj[v])
            if (comp[v] != comp[w]) sets[comp[v]].insert(comp[w]);   // arrow between components
    vector<vector<int>> dag(k);
    for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) dag[i].assign(sets[i].begin(), sets[i].end());
    return dag;
}

// the components in an order where every arrow goes forward
vector<int> condensedOrder(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, const vector<int>& comp, int k) {
    return topoOrder(condense(adj, comp, k));                    // piece: topological order
}
--go
// comp[v] in 0..k-1. Returns the condensation DAG as adjacency lists over component ids.
func condense(adj [][]int, comp []int, k int) [][]int {
    sets := make([]map[int]bool, k) // sets drop duplicate arrows
    for i := range sets {
        sets[i] = map[int]bool{}
    }
    for v := range adj {
        for _, w := range adj[v] {
            if comp[v] != comp[w] { // arrow between components
                sets[comp[v]][comp[w]] = true
            }
        }
    }
    dag := make([][]int, k)
    for c, s := range sets {
        for d := range s {
            dag[c] = append(dag[c], d)
        }
        sort.Ints(dag[c])
    }
    return dag
}

// the components in an order where every arrow goes forward
func condensedOrder(adj [][]int, comp []int, k int) []int {
    return topoOrder(condense(adj, comp, k)) // piece: topological order
}
### eulerpath
--idea
An Euler path walks along every edge of a graph exactly once. An Euler circuit does the same and ends where it started. It is the old puzzle of the bridges of Konigsberg: can you cross every bridge exactly once? Note that it is about edges, not nodes. (Visiting every node exactly once is a different and much harder problem, see `hamilton`.)

There is a simple test. In an undirected graph, walking through a node uses edges in pairs (one to arrive, one to leave), so every node must have an even number of edges, except the start and the end of a path, which may have an odd number. And all edges must be in one connected piece. So: an Euler circuit exists when every degree is even and the edges are connected; an Euler path (not a circuit) exists when exactly two nodes have odd degree and the edges are connected.
--build
1. Count the degree of every node: how many edge ends touch it (a self-loop adds 2).
2. Count the nodes with odd degree. For an undirected graph, the count must be 0 (circuit) or 2 (path). Any other count means no Euler path.
3. Connectivity: pick a node with degree above zero and call `dfsIter(adj, s)` (code piece `u-iterdfs`). Every node with degree above zero must be reached. Nodes with degree 0 are ignored, since they have no edges to walk.
4. If both tests pass, the Euler path exists. Build it with Hierholzer's algorithm (`hierholzer`).
5. Directed graphs have a different test: for every node, in-degree equals out-degree (circuit), or exactly one node has out minus in equal to 1 (the start) and exactly one has in minus out equal to 1 (the end), with all other nodes balanced. Connectivity is tested on the underlying undirected graph.
6. Why the parity test works: each time the walk passes through a node it uses one edge in and one edge out, so passes consume two edges at that node. Only a node where the walk begins or ends can have a leftover single edge.
--think
- Signal: "draw the picture without lifting the pen", "walk every road exactly once", "use every domino/edge once", "reconstruct a sequence from overlapping pieces" (de Bruijn graph: pieces are edges), "airline itinerary using every ticket once".
- First decide what the nodes and edges are. In the "domino" and "word chain" problems, the things you must use once are the edges, and the shared ends are the nodes.
- Euler path (every edge once) is easy (linear time). Hamiltonian path (every node once) is hard (exponential). Do not mix them up.
- The test only says whether a path exists. To output it, use `hierholzer`.
- Pitfalls: isolated nodes (they do not break connectivity, since they have no edges), self-loops adding two to the degree, counting multi-edges once, and the direction rules for directed graphs.
--cost
Time O(V + E), space O(V + E).
--uses
u-iterdfs
--java
// undirected multigraph; edges[i] = {u, v}. Returns 2 for a circuit, 1 for a path only, 0 for neither.
static int eulerKind(int n, int[][] edges) {
    List<List<Integer>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    int[] deg = new int[n];
    for (int[] e : edges) {
        adj.get(e[0]).add(e[1]); adj.get(e[1]).add(e[0]);
        deg[e[0]]++; deg[e[1]]++;
    }
    int odd = 0, start = -1;
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
        if (deg[v] % 2 == 1) odd++;
        if (deg[v] > 0 && start == -1) start = v;
    }
    if (start == -1) return 2;                          // no edges: the empty walk
    boolean[] seen = dfsIter(adj, start);               // piece: what the edges connect
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (deg[v] > 0 && !seen[v]) return 0;   // edges not in one piece
    return odd == 0 ? 2 : odd == 2 ? 1 : 0;
}
--py
def euler_kind(n, edges):
    """Undirected multigraph, edges is a list of (u, v).
    Returns 2 for a circuit, 1 for a path only, 0 for neither."""
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    deg = [0] * n
    for u, v in edges:
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
        deg[u] += 1
        deg[v] += 1
    odd = sum(d % 2 for d in deg)
    start = next((v for v in range(n) if deg[v] > 0), -1)
    if start == -1:
        return 2                                        # no edges: the empty walk
    seen = dfs_iter(adj, start)                         # piece: what the edges connect
    if any(deg[v] > 0 and not seen[v] for v in range(n)):
        return 0                                        # edges not in one piece
    return 2 if odd == 0 else 1 if odd == 2 else 0
--cpp
// undirected multigraph; edges[i] = {u, v}. Returns 2 for a circuit, 1 for a path only, 0 for neither.
int eulerKind(int n, const vector<pair<int,int>>& edges) {
    vector<vector<int>> adj(n);
    vector<int> deg(n, 0);
    for (auto [u, v] : edges) {
        adj[u].push_back(v); adj[v].push_back(u);
        deg[u]++; deg[v]++;
    }
    int odd = 0, start = -1;
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
        if (deg[v] % 2 == 1) odd++;
        if (deg[v] > 0 && start == -1) start = v;
    }
    if (start == -1) return 2;                          // no edges: the empty walk
    vector<bool> seen = dfsIter(adj, start);            // piece: what the edges connect
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (deg[v] > 0 && !seen[v]) return 0;   // edges not in one piece
    return odd == 0 ? 2 : odd == 2 ? 1 : 0;
}
--go
// undirected multigraph; edges[i] = {u, v}. Returns 2 for a circuit, 1 for a path only, 0 for neither.
func eulerKind(n int, edges [][2]int) int {
    adj := make([][]int, n)
    deg := make([]int, n)
    for _, e := range edges {
        adj[e[0]] = append(adj[e[0]], e[1])
        adj[e[1]] = append(adj[e[1]], e[0])
        deg[e[0]]++
        deg[e[1]]++
    }
    odd, start := 0, -1
    for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
        if deg[v]%2 == 1 {
            odd++
        }
        if deg[v] > 0 && start == -1 {
            start = v
        }
    }
    if start == -1 { // no edges: the empty walk
        return 2
    }
    seen := dfsIter(adj, start) // piece: what the edges connect
    for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
        if deg[v] > 0 && !seen[v] { // edges not in one piece
            return 0
        }
    }
    switch odd {
    case 0:
        return 2
    case 2:
        return 1
    }
    return 0
}
### hierholzer
--idea
Hierholzer's algorithm builds an Euler path or circuit (a walk that uses every edge exactly once) once you know one exists. The picture: start walking and never reuse an edge, until you get stuck. In a graph where all degrees are even you can only get stuck back at the start, which gives a closed loop. If edges remain, some node on that loop still has unused edges; walk a new loop from there and splice it into the first one at that node.

The code does the splicing for you with a stack. Walk forward pushing nodes. When the current node has no unused edge, it is finished: pop it and write it to the answer. Because a node is written only when it has nothing left to explore, the answer comes out in reverse order, with side loops already spliced in at the right place.
--build
1. Store each edge in both endpoint lists as `(neighbour, edge id)`. Keep `used[id]` and a pointer `ptr[v]` to the next unchecked entry of `adj[v]`.
2. Check the degrees: with 0 odd-degree nodes start anywhere that has an edge; with exactly 2, start at one of the odd nodes; otherwise return empty (see `eulerpath`).
3. `push(st, start)` using the stack moves from code piece `u-stackop`.
4. While the stack is not empty: let `v = peek(st)`. Skip entries of `adj[v]` whose edge is already used.
5. If `v` has no unused edge left, `pop(st)` it and append it to `path`.
6. Otherwise take the next unused edge `(w, id)`, set `used[id] = true`, and `push(st, w)`.
7. When the stack is empty, reverse `path`. If it has `E + 1` nodes (`E` edges), it is the Euler path. If it is shorter, some edges were unreachable and no Euler path exists.
8. Invariant: the stack always holds a trail (a walk with no repeated edge) from `start` to the current node. A node leaves it only when stuck, and being stuck means every edge at it is used, so it belongs at that spot of the final tour.
--think
- Signal: you must output the actual walk: "print the route", "reconstruct the sequence", "find the itinerary that uses every ticket", "order the dominoes".
- Do not use plain DFS with backtracking, which can take exponential time. Hierholzer is linear because each edge is used once and each pointer only moves forward.
- For lexicographically smallest itinerary, sort each adjacency list first so the smallest edge is tried first (and use the pointer from the front).
- For directed graphs, drop the second copy of each edge (store only `u -> v`), start at the node with out-degree minus in-degree equal to 1 (or anywhere for a circuit), and the same stack loop works. The result comes out reversed, as here.
- It is also the standard way to construct de Bruijn sequences.
- Pitfalls: forgetting to mark both copies of an undirected edge as used (using the edge id avoids this), not reversing at the end, and not checking the final length (a graph with edges in two pieces gives a short path).
--cost
Time O(V + E), space O(V + E).
--uses
u-stackop
--java
// undirected multigraph; edges[i] = {u, v}. Returns the nodes of an Euler path in order, or an empty list.
static List<Integer> eulerPath(int n, int[][] edges) {
    List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    int[] deg = new int[n];
    for (int i = 0; i < edges.length; i++) {
        adj.get(edges[i][0]).add(new int[]{edges[i][1], i});
        adj.get(edges[i][1]).add(new int[]{edges[i][0], i});
        deg[edges[i][0]]++; deg[edges[i][1]]++;
    }
    int odd = 0, start = -1;
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
        if (deg[v] % 2 == 1) { odd++; if (start == -1 || deg[start] % 2 == 0) start = v; }
        else if (deg[v] > 0 && start == -1) start = v;
    }
    if (odd != 0 && odd != 2) return new ArrayList<>();
    if (start == -1) return new ArrayList<>(List.of(0));   // no edges at all
    boolean[] used = new boolean[edges.length];
    int[] ptr = new int[n];
    Deque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
    List<Integer> path = new ArrayList<>();
    push(st, start);
    while (!st.isEmpty()) {
        int v = peek(st);
        while (ptr[v] < adj.get(v).size() && used[adj.get(v).get(ptr[v])[1]]) ptr[v]++;
        if (ptr[v] == adj.get(v).size()) {
            path.add(pop(st));                              // stuck: v is final
        } else {
            int[] e = adj.get(v).get(ptr[v]);
            used[e[1]] = true;                              // use the edge in both directions
            push(st, e[0]);
        }
    }
    Collections.reverse(path);
    return path.size() == edges.length + 1 ? path : new ArrayList<>();   // edges in two pieces
}
--py
def euler_path(n, edges):
    """Undirected multigraph, edges is a list of (u, v).
    Returns the nodes of an Euler path in order, or [] if none exists."""
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    deg = [0] * n
    for i, (u, v) in enumerate(edges):
        adj[u].append((v, i))
        adj[v].append((u, i))
        deg[u] += 1
        deg[v] += 1
    odd = [v for v in range(n) if deg[v] % 2 == 1]
    if len(odd) not in (0, 2):
        return []
    if odd:
        start = odd[0]
    else:
        start = next((v for v in range(n) if deg[v] > 0), -1)
        if start == -1:
            return [0]                                  # no edges at all
    used = [False] * len(edges)
    ptr = [0] * n
    st, path = [], []
    push(st, start)
    while st:
        v = peek(st)
        while ptr[v] < len(adj[v]) and used[adj[v][ptr[v]][1]]:
            ptr[v] += 1
        if ptr[v] == len(adj[v]):
            path.append(pop(st))                        # stuck: v is final
        else:
            w, i = adj[v][ptr[v]]
            used[i] = True                              # use the edge in both directions
            push(st, w)
    path.reverse()
    return path if len(path) == len(edges) + 1 else []  # edges in two pieces
--cpp
// undirected multigraph; edges[i] = {u, v}. Returns the nodes of an Euler path in order, or empty.
vector<int> eulerPath(int n, const vector<pair<int,int>>& edges) {
    vector<vector<pair<int,int>>> adj(n);
    vector<int> deg(n, 0);
    for (int i = 0; i < (int)edges.size(); i++) {
        auto [u, v] = edges[i];
        adj[u].push_back({v, i});
        adj[v].push_back({u, i});
        deg[u]++; deg[v]++;
    }
    int odd = 0, start = -1;
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
        if (deg[v] % 2 == 1) { odd++; if (start == -1 || deg[start] % 2 == 0) start = v; }
        else if (deg[v] > 0 && start == -1) start = v;
    }
    if (odd != 0 && odd != 2) return {};
    if (start == -1) return {0};                        // no edges at all
    vector<char> used(edges.size(), 0);
    vector<int> ptr(n, 0), path;
    stack<int> st;
    push(st, start);
    while (!st.empty()) {
        int v = peek(st);
        while (ptr[v] < (int)adj[v].size() && used[adj[v][ptr[v]].second]) ptr[v]++;
        if (ptr[v] == (int)adj[v].size()) {
            path.push_back(pop(st));                    // stuck: v is final
        } else {
            auto [w, id] = adj[v][ptr[v]];
            used[id] = 1;                               // use the edge in both directions
            push(st, w);
        }
    }
    reverse(path.begin(), path.end());
    return path.size() == edges.size() + 1 ? path : vector<int>();   // edges in two pieces
}
--go
// undirected multigraph; edges[i] = {u, v}. Returns the nodes of an Euler path in order, or nil.
func eulerPath(n int, edges [][2]int) []int {
    type arc struct{ to, id int }
    adj := make([][]arc, n)
    deg := make([]int, n)
    for i, e := range edges {
        adj[e[0]] = append(adj[e[0]], arc{e[1], i})
        adj[e[1]] = append(adj[e[1]], arc{e[0], i})
        deg[e[0]]++
        deg[e[1]]++
    }
    odd, start := 0, -1
    for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
        if deg[v]%2 == 1 {
            odd++
            if start == -1 || deg[start]%2 == 0 {
                start = v // an odd node must begin the path
            }
        } else if deg[v] > 0 && start == -1 {
            start = v
        }
    }
    if odd != 0 && odd != 2 {
        return nil
    }
    if start == -1 { // no edges at all
        return []int{0}
    }
    used := make([]bool, len(edges))
    ptr := make([]int, n)
    var st, path []int
    push(&st, start) // piece
    for len(st) > 0 {
        v := peek(&st) // piece
        for ptr[v] < len(adj[v]) && used[adj[v][ptr[v]].id] {
            ptr[v]++
        }
        if ptr[v] == len(adj[v]) {
            path = append(path, pop(&st)) // stuck: v is final
        } else {
            e := adj[v][ptr[v]]
            used[e.id] = true // use the edge in both directions
            push(&st, e.to)
        }
    }
    if len(path) != len(edges)+1 { // edges in two pieces
        return nil
    }
    for i, j := 0, len(path)-1; i < j; i, j = i+1, j-1 {
        path[i], path[j] = path[j], path[i]
    }
    return path
}
### hamilton
--idea
A Hamiltonian path visits every node of a graph exactly once. It is the "visit every city once" puzzle. It sounds close to an Euler path (every edge once, solved easily), but it is far harder: no fast algorithm is known, and the problem is NP-complete, which in practice means that the best known methods take exponential time. So it is only asked on small graphs, usually 20 nodes or fewer.

The standard method is dynamic programming over subsets. A subset of nodes is stored as a number whose binary digits mark which nodes are included (a bitmask). For each subset `mask`, remember which nodes can be the last node of some path that visits exactly the nodes in `mask`. Growing a path by one node turns a smaller subset into a bigger one, so you fill the table from small masks up to the full set.
--build
1. Let `n` be the node count (small). A mask is a number from `0` to `2^n - 1`: bit `i` is on when node `i` is in the subset.
2. Make `end[mask]`, itself a bitmask: bit `v` is on when some path visits exactly the nodes of `mask` and finishes at `v`. Start with `end[1 << v] = 1 << v` for every node `v` (a path of one node).
3. Loop over masks from small to large (counting up works, because adding a node always makes the number bigger). For each mask, `itemsIn(end[mask], n)` (code piece `u-maskiter`) lists the possible last nodes `v`.
4. For each such `v` and each neighbour `w` of `v` with bit `w` off in `mask`, switch on bit `w` of `end[mask | (1 << w)]`: the path can extend to `w`.
5. When done, a Hamiltonian path exists exactly when `end[(1 << n) - 1] != 0`. A Hamiltonian cycle needs one more test: some last node `v` in that set has an edge back to a fixed start, so run the DP with only the start node seeded, `end[1 << start] = 1 << start`.
6. Invariant: `end[mask]` holds exactly the nodes that can finish a path through all of `mask`, because every such path, minus its last node, is a path counted in a smaller mask.
7. To print the path, store a parent for each `(mask, v)` or walk backward: from the full mask pick a last node, then a predecessor in the smaller mask, and so on.
--think
- Signal: "visit every node exactly once", "shortest route through all cities" (travelling salesman: the same DP with costs), "arrange items in an order where neighbours must be compatible", and `n` at most about 20. If `n` is 100, this is the wrong problem or it has special structure.
- Euler path (every edge once) is a different, easy problem (`eulerpath`). Hamiltonian is the hard one.
- Complexity: `2^n` masks times `n` last nodes times `n` neighbours. With the bitmask trick, `end[mask]` is one machine word and the neighbour check can also be a mask test (store `nbr[v]` as a bitmask and compute `nbr[v] & ~mask`), which removes a factor of `n`.
- For travelling salesman with edge costs, replace the boolean with `best[mask][v]`, the cheapest path, and take minimums.
- Special graphs are easy: a tournament (every pair has one directed edge) always has a Hamiltonian path, and DAGs have one only if the topological order is a chain.
- Pitfalls: using `int` shifts when `n` reaches 31, memory (`2^20` ints is 4 MB, fine, but `2^20 times 20` is not), and forgetting that bit order is arbitrary but must be consistent.
--cost
Time O(2^n times n^2) as written (O(2^n times n) with neighbour masks), space O(2^n).
--uses
u-maskiter
--java
// adj.get(v) lists neighbours (undirected edges appear in both lists). True if a Hamiltonian path exists. n <= 20.
static boolean hasHamiltonPath(List<List<Integer>> adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    int[] end = new int[1 << n];                       // end[mask]: bitmask of possible last nodes
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) end[1 << v] = 1 << v;
    for (int mask = 1; mask < (1 << n); mask++) {
        for (int v : itemsIn(end[mask], n))            // piece: each possible last node
            for (int w : adj.get(v))
                if ((mask >> w & 1) == 0) end[mask | (1 << w)] |= 1 << w;   // extend the path to w
    }
    return end[(1 << n) - 1] != 0;
}
--py
def has_hamilton_path(adj):
    """adj[v] lists neighbours (undirected edges appear in both lists). n <= 20."""
    n = len(adj)
    end = [0] * (1 << n)                      # end[mask]: bitmask of possible last nodes
    for v in range(n):
        end[1 << v] = 1 << v
    for mask in range(1, 1 << n):
        for v in items_in(end[mask], n):      # piece: each possible last node
            for w in adj[v]:
                if not mask >> w & 1:
                    end[mask | (1 << w)] |= 1 << w        # extend the path to w
    return end[(1 << n) - 1] != 0
--cpp
// adj[v] lists neighbours (undirected edges appear in both lists). True if a Hamiltonian path exists. n <= 20.
bool hasHamiltonPath(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<int> end(1 << n, 0);                        // end[mask]: bitmask of possible last nodes
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) end[1 << v] = 1 << v;
    for (int mask = 1; mask < (1 << n); mask++) {
        for (int v : itemsIn(end[mask], n))            // piece: each possible last node
            for (int w : adj[v])
                if (!(mask >> w & 1)) end[mask | (1 << w)] |= 1 << w;   // extend the path to w
    }
    return end[(1 << n) - 1] != 0;
}
--go
// adj[v] lists neighbours (undirected edges appear in both lists). True if a Hamiltonian path exists. n <= 20.
func hasHamiltonPath(adj [][]int) bool {
    n := len(adj)
    end := make([]int, 1<<n) // end[mask]: bitmask of possible last nodes
    for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
        end[1<<v] = 1 << v
    }
    for mask := 1; mask < 1<<n; mask++ {
        for _, v := range itemsIn(end[mask], n) { // piece: each possible last node
            for _, w := range adj[v] {
                if mask>>w&1 == 0 {
                    end[mask|1<<w] |= 1 << w // extend the path to w
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return end[1<<n-1] != 0
}
### funcgraph
--idea
A functional graph has exactly one outgoing edge from every node: node `v` points to `f[v]`. Think of a rule "from each town you must go to the town its sign points to". If you start anywhere and keep following the arrows, you must eventually revisit a node (there are finitely many), and from then on you loop forever. So every start walks along a tail (a path of nodes you never see again) and then enters a cycle.

Each connected piece of a functional graph has exactly one cycle, with trees of tails hanging into it, all pointing toward the cycle. Many puzzles (a pseudo-random generator, "what is the value after a million steps", a game where each position has one forced move) are functional graphs in disguise.
--build
1. Store `f[v]`, the node that `v` points to.
2. To analyse the walk from `start`: keep `parent` filled with `-1` and `pos` filled with `-1` (position of each node in the walk). Set `v = start`, `step = 0`.
3. While `pos[v] == -1`: set `pos[v] = step`, step up, then move `u = f[v]`. If `pos[u] == -1` set `parent[u] = v`. Then `v = u`.
4. The loop ends at the first node `v` that was visited before, and the previous node `last` is where the walk stopped. The path `start ... last` is rebuilt with `pathTo(parent, last)` (code piece `u-parentpath`).
5. The entry point of the cycle is `entry = f[last]`. Its index in the path is the tail length `mu`, and the cycle length is `path length - mu`.
6. The nodes from index `mu` to the end of the path are the cycle itself.
7. Invariant: all nodes of the path are distinct, and the first node to repeat must be the entry to the cycle, since every node before it was reached only once.
8. To find where you are after `k` steps for a huge `k`: if `k < mu`, take the node at index `k`; otherwise take index `mu + (k - mu) mod cycle length`. For many starts at once, use binary lifting (`kthanc`) on `f`.
--think
- Signal: "every node has exactly one next", "repeat an operation and see when it cycles", "after 10^18 steps", "which values loop", "detect the cycle in a sequence defined by `x_next = g(x)`".
- Cycle detection without extra memory is the tortoise and hare trick (Floyd): one pointer moves one step, the other two, and they meet inside the cycle. Use it when memory is tight and the nodes are not numbered. Hashing the visited positions is simpler when you can afford it.
- To find the nodes that lie on any cycle in the whole graph, peel nodes of in-degree 0 repeatedly (like topological sort `kahn`): what remains are the cycle nodes.
- Reversed edges form a forest hanging into each cycle: DP on those trees gives, for example, the number of steps until a node reaches the cycle.
- Strongly connected components (`scc`) of a functional graph are exactly its cycles and the single nodes of the tails.
- Pitfalls: confusing tail length with path length, starting the cycle index count wrongly (the entry node is `f[last]`, not `last`), and for a permutation (every node also has exactly one incoming edge) there are no tails at all.
--cost
Time O(tail + cycle) for one start, at most O(V). Space O(V).
--uses
u-parentpath
--java
// f[v] = the node v points to. Returns the walk from start up to (not including) the first repeat.
// The cycle entry is f[last element]; the tail length is the index of that entry in the list.
static List<Integer> walkUntilRepeat(int[] f, int start) {
    int n = f.length;
    int[] parent = new int[n];
    boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
    Arrays.fill(parent, -1);
    int v = start, last = start;
    while (!seen[v]) {
        seen[v] = true;
        last = v;
        int u = f[v];
        if (!seen[u]) parent[u] = v;
        v = u;
    }
    return pathTo(parent, last);                        // piece: start ... last
}

static int[] tailAndCycle(int[] f, int start) {
    List<Integer> path = walkUntilRepeat(f, start);
    int entry = f[path.get(path.size() - 1)];
    int tail = path.indexOf(entry);
    return new int[]{tail, path.size() - tail};
}
--py
def walk_until_repeat(f, start):
    """f[v] = the node v points to. Returns the walk from start up to (not including) the first repeat.
    The cycle entry is f[last element]; the tail length is the index of that entry in the list."""
    parent = [-1] * len(f)
    seen = [False] * len(f)
    v = last = start
    while not seen[v]:
        seen[v] = True
        last = v
        u = f[v]
        if not seen[u]:
            parent[u] = v
        v = u
    return path_to(parent, last)                        # piece: start ... last

def tail_and_cycle(f, start):
    path = walk_until_repeat(f, start)
    entry = f[path[-1]]
    tail = path.index(entry)
    return tail, len(path) - tail
--cpp
// f[v] = the node v points to. Returns the walk from start up to (not including) the first repeat.
// The cycle entry is f[last element]; the tail length is the index of that entry in the list.
vector<int> walkUntilRepeat(const vector<int>& f, int start) {
    int n = f.size();
    vector<int> parent(n, -1);
    vector<char> seen(n, 0);
    int v = start, last = start;
    while (!seen[v]) {
        seen[v] = 1;
        last = v;
        int u = f[v];
        if (!seen[u]) parent[u] = v;
        v = u;
    }
    return pathTo(parent, last);                        // piece: start ... last
}

pair<int,int> tailAndCycle(const vector<int>& f, int start) {
    vector<int> path = walkUntilRepeat(f, start);
    int entry = f[path.back()];
    int tail = find(path.begin(), path.end(), entry) - path.begin();
    return {tail, (int)path.size() - tail};
}
--go
// f[v] = the node v points to. Returns the walk from start up to (not including) the first repeat.
// The cycle entry is f[last element]; the tail length is the index of that entry in the list.
func walkUntilRepeat(f []int, start int) []int {
    n := len(f)
    parent := make([]int, n)
    for i := range parent {
        parent[i] = -1
    }
    seen := make([]bool, n)
    v, last := start, start
    for !seen[v] {
        seen[v] = true
        last = v
        if u := f[v]; !seen[u] {
            parent[u] = v
        }
        v = f[v]
    }
    return pathTo(parent, last) // piece: start ... last
}

func tailAndCycle(f []int, start int) (tail, cycle int) {
    path := walkUntilRepeat(f, start)
    entry := f[path[len(path)-1]]
    for path[tail] != entry { // index of the entry node
        tail++
    }
    return tail, len(path) - tail
}
### graphcolor
--idea
Graph coloring means giving every node a colour so that no two nodes joined by an edge share a colour. The smallest number of colours that works is the chromatic number. Typical uses: schedule exams so that no student has two at once (nodes are exams, an edge means a shared student, colours are time slots), assign radio frequencies, or fill a map so neighbouring countries differ.

With two colours the question is the bipartite check (`bipartite`) and is easy. With three or more colours it is NP-complete: no fast general method is known. So for small graphs you search with backtracking, and for large graphs you settle for a greedy answer that is good but not always the smallest.
--build
1. Greedy colouring (fast, not always optimal): go through the nodes in some order; give each node the smallest colour not used by an already coloured neighbour. It never needs more than `maxDegree + 1` colours, because a node has at most `maxDegree` neighbours blocking colours.
2. Exact test "can this graph use `k` colours": backtracking. Colour nodes `0, 1, 2, ...` in order. `place(v)` tries every colour `c` for node `v` from `0` up to `min(used, k - 1)`, where `used` is how many different colours appear so far.
3. A colour `c` is allowed only if no neighbour of `v` already has colour `c`. Set `color[v] = c` and recurse to `place(v + 1)` with `used` raised to `max(used, c + 1)`.
4. If the recursion returns true, the colouring is complete. Otherwise undo with `color[v] = -1` and try the next colour. If no colour works, return false.
5. Trying only up to `used` removes symmetric duplicates: a new colour name, such as colour 3 when 0 to 2 are in use, is interchangeable with any other unused name, so only one of them needs testing.
6. The chromatic number is the smallest `k` for which the test succeeds. Try `k = 1, 2, 3, ...`.
--think
- Signal: "no two conflicting items together", "minimum number of groups so that no group has a conflict", "assign slots/frequencies/registers", "colour a map". The graph is the conflict graph: nodes are things, edges join conflicting things.
- Check the easy cases first. No odd cycle means 2 colours (`bipartite`). A tree needs 2. Planar graphs need at most 4 (see `planar`). Interval conflicts (meetings that overlap in time) form interval graphs, where greedy by start time is optimal: that is the "minimum rooms" problem.
- A large clique (nodes that all conflict pairwise, `clique`) forces at least that many colours. The chromatic number is at least the clique size.
- Order matters for greedy. Colouring high-degree nodes first (the Welsh-Powell order) usually gives fewer colours.
- Backtracking is exponential. Fine up to about 30 to 50 nodes with pruning. Beyond that, use heuristics or exploit the structure.
- Pitfalls: edge colouring (colour edges so edges sharing a node differ) is a different problem, and self-loops make colouring impossible.
--cost
Greedy: O(V + E). Exact backtracking: exponential in the worst case, so keep `n` small.
--java
// returns color[v] in 0..k-1 using at most k colours, or null if impossible
static int[] kColoring(List<List<Integer>> adj, int k) {
    int[] color = new int[adj.size()];
    Arrays.fill(color, -1);
    return place(adj, color, 0, k, 0) ? color : null;
}

static boolean place(List<List<Integer>> adj, int[] color, int v, int k, int used) {
    if (v == adj.size()) return true;
    for (int c = 0; c <= Math.min(used, k - 1); c++) {      // only one new colour name is tried
        boolean ok = true;
        for (int w : adj.get(v)) if (color[w] == c) { ok = false; break; }
        if (!ok) continue;
        color[v] = c;
        if (place(adj, color, v + 1, k, Math.max(used, c + 1))) return true;
        color[v] = -1;                                       // undo and try the next colour
    }
    return false;
}
--py
def k_coloring(adj, k):
    """Returns color[v] in 0..k-1 using at most k colours, or None if impossible."""
    n = len(adj)
    color = [-1] * n

    def place(v, used):
        if v == n:
            return True
        for c in range(min(used, k - 1) + 1):        # only one new colour name is tried
            if any(color[w] == c for w in adj[v]):
                continue
            color[v] = c
            if place(v + 1, max(used, c + 1)):
                return True
            color[v] = -1                            # undo and try the next colour
        return False

    return color if place(0, 0) else None
--cpp
bool placeColor(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, vector<int>& color, int v, int k, int used) {
    if (v == (int)adj.size()) return true;
    for (int c = 0; c <= min(used, k - 1); c++) {            // only one new colour name is tried
        bool ok = true;
        for (int w : adj[v]) if (color[w] == c) { ok = false; break; }
        if (!ok) continue;
        color[v] = c;
        if (placeColor(adj, color, v + 1, k, max(used, c + 1))) return true;
        color[v] = -1;                                       // undo and try the next colour
    }
    return false;
}

// returns color[v] in 0..k-1 using at most k colours, or an empty vector if impossible
vector<int> kColoring(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int k) {
    vector<int> color(adj.size(), -1);
    return placeColor(adj, color, 0, k, 0) ? color : vector<int>();
}
--go
// returns color[v] in 0..k-1 using at most k colours, or nil if impossible
func kColoring(adj [][]int, k int) []int {
    color := make([]int, len(adj))
    for i := range color {
        color[i] = -1
    }
    if place(adj, color, 0, k, 0) {
        return color
    }
    return nil
}

func place(adj [][]int, color []int, v, k, used int) bool {
    if v == len(adj) {
        return true
    }
    for c := 0; c <= min(used, k-1); c++ { // only one new colour name is tried
        ok := true
        for _, w := range adj[v] {
            if color[w] == c {
                ok = false
                break
            }
        }
        if !ok {
            continue
        }
        color[v] = c
        if place(adj, color, v+1, k, max(used, c+1)) {
            return true
        }
        color[v] = -1 // undo and try the next colour
    }
    return false
}
### flow
--idea
A flow network is a directed graph where every edge has a capacity (the most it can carry), with a source `s` where material enters and a sink `t` where it leaves. Think of water pipes: each pipe has a maximum rate, and you want the most water per second from `s` to `t`. A flow assigns an amount to each edge, no larger than its capacity, with the rule that at every node other than `s` and `t`, what flows in equals what flows out.

The method of Ford and Fulkerson is: find any path from `s` to `t` that still has spare capacity, push as much as its narrowest edge allows, and repeat. The catch is that an early choice can block a better overall plan, so the method also lets you undo flow. For each edge, keep a reverse edge whose capacity is the amount already sent. Pushing flow along the reverse edge cancels earlier flow. The graph of remaining capacities, including reverse edges, is the residual graph.
--build
1. Store capacities in a table `cap[u][v]` (0 where there is no edge). This table is also the residual graph. Parallel edges are added together.
2. Repeat: search the residual graph from `s` with BFS, following only edges with `cap[u][v] > 0`. Record `parent[v]` for each node reached, with `parent[s] = -1`, and `-2` for nodes not reached yet. Using BFS makes this the Edmonds-Karp variant, which guarantees few rounds.
3. If `t` was not reached, stop: no more flow can pass. The total pushed so far is the maximum flow.
4. Otherwise rebuild the path with `pathTo(parent, t)` (code piece `u-parentpath`).
5. The bottleneck is the smallest `cap[a][b]` along the path. Subtract it from `cap[a][b]` and add it to `cap[b][a]` for every consecutive pair `(a, b)`: this uses the forward capacity and creates room to undo it.
6. Add the bottleneck to the total, and go back to step 2.
7. Invariant: after every round the numbers in `cap` are exactly the capacities left over in the residual graph for a valid flow, so the total sent is always a feasible flow.
8. Why the result is maximum: when `t` is unreachable, let `S` be the nodes still reachable from `s`. Every original edge leaving `S` is full and every edge entering `S` carries nothing, so the flow equals the capacity of the cut between `S` and the rest. No flow can exceed any cut, so this one is maximum (see `mincut`).
--think
- Signal: "maximum amount that can pass", "how many disjoint paths", "assign things to slots with limits", "maximum matching" in bipartite graphs (give every edge capacity 1, add a source to the left side and a sink to the right), "minimum cut" (same number), "project selection" and "closure" problems.
- The key modelling step is to decide what the nodes and capacities are. Split a node into two (`in` and `out`) joined by an edge of capacity `c` to limit the flow through a node. Undirected edges become two directed edges, each with the capacity.
- Edge-disjoint paths: all capacities 1. Vertex-disjoint paths: split nodes with capacity 1 on the inner edge.
- Edmonds-Karp with a matrix is for small graphs. For bigger graphs use an adjacency list with paired edges and Dinic's algorithm (`dinic`), or push-relabel (`pushrelabel`). With costs, see `mcmf`.
- Integrality: if all capacities are whole numbers, there is a maximum flow made of whole numbers. That is why flow solves matching.
- Pitfalls: forgetting the reverse edges (the answer becomes just a greedy, often wrong), a source equal to the sink, and storing parallel edges by overwriting instead of adding.
--cost
Time O(V times E squared) for Edmonds-Karp (this matrix version is O(V^2) per search, so O(V^3 times E) at worst), space O(V squared). Dinic is far faster.
--uses
u-parentpath
--java
// cap[u][v]: capacity. On return cap holds the residual capacities. Returns the maximum flow from s to t.
static long maxFlow(long[][] cap, int s, int t) {
    if (s == t) return 0;
    int n = cap.length;
    long total = 0;
    while (true) {
        int[] parent = new int[n];
        Arrays.fill(parent, -2);                        // -2 = not reached
        parent[s] = -1;
        ArrayDeque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
        q.add(s);
        while (!q.isEmpty() && parent[t] == -2) {
            int v = q.poll();
            for (int w = 0; w < n; w++)
                if (parent[w] == -2 && cap[v][w] > 0) { parent[w] = v; q.add(w); }
        }
        if (parent[t] == -2) return total;              // no augmenting path left
        List<Integer> path = pathTo(parent, t);         // piece: s ... t
        long push = Long.MAX_VALUE;
        for (int i = 0; i + 1 < path.size(); i++) push = Math.min(push, cap[path.get(i)][path.get(i + 1)]);
        for (int i = 0; i + 1 < path.size(); i++) {
            cap[path.get(i)][path.get(i + 1)] -= push;  // use the forward capacity
            cap[path.get(i + 1)][path.get(i)] += push;  // and allow undoing it
        }
        total += push;
    }
}
--py
def max_flow(cap, s, t):
    """cap[u][v]: capacity. On return cap holds the residual capacities. Returns the maximum flow."""
    if s == t:
        return 0
    n = len(cap)
    total = 0
    while True:
        parent = [-2] * n                       # -2 = not reached
        parent[s] = -1
        q = deque([s])
        while q and parent[t] == -2:
            v = q.popleft()
            for w in range(n):
                if parent[w] == -2 and cap[v][w] > 0:
                    parent[w] = v
                    q.append(w)
        if parent[t] == -2:
            return total                        # no augmenting path left
        path = path_to(parent, t)               # piece: s ... t
        push = min(cap[a][b] for a, b in zip(path, path[1:]))
        for a, b in zip(path, path[1:]):
            cap[a][b] -= push                   # use the forward capacity
            cap[b][a] += push                   # and allow undoing it
        total += push
--cpp
// cap[u][v]: capacity. On return cap holds the residual capacities. Returns the maximum flow from s to t.
long long maxFlow(vector<vector<long long>>& cap, int s, int t) {
    if (s == t) return 0;
    int n = cap.size();
    long long total = 0;
    while (true) {
        vector<int> parent(n, -2);                      // -2 = not reached
        parent[s] = -1;
        queue<int> q;
        q.push(s);
        while (!q.empty() && parent[t] == -2) {
            int v = q.front(); q.pop();
            for (int w = 0; w < n; w++)
                if (parent[w] == -2 && cap[v][w] > 0) { parent[w] = v; q.push(w); }
        }
        if (parent[t] == -2) return total;              // no augmenting path left
        vector<int> path = pathTo(parent, t);           // piece: s ... t
        long long push = LLONG_MAX;
        for (size_t i = 0; i + 1 < path.size(); i++) push = min(push, cap[path[i]][path[i + 1]]);
        for (size_t i = 0; i + 1 < path.size(); i++) {
            cap[path[i]][path[i + 1]] -= push;          // use the forward capacity
            cap[path[i + 1]][path[i]] += push;          // and allow undoing it
        }
        total += push;
    }
}
--go
// capa[u][v]: capacity. On return capa holds the residual capacities. Returns the maximum flow from s to t.
func maxFlow(capa [][]int, s, t int) int {
    if s == t {
        return 0
    }
    n, total := len(capa), 0
    for {
        parent := make([]int, n)
        for i := range parent {
            parent[i] = -2 // -2 = not reached
        }
        parent[s] = -1
        for q := []int{s}; len(q) > 0 && parent[t] == -2; q = q[1:] {
            for w := 0; w < n; w++ {
                if parent[w] == -2 && capa[q[0]][w] > 0 {
                    parent[w] = q[0]
                    q = append(q, w)
                }
            }
        }
        if parent[t] == -2 { // no augmenting path left
            return total
        }
        path := pathTo(parent, t) // piece: s ... t
        push := 1 << 62
        for i := 0; i+1 < len(path); i++ {
            push = min(push, capa[path[i]][path[i+1]])
        }
        for i := 0; i+1 < len(path); i++ {
            capa[path[i]][path[i+1]] -= push // use the forward capacity
            capa[path[i+1]][path[i]] += push // and allow undoing it
        }
        total += push
    }
}
### mincut
--idea
A cut separates the source `s` from the sink `t`: split the nodes into two groups, `S` containing `s` and the rest containing `t`. The capacity of the cut is the total capacity of the edges that go from `S` to the other side. The min cut is the cheapest way to cut every route from `s` to `t`. Picture the cheapest set of roads to close so nobody can drive from `s` to `t`.

The max-flow min-cut theorem says the capacity of the cheapest cut equals the value of the maximum flow. You cannot push more than the narrowest cut allows, and the maximum flow reaches exactly that. So you compute the max flow, and then read the cut off the final residual graph: `S` is everything still reachable from `s` using edges with spare capacity.
--build
1. Run the max flow algorithm (`flow`, the standard form `maxFlow(res, s, t)`) on a copy `res` of the capacity table. It leaves `res` as the residual capacities.
2. Build an adjacency list of the residual graph: add an edge `u -> v` whenever `res[u][v] > 0`.
3. Run `bfs(adj, s)` (code piece `u-bfsloop`). Every node with distance not `-1` is reachable from `s`: that is the group `S`.
4. The cut edges are the original edges `(u, v)` with `u` in `S`, `v` not in `S`, and original capacity above 0.
5. Their capacities add up to the max flow value. If you only need the value, step 1 is enough.
6. Why this is a cut and is cheapest: `t` is not in `S` (otherwise another augmenting path existed). Every original edge leaving `S` has no residual capacity left (otherwise its far end would be reachable), so it is full, so the flow crossing the cut equals the cut capacity. No flow beats any cut, so the cut is minimum.
--think
- Signal: "minimum cost to disconnect", "cheapest set of edges to remove so A cannot reach B", "separate two groups at minimum penalty" (image segmentation, project selection: pick profitable projects that need costly tools), "bottleneck of a network".
- Minimum vertex cut (remove nodes instead of edges) uses node splitting with capacity 1 (or the node's cost) on the inner edge, then the same method.
- Undirected graph: give each undirected edge capacity in both directions.
- For the min cut between every pair of nodes at once, build a Gomory-Hu tree (`gomory`). For a global min cut with no fixed `s` and `t`, there are special algorithms (Stoer-Wagner).
- When asked for which edges, make sure you report edges from `S` to the other side and not the reverse, and that you use the original capacities, not the residual ones.
- Pitfalls: reading the cut from the wrong side (use the nodes reachable from `s`, not those that reach `t`), when several minimum cuts exist this finds the one closest to `s`, and infinite-capacity edges must use a large finite number.
--cost
The cost of the max flow, plus O(V squared) to scan the residual table and O(V + E) for the BFS.
--uses
u-bfsloop
--java
// cap[u][v]: capacities. Returns the cut edges {u, v} of a minimum s-t cut. Uses maxFlow from the flow lesson.
static List<int[]> minCut(long[][] cap, int s, int t) {
    int n = cap.length;
    long[][] res = new long[n][];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) res[i] = cap[i].clone();
    maxFlow(res, s, t);                                  // res becomes the residual graph
    List<List<Integer>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int u = 0; u < n; u++) {
        adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (res[u][v] > 0) adj.get(u).add(v);
    }
    int[] dist = bfs(adj, s);                            // piece: dist >= 0 means reachable from s
    List<int[]> cut = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int u = 0; u < n; u++)
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++)
            if (dist[u] >= 0 && dist[v] < 0 && cap[u][v] > 0) cut.add(new int[]{u, v});
    return cut;
}
--py
def min_cut(cap, s, t):
    """cap[u][v]: capacities. Returns the cut edges (u, v) of a minimum s-t cut.
    Uses max_flow from the flow lesson."""
    n = len(cap)
    res = [row[:] for row in cap]
    max_flow(res, s, t)                          # res becomes the residual graph
    adj = [[v for v in range(n) if res[u][v] > 0] for u in range(n)]
    dist = bfs(adj, s)                           # piece: dist >= 0 means reachable from s
    return [(u, v) for u in range(n) for v in range(n)
            if dist[u] >= 0 and dist[v] < 0 and cap[u][v] > 0]
--cpp
// cap[u][v]: capacities. Returns the cut edges {u, v} of a minimum s-t cut. Uses maxFlow from the flow lesson.
vector<pair<int,int>> minCut(const vector<vector<long long>>& cap, int s, int t) {
    int n = cap.size();
    vector<vector<long long>> res = cap;
    maxFlow(res, s, t);                                  // res becomes the residual graph
    vector<vector<int>> adj(n);
    for (int u = 0; u < n; u++)
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (res[u][v] > 0) adj[u].push_back(v);
    vector<int> dist = bfs(adj, s);                      // piece: dist >= 0 means reachable from s
    vector<pair<int,int>> cut;
    for (int u = 0; u < n; u++)
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++)
            if (dist[u] >= 0 && dist[v] < 0 && cap[u][v] > 0) cut.push_back({u, v});
    return cut;
}
--go
// capa[u][v]: capacities. Returns the cut edges {u, v} of a minimum s-t cut. Uses maxFlow from the flow lesson.
func minCut(capa [][]int, s, t int) [][2]int {
    n := len(capa)
    res := make([][]int, n)
    for i := range res {
        res[i] = append([]int(nil), capa[i]...)
    }
    maxFlow(res, s, t) // res becomes the residual graph
    adj := make([][]int, n)
    for u := range adj {
        for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
            if res[u][v] > 0 {
                adj[u] = append(adj[u], v)
            }
        }
    }
    dist := bfs(adj, s) // piece: dist >= 0 means reachable from s
    var cut [][2]int
    for u := 0; u < n; u++ {
        for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
            if dist[u] >= 0 && dist[v] < 0 && capa[u][v] > 0 {
                cut = append(cut, [2]int{u, v})
            }
        }
    }
    return cut
}
### matching
--idea
In a bipartite graph the nodes split into a left side and a right side, and every edge joins a left node to a right node. A matching is a set of edges where no node is used twice. A maximum matching has as many edges as possible. Typical story: workers on the left, jobs on the right, an edge when a worker can do a job, and you want to give as many workers a job as possible, one job each.

The method grows a matching with augmenting paths. An augmenting path starts at an unmatched left node, alternates between a non-matching edge and a matching edge, and ends at an unmatched right node. Flip every edge on it (matching edges become free, free edges become matching) and the matching grows by exactly one. A greedy first choice can be undone this way: if the job you want is taken, ask whether its current worker can move to another job. This is Kuhn's algorithm, and it is a depth-first search.
--build
1. Left nodes are `0 .. L-1`, right nodes are `0 .. R-1`. `adj[u]` lists the right nodes that left node `u` can be paired with. Keep `matchR[r]`, the left node currently paired with right node `r`, or `-1` if free.
2. For each left node `u` in turn, make a fresh `seen` array of size `R` (all false) and call `augment(u)`.
3. `augment(u)`: for each right node `r` in `adj[u]` that is not yet `seen`: mark `seen[r]`. If `r` is free (`matchR[r] == -1`), or its current partner `matchR[r]` can move to another right node (`augment(matchR[r])` returns true), then set `matchR[r] = u` and return true.
4. If no right node works, return false: `u` stays unmatched for now.
5. The answer is the number of right nodes with `matchR[r] != -1`. The pairs are `(matchR[r], r)`.
6. Invariant: after processing the first `i` left nodes, the matching is maximum for the graph restricted to those `i` nodes. Marking `seen` per attempt stops the search from looping and from testing the same right node twice in one search.
7. Why it is correct: a matching is maximum exactly when no augmenting path exists (Berge's lemma). If `augment(u)` fails, there is no augmenting path from `u`, and later changes cannot create one, so `u` can be skipped for good.
--think
- Signal: "assign each A to a different B", "pair up", "maximum number of non-conflicting pairs", "cover all", a grid of dominoes on a board (cells are nodes, colour the board like a chess board to get two sides), "minimum number of lines to cover points" (via vertex cover).
- Check that the graph really is bipartite (`bipartite`). If edges can join two nodes on the same side, you need general matching (`blossom`).
- Equivalent to max flow with unit capacities: source to left, left to right, right to sink (`flow`). Kuhn's is simpler to write and fine for up to a few thousand nodes.
- Faster: Hopcroft-Karp (`hopcroft`) finds many augmenting paths per round, O(E times square root of V). Weighted version (cheapest assignment): the Hungarian algorithm (`hungarian`).
- König's theorem: in a bipartite graph, the size of a maximum matching equals the size of a minimum vertex cover (fewest nodes touching every edge). The maximum independent set is `V` minus that size. See `treematch` for the tree case.
- Pitfalls: reusing the `seen` array between different start nodes (it must be cleared for each), recursion depth on long alternating chains, and mixing up left and right indices.
--cost
Time O(V times E), space O(V). Hopcroft-Karp improves this to O(E times square root of V).
--java
// adj.get(u): right nodes that left node u may take; R = number of right nodes.
// Returns matchR, where matchR[r] is the left node paired with r (or -1).
static int[] maxMatching(List<List<Integer>> adj, int R) {
    int[] matchR = new int[R];
    Arrays.fill(matchR, -1);
    for (int u = 0; u < adj.size(); u++)
        augment(adj, u, new boolean[R], matchR);          // fresh seen for every start
    return matchR;
}

static boolean augment(List<List<Integer>> adj, int u, boolean[] seen, int[] matchR) {
    for (int r : adj.get(u)) {
        if (seen[r]) continue;
        seen[r] = true;
        if (matchR[r] == -1 || augment(adj, matchR[r], seen, matchR)) {   // free, or its owner can move
            matchR[r] = u;
            return true;
        }
    }
    return false;
}
--py
import sys

def max_matching(adj, R):
    """adj[u]: right nodes that left node u may take; R = number of right nodes.
    Returns match_r, where match_r[r] is the left node paired with r (or -1)."""
    sys.setrecursionlimit(10000 + 4 * len(adj))
    match_r = [-1] * R

    def augment(u, seen):
        for r in adj[u]:
            if seen[r]:
                continue
            seen[r] = True
            if match_r[r] == -1 or augment(match_r[r], seen):   # free, or its owner can move
                match_r[r] = u
                return True
        return False

    for u in range(len(adj)):
        augment(u, [False] * R)                                  # fresh seen for every start
    return match_r
--cpp
bool augment(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int u, vector<char>& seen, vector<int>& matchR) {
    for (int r : adj[u]) {
        if (seen[r]) continue;
        seen[r] = 1;
        if (matchR[r] == -1 || augment(adj, matchR[r], seen, matchR)) {   // free, or its owner can move
            matchR[r] = u;
            return true;
        }
    }
    return false;
}

// adj[u]: right nodes that left node u may take; R = number of right nodes.
// Returns matchR, where matchR[r] is the left node paired with r (or -1).
vector<int> maxMatching(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int R) {
    vector<int> matchR(R, -1);
    for (int u = 0; u < (int)adj.size(); u++) {
        vector<char> seen(R, 0);                         // fresh seen for every start
        augment(adj, u, seen, matchR);
    }
    return matchR;
}
--go
// adj[u]: right nodes that left node u may take; R = number of right nodes.
// Returns matchR, where matchR[r] is the left node paired with r (or -1).
func maxMatching(adj [][]int, R int) []int {
    matchR := make([]int, R)
    for i := range matchR {
        matchR[i] = -1
    }
    for u := range adj {
        augment(adj, u, make([]bool, R), matchR) // fresh seen for every start
    }
    return matchR
}

func augment(adj [][]int, u int, seen []bool, matchR []int) bool {
    for _, r := range adj[u] {
        if seen[r] {
            continue
        }
        seen[r] = true
        if matchR[r] == -1 || augment(adj, matchR[r], seen, matchR) { // free, or its owner can move
            matchR[r] = u
            return true
        }
    }
    return false
}
### stablematch
--idea
The stable matching problem pairs two equal-sized groups, say students and schools, or men and women in the classic telling. Everyone ranks everyone on the other side. A matching is unstable if there is a pair who are not matched together, but each prefers the other to their current partner: they would both leave. A matching with no such pair is stable. It always exists, and the Gale-Shapley algorithm finds one.
