The method is proposals. Everyone on the proposing side asks their favourite first. A receiver keeps the best offer so far and rejects the rest. Rejected proposers ask their next favourite. Receivers only ever trade up, proposers only ever go down their list, so the process stops, and when it stops no pair could break up: any proposer who prefers someone else already asked that person and was turned down for a better offer.
--build
1. Inputs: `pref[m]`, the list of receivers for proposer `m`, best first; and `rank[w][m]`, how receiver `w` ranks proposer `m` (smaller is better). Precomputing `rank` makes each comparison constant time.
2. Keep `next[m]`, the position in `pref[m]` of the next receiver `m` will ask (start at 0), `husband[w]` (the current partner of `w`, or `-1`) and `wife[m]`.
3. Put every proposer in a queue of free proposers, using `enqueue` (code piece `u-queueop`).
4. While the queue is not empty: `m = dequeue(free)`. Let `w = pref[m][next[m]]` and increase `next[m]`.
5. If `w` is free, they become a pair.
6. Otherwise let `h = husband[w]`. If `w` prefers `m` (`rank[w][m] < rank[w][h]`), then `m` and `w` pair up and `h` goes back with `enqueue(free, h)`. If not, `m` was turned down: `enqueue(free, m)` to ask the next one.
7. When the queue is empty, `wife[m]` is the partner of each proposer. With equal group sizes and complete lists everyone gets matched.
8. Invariant: each receiver's partner only improves over time, and each proposer's partner only gets worse than the one before, so no proposer asks the same receiver twice and the loop ends after at most `n squared` proposals.
--think
- Signal: two sides with preferences, "no pair who would both rather swap", assigning residents to hospitals, students to colleges, rooms to roommates (the one-sided variant has no guarantee).
- The result favours the proposing side: every proposer gets the best partner they can have in any stable matching, and every receiver gets the worst. Choose the proposing side deliberately.
- Different from `hungarian`, which minimises a total cost, and from `matching`, which ignores preferences. Here nothing is optimised except stability.
- With capacities (a school takes `c` students) keep a heap of the best `c` offers per receiver and reject the worst when over capacity.
- Incomplete lists are possible: skip someone who is not on a list, and some may stay unmatched.
- Pitfalls: confusing `rank` (position of a person in a list) with the person at a position, comparing in the wrong direction, and re-asking a receiver who already refused (always advance `next`).
--cost
Time O(n squared), space O(n squared) for the preference tables.
--uses
u-queueop
--java
// pref[m]: receivers in order of preference (best first); rank[w][m]: how w ranks m (smaller = better).
// Returns wife[m], the receiver each proposer ends with.
static int[] galeShapley(int[][] pref, int[][] rank) {
    int n = pref.length;
    int[] next = new int[n], husband = new int[n], wife = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(husband, -1);
    Deque<Integer> free = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int m = 0; m < n; m++) enqueue(free, m);
    while (!free.isEmpty()) {
        int m = dequeue(free);
        int w = pref[m][next[m]++];                        // best receiver not asked yet
        int h = husband[w];
        if (h == -1) { husband[w] = m; wife[m] = w; }
        else if (rank[w][m] < rank[w][h]) {                // w trades up
            husband[w] = m; wife[m] = w;
            enqueue(free, h);                              // the old partner is free again
        } else enqueue(free, m);                           // turned down: ask the next one
    }
    return wife;
}
--py
from collections import deque

def gale_shapley(pref, rank):
    """pref[m]: receivers in order of preference (best first); rank[w][m]: how w ranks m (smaller = better).
    Returns wife, where wife[m] is the receiver each proposer ends with."""
    n = len(pref)
    nxt = [0] * n
    husband = [-1] * n
    wife = [-1] * n
    free = deque()
    for m in range(n):
        enqueue(free, m)
    while free:
        m = dequeue(free)
        w = pref[m][nxt[m]]                       # best receiver not asked yet
        nxt[m] += 1
        h = husband[w]
        if h == -1:
            husband[w], wife[m] = m, w
        elif rank[w][m] < rank[w][h]:             # w trades up
            husband[w], wife[m] = m, w
            enqueue(free, h)                      # the old partner is free again
        else:
            enqueue(free, m)                      # turned down: ask the next one
    return wife
--cpp
// pref[m]: receivers in order of preference (best first); rank[w][m]: how w ranks m (smaller = better).
// Returns wife[m], the receiver each proposer ends with.
vector<int> galeShapley(const vector<vector<int>>& pref, const vector<vector<int>>& rank) {
    int n = pref.size();
    vector<int> next(n, 0), husband(n, -1), wife(n, -1);
    queue<int> freeMen;
    for (int m = 0; m < n; m++) enqueue(freeMen, m);
    while (!freeMen.empty()) {
        int m = dequeue(freeMen);
        int w = pref[m][next[m]++];                        // best receiver not asked yet
        int h = husband[w];
        if (h == -1) { husband[w] = m; wife[m] = w; }
        else if (rank[w][m] < rank[w][h]) {                // w trades up
            husband[w] = m; wife[m] = w;
            enqueue(freeMen, h);                           // the old partner is free again
        } else enqueue(freeMen, m);                        // turned down: ask the next one
    }
    return wife;
}
--go
// pref[m]: receivers in order of preference (best first); rank[w][m]: how w ranks m (smaller = better).
// Returns wife[m], the receiver each proposer ends with.
func galeShapley(pref, rank [][]int) []int {
    n := len(pref)
    next, husband, wife := make([]int, n), make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
    for i := range husband {
        husband[i] = -1
    }
    var free []int
    for m := 0; m < n; m++ {
        enqueue(&free, m) // piece
    }
    for len(free) > 0 {
        m := dequeue(&free) // piece
        w := pref[m][next[m]] // best receiver not asked yet
        next[m]++
        if h := husband[w]; h == -1 {
            husband[w], wife[m] = m, w
        } else if rank[w][m] < rank[w][h] { // w trades up
            husband[w], wife[m] = m, w
            enqueue(&free, h) // the old partner is free again
        } else {
            enqueue(&free, m) // turned down: ask the next one
        }
    }
    return wife
}
### johnson
--idea
Johnson's algorithm finds shortest paths between every pair of nodes in a sparse graph that may have negative edges. Running Dijkstra from every node is fast, but Dijkstra breaks with negative edges. Floyd-Warshall handles them but costs `V` cubed. Johnson fixes the weights first, so that Dijkstra becomes safe, without changing which paths are shortest.

The trick is a potential `h[v]` for every node. Replace each edge weight `w(u, v)` by `w(u, v) + h[u] - h[v]`. For any path from `a` to `b`, the extra terms telescope: they add up to `h[a] - h[b]`, the same for every path between the same two nodes. So the shortest path stays the same, only its length shifts by a known amount. If `h` is the shortest distance from a made-up source joined to every node by a zero edge, then `h[v] <= h[u] + w(u, v)`, so every new weight is at least 0. That `h` comes from Bellman-Ford, which also reports negative cycles.
--build
1. Compute the potentials `h` with Bellman-Ford. Start with `h[v] = 0` for all `v` (this is what a source joined to everyone by zero edges would give after its first round). Repeat up to `n` rounds: for each edge `(u, v, w)`, call `relax(h, parent, u, v, w)` (code piece `u-relax`). If a round changes nothing, stop. If round `n` still changes something, there is a negative cycle: return that no answer exists.
2. Reweight: for every edge, `w2 = w + h[u] - h[v]`. All are now at least 0. Build the adjacency list with `w2`.
3. For every source `s`, run Dijkstra on the reweighted graph. Use `newPq`, `pushPq` and `popPq` (code piece `u-pq`) for the heap, and `relax` again for the improvement step: when it returns true, push the new distance.
4. Convert back: the true distance from `s` to `t` is `dist2[t] - h[s] + h[t]`. Unreachable pairs stay infinite.
5. Invariant for step 2: the reweighted length of any path from `a` to `b` equals its original length plus `h[a] - h[b]`, so the order of paths between a fixed pair never changes.
--think
- Signal: all-pairs shortest paths, a sparse graph (edges about the same size as nodes), and some negative edges but no negative cycle. With no negative edges just run Dijkstra from every node, skipping step 1.
- Compare: for dense graphs or small `n` (a few hundred) Floyd-Warshall (`floyd`) is simpler. For one source with negative edges, Bellman-Ford (`bellman`) or SPFA (`spfa`) is enough.
- The potential idea appears elsewhere: A* with a consistent heuristic is Dijkstra on reweighted edges, and min-cost flow (`mcmf`) uses potentials to keep reduced costs non-negative.
- Negative cycles: Bellman-Ford finds them (`negcycle`). If one exists the shortest paths through it are undefined.
- Pitfalls: forgetting to add `h[t] - h[s]` back, using a too small "infinity", and starting Bellman-Ford from one real node (it must start from all nodes at 0 so every node gets a potential).
--cost
Time O(V times E) for Bellman-Ford plus O(V times E log V) for the Dijkstra runs; space O(V squared) for the answer.
--uses
u-relax
u-pq
--java
// edges[i] = {u, v, w}. Returns dist[s][t], or null if there is a negative cycle. Unreachable = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4.
static long[][] johnson(int n, int[][] edges) {
    final long INF = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4;
    long[] h = new long[n];                               // potentials, all 0 to start
    int[] par = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(par, -1);
    for (int round = 0; round < n; round++) {             // Bellman-Ford
        boolean changed = false;
        for (int[] e : edges) if (relax(h, par, e[0], e[1], e[2])) changed = true;   // piece
        if (!changed) break;
        if (round == n - 1) return null;                  // negative cycle
    }
    List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int[] e : edges) adj.get(e[0]).add(new int[]{e[1], (int) (e[2] + h[e[0]] - h[e[1]])});   // now >= 0
    long[][] res = new long[n][n];
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {                         // Dijkstra from every source
        long[] dist = new long[n];
        Arrays.fill(dist, INF);
        int[] parent = new int[n];
        Arrays.fill(parent, -1);
        dist[s] = 0;
        PriorityQueue<long[]> pq = newPq();
        pushPq(pq, 0, s);
        while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
            long[] cur = popPq(pq);
            int v = (int) cur[1];
            if (cur[0] > dist[v]) continue;               // stale entry
            for (int[] e : adj.get(v))
                if (relax(dist, parent, v, e[0], e[1])) pushPq(pq, dist[e[0]], e[0]);
        }
        for (int t = 0; t < n; t++) res[s][t] = dist[t] >= INF ? INF : dist[t] - h[s] + h[t];   // undo the shift
    }
    return res;
}
--py
def johnson(n, edges):
    """edges is a list of (u, v, w). Returns dist[s][t], or None if there is a negative cycle."""
    INF = float("inf")
    h = [0] * n                                           # potentials, all 0 to start
    par = [-1] * n
    for rnd in range(n):                                  # Bellman-Ford
        changed = False
        for u, v, w in edges:
            if relax(h, par, u, v, w):                    # piece
                changed = True
        if not changed:
            break
        if rnd == n - 1:
            return None                                   # negative cycle
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    for u, v, w in edges:
        adj[u].append((v, w + h[u] - h[v]))               # now >= 0
    res = []
    for s in range(n):                                    # Dijkstra from every source
        dist = [INF] * n
        parent = [-1] * n
        dist[s] = 0
        pq = []
        push_pq(pq, 0, s)
        while pq:
            d, v = pop_pq(pq)
            if d > dist[v]:
                continue                                  # stale entry
            for w, c in adj[v]:
                if relax(dist, parent, v, w, c):
                    push_pq(pq, dist[w], w)
        res.append([dist[t] - h[s] + h[t] if dist[t] < INF else INF for t in range(n)])   # undo the shift
    return res
--cpp
// edges[i] = {u, v, w}. Returns dist[s][t], or an empty table if there is a negative cycle. Unreachable = LLONG_MAX / 4.
vector<vector<long long>> johnson(int n, const vector<array<int,3>>& edges) {
    const long long INF = LLONG_MAX / 4;
    vector<long long> h(n, 0);                            // potentials, all 0 to start
    vector<int> par(n, -1);
    for (int round = 0; round < n; round++) {             // Bellman-Ford
        bool changed = false;
        for (auto& [u, v, w] : edges) if (relax(h, par, u, v, w)) changed = true;   // piece
        if (!changed) break;
        if (round == n - 1) return {};                    // negative cycle
    }
    vector<vector<pair<int,int>>> adj(n);
    for (auto& [u, v, w] : edges) adj[u].push_back({v, (int)(w + h[u] - h[v])});   // now >= 0
    vector<vector<long long>> res(n, vector<long long>(n, INF));
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {                         // Dijkstra from every source
        vector<long long> dist(n, INF);
        vector<int> parent(n, -1);
        dist[s] = 0;
        MinPq pq;
        pushPq(pq, 0, s);
        while (!pq.empty()) {
            auto [d, v] = popPq(pq);
            if (d > dist[v]) continue;                    // stale entry
            for (auto [w, c] : adj[v])
                if (relax(dist, parent, v, w, c)) pushPq(pq, dist[w], w);
        }
        for (int t = 0; t < n; t++) if (dist[t] < INF) res[s][t] = dist[t] - h[s] + h[t];   // undo the shift
    }
    return res;
}
--go
// edges[i] = {u, v, w}. Returns dist[s][t], or nil if there is a negative cycle. Unreachable = 1<<60.
func johnson(n int, edges [][3]int) [][]int {
    const inf = 1 << 60
    h := make([]int, n) // potentials, all 0 to start
    par := make([]int, n)
    for i := range par {
        par[i] = -1
    }
    for round := 0; round < n; round++ { // Bellman-Ford
        changed := false
        for _, e := range edges {
            if relax(h, par, e[0], e[1], e[2]) { // piece
                changed = true
            }
        }
        if !changed {
            break
        }
        if round == n-1 { // negative cycle
            return nil
        }
    }
    adj := make([][][2]int, n)
    for _, e := range edges {
        adj[e[0]] = append(adj[e[0]], [2]int{e[1], e[2] + h[e[0]] - h[e[1]]}) // now >= 0
    }
    res := make([][]int, n)
    for s := range res { // Dijkstra from every source
        dist, parent := make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
        for i := range dist {
            dist[i], parent[i] = inf, -1
        }
        dist[s] = 0
        pq := newPq()
        pushPq(pq, 0, s)
        for len(*pq) > 0 {
            cur := popPq(pq)
            v := cur[1]
            if cur[0] > dist[v] { // stale entry
                continue
            }
            for _, e := range adj[v] {
                if relax(dist, parent, v, e[0], e[1]) {
                    pushPq(pq, dist[e[0]], e[0])
                }
            }
        }
        res[s] = make([]int, n)
        for t := range res[s] {
            res[s][t] = inf
            if dist[t] < inf {
                res[s][t] = dist[t] - h[s] + h[t] // undo the shift
            }
        }
    }
    return res
}
### kthpath
--idea
The k shortest paths problem asks for the cheapest route, then the second cheapest, then the third, up to the `k`-th, between two nodes. Maps apps show a few alternative routes: that is this problem. A plain shortest path search keeps only the single best arrival at each node and throws the rest away. To find the second best route you must keep the second best arrival as well.

The simple trick: run Dijkstra, but allow each node to be settled up to `k` times instead of once. The first time `t` is taken from the heap is the best route, the second time is the next best, and so on. This version counts walks, which may revisit nodes. If you need simple paths (no repeated node), use Yen's algorithm, described in the thinking notes.
--build
1. Make a min-heap with `newPq()` (code piece `u-pq`), keyed by route cost. Make `cnt[v] = 0`, the number of times `v` has been taken from the heap.
2. `pushPq(pq, 0, s)`.
3. Take the cheapest `(d, v)` with `popPq(pq)`. If `cnt[v]` is already `k`, skip it: `k` better routes to `v` are known, and anything through `v` using a worse route cannot be among the first `k` routes to `t`.
4. Otherwise add one to `cnt[v]`. If `v == t`, record `d` as the next answer, and stop once `k` answers exist.
5. For every edge `(v, w, c)`, `pushPq(pq, d + c, w)`. Unlike Dijkstra, push without comparing to a stored distance, because worse arrivals still matter.
6. Return the recorded costs: they come out in non-decreasing order.
7. Invariant: the `j`-th time a node is taken from the heap, its cost is the `j`-th smallest walk cost from `s` to it. This works because edge costs are not negative, so extending a walk never makes it cheaper.
--think
- Signal: "the `k`-th shortest path", "second shortest route", "list the best 3 routes", "shortest path that is strictly longer than the shortest".
- Walks versus paths. This lesson allows repeated nodes. If the answer must be a simple path (no repeated node), use Yen's algorithm: take the best path, then for each spur node on it block the edge used and the earlier nodes and find a new shortest path; collect the candidates in a heap. It is a longer algorithm that runs Dijkstra many times.
- Equal costs: two different routes with the same cost count as two entries here. If the problem wants distinct costs, skip answers equal to the previous one.
- The second shortest path only (strictly longer than the best): keep two distances per node and update both: a standard two-state Dijkstra.
- On a DAG or when `k` is large, other methods (Eppstein) exist. For small `k` this one is right.
- Pitfalls: with zero-cost cycles the same node could be popped repeatedly, but the `cnt` limit still ends the search; negative edges break it; and `s == t` returns the empty route of cost 0 as the first answer.
--cost
Time O(k times E log(k times E)), space O(k times V) for heap entries.
--uses
u-pq
--java
// adj.get(v) holds {neighbour, cost >= 0}; returns the costs of the k cheapest walks from s to t (fewer if not enough exist)
static List<Long> kShortestWalks(List<List<int[]>> adj, int s, int t, int k) {
    int[] cnt = new int[adj.size()];
    List<Long> answers = new ArrayList<>();
    PriorityQueue<long[]> pq = newPq();
    pushPq(pq, 0, s);
    while (!pq.isEmpty() && answers.size() < k) {
        long[] cur = popPq(pq);
        int v = (int) cur[1];
        if (cnt[v] == k) continue;                    // k better walks to v are already known
        cnt[v]++;
        if (v == t) answers.add(cur[0]);              // next best walk to the target
        for (int[] e : adj.get(v)) pushPq(pq, cur[0] + e[1], e[0]);
    }
    return answers;
}
--py
def k_shortest_walks(adj, s, t, k):
    """adj[v] is a list of (neighbour, cost >= 0). Costs of the k cheapest walks from s to t."""
    cnt = [0] * len(adj)
    answers = []
    pq = []
    push_pq(pq, 0, s)
    while pq and len(answers) < k:
        d, v = pop_pq(pq)
        if cnt[v] == k:                   # k better walks to v are already known
            continue
        cnt[v] += 1
        if v == t:
            answers.append(d)             # next best walk to the target
        for w, c in adj[v]:
            push_pq(pq, d + c, w)
    return answers
--cpp
// adj[v] holds {neighbour, cost >= 0}; returns the costs of the k cheapest walks from s to t
vector<long long> kShortestWalks(const vector<vector<pair<int,int>>>& adj, int s, int t, int k) {
    vector<int> cnt(adj.size(), 0);
    vector<long long> answers;
    MinPq pq;
    pushPq(pq, 0, s);
    while (!pq.empty() && (int)answers.size() < k) {
        auto [d, v] = popPq(pq);
        if (cnt[v] == k) continue;                    // k better walks to v are already known
        cnt[v]++;
        if (v == t) answers.push_back(d);             // next best walk to the target
        for (auto [w, c] : adj[v]) pushPq(pq, d + c, w);
    }
    return answers;
}
--go
// adj[v] holds {neighbour, cost >= 0}; returns the costs of the k cheapest walks from s to t (fewer if not enough exist)
func kShortestWalks(adj [][][2]int, s, t, k int) []int {
    cnt := make([]int, len(adj))
    var answers []int
    pq := newPq()
    pushPq(pq, 0, s)
    for len(*pq) > 0 && len(answers) < k {
        cur := popPq(pq)
        v := cur[1]
        if cnt[v] == k { // k better walks to v are already known
            continue
        }
        cnt[v]++
        if v == t { // next best walk to the target
            answers = append(answers, cur[0])
        }
        for _, e := range adj[v] {
            pushPq(pq, cur[0]+e[1], e[0])
        }
    }
    return answers
}
### boruvka
--idea
Boruvka's algorithm builds a minimum spanning tree (the cheapest set of edges joining all nodes) by merging in rounds. Start with every node as its own component. In each round, every component picks the cheapest edge that leaves it, and all those edges are added at once. Components that get joined merge. The number of components at least halves each round, so after about `log V` rounds there is one tree.

Why the chosen edges are safe: the cheapest edge leaving any group of nodes always belongs to some minimum spanning tree (the cut property, as in Kruskal and Prim). The only danger is a tie creating a cycle, such as three components each choosing a different edge of the same weight. Breaking ties by edge index makes all weights effectively distinct, which rules out cycles.
--build
1. Keep a disjoint set union (DSU, see `dsu`) in two arrays `p` and `sz`. The code pieces give `find(x)` (the leader of the component of `x`, piece `u-find`) and `union(a, b)` (merge two components by size, piece `u-dsuunion`; it is `unite` in C++). Set `comps = n` and `total = 0`.
2. Repeat while `comps > 1`: make `cheapest[r] = -1` for every leader `r`.
3. For each edge `i = (u, v, w)`: let `ru = find(u)`, `rv = find(v)` (piece calls). Skip it if they are equal (it is inside one component). Otherwise, if `cheapest[ru]` is empty or the pair `(w, i)` is smaller than the pair of the current choice, set `cheapest[ru] = i`; do the same for `rv`. Comparing `(w, i)` is the tie-break.
4. If no component found an outgoing edge, the graph is disconnected: stop and report that.
5. For each leader `r` with a chosen edge `i`: recompute `ru`, `rv` with `find` (earlier merges this round may have joined them). If they differ, call the piece `union(ru, rv)`, add `w` to `total`, and subtract one from `comps`.
6. When `comps == 1`, `total` is the weight of the minimum spanning tree.
7. Invariant: at every point the chosen edges form a forest that lies inside one minimum spanning tree. The check in step 5 skips an edge whose ends were already joined, so no cycle is created.
--think
- Signal: minimum spanning tree where edges arrive in a form that is hard to sort or the graph is implicit: "cheapest edge out of each group" is easy to compute directly (for points in the plane, a nearest-neighbour query), and the algorithm needs only `log V` rounds of it.
- It is the basis for parallel MST and for problems like "minimum spanning tree of a graph whose edges come from a structure": e.g. XOR minimum spanning tree (the cheapest edge leaving a group is found with a binary trie).
- Plain graphs: Kruskal (`mst`) is shorter, Prim (`prim`) is good on dense graphs. Use Boruvka when the per-round work can be done faster than by sorting everything, or you need the rounds.
- Distinct weights are not needed if you break ties consistently, as here.
- Pitfalls: not re-running `find` before merging in step 5 (a cycle sneaks in), forgetting the tie-break, and detecting a disconnected graph (no component has an outgoing edge, so the loop would never end).
--cost
Time O(E log V), space O(V + E).
--uses
u-find
u-dsuunion
--java
class Boruvka {
    int[] p, sz;                                   // the arrays the DSU pieces work on
    // find(x) and union(a, b) come from the code pieces

    // edges[i] = {u, v, w}; returns the MST weight, or -1 if the graph is not connected
    long boruvka(int n, int[][] edges) {
        p = new int[n]; sz = new int[n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { p[i] = i; sz[i] = 1; }
        int comps = n;
        long total = 0;
        while (comps > 1) {
            int[] cheapest = new int[n];
            Arrays.fill(cheapest, -1);
            for (int i = 0; i < edges.length; i++) {
                int ru = find(edges[i][0]), rv = find(edges[i][1]);      // piece
                if (ru == rv) continue;                                  // inside one component
                for (int r : new int[]{ru, rv}) {
                    int c = cheapest[r];
                    if (c == -1 || edges[i][2] < edges[c][2] || (edges[i][2] == edges[c][2] && i < c)) cheapest[r] = i;
                }
            }
            boolean any = false;
            for (int r = 0; r < n; r++) {
                if (cheapest[r] == -1) continue;
                int[] e = edges[cheapest[r]];
                int ru = find(e[0]), rv = find(e[1]);                    // piece
                if (ru == rv) continue;                                  // joined earlier this round
                union(ru, rv);                                           // piece
                total += e[2];
                comps--;
                any = true;
            }
            if (!any) return -1;                                         // no edge leaves any component
        }
        return total;
    }
}
--py
p, sz = [], []                                    # the lists the DSU pieces work on
# find(x) and union(a, b) come from the code pieces

def boruvka(n, edges):
    """edges is a list of (u, v, w). Returns the MST weight, or -1 if the graph is not connected."""
    global p, sz
    p, sz = list(range(n)), [1] * n
    comps, total = n, 0
    while comps > 1:
        cheapest = [-1] * n
        for i, (u, v, w) in enumerate(edges):
            ru, rv = find(u), find(v)                     # piece
            if ru == rv:
                continue                                  # inside one component
            for r in (ru, rv):
                c = cheapest[r]
                if c == -1 or (w, i) < (edges[c][2], c):  # tie-break by edge index
                    cheapest[r] = i
        merged = False
        for r in range(n):
            if cheapest[r] == -1:
                continue
            u, v, w = edges[cheapest[r]]
            ru, rv = find(u), find(v)                     # piece
            if ru == rv:
                continue                                  # joined earlier this round
            union(ru, rv)                                 # piece
            total += w
            comps -= 1
            merged = True
        if not merged:
            return -1                                     # no edge leaves any component
    return total
--cpp
vector<int> p, sz;                                       // the arrays the DSU pieces work on
// find(x) and unite(a, b) come from the code pieces

// edges[i] = {u, v, w}; returns the MST weight, or -1 if the graph is not connected
long long boruvka(int n, const vector<array<int,3>>& edges) {
    p.resize(n); sz.assign(n, 1);
    iota(p.begin(), p.end(), 0);
    int comps = n;
    long long total = 0;
    while (comps > 1) {
        vector<int> cheapest(n, -1);
        for (int i = 0; i < (int)edges.size(); i++) {
            int ru = find(edges[i][0]), rv = find(edges[i][1]);      // piece
            if (ru == rv) continue;                                  // inside one component
            for (int r : {ru, rv}) {
                int c = cheapest[r];
                if (c == -1 || make_pair(edges[i][2], i) < make_pair(edges[c][2], c)) cheapest[r] = i;   // tie-break by index
            }
        }
        bool merged = false;
        for (int r = 0; r < n; r++) {
            if (cheapest[r] == -1) continue;
            auto& e = edges[cheapest[r]];
            int ru = find(e[0]), rv = find(e[1]);                    // piece
            if (ru == rv) continue;                                  // joined earlier this round
            unite(ru, rv);                                           // piece
            total += e[2];
            comps--;
            merged = true;
        }
        if (!merged) return -1;                                      // no edge leaves any component
    }
    return total;
}
--go
var p, sz []int // the arrays the DSU pieces work on
// find(x) and union(a, b) come from the code pieces

// edges[i] = {u, v, w}; returns the MST weight, or -1 if the graph is not connected
func boruvka(n int, edges [][3]int) int {
    p, sz = make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
    for i := range p {
        p[i], sz[i] = i, 1
    }
    comps, total := n, 0
    for comps > 1 {
        cheapest := make([]int, n)
        for i := range cheapest {
            cheapest[i] = -1
        }
        for i, e := range edges {
            ru, rv := find(e[0]), find(e[1]) // piece
            if ru == rv {                    // inside one component
                continue
            }
            for _, r := range [2]int{ru, rv} {
                c := cheapest[r]
                if c == -1 || e[2] < edges[c][2] || (e[2] == edges[c][2] && i < c) {
                    cheapest[r] = i
                }
            }
        }
        any := false
        for r := 0; r < n; r++ {
            if cheapest[r] == -1 {
                continue
            }
            e := edges[cheapest[r]]
            ru, rv := find(e[0]), find(e[1]) // piece
            if ru == rv {                    // joined earlier this round
                continue
            }
            union(ru, rv) // piece
            total += e[2]
            comps--
            any = true
        }
        if !any { // no edge leaves any component
            return -1
        }
    }
    return total
}
### mst2
--idea
The second-best spanning tree is the cheapest spanning tree that is not the minimum spanning tree (MST). Why ask? Because if one road of the best plan closes, you want the next best plan, or you want to know how much the best plan can be beaten by one swap.

The key fact: the second-best tree differs from the MST by exactly one edge swap. Take the MST. Add one edge `e = (u, v)` that is not in it. This creates exactly one cycle, the MST path from `u` to `v` plus `e`. Removing the heaviest edge of that path gives a new spanning tree of weight `MST + w(e) - (heaviest edge on the path)`. Try every non-tree edge and keep the smallest result.
--build
1. Build the MST with Kruskal: sort edges by weight and take each edge whose ends are in different components (the `find` helper from `mst`). Remember which edges were taken (`inTree[i]`) and the total weight. If fewer than `n - 1` edges were taken, the graph is not connected: return -1.
2. Build the adjacency list of the MST only.
3. For every node `s`, walk the MST from `s` and fill `mx[s][t]`, the heaviest edge on the tree path from `s` to `t`: when you step from `v` to a new node `w` over an edge of weight `c`, `mx[s][w] = max(mx[s][v], c)`.
4. For every edge `i = (u, v, w)` not in the tree and with `u != v`: the candidate weight is `total + w - mx[u][v]`.
5. The answer is the smallest candidate, or -1 if there are no candidates (the graph is itself a tree).
6. Invariant behind step 4: the heaviest edge on the path is the best one to drop. Any other drop gives a heavier tree, and dropping an edge not on the cycle would leave the graph disconnected.
7. For large graphs replace the `mx` table (size `n` squared) with binary lifting that stores the maximum edge on each jump, built like in `lca`, so each query takes `log n` time.
--think
- Signal: "second minimum spanning tree", "the best tree after forbidding one edge of the best tree", "how much more must we pay if the cheapest plan is not allowed".
- This version allows a second tree of the same weight as the MST (when two different trees tie). If the problem wants strictly larger weight, keep two values on each path, the heaviest and the second heaviest distinct edge weight, and use the second one when the heaviest equals `w`.
- The `n` squared table is fine up to about 3000 nodes. For `n` up to 100,000 use the lifting version: the maximum edge on a path is the maximum over the two jumps up to the LCA.
- Related: the best MST that must include or exclude a given edge uses the same cycle argument. The k-th best spanning tree is a harder problem (enumerate by branching).
- Pitfalls: skipping self-loops (they never form a tree), forgetting that the swapped-in edge must not be a tree edge, and `int` overflow in the total.
--cost
Time O(E log E + V squared) with the table, or O(E log V) with binary lifting. Space O(V squared) for the table.
--java
// edges[i] = {u, v, w}; returns the weight of the second-best spanning tree, or -1 if none exists
static long secondBestMst(int n, int[][] edges) {
    int m = edges.length;
    Integer[] order = new Integer[m];
    for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) order[i] = i;
    Arrays.sort(order, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(edges[a][2], edges[b][2]));
    int[] parent = new int[n];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) parent[i] = i;
    boolean[] inTree = new boolean[m];
    List<List<int[]>> tree = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) tree.add(new ArrayList<>());
    long total = 0; int taken = 0;
    for (int i : order) {
        int ru = findRoot(parent, edges[i][0]), rv = findRoot(parent, edges[i][1]);   // find from the mst lesson
        if (ru == rv) continue;
        parent[ru] = rv; inTree[i] = true; total += edges[i][2]; taken++;
        tree.get(edges[i][0]).add(new int[]{edges[i][1], edges[i][2]});
        tree.get(edges[i][1]).add(new int[]{edges[i][0], edges[i][2]});
    }
    if (taken != n - 1) return -1;
    int[][] mx = new int[n][n];                       // mx[s][t]: heaviest edge on the tree path s..t
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        ArrayDeque<int[]> st = new ArrayDeque<>();    // {node, parent}
        st.push(new int[]{s, -1});
        while (!st.isEmpty()) {
            int[] cur = st.pop();
            for (int[] e : tree.get(cur[0]))
                if (e[0] != cur[1]) { mx[s][e[0]] = Math.max(mx[s][cur[0]], e[1]); st.push(new int[]{e[0], cur[0]}); }
        }
    }
    long best = Long.MAX_VALUE;
    for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) {
        if (inTree[i] || edges[i][0] == edges[i][1]) continue;
        best = Math.min(best, total + edges[i][2] - mx[edges[i][0]][edges[i][1]]);   // swap one edge
    }
    return best == Long.MAX_VALUE ? -1 : best;
}
--py
def second_best_mst(n, edges):
    """edges is a list of (u, v, w). Returns the weight of the second-best spanning tree, or -1."""
    m = len(edges)
    parent = list(range(n))

    def find(x):
        while parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = parent[parent[x]]
            x = parent[x]
        return x

    in_tree = [False] * m
    tree = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    total = taken = 0
    for i in sorted(range(m), key=lambda i: edges[i][2]):
        u, v, w = edges[i]
        ru, rv = find(u), find(v)
        if ru == rv:
            continue
        parent[ru] = rv
        in_tree[i] = True
        total += w
        taken += 1
        tree[u].append((v, w))
        tree[v].append((u, w))
    if taken != n - 1:
        return -1
    mx = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]          # mx[s][t]: heaviest edge on the tree path s..t
    for s in range(n):
        stack = [(s, -1)]
        while stack:
            v, p = stack.pop()
            for w, c in tree[v]:
                if w != p:
                    mx[s][w] = max(mx[s][v], c)
                    stack.append((w, v))
    best = None
    for i, (u, v, w) in enumerate(edges):
        if in_tree[i] or u == v:
            continue
        cand = total + w - mx[u][v]           # swap one edge
        if best is None or cand < best:
            best = cand
    return -1 if best is None else best
--cpp
// edges[i] = {u, v, w}; returns the weight of the second-best spanning tree, or -1 if none exists
long long secondBestMst(int n, const vector<array<int,3>>& edges) {
    int m = edges.size();
    vector<int> order(m), parent(n);
    iota(order.begin(), order.end(), 0);
    sort(order.begin(), order.end(), [&](int a, int b) { return edges[a][2] < edges[b][2]; });
    iota(parent.begin(), parent.end(), 0);
    vector<char> inTree(m, 0);
    vector<vector<pair<int,int>>> tree(n);
    long long total = 0; int taken = 0;
    for (int i : order) {
        auto [u, v, w] = edges[i];
        int ru = findRoot(parent, u), rv = findRoot(parent, v);   // find from the mst lesson
        if (ru == rv) continue;
        parent[ru] = rv; inTree[i] = 1; total += w; taken++;
        tree[u].push_back({v, w}); tree[v].push_back({u, w});
    }
    if (taken != n - 1) return -1;
    vector<vector<int>> mx(n, vector<int>(n, 0));     // mx[s][t]: heaviest edge on the tree path s..t
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        vector<pair<int,int>> st = {{s, -1}};
        while (!st.empty()) {
            auto [v, p] = st.back(); st.pop_back();
            for (auto [w, c] : tree[v])
                if (w != p) { mx[s][w] = max(mx[s][v], c); st.push_back({w, v}); }
        }
    }
    long long best = LLONG_MAX;
    for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) {
        auto [u, v, w] = edges[i];
        if (inTree[i] || u == v) continue;
        best = min(best, total + w - mx[u][v]);       // swap one edge
    }
    return best == LLONG_MAX ? -1 : best;
}
--go
// edges[i] = {u, v, w}; returns the weight of the second-best spanning tree, or -1 if none exists
func secondBestMst(n int, edges [][3]int) int {
    m := len(edges)
    order := make([]int, m)
    for i := range order {
        order[i] = i
    }
    sort.Slice(order, func(a, b int) bool { return edges[order[a]][2] < edges[order[b]][2] })
    parent := make([]int, n)
    for i := range parent {
        parent[i] = i
    }
    inTree := make([]bool, m)
    tree := make([][][2]int, n)
    total, taken := 0, 0
    for _, i := range order {
        e := edges[i]
        ru, rv := findRoot(parent, e[0]), findRoot(parent, e[1]) // find from the mst lesson
        if ru == rv {
            continue
        }
        parent[ru], inTree[i] = rv, true
        total += e[2]
        taken++
        tree[e[0]] = append(tree[e[0]], [2]int{e[1], e[2]})
        tree[e[1]] = append(tree[e[1]], [2]int{e[0], e[2]})
    }
    if taken != n-1 {
        return -1
    }
    mx := make([][]int, n) // mx[s][t]: heaviest edge on the tree path s..t
    for s := range mx {
        mx[s] = make([]int, n)
        for st := [][2]int{{s, -1}}; len(st) > 0; { // {node, parent}
            cur := st[len(st)-1]
            st = st[:len(st)-1]
            for _, e := range tree[cur[0]] {
                if e[0] != cur[1] {
                    mx[s][e[0]] = max(mx[s][cur[0]], e[1])
                    st = append(st, [2]int{e[0], cur[0]})
                }
            }
        }
    }
    best := -1
    for i, e := range edges {
        if inTree[i] || e[0] == e[1] {
            continue
        }
        if c := total + e[2] - mx[e[0]][e[1]]; best == -1 || c < best { // swap one edge
            best = c
        }
    }
    return best
}
### lcatarjan
--idea
Tarjan's offline LCA answers many "lowest common ancestor" queries in one depth-first walk of the tree, with a disjoint set union (DSU, a structure that merges groups and finds a group's leader quickly). "Offline" means you know all queries before you start. You read them all, then answer them together while the DFS runs.

The idea: while the DFS is inside node `v`, every node already finished is merged into the group of its nearest ancestor that is still open (still on the current path). So for a finished node `u`, `find(u)` gives exactly the lowest open ancestor of `u`, which is the LCA of `u` and the node `v` we are standing on. That means that when the DFS reaches `v`, any query `(v, u)` whose other end `u` was already seen can be answered immediately with `find(u)`.
--build
1. Store, for every node, the queries it takes part in: `qs[v]` holds pairs `(other end, query id)`. A query `(a, b)` is stored at both `a` and `b`.
2. Make `dsu[x] = x` for every node, `visited[x] = false`, and an answer array `ans`.
3. Run an iterative DFS from the root with a stack and a pointer `it[v]` to the next neighbour. When a node `v` is entered for the first time, set `visited[v] = true` and look at every query `(u, id)` in `qs[v]`: if `visited[u]` is true, set `ans[id] = find(u)`.
4. When the DFS goes down to a child `w`, set `parent[w] = v` and push it.
5. When a node `v` has no more children to visit, pop it. Then merge it into its parent: set `dsu[v] = parent[v]`. From now on, `find(v)` returns the parent's group leader, which is the parent (still open) or the leader of the parent's group.
6. When the stack is empty, every query has been answered (each was answered at the later of its two ends).
7. Invariant: while the DFS stands on `v`, every visited node `u` belongs to the group led by its lowest ancestor that is on the current path. That ancestor is the LCA of `u` and `v`. Why: the path from `u` up to the root and the path from `v` up to the root first meet at the LCA, and everything below the LCA on `u`'s side has already been finished and merged upward to it.
--think
- Signal: "answer `q` LCA queries" when all queries are given up front, and the tree is large. It is also the standard building block when LCA queries come mixed with other offline DFS work.
- If queries are online (each depends on the previous answer), use binary lifting (`lca`) or the Euler tour with a sparse table (`lcaeuler`).
- Compared with binary lifting: this uses O(V + Q) memory and nearly linear time, with no `log` table. It is harder to remember, but it fits well when you process queries during a DFS anyway.
- The DSU needs path compression to be fast; here the leader of a group is not chosen by size, because the leader must be the open ancestor, so linking is always child to parent.
- The same pattern (merge finished subtrees upward during DFS) gives small-to-large merging and other offline tree tricks.
- Pitfalls: answering a query when only one end has been visited (check `visited[u]`), forgetting to store the query at both ends, and recursion depth (the code here is iterative).
--cost
Time O((V + Q) times alpha(V)), nearly linear, where alpha is the very slowly growing inverse Ackermann function. Space O(V + Q).
--java
// queries[i] = {a, b}; returns ans[i] = LCA of a and b in the tree rooted at root
static int[] offlineLca(List<List<Integer>> adj, int root, int[][] queries) {
    int n = adj.size();
    List<List<int[]>> qs = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) qs.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int i = 0; i < queries.length; i++) {          // each query is stored at both ends
        qs.get(queries[i][0]).add(new int[]{queries[i][1], i});
        qs.get(queries[i][1]).add(new int[]{queries[i][0], i});
    }
    int[] dsu = new int[n], parent = new int[n], it = new int[n], ans = new int[queries.length];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) dsu[i] = i;
    Arrays.fill(parent, -1);
    boolean[] visited = new boolean[n];
    ArrayDeque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
    st.push(root);
    while (!st.isEmpty()) {
        int v = st.peek();
        if (!visited[v]) {                              // entering v for the first time
            visited[v] = true;
            for (int[] q : qs.get(v)) if (visited[q[0]]) ans[q[1]] = findSet(dsu, q[0]);
        }
        if (it[v] < adj.get(v).size()) {
            int w = adj.get(v).get(it[v]++);
            if (w != parent[v]) { parent[w] = v; st.push(w); }
        } else {
            st.pop();                                   // v is finished: merge it into its parent
            if (parent[v] != -1) dsu[v] = parent[v];
        }
    }
    return ans;
}

static int findSet(int[] dsu, int x) {
    while (dsu[x] != x) { dsu[x] = dsu[dsu[x]]; x = dsu[x]; }
    return x;
}
--py
def offline_lca(adj, root, queries):
    """queries is a list of (a, b). Returns ans[i] = LCA of a and b in the tree rooted at root."""
    n = len(adj)
    qs = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    for i, (a, b) in enumerate(queries):              # each query is stored at both ends
        qs[a].append((b, i))
        qs[b].append((a, i))
    dsu = list(range(n))
    parent = [-1] * n
    it = [0] * n
    visited = [False] * n
    ans = [-1] * len(queries)

    def find(x):
        while dsu[x] != x:
            dsu[x] = dsu[dsu[x]]
            x = dsu[x]
        return x

    stack = [root]
    while stack:
        v = stack[-1]
        if not visited[v]:                            # entering v for the first time
            visited[v] = True
            for u, qid in qs[v]:
                if visited[u]:
                    ans[qid] = find(u)
        if it[v] < len(adj[v]):
            w = adj[v][it[v]]
            it[v] += 1
            if w != parent[v]:
                parent[w] = v
                stack.append(w)
        else:
            stack.pop()                               # v is finished: merge it into its parent
            if parent[v] != -1:
                dsu[v] = parent[v]
    return ans
--cpp
int findSet(vector<int>& dsu, int x) {
    while (dsu[x] != x) { dsu[x] = dsu[dsu[x]]; x = dsu[x]; }
    return x;
}

// queries[i] = {a, b}; returns ans[i] = LCA of a and b in the tree rooted at root
vector<int> offlineLca(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int root, const vector<pair<int,int>>& queries) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<vector<pair<int,int>>> qs(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < (int)queries.size(); i++) {     // each query is stored at both ends
        qs[queries[i].first].push_back({queries[i].second, i});
        qs[queries[i].second].push_back({queries[i].first, i});
    }
    vector<int> dsu(n), parent(n, -1), it(n, 0), ans(queries.size(), -1), st = {root};
    iota(dsu.begin(), dsu.end(), 0);
    vector<char> visited(n, 0);
    while (!st.empty()) {
        int v = st.back();
        if (!visited[v]) {                              // entering v for the first time
            visited[v] = 1;
            for (auto [u, id] : qs[v]) if (visited[u]) ans[id] = findSet(dsu, u);
        }
        if (it[v] < (int)adj[v].size()) {
            int w = adj[v][it[v]++];
            if (w != parent[v]) { parent[w] = v; st.push_back(w); }
        } else {
            st.pop_back();                              // v is finished: merge it into its parent
            if (parent[v] != -1) dsu[v] = parent[v];
        }
    }
    return ans;
}
--go
// queries[i] = {a, b}; returns ans[i] = LCA of a and b in the tree rooted at root
func offlineLca(adj [][]int, root int, queries [][2]int) []int {
    n := len(adj)
    type qry struct{ other, id int }
    qs := make([][]qry, n)
    for i, q := range queries { // each query is stored at both ends
        qs[q[0]] = append(qs[q[0]], qry{q[1], i})
        qs[q[1]] = append(qs[q[1]], qry{q[0], i})
    }
    dsu, parent, it := make([]int, n), make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
    for i := range dsu {
        dsu[i], parent[i] = i, -1
    }
    ans := make([]int, len(queries))
    visited := make([]bool, n)
    st := []int{root}
    for len(st) > 0 {
        v := st[len(st)-1]
        if !visited[v] { // entering v for the first time
            visited[v] = true
            for _, q := range qs[v] {
                if visited[q.other] {
                    ans[q.id] = findSet(dsu, q.other)
                }
            }
        }
        if it[v] < len(adj[v]) {
            w := adj[v][it[v]]
            it[v]++
            if w != parent[v] {
                parent[w] = v
                st = append(st, w)
            }
        } else {
            st = st[:len(st)-1] // v is finished: merge it into its parent
            if parent[v] != -1 {
                dsu[v] = parent[v]
            }
        }
    }
    return ans
}

func findSet(dsu []int, x int) int {
    for dsu[x] != x {
        dsu[x] = dsu[dsu[x]]
        x = dsu[x]
    }
    return x
}
### hld
--idea
Heavy-light decomposition (HLD) cuts a tree into vertical chains so that any path between two nodes crosses only about `log n` chains. Then a question about a path (the sum or maximum of node values along it, with updates) turns into a few questions about ranges of an array, which a segment tree or Fenwick tree answers quickly.

How the chains are chosen: for each node, its heavy child is the child with the largest subtree. The edge to the heavy child is a heavy edge, all other child edges are light. Heavy edges join into chains. Walking from any node up to the root, every light edge you cross at least doubles the subtree size (the child you leave is not the heaviest, so the parent's subtree is more than twice as big), which can only happen `log n` times. So a path touches at most `log n` chains, and each chain is a contiguous run in the array.
--build
1. Root the tree. With a BFS order record `parent[v]` and `depth[v]`.
2. Process nodes in reverse BFS order. Each node adds its size to its parent's `size`. While doing so keep `heavy[p]`, the child of `p` with the largest `size`.
3. Assign array positions. Go through nodes in BFS order. A node `v` starts a chain if it is the root or not the heavy child of its parent. For a chain start, walk down `u = v, heavy[v], heavy[heavy[v]], ...` and give each node `head[u] = v` and the next free position `pos[u]`. Each chain gets one unbroken run of positions.
4. Put the node values in a segment tree `t` of size `2n` at `pos[v]` and build it bottom up. The segment tree comes from the code pieces: `update(i, v)` (piece `u-segupd`) changes one cell, and `query(l, r)` (piece `u-segq`) sums positions `l .. r-1`. A change to node `v` is `update(pos[v], x)`. The pieces work on `n` and `t` and use `int` sums.
5. To query a path from `u` to `v`: while `head[u] != head[v]`, take the one whose chain head is deeper (say `u`). Call `query(pos[head[u]], pos[u] + 1)`, which covers the part of the chain from its top down to `u`. Add it to the answer and jump `u = parent[head[u]]`.
6. Now `u` and `v` are on the same chain. Let `u` be the shallower one and call `query(pos[u], pos[v] + 1)`.
7. Invariant: each loop pass removes one chain from the path, and the part of the path already counted is exactly the part below the new `u`. At most `log n` passes occur by the heavy-edge argument.
8. The node where the loop ends (the shallower of `u` and `v`) is the LCA, so HLD also answers LCA queries without a lifting table.
--think
- Signal: "queries on the path between two nodes of a tree with updates": sum, maximum, minimum, XOR; "add a value to every node on a path". Static trees only. Without updates, simpler tools such as prefix sums on the tree plus LCA (`lca`) are enough.
- For queries about a whole subtree (not a path) the Euler tour (`euler`) is enough. HLD can do both if you number the heavy child first, so that every subtree is also a contiguous range.
- Values on edges instead of nodes: store each edge's value at its lower endpoint, and in the last same-chain step exclude the shallower node itself (`pos[u] + 1`).
- Alternatives: centroid decomposition (`centroid`) for distance-based queries, link-cut trees when the tree itself changes.
- Order matters for complexity: the segment tree gives `log n` per range, so a path query is O(log squared n).
- Pitfalls: querying chains in the wrong direction (always pick the deeper chain head), forgetting that `head` and not the current node decides the range start, and using plain recursion for the sizes on deep trees.
--cost
Build O(n). Path query or point update O(log squared n). Space O(n).
--uses
u-segupd
u-segq
--java
class Hld {
    int n;
    int[] parent, depth, head, pos, heavy, size;
    int[] t;                                               // segment tree over positions (sums), used by the pieces
    // update(i, v) and query(l, r) come from the code pieces

    Hld(List<List<Integer>> adj, int[] vals, int root) {
        n = adj.size();
        parent = new int[n]; depth = new int[n]; head = new int[n]; pos = new int[n];
        heavy = new int[n]; size = new int[n];
        Arrays.fill(heavy, -1); Arrays.fill(size, 1);
        int[] order = new int[n];
        int cnt = 0;
        order[cnt++] = root; parent[root] = -1;
        for (int i = 0; i < cnt; i++) {                    // BFS order
            int v = order[i];
            for (int w : adj.get(v))
                if (w != parent[v]) { parent[w] = v; depth[w] = depth[v] + 1; order[cnt++] = w; }
        }
        for (int i = n - 1; i > 0; i--) {                  // sizes and heavy children, leaves first
            int v = order[i], p = parent[v];
            size[p] += size[v];
            if (heavy[p] == -1 || size[v] > size[heavy[p]]) heavy[p] = v;
        }
        int cur = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {                      // lay each chain out contiguously
            int v = order[i];
            if (parent[v] == -1 || heavy[parent[v]] != v)
                for (int u = v; u != -1; u = heavy[u]) { head[u] = v; pos[u] = cur++; }
        }
        t = new int[2 * n];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) t[n + pos[v]] = vals[v];
        for (int i = n - 1; i >= 1; i--) t[i] = t[2 * i] + t[2 * i + 1];
    }

    void setValue(int v, int x) { update(pos[v], x); }     // piece: point update

    int pathSum(int u, int v) {
        int s = 0;
        while (head[u] != head[v]) {
            if (depth[head[u]] < depth[head[v]]) { int x = u; u = v; v = x; }   // deeper chain first
            s += query(pos[head[u]], pos[u] + 1);          // piece: top of the chain down to u
            u = parent[head[u]];                           // jump over the chain
        }
        if (depth[u] > depth[v]) { int x = u; u = v; v = x; }
        return s + query(pos[u], pos[v] + 1);              // piece: same chain now
    }
}
--py
n, t = 0, []                                               # the segment tree the pieces work on
parent, depth, head, pos = [], [], [], []
# update(i, v) and query(l, r) come from the code pieces

def build_hld(adj, vals, root=0):
    global n, t, parent, depth, head, pos
    n = len(adj)
    parent, depth = [-1] * n, [0] * n
    order = [root]
    for v in order:                                        # BFS order
        for w in adj[v]:
            if w != parent[v]:
                parent[w] = v
                depth[w] = depth[v] + 1
                order.append(w)
    size = [1] * n
    heavy = [-1] * n
    for v in reversed(order[1:]):                          # sizes and heavy children, leaves first
        p = parent[v]
        size[p] += size[v]
        if heavy[p] == -1 or size[v] > size[heavy[p]]:
            heavy[p] = v
    head, pos = [0] * n, [0] * n
    cur = 0
    for v in order:                                        # lay each chain out contiguously
        if parent[v] == -1 or heavy[parent[v]] != v:
            u = v
            while u != -1:
                head[u], pos[u] = v, cur
                cur += 1
                u = heavy[u]
    t = [0] * (2 * n)
    for v in range(n):
        t[n + pos[v]] = vals[v]
    for i in range(n - 1, 0, -1):
        t[i] = t[2 * i] + t[2 * i + 1]

def set_value(v, x):
    update(pos[v], x)                                      # piece: point update

def path_sum(u, v):
    s = 0
    while head[u] != head[v]:
        if depth[head[u]] < depth[head[v]]:
            u, v = v, u                                    # deeper chain first
        s += query(pos[head[u]], pos[u] + 1)               # piece: top of the chain down to u
        u = parent[head[u]]                                # jump over the chain
    if depth[u] > depth[v]:
        u, v = v, u
    return s + query(pos[u], pos[v] + 1)                   # piece: same chain now
--cpp
struct Hld {
    int n;
    vector<int> parent, depth, head, pos, heavy, size;
    vector<int> t;                                         // segment tree over positions (sums), used by the pieces
    // update(i, v) and query(l, r) come from the code pieces

    Hld(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, const vector<int>& vals, int root = 0) {
        n = adj.size();
        parent.assign(n, -1); depth.assign(n, 0); head.assign(n, 0); pos.assign(n, 0);
        heavy.assign(n, -1); size.assign(n, 1);
        vector<int> order = {root};
        for (size_t i = 0; i < order.size(); i++) {        // BFS order
            int v = order[i];
            for (int w : adj[v])
                if (w != parent[v]) { parent[w] = v; depth[w] = depth[v] + 1; order.push_back(w); }
        }
        for (int i = n - 1; i > 0; i--) {                  // sizes and heavy children, leaves first
            int v = order[i], p = parent[v];
            size[p] += size[v];
            if (heavy[p] == -1 || size[v] > size[heavy[p]]) heavy[p] = v;
        }
        int cur = 0;
        for (int v : order)                                // lay each chain out contiguously
            if (parent[v] == -1 || heavy[parent[v]] != v)
                for (int u = v; u != -1; u = heavy[u]) { head[u] = v; pos[u] = cur++; }
        t.assign(2 * n, 0);
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) t[n + pos[v]] = vals[v];
        for (int i = n - 1; i >= 1; i--) t[i] = t[2 * i] + t[2 * i + 1];
    }

    void setValue(int v, int x) { update(pos[v], x); }     // piece: point update

    int pathSum(int u, int v) {
        int s = 0;
        while (head[u] != head[v]) {
            if (depth[head[u]] < depth[head[v]]) swap(u, v);        // deeper chain first
            s += query(pos[head[u]], pos[u] + 1);          // piece: top of the chain down to u
            u = parent[head[u]];                           // jump over the chain
        }
        if (depth[u] > depth[v]) swap(u, v);
        return s + query(pos[u], pos[v] + 1);              // piece: same chain now
    }
};
--go
var n int   // number of nodes
var t []int // segment tree over positions (sums), used by the pieces
// update(i, v) and query(l, r) come from the code pieces

type Hld struct {
    parent, depth, head, pos, heavy, size []int
}

func newHld(adj [][]int, vals []int, root int) *Hld {
    n = len(adj)
    h := &Hld{make([]int, n), make([]int, n), make([]int, n), make([]int, n), make([]int, n), make([]int, n)}
    for i := range h.heavy {
        h.heavy[i], h.size[i] = -1, 1
    }
    h.parent[root] = -1
    order := []int{root}
    for i := 0; i < len(order); i++ { // BFS order
        v := order[i]
        for _, w := range adj[v] {
            if w != h.parent[v] {
                h.parent[w], h.depth[w] = v, h.depth[v]+1
                order = append(order, w)
            }
        }
    }
    for i := n - 1; i > 0; i-- { // sizes and heavy children, leaves first
        v := order[i]
        p := h.parent[v]
        h.size[p] += h.size[v]
        if h.heavy[p] == -1 || h.size[v] > h.size[h.heavy[p]] {
            h.heavy[p] = v
        }
    }
    cur := 0
    for _, v := range order { // lay each chain out contiguously
        if h.parent[v] == -1 || h.heavy[h.parent[v]] != v {
            for u := v; u != -1; u = h.heavy[u] {
                h.head[u], h.pos[u] = v, cur
                cur++
            }
        }
    }
    t = make([]int, 2*n)
    for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
        t[n+h.pos[v]] = vals[v]
    }
    for i := n - 1; i >= 1; i-- {
        t[i] = t[2*i] + t[2*i+1]
    }
    return h
}

func (h *Hld) setValue(v, x int) { update(h.pos[v], x) } // piece: point update

func (h *Hld) pathSum(u, v int) int {
    s := 0
    for h.head[u] != h.head[v] {
        if h.depth[h.head[u]] < h.depth[h.head[v]] { // deeper chain first
            u, v = v, u
        }
        s += query(h.pos[h.head[u]], h.pos[u]+1) // piece: top of the chain down to u
        u = h.parent[h.head[u]]                  // jump over the chain
    }
    if h.depth[u] > h.depth[v] {
        u, v = v, u
    }
    return s + query(h.pos[u], h.pos[v]+1) // piece: same chain now
}
### centroid
--idea
A centroid of a tree is a node whose removal leaves no piece with more than half of the nodes. Every tree has one. Centroid decomposition picks the centroid, removes it, and repeats the same thing on each remaining piece. Because each piece has at most half the nodes, the nesting is at most `log n` levels deep. The chosen centroids form a new tree, the centroid tree, of height about `log n`.

Why it is useful: any path between two nodes `a` and `b` passes through the centroid that first separates them (the highest centroid in the centroid tree that lies on their path). So a question about paths can be answered at only `log n` centroids per node, namely the centroid ancestors of each node. Typical example below: after marking some nodes as "red", report the distance from any node to the nearest red node.
--build
1. Keep `removed[v]`, false at the start, and a stack `work` holding the start node of each piece still to decompose. Push node 0.
2. Pop a start node `s`. Collect its piece by a search that ignores removed nodes: record each node and its parent in the search, in order of discovery. Let `total` be the number of nodes.
3. Find the centroid: compute subtree sizes in the search tree by going through the collected nodes in reverse order. A node `c` is the centroid when the biggest of its child subtrees and `total - size[c]` (the part above) are both at most `total / 2`.
4. Run a breadth-first search from `c` inside the piece and, for each node `v` met at distance `d`, append `(c, d)` to the list `anc[v]`. So `anc[v]` ends up listing every centroid ancestor of `v` together with the distance to it, from the top level down.
5. Mark `removed[c] = true`. Push every non-removed neighbour of `c` onto `work`: each starts a smaller piece.
6. Stop when `work` is empty. Every node has been a centroid exactly once.
7. To use it: keep `best[c]`, the distance from centroid `c` to the nearest red node in its piece (infinite at the start). To mark `v` red, for each `(c, d)` in `anc[v]` set `best[c] = min(best[c], d)`. To ask for the nearest red node to `v`, take the minimum over `(c, d)` in `anc[v]` of `best[c] + d`.
8. Invariant for the query: the true shortest path from `v` to its nearest red node `r` passes through the top centroid that separates them (or one of them is that centroid), and at that centroid the sum `best[c] + d` is exact. Other centroids give sums that correspond to real walks, so they never underestimate.
--think
- Signal: "count or minimise over all paths in a tree": paths of length exactly `k`, nearest marked node, sum of distances to marked nodes, "for each node, the number of nodes within distance `d`". Combine pairs of nodes in different child subtrees of a centroid, handling "same subtree" by subtracting.
- The tree must not change shape (nodes and edges are fixed); marks can change freely.
- The split rule gives `log n` depth, so total work is `n log n` for building plus `log n` per update or query.
- Compare with HLD (`hld`): HLD answers path aggregates (sum, max on the exact path). Centroid decomposition answers distance and counting questions between pairs. Smaller tools: if you only need the farthest pair, see `treediam`.
- Pitfalls: using recursion for the piece search on deep trees (use an explicit stack or BFS as here), forgetting to skip removed nodes, and computing the centroid with sizes from the original tree rather than from the current piece.
--cost
Build O(n log n) time and space. Each mark or query O(log n).
--java
static class CentroidNearest {
    static final int INF = 1_000_000_000;
    List<List<int[]>> anc = new ArrayList<>();            // anc.get(v): {centroid, distance to it}
    int[] best;                                           // best[c]: distance to the nearest red node in c's piece

    CentroidNearest(List<List<Integer>> adj) {
        int n = adj.size();
        best = new int[n];
        Arrays.fill(best, INF);
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) anc.add(new ArrayList<>());
        boolean[] removed = new boolean[n];
        int[] par = new int[n], sz = new int[n], dist = new int[n];
        ArrayDeque<Integer> work = new ArrayDeque<>();
        work.push(0);
        while (!work.isEmpty()) {
            int s = work.pop();
            List<Integer> comp = new ArrayList<>();       // the piece, in discovery order
            comp.add(s); par[s] = -1;
            for (int i = 0; i < comp.size(); i++) {
                int v = comp.get(i);
                sz[v] = 1;
                for (int w : adj.get(v)) if (!removed[w] && w != par[v]) { par[w] = v; comp.add(w); }
            }
            int total = comp.size();
            for (int i = total - 1; i > 0; i--) sz[par[comp.get(i)]] += sz[comp.get(i)];
            int c = s;
            for (int v : comp) {                          // centroid: no part above half
                int big = total - sz[v];
                for (int w : adj.get(v)) if (!removed[w] && w != par[v]) big = Math.max(big, sz[w]);
                if (big * 2 <= total) { c = v; break; }
            }
            ArrayDeque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();   // distances from c inside the piece
            dist[c] = 0; par[c] = -1; q.add(c);
            while (!q.isEmpty()) {
                int v = q.poll();
                anc.get(v).add(new int[]{c, dist[v]});
                for (int w : adj.get(v)) if (!removed[w] && w != par[v]) { par[w] = v; dist[w] = dist[v] + 1; q.add(w); }
            }
            removed[c] = true;
            for (int w : adj.get(c)) if (!removed[w]) work.push(w);
        }
    }

    void mark(int v) { for (int[] a : anc.get(v)) best[a[0]] = Math.min(best[a[0]], a[1]); }

    int nearest(int v) {                                  // INF if nothing is marked
        int res = INF;
        for (int[] a : anc.get(v)) res = Math.min(res, best[a[0]] + a[1]);
        return res;
    }
}
--py
class CentroidNearest:
    INF = 10 ** 9

    def __init__(self, adj):
        n = len(adj)
        self.best = [self.INF] * n              # best[c]: distance to the nearest red node in c's piece
        self.anc = [[] for _ in range(n)]       # anc[v]: (centroid, distance to it)
        removed = [False] * n
        par = [-1] * n
        work = [0]
        while work:
            s = work.pop()
            comp = [s]                          # the piece, in discovery order
            par[s] = -1
            for v in comp:
                for w in adj[v]:
                    if not removed[w] and w != par[v]:
                        par[w] = v
                        comp.append(w)
            total = len(comp)
            sz = {v: 1 for v in comp}
            for v in reversed(comp[1:]):
                sz[par[v]] += sz[v]
            c = s
            for v in comp:                      # centroid: no part above half
                big = total - sz[v]
                for w in adj[v]:
                    if not removed[w] and w != par[v]:
                        big = max(big, sz[w])
                if big * 2 <= total:
                    c = v
                    break
            dist = {c: 0}                       # distances from c inside the piece
            par[c] = -1
            q = [c]
            for v in q:
                self.anc[v].append((c, dist[v]))
                for w in adj[v]:
                    if not removed[w] and w != par[v]:
                        par[w] = v
                        dist[w] = dist[v] + 1
                        q.append(w)
            removed[c] = True
            work.extend(w for w in adj[c] if not removed[w])

    def mark(self, v):
        for c, d in self.anc[v]:
            self.best[c] = min(self.best[c], d)

    def nearest(self, v):                       # INF if nothing is marked
        return min(self.best[c] + d for c, d in self.anc[v])
--cpp
struct CentroidNearest {
    static constexpr int INF = 1000000000;
    vector<vector<pair<int,int>>> anc;                      // anc[v]: {centroid, distance to it}
    vector<int> best;                                       // best[c]: distance to the nearest red node in c's piece

    CentroidNearest(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
        int n = adj.size();
        best.assign(n, INF);
        anc.assign(n, {});
        vector<char> removed(n, 0);
        vector<int> par(n, -1), sz(n, 0), dist(n, 0), work = {0};
        while (!work.empty()) {
            int s = work.back(); work.pop_back();
            vector<int> comp = {s};                         // the piece, in discovery order
            par[s] = -1;
            for (size_t i = 0; i < comp.size(); i++) {
                int v = comp[i];
                sz[v] = 1;
                for (int w : adj[v]) if (!removed[w] && w != par[v]) { par[w] = v; comp.push_back(w); }
            }
            int total = comp.size();
            for (int i = total - 1; i > 0; i--) sz[par[comp[i]]] += sz[comp[i]];
            int c = s;
            for (int v : comp) {                            // centroid: no part above half
                int big = total - sz[v];
                for (int w : adj[v]) if (!removed[w] && w != par[v]) big = max(big, sz[w]);
                if (big * 2 <= total) { c = v; break; }
            }
            vector<int> q = {c};                            // distances from c inside the piece
            dist[c] = 0; par[c] = -1;
            for (size_t i = 0; i < q.size(); i++) {
                int v = q[i];
                anc[v].push_back({c, dist[v]});
                for (int w : adj[v]) if (!removed[w] && w != par[v]) { par[w] = v; dist[w] = dist[v] + 1; q.push_back(w); }
            }
            removed[c] = 1;
            for (int w : adj[c]) if (!removed[w]) work.push_back(w);
        }
    }

    void mark(int v) { for (auto [c, d] : anc[v]) best[c] = min(best[c], d); }

    int nearest(int v) const {                              // INF if nothing is marked
        int res = INF;
        for (auto [c, d] : anc[v]) res = min(res, best[c] + d);
        return res;
    }
};
--go
const inf = 1_000_000_000

type CentroidNearest struct {
    anc  [][][2]int // anc[v]: {centroid, distance to it}
    best []int      // best[c]: distance to the nearest red node in c's piece
}

func newCentroidNearest(adj [][]int) *CentroidNearest {
    n := len(adj)
    cn := &CentroidNearest{anc: make([][][2]int, n), best: make([]int, n)}
    for i := range cn.best {
        cn.best[i] = inf
    }
    removed := make([]bool, n)
    par, sz, dist := make([]int, n), make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
    for work := []int{0}; len(work) > 0; {
        s := work[len(work)-1]
        work = work[:len(work)-1]
        comp := []int{s} // the piece, in discovery order
        par[s] = -1
        for i := 0; i < len(comp); i++ {
            v := comp[i]
            sz[v] = 1
            for _, w := range adj[v] {
                if !removed[w] && w != par[v] {
                    par[w] = v
                    comp = append(comp, w)
                }
            }
        }
        total := len(comp)
        for i := total - 1; i > 0; i-- {
            sz[par[comp[i]]] += sz[comp[i]]
        }
        c := s
        for _, v := range comp { // centroid: no part above half
            big := total - sz[v]
            for _, w := range adj[v] {
                if !removed[w] && w != par[v] {
                    big = max(big, sz[w])
                }
            }
            if big*2 <= total {
                c = v
                break
            }
        }
        dist[c], par[c] = 0, -1 // distances from c inside the piece
        for q := []int{c}; len(q) > 0; q = q[1:] {
            v := q[0]
            cn.anc[v] = append(cn.anc[v], [2]int{c, dist[v]})
            for _, w := range adj[v] {
                if !removed[w] && w != par[v] {
                    par[w], dist[w] = v, dist[v]+1
                    q = append(q, w)
                }
            }
        }
        removed[c] = true
        for _, w := range adj[c] {
            if !removed[w] {
                work = append(work, w)
            }
        }
    }
    return cn
}

func (cn *CentroidNearest) mark(v int) {
    for _, a := range cn.anc[v] {
        cn.best[a[0]] = min(cn.best[a[0]], a[1])
    }
}

func (cn *CentroidNearest) nearest(v int) int { // inf if nothing is marked
    res := inf
    for _, a := range cn.anc[v] {
        res = min(res, cn.best[a[0]]+a[1])
    }
    return res
}
### vtree
--idea
A virtual tree (also called an auxiliary tree) is a small tree built from just the `k` nodes you care about, called key nodes, inside a huge rooted tree. It keeps the key nodes plus every lowest common ancestor (LCA, the deepest shared ancestor) of pairs of them, and an edge joins each node to its nearest ancestor that is also in the set. So long chains of unimportant nodes collapse into a single edge, and the virtual tree has fewer than `2k` nodes.

Why it matters: a problem may give many queries, each with a small set of key nodes, while the real tree is large. Running a tree DP over the whole tree for each query is too slow. Running it over the virtual tree costs only about `k log k`, and the total size of all query sets is small.
--build
1. Preparation: build the LCA structure for the whole tree (`lca`) and the entry and exit times `tin` and `tout` of a DFS (`euler`). Node `a` is an ancestor of `b` exactly when `tin[a] <= tin[b] <= tout[a]`.
2. Take the key nodes. Sort them by `tin`.
3. For each pair of neighbours in this sorted list, compute their LCA with `lca(a, b)` and add it to the set. Sort the set by `tin` again and drop duplicates. The result is closed under taking LCAs: it contains every branching point.
4. Build the edges with a stack. Go through the nodes in `tin` order. For node `v`: while the stack is not empty and the top is not an ancestor of `v`, pop it. If the stack is not empty, the top is the nearest ancestor of `v` in the set, so add the edge `(top, v)`. Then push `v`.
5. The first node in `tin` order is the root of the virtual tree (the LCA of all key nodes).
6. Invariant: the stack always holds a chain of ancestors, each an ancestor of the next, which is the path from the virtual root to the last node added. A popped node can never be an ancestor of a later node, because later nodes have larger `tin` and lie outside its range.
7. To run a DP, use the edges (with the real distance or weight of each edge as depth difference) and process in reverse `tin` order.
--think
- Signal: many queries on one big tree, each giving a small list of nodes ("`k` cities are important"), with the sum of `k` bounded. Questions like "minimum cut to separate the key nodes from the root", "total length of the union of paths between key nodes", "number of key pairs with some property".
- Why adding the LCAs of neighbours in `tin` order is enough: the LCA of any two key nodes equals the LCA of two consecutive nodes between them in `tin` order. So no branching point is missed.
- Compare: HLD (`hld`) handles path queries on the whole tree. The virtual tree shrinks the tree itself so that a DP is cheap. Sparse tables on the Euler tour (`lcaeuler`) give constant-time LCA, which makes the build `k log k` dominated by sorting.
- Edge lengths: the distance of a virtual edge `(p, v)` is `depth[v] - depth[p]`.
- Pitfalls: forgetting to include the root when the DP needs it (add it to the key set first), duplicates after adding LCAs, and sorting by node id instead of by `tin`.
--cost
Preparation O(n log n). Each query with `k` key nodes costs O(k log k).
--uses
u-lcaq
--java
// uses buildLca/lca from the lca lesson and eulerTour from the euler lesson (build both once for the tree).
// Returns the edges {parent, child} of the virtual tree of the key nodes.
static List<int[]> virtualTree(List<List<Integer>> adj, int root, int[] keys) {
    int[][] tour = eulerTour(adj, root);                // euler lesson: entry and exit times
    int[] tin = tour[0], tout = tour[1];
    buildLca(adj, root);                                // piece: LCA by lifting
    List<Integer> nodes = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int k : keys) nodes.add(k);
    nodes.sort((a, b) -> Integer.compare(tin[a], tin[b]));
    for (int i = 0; i + 1 < keys.length; i++) nodes.add(lca(nodes.get(i), nodes.get(i + 1)));   // branching points
    TreeSet<Integer> byTime = new TreeSet<>((a, b) -> Integer.compare(tin[a], tin[b]));
    byTime.addAll(nodes);                               // sorted by tin, duplicates dropped
    List<int[]> edges = new ArrayList<>();
    ArrayDeque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int v : byTime) {
        while (!st.isEmpty() && !(tin[st.peek()] <= tin[v] && tin[v] <= tout[st.peek()])) st.pop();
        if (!st.isEmpty()) edges.add(new int[]{st.peek(), v});      // nearest ancestor in the set
        st.push(v);
    }
    return edges;
}
--py
def virtual_tree(adj, root, keys):
    """Uses Lca from the lca lesson and euler_tour from the euler lesson.
    Returns the edges (parent, child) of the virtual tree of the key nodes."""
    tin, tout, _ = euler_tour(adj, root)                # piece: entry and exit times
    lca = Lca(adj, root)                                # piece: LCA by lifting
    nodes = sorted(keys, key=lambda v: tin[v])
    nodes += [lca.query(a, b) for a, b in zip(nodes, nodes[1:])]    # branching points
    nodes = sorted(set(nodes), key=lambda v: tin[v])    # sorted by tin, duplicates dropped
    edges, stack = [], []
    for v in nodes:
        while stack and not (tin[stack[-1]] <= tin[v] <= tout[stack[-1]]):
            stack.pop()
        if stack:
            edges.append((stack[-1], v))                # nearest ancestor in the set
        stack.append(v)
    return edges
--cpp
// uses Lca from the lca lesson and eulerTour from the euler lesson.
// Returns the edges {parent, child} of the virtual tree of the key nodes.
vector<pair<int,int>> virtualTree(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int root, vector<int> keys) {
    vector<int> tin, tout, order;
    eulerTour(adj, root, tin, tout, order);             // euler lesson: entry and exit times
    Lca lca(adj, root);                                 // piece: LCA by lifting
    auto byTin = [&](int a, int b) { return tin[a] < tin[b]; };
    sort(keys.begin(), keys.end(), byTin);
    vector<int> nodes = keys;
    for (size_t i = 0; i + 1 < keys.size(); i++) nodes.push_back(lca.query(keys[i], keys[i + 1]));   // branching points
    sort(nodes.begin(), nodes.end(), byTin);
    nodes.erase(unique(nodes.begin(), nodes.end()), nodes.end());     // duplicates dropped
    vector<pair<int,int>> edges;
    vector<int> st;
    for (int v : nodes) {
        while (!st.empty() && !(tin[st.back()] <= tin[v] && tin[v] <= tout[st.back()])) st.pop_back();
        if (!st.empty()) edges.push_back({st.back(), v});         // nearest ancestor in the set
        st.push_back(v);
    }
    return edges;
}
--go
// uses buildLca/lca from the lca lesson and eulerTour from the euler lesson (build both once for the tree).
// Returns the edges {parent, child} of the virtual tree of the key nodes.
