func virtualTree(adj [][]int, root int, keys []int) [][2]int {
    tin, tout, _ := eulerTour(adj, root) // euler lesson: entry and exit times
    t := buildLca(adj, root)             // piece: LCA by lifting
    nodes := append([]int(nil), keys...)
    sort.Slice(nodes, func(a, b int) bool { return tin[nodes[a]] < tin[nodes[b]] })
    for i := 0; i+1 < len(keys); i++ {
        nodes = append(nodes, t.lca(nodes[i], nodes[i+1])) // branching points
    }
    sort.Slice(nodes, func(a, b int) bool { return tin[nodes[a]] < tin[nodes[b]] }) // sorted by tin
    var edges [][2]int
    var st []int
    for i, v := range nodes {
        if i > 0 && nodes[i-1] == v { // drop duplicates
            continue
        }
        for len(st) > 0 && !(tin[st[len(st)-1]] <= tin[v] && tin[v] <= tout[st[len(st)-1]]) {
            st = st[:len(st)-1]
        }
        if len(st) > 0 { // nearest ancestor in the set
            edges = append(edges, [2]int{st[len(st)-1], v})
        }
        st = append(st, v)
    }
    return edges
}
### treeiso
--idea
Two trees are isomorphic when they have the same shape: you can rename the nodes of one so that it becomes exactly the other. The names of the nodes do not matter, only the structure. A family tree drawn with different people in the same pattern is isomorphic to another one.

For rooted trees there is a clean method: give every shape a number. A leaf is one shape. A node's shape is decided by the multiset of the shapes of its children, so list the children's numbers in sorted order and look that list up in a table. If the list is new, give it the next free number. Two rooted trees are the same shape exactly when their roots get the same number. For unrooted trees, root them at their center (see `treecenter`), since the center is the same node (or pair of nodes) in any isomorphic copy.
--build
1. Make one table that maps "sorted list of child numbers" to a number. Share this table between both trees, otherwise their numbers cannot be compared.
2. `canon(root)`: find a BFS order from the root, remembering each node's parent. Process the nodes in reverse BFS order (children before parents).
3. For node `v`: sort the numbers collected from its children, look the list up in the table (add it with the next number if it is missing), and call that number `id[v]`. Then add `id[v]` to the list of its parent.
4. `canon(root)` returns `id[root]`.
5. For unrooted trees, find the 1 or 2 centers with `treeCenter` (standard form from the `treecenter` lesson). Compute `canon` rooted at each center and keep the smallest number as the tree's signature. A tree with two centers is rooted at each of them in turn; taking the minimum makes the signature independent of which one came first.
6. The trees are isomorphic exactly when the sizes agree and the signatures are equal.
7. Invariant: two nodes get the same number if and only if the subtrees below them have the same shape. By induction on height: equal numbers mean equal sorted lists of children numbers, which by the induction means the children match one for one in shape.
--think
- Signal: "are these two trees the same up to renaming", "count the number of distinct tree shapes in a set", "find duplicate subtrees", "group identical subtrees".
- Using a shared table gives exact answers, with no hash collisions. Hashing a tree to a single number with random values (a polynomial or XOR hash of sorted children) is shorter but can collide with a tiny probability. The table approach is the safe one in a contest and also gives each distinct subtree shape its own small id.
- Counting duplicate subtrees in one tree: the same `canon` pass gives every node an id; nodes with equal id have identical subtrees.
- Rooted trees with ordered children (left and right matter) skip the sorting step.
- For general graphs, isomorphism is a much harder problem; for trees it is easy because of the root and the recursive structure.
- Pitfalls: forgetting to share the table, rooting unrooted trees at arbitrary nodes (give different answers for the same shape), sorting the child list as strings instead of numbers, and recursion depth (the code here is iterative).
--cost
Time O(n log n) from sorting the child lists (total child list length is n), space O(n).
--java
// uses treeCenter from the treecenter lesson. True if the two (unrooted) trees have the same shape.
static boolean treesIsomorphic(List<List<Integer>> a, List<List<Integer>> b) {
    if (a.size() != b.size()) return false;
    Map<List<Integer>, Integer> table = new HashMap<>();       // shared between both trees
    return signature(a, table) == signature(b, table);
}

static int signature(List<List<Integer>> adj, Map<List<Integer>, Integer> table) {
    int best = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
    for (int c : treeCenter(adj)) best = Math.min(best, canon(adj, c, table));   // root at each center
    return best;
}

static int canon(List<List<Integer>> adj, int root, Map<List<Integer>, Integer> table) {
    int n = adj.size();
    int[] parent = new int[n], order = new int[n], id = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(parent, -1);
    int cnt = 0;
    order[cnt++] = root;
    for (int i = 0; i < cnt; i++)
        for (int w : adj.get(order[i])) if (w != parent[order[i]]) { parent[w] = order[i]; order[cnt++] = w; }
    List<List<Integer>> kids = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) kids.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) {                         // children before parents
        int v = order[i];
        Collections.sort(kids.get(v));
        Integer got = table.get(kids.get(v));
        if (got == null) { got = table.size(); table.put(kids.get(v), got); }
        id[v] = got;
        if (parent[v] != -1) kids.get(parent[v]).add(id[v]);
    }
    return id[root];
}
--py
def trees_isomorphic(a, b):
    """Uses tree_center from the treecenter lesson. True if the two (unrooted) trees have the same shape."""
    if len(a) != len(b):
        return False
    table = {}                                       # shared between both trees

    def canon(adj, root):
        parent = {root: -1}
        order = [root]
        for v in order:
            for w in adj[v]:
                if w != parent[v]:
                    parent[w] = v
                    order.append(w)
        kids = {v: [] for v in order}
        ident = {}
        for v in reversed(order):                    # children before parents
            key = tuple(sorted(kids[v]))
            ident[v] = table.setdefault(key, len(table))
            if parent[v] != -1:
                kids[parent[v]].append(ident[v])
        return ident[root]

    def signature(adj):
        return min(canon(adj, c) for c in tree_center(adj))      # root at each center

    return signature(a) == signature(b)
--cpp
int canonId(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int root, map<vector<int>, int>& table) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<int> parent(n, -1), order = {root}, id(n, 0);
    for (size_t i = 0; i < order.size(); i++)
        for (int w : adj[order[i]]) if (w != parent[order[i]]) { parent[w] = order[i]; order.push_back(w); }
    vector<vector<int>> kids(n);
    for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) {                         // children before parents
        int v = order[i];
        sort(kids[v].begin(), kids[v].end());
        auto it = table.find(kids[v]);
        if (it == table.end()) it = table.insert({kids[v], (int)table.size()}).first;
        id[v] = it->second;
        if (parent[v] != -1) kids[parent[v]].push_back(id[v]);
    }
    return id[root];
}

int signatureOf(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, map<vector<int>, int>& table) {
    int best = INT_MAX;
    for (int c : treeCenter(adj)) best = min(best, canonId(adj, c, table));   // root at each center
    return best;
}

// uses treeCenter from the treecenter lesson. True if the two (unrooted) trees have the same shape.
bool treesIsomorphic(const vector<vector<int>>& a, const vector<vector<int>>& b) {
    if (a.size() != b.size()) return false;
    map<vector<int>, int> table;                               // shared between both trees
    return signatureOf(a, table) == signatureOf(b, table);
}
--go
// uses treeCenter from the treecenter lesson. True if the two (unrooted) trees have the same shape.
func treesIsomorphic(a, b [][]int) bool {
    if len(a) != len(b) {
        return false
    }
    table := map[string]int{} // shared between both trees
    return signature(a, table) == signature(b, table)
}

func signature(adj [][]int, table map[string]int) int {
    best := 1 << 60
    for _, c := range treeCenter(adj) { // root at each center
        best = min(best, canon(adj, c, table))
    }
    return best
}

func canon(adj [][]int, root int, table map[string]int) int {
    n := len(adj)
    parent, id := make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
    for i := range parent {
        parent[i] = -1
    }
    order := []int{root}
    for i := 0; i < len(order); i++ {
        for _, w := range adj[order[i]] {
            if w != parent[order[i]] {
                parent[w] = order[i]
                order = append(order, w)
            }
        }
    }
    kids := make([][]int, n)
    for i := n - 1; i >= 0; i-- { // children before parents
        v := order[i]
        sort.Ints(kids[v])
        key := fmt.Sprint(kids[v]) // a slice cannot be a map key, its text can
        got, ok := table[key]
        if !ok {
            got = len(table)
            table[key] = got
        }
        id[v] = got
        if parent[v] != -1 {
            kids[parent[v]] = append(kids[parent[v]], got)
        }
    }
    return id[root]
}
### tarjan
--idea
Tarjan's algorithm finds all strongly connected components (SCCs, groups of nodes that can all reach each other) in a single depth-first search. Where Kosaraju (`kosaraju`) needs a reversed copy of the graph and two passes, Tarjan keeps two numbers per node and a stack.

During the DFS every node gets `index[v]`, the order in which it was first visited. It also gets `low[v]`, the smallest `index` that `v` can reach by going down through its DFS subtree and then taking at most one edge back to a node still on the stack. If `low[v] == index[v]`, then `v` cannot reach anything older than itself, so `v` is the root (the first-visited node) of an SCC, and the SCC is exactly the nodes above `v` on the stack.
--build
1. Make `index[v] = -1` for every node (unvisited), `low[v]`, `onStack[v] = false`, an empty node stack `st`, a counter `idx = 0`, and `comp[v] = -1` with `count = 0`.
2. For each unvisited start node, run an iterative DFS: a call stack of nodes plus `it[v]`, the next neighbour index to try. On entering `v`: set `index[v] = low[v] = idx`, add one to `idx`, push `v` on the node stack, set `onStack[v] = true`.
3. For each neighbour `w` of `v`: if `w` is unvisited, go down into it. If `w` is already on the node stack, it is an edge back into the current search, so set `low[v] = min(low[v], index[w])`. If `w` is visited but not on the stack, it belongs to an SCC that is already complete: ignore it.
4. When `v` has no more neighbours: if `low[v] == index[v]`, pop the node stack down to and including `v`, giving all of those nodes the id `count`, clearing their `onStack`, then add one to `count`. Whether or not `v` was a root, then tell the parent `p` on the call stack: `low[p] = min(low[p], low[v])`.
5. When all nodes are visited, `comp` has the SCC ids and `count` is their number.
6. Invariant: the node stack holds exactly the visited nodes whose SCC is not finished, in visit order, and every one of them can still reach (through the stack) the current search path. A node leaves the stack only together with its whole SCC.
7. Numbering: SCCs come out in reverse topological order of the condensation (`condense`): the first SCC finished is a sink (no arrows to other SCCs), and every arrow between components goes from a higher id to a lower id.
--think
- Signal: same as `scc` and `kosaraju`. Tarjan is the one to choose when memory or speed matter: no reversed graph, one pass.
- The subtle part is the stack test. An edge to a node that is visited but off the stack goes to a finished SCC and must not lower `low`. Using `index[w]` instead of `low[w]` for back edges is fine and is standard.
- The same `low` idea finds bridges and articulation points (`bridges`) in undirected graphs, and biconnected components (`bcc`). Learn it once here.
- Components come out in reverse topological order, so DP over the condensation should go from component 0 upward for sinks first. (Kosaraju gives the opposite order.)
- For 2-SAT (`twosat`) either algorithm works: compare component ids of a literal and its negation.
- Pitfalls: recursion depth on large graphs (hence the explicit stacks here), forgetting to update the parent's `low` after returning from a child, and mixing the call stack (the current path) with the node stack (the nodes of unfinished SCCs). They are different stacks.
--cost
Time O(V + E), space O(V).
--java
// returns comp[v]; ids are 0..k-1 in reverse topological order of the condensation
static int[] tarjan(List<List<Integer>> adj) {
    int n = adj.size(), idx = 0, count = 0;
    int[] index = new int[n], low = new int[n], comp = new int[n], it = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(index, -1); Arrays.fill(comp, -1);
    boolean[] onStack = new boolean[n];
    ArrayDeque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();           // nodes of unfinished SCCs
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        if (index[s] != -1) continue;
        ArrayDeque<Integer> call = new ArrayDeque<>();     // the current DFS path
        index[s] = low[s] = idx++; st.push(s); onStack[s] = true; call.push(s);
        while (!call.isEmpty()) {
            int v = call.peek();
            if (it[v] < adj.get(v).size()) {
                int w = adj.get(v).get(it[v]++);
                if (index[w] == -1) {
                    index[w] = low[w] = idx++; st.push(w); onStack[w] = true; call.push(w);
                } else if (onStack[w]) low[v] = Math.min(low[v], index[w]);   // edge back into the stack
            } else {
                call.pop();
                if (low[v] == index[v]) {                  // v is the root of an SCC
                    int w;
                    do { w = st.pop(); onStack[w] = false; comp[w] = count; } while (w != v);
                    count++;
                }
                if (!call.isEmpty()) low[call.peek()] = Math.min(low[call.peek()], low[v]);
            }
        }
    }
    return comp;
}
--py
def tarjan(adj):
    """Returns (comp, count); ids are in reverse topological order of the condensation."""
    n = len(adj)
    index = [-1] * n
    low = [0] * n
    comp = [-1] * n
    on_stack = [False] * n
    it = [0] * n
    st = []                                           # nodes of unfinished SCCs
    idx = count = 0
    for s in range(n):
        if index[s] != -1:
            continue
        index[s] = low[s] = idx
        idx += 1
        st.append(s)
        on_stack[s] = True
        call = [s]                                    # the current DFS path
        while call:
            v = call[-1]
            if it[v] < len(adj[v]):
                w = adj[v][it[v]]
                it[v] += 1
                if index[w] == -1:
                    index[w] = low[w] = idx
                    idx += 1
                    st.append(w)
                    on_stack[w] = True
                    call.append(w)
                elif on_stack[w]:
                    low[v] = min(low[v], index[w])    # edge back into the stack
            else:
                call.pop()
                if low[v] == index[v]:                # v is the root of an SCC
                    while True:
                        w = st.pop()
                        on_stack[w] = False
                        comp[w] = count
                        if w == v:
                            break
                    count += 1
                if call:
                    low[call[-1]] = min(low[call[-1]], low[v])
    return comp, count
--cpp
// returns comp[v]; ids are 0..k-1 in reverse topological order of the condensation
vector<int> tarjan(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
    int n = adj.size(), idx = 0, count = 0;
    vector<int> index(n, -1), low(n, 0), comp(n, -1), it(n, 0), st;   // st: nodes of unfinished SCCs
    vector<char> onStack(n, 0);
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        if (index[s] != -1) continue;
        index[s] = low[s] = idx++; st.push_back(s); onStack[s] = 1;
        vector<int> call = {s};                            // the current DFS path
        while (!call.empty()) {
            int v = call.back();
            if (it[v] < (int)adj[v].size()) {
                int w = adj[v][it[v]++];
                if (index[w] == -1) {
                    index[w] = low[w] = idx++; st.push_back(w); onStack[w] = 1; call.push_back(w);
                } else if (onStack[w]) low[v] = min(low[v], index[w]);   // edge back into the stack
            } else {
                call.pop_back();
                if (low[v] == index[v]) {                  // v is the root of an SCC
                    int w;
                    do { w = st.back(); st.pop_back(); onStack[w] = 0; comp[w] = count; } while (w != v);
                    count++;
                }
                if (!call.empty()) low[call.back()] = min(low[call.back()], low[v]);
            }
        }
    }
    return comp;
}
--go
// returns comp[v]; ids are 0..k-1 in reverse topological order of the condensation
func tarjan(adj [][]int) []int {
    n, idx, count := len(adj), 0, 0
    index, low, comp, it := make([]int, n), make([]int, n), make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
    for i := range index {
        index[i], comp[i] = -1, -1
    }
    onStack := make([]bool, n)
    var st []int // nodes of unfinished SCCs
    for s := 0; s < n; s++ {
        if index[s] != -1 {
            continue
        }
        index[s], low[s] = idx, idx
        idx++
        st = append(st, s)
        onStack[s] = true
        for call := []int{s}; len(call) > 0; { // the current DFS path
            v := call[len(call)-1]
            if it[v] < len(adj[v]) {
                w := adj[v][it[v]]
                it[v]++
                if index[w] == -1 {
                    index[w], low[w] = idx, idx
                    idx++
                    st = append(st, w)
                    onStack[w] = true
                    call = append(call, w)
                } else if onStack[w] { // edge back into the stack
                    low[v] = min(low[v], index[w])
                }
            } else {
                call = call[:len(call)-1]
                if low[v] == index[v] { // v is the root of an SCC
                    for w := -1; w != v; {
                        w = st[len(st)-1]
                        st = st[:len(st)-1]
                        onStack[w], comp[w] = false, count
                    }
                    count++
                }
                if len(call) > 0 {
                    p := call[len(call)-1]
                    low[p] = min(low[p], low[v])
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return comp
}
### bridges
--idea
In an undirected graph, a bridge is an edge whose removal splits a connected piece in two. A cut point (also called an articulation point) is a node whose removal does the same. They mark the weak spots of a network: the one cable or the one router that, if it fails, cuts the network apart.

DFS finds them with two numbers per node. `tin[v]` is the time `v` was first visited. `low[v]` is the smallest `tin` reachable from the subtree of `v` using any number of tree edges down and then one non-tree edge up. For a tree edge `v -> w` (where `w` is a child), if `low[w] > tin[v]`, then nothing in `w`'s subtree can climb back to `v` or above except through this very edge, so it is a bridge. If `low[w] >= tin[v]`, nothing in `w`'s subtree gets above `v`, so removing `v` cuts `w`'s subtree off: `v` is a cut point (with a special rule for the root).
--build
1. Store every edge with its id: `adj[v]` holds pairs `(neighbour, edge id)`. Ids matter, because two parallel edges between the same nodes are not bridges, and a node-based parent check cannot see that.
2. Make `tin[v] = -1` (unvisited), `low[v]`, `parentEdge[v] = -1`, and `it[v] = 0`. Run an iterative DFS from each unvisited node.
3. On entering `v`: `tin[v] = low[v] = timer`, add one to `timer`.
4. For each `(w, id)` of `v`: if `id == parentEdge[v]`, skip it (the edge we came by). If `w` is unvisited, it is a tree edge: set `parentEdge[w] = id` and go down into `w`. If `w` is visited, it is a back edge: `low[v] = min(low[v], tin[w])`.
5. When `v` is finished and has a parent `p`: set `low[p] = min(low[p], low[v])`. If `low[v] > tin[p]`, the edge `parentEdge[v]` is a bridge. If `low[v] >= tin[p]` and `p` is not the root, `p` is a cut point.
6. The root is a cut point exactly when it has two or more DFS children (they are only connected through the root).
7. Invariant: when `v` finishes, `low[v]` is the earliest time reachable from its subtree through one back edge. A back edge to `p` itself gives `low[v] = tin[p]`, which is not smaller than `tin[p]`, so the tree edge is not a bridge (the cycle through `p` protects it), but `p` is still a cut point.
8. For biconnected components (the groups that stay connected after any single node is removed) see `bcc`.
--think
- Signal: "single point of failure", "critical connection", "which road, if closed, disconnects the town", "edges that are in no cycle", "must-use edge". A bridge is exactly an edge in no cycle.
- Bridge tree: contract each 2-edge-connected component (a component with no bridge) into one node. The bridges join them into a tree. Many problems ("minimum edges to add to make the graph 2-edge-connected") become easy on that tree: the answer is `(leaves + 1) / 2`.
- Parallel edges: with edge ids, a doubled edge is correctly not a bridge. With only a parent node check you would wrongly mark it.
- For a directed graph, the analogue is strongly connected components (`tarjan`), not bridges.
- Offline alternative for small graphs: remove each edge and test connectivity, `O(E (V + E))`. Fine for a few hundred edges and a good way to check your code.
- Pitfalls: recursion depth (the code uses an explicit stack), root handling for cut points, using `low[w] >= tin[v]` for bridges (that is the cut point test; bridges need strict `>`), and self-loops (ignore them; they never matter).
--cost
Time O(V + E), space O(V + E).
--java
// edges[i] = {u, v}. Returns {bridge edge ids, cut point nodes}, each sorted.
static List<List<Integer>> bridgesAndCutPoints(int n, int[][] edges) {
    List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int i = 0; i < edges.length; i++) {
        adj.get(edges[i][0]).add(new int[]{edges[i][1], i});
        adj.get(edges[i][1]).add(new int[]{edges[i][0], i});
    }
    int[] tin = new int[n], low = new int[n], pe = new int[n], it = new int[n], children = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(tin, -1); Arrays.fill(pe, -1);
    boolean[] cut = new boolean[n];
    List<Integer> bridges = new ArrayList<>();
    int timer = 0;
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        if (tin[s] != -1) continue;
        tin[s] = low[s] = timer++;
        ArrayDeque<Integer> call = new ArrayDeque<>();
        call.push(s);
        while (!call.isEmpty()) {
            int v = call.peek();
            if (it[v] < adj.get(v).size()) {
                int[] e = adj.get(v).get(it[v]++);
                if (e[1] == pe[v]) continue;                       // the edge we came by
                if (tin[e[0]] == -1) {                             // tree edge: go down
                    pe[e[0]] = e[1]; tin[e[0]] = low[e[0]] = timer++; call.push(e[0]);
                } else low[v] = Math.min(low[v], tin[e[0]]);       // back edge
            } else {
                call.pop();
                if (call.isEmpty()) continue;                      // v was the root
                int p = call.peek();
                low[p] = Math.min(low[p], low[v]);
                children[p]++;
                if (low[v] > tin[p]) bridges.add(pe[v]);           // nothing climbs past this edge
                if (low[v] >= tin[p] && pe[p] != -1) cut[p] = true;  // p is not the root
            }
        }
        if (children[s] >= 2) cut[s] = true;                       // root rule
    }
    List<Integer> cuts = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (cut[v]) cuts.add(v);
    Collections.sort(bridges);
    return List.of(bridges, cuts);
}
--py
def bridges_and_cut_points(n, edges):
    """edges is a list of (u, v). Returns (sorted bridge edge ids, sorted cut point nodes)."""
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    for i, (u, v) in enumerate(edges):
        adj[u].append((v, i))
        adj[v].append((u, i))
    tin = [-1] * n
    low = [0] * n
    pe = [-1] * n                                     # parent edge id
    it = [0] * n
    children = [0] * n
    cut = [False] * n
    bridges = []
    timer = 0
    for s in range(n):
        if tin[s] != -1:
            continue
        tin[s] = low[s] = timer
        timer += 1
        call = [s]
        while call:
            v = call[-1]
            if it[v] < len(adj[v]):
                w, i = adj[v][it[v]]
                it[v] += 1
                if i == pe[v]:
                    continue                          # the edge we came by
                if tin[w] == -1:                      # tree edge: go down
                    pe[w] = i
                    tin[w] = low[w] = timer
                    timer += 1
                    call.append(w)
                else:
                    low[v] = min(low[v], tin[w])      # back edge
            else:
                call.pop()
                if not call:
                    continue                          # v was the root
                p = call[-1]
                low[p] = min(low[p], low[v])
                children[p] += 1
                if low[v] > tin[p]:
                    bridges.append(pe[v])             # nothing climbs past this edge
                if low[v] >= tin[p] and pe[p] != -1:
                    cut[p] = True                     # p is not the root
        if children[s] >= 2:
            cut[s] = True                             # root rule
    return sorted(bridges), [v for v in range(n) if cut[v]]
--cpp
// edges[i] = {u, v}. Fills bridges (edge ids) and cuts (nodes), both sorted.
void bridgesAndCutPoints(int n, const vector<pair<int,int>>& edges, vector<int>& bridges, vector<int>& cuts) {
    vector<vector<pair<int,int>>> adj(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < (int)edges.size(); i++) {
        adj[edges[i].first].push_back({edges[i].second, i});
        adj[edges[i].second].push_back({edges[i].first, i});
    }
    vector<int> tin(n, -1), low(n, 0), pe(n, -1), it(n, 0), children(n, 0);
    vector<char> cut(n, 0);
    int timer = 0;
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        if (tin[s] != -1) continue;
        tin[s] = low[s] = timer++;
        vector<int> call = {s};
        while (!call.empty()) {
            int v = call.back();
            if (it[v] < (int)adj[v].size()) {
                auto [w, id] = adj[v][it[v]++];
                if (id == pe[v]) continue;                         // the edge we came by
                if (tin[w] == -1) {                                // tree edge: go down
                    pe[w] = id; tin[w] = low[w] = timer++; call.push_back(w);
                } else low[v] = min(low[v], tin[w]);               // back edge
            } else {
                call.pop_back();
                if (call.empty()) continue;                        // v was the root
                int p = call.back();
                low[p] = min(low[p], low[v]);
                children[p]++;
                if (low[v] > tin[p]) bridges.push_back(pe[v]);     // nothing climbs past this edge
                if (low[v] >= tin[p] && pe[p] != -1) cut[p] = 1;   // p is not the root
            }
        }
        if (children[s] >= 2) cut[s] = 1;                          // root rule
    }
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (cut[v]) cuts.push_back(v);
    sort(bridges.begin(), bridges.end());
}
--go
// edges[i] = {u, v}. Returns the bridge edge ids and the cut point nodes, each sorted.
func bridgesAndCutPoints(n int, edges [][2]int) (bridges, cuts []int) {
    type arc struct{ to, id int }
    adj := make([][]arc, n)
    for i, e := range edges {
        adj[e[0]] = append(adj[e[0]], arc{e[1], i})
        adj[e[1]] = append(adj[e[1]], arc{e[0], i})
    }
    tin, low, pe, it, children := make([]int, n), make([]int, n), make([]int, n), make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
    for i := range tin {
        tin[i], pe[i] = -1, -1
    }
    cut := make([]bool, n)
    timer := 0
    for s := 0; s < n; s++ {
        if tin[s] != -1 {
            continue
        }
        tin[s], low[s] = timer, timer
        timer++
        for call := []int{s}; len(call) > 0; {
            v := call[len(call)-1]
            if it[v] < len(adj[v]) {
                e := adj[v][it[v]]
                it[v]++
                if e.id == pe[v] { // the edge we came by
                    continue
                }
                if tin[e.to] == -1 { // tree edge: go down
                    pe[e.to], tin[e.to], low[e.to] = e.id, timer, timer
                    timer++
                    call = append(call, e.to)
                } else { // back edge
                    low[v] = min(low[v], tin[e.to])
                }
            } else {
                call = call[:len(call)-1]
                if len(call) == 0 { // v was the root
                    continue
                }
                p := call[len(call)-1]
                low[p] = min(low[p], low[v])
                children[p]++
                if low[v] > tin[p] { // nothing climbs past this edge
                    bridges = append(bridges, pe[v])
                }
                if low[v] >= tin[p] && pe[p] != -1 { // p is not the root
                    cut[p] = true
                }
            }
        }
        if children[s] >= 2 { // root rule
            cut[s] = true
        }
    }
    for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
        if cut[v] {
            cuts = append(cuts, v)
        }
    }
    sort.Ints(bridges)
    return
}
### bcc
--idea
A biconnected component (BCC, also called a block) is a maximal part of an undirected graph that stays connected when any one node is removed. Equivalent: any two edges in the block lie on a common simple cycle (a loop with no repeated node), and a lone edge that is in no cycle is a block on its own. Blocks are glued together at cut points (nodes whose removal disconnects the graph, see `bridges`). Each edge belongs to exactly one block; a cut point belongs to several.

The algorithm is the bridges DFS plus a stack of edges. Push each edge when the DFS first walks along it. When you return from a child `v` to its parent `p` and find `low[v] >= tin[p]` (nothing below `v` climbs above `p`), then `p` is the top of a block: pop edges off the stack down to the tree edge `(p, v)`. Those edges are exactly that block.
--build
1. Compute everything as in `bridges`: adjacency pairs `(neighbour, edge id)`, `tin`, `low`, `parentEdge`, and an iterative DFS with `it[v]`. Add an empty stack of edge ids `st`, using the stack moves `push`, `peek` and `pop` from code piece `u-stackop`.
2. When the DFS goes down a tree edge `id` to a new node `w`: `push(st, id)`.
3. When the DFS meets a visited node `w` through a non-parent edge `id`, and `tin[w] < tin[v]` (it is an edge up to an ancestor, not a second look at an edge to a descendant): `push(st, id)` and `low[v] = min(low[v], tin[w])`. Skip the edge if `tin[w] > tin[v]`: it was already pushed from the other end.
4. When node `v` finishes and its parent is `p`: `low[p] = min(low[p], low[v])`. Then, if `low[v] >= tin[p]`, start a new block: `pop(st)` ids until you pop `parentEdge[v]`; collect them as one block.
5. When all nodes are done, the collected blocks partition the edges. Isolated nodes (no edges) have no block.
6. Why the pop is right: edges pushed after `parentEdge[v]` were pushed while exploring `v`'s subtree, and `low[v] >= tin[p]` says that subtree is sealed off from everything above `p` except through `p`. So those edges, together with `(p, v)`, form one block whose only link to the rest is the cut point `p`.
7. A bridge appears as a block with a single edge.
--think
- Signal: "after removing any one node the rest stays connected", "split into parts connected through single nodes", "can this network survive one router failing", problems that ask for blocks, or for the block-cut tree.
- The block-cut tree has a node for every block and for every cut point, joining each cut point to the blocks that contain it. It is a tree, and path questions through articulation points ("which nodes must every route from A to B pass through") are answered on it.
- Do not mix with 2-edge-connected components (groups with no bridge). Those are computed from bridges alone, and two such groups can share a node. BCCs are the node-removal version, where blocks share cut points.
- A block with at least three nodes is 2-vertex-connected; a block with two nodes is a bridge.
- Pitfalls: pushing the same edge twice (the `tin[w] < tin[v]` guard), parallel edges (handled by edge ids: the second copy is a back edge), self-loops (skip them), and forgetting that a cut point is in several blocks.
--cost
Time O(V + E), space O(V + E).
--uses
u-stackop
--java
// edges[i] = {u, v}. Returns the blocks, each a list of edge ids.
static List<List<Integer>> biconnected(int n, int[][] edges) {
    List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int i = 0; i < edges.length; i++) {
        adj.get(edges[i][0]).add(new int[]{edges[i][1], i});
        adj.get(edges[i][1]).add(new int[]{edges[i][0], i});
    }
    int[] tin = new int[n], low = new int[n], pe = new int[n], it = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(tin, -1); Arrays.fill(pe, -1);
    Deque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();                // stack of edge ids
    List<List<Integer>> blocks = new ArrayList<>();
    int timer = 0;
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        if (tin[s] != -1) continue;
        tin[s] = low[s] = timer++;
        ArrayDeque<Integer> call = new ArrayDeque<>();
        call.push(s);
        while (!call.isEmpty()) {
            int v = call.peek();
            if (it[v] < adj.get(v).size()) {
                int[] e = adj.get(v).get(it[v]++);
                if (e[1] == pe[v]) continue;
                if (tin[e[0]] == -1) {                             // tree edge
                    pe[e[0]] = e[1]; tin[e[0]] = low[e[0]] = timer++;
                    push(st, e[1]); call.push(e[0]);
                } else if (tin[e[0]] < tin[v]) {                   // edge up to an ancestor
                    push(st, e[1]);
                    low[v] = Math.min(low[v], tin[e[0]]);
                }
            } else {
                call.pop();
                if (call.isEmpty()) continue;
                int p = call.peek();
                low[p] = Math.min(low[p], low[v]);
                if (low[v] >= tin[p]) {                            // p closes a block
                    List<Integer> block = new ArrayList<>();
                    int id;
                    do { id = pop(st); block.add(id); } while (id != pe[v]);
                    blocks.add(block);
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return blocks;
}
--py
def biconnected(n, edges):
    """edges is a list of (u, v). Returns the blocks, each a list of edge ids."""
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    for i, (u, v) in enumerate(edges):
        adj[u].append((v, i))
        adj[v].append((u, i))
    tin = [-1] * n
    low = [0] * n
    pe = [-1] * n
    it = [0] * n
    st = []                                           # stack of edge ids
    blocks = []
    timer = 0
    for s in range(n):
        if tin[s] != -1:
            continue
        tin[s] = low[s] = timer
        timer += 1
        call = [s]
        while call:
            v = call[-1]
            if it[v] < len(adj[v]):
                w, i = adj[v][it[v]]
                it[v] += 1
                if i == pe[v]:
                    continue
                if tin[w] == -1:                      # tree edge
                    pe[w] = i
                    tin[w] = low[w] = timer
                    timer += 1
                    push(st, i)
                    call.append(w)
                elif tin[w] < tin[v]:                 # edge up to an ancestor
                    push(st, i)
                    low[v] = min(low[v], tin[w])
            else:
                call.pop()
                if not call:
                    continue
                p = call[-1]
                low[p] = min(low[p], low[v])
                if low[v] >= tin[p]:                  # p closes a block
                    block = []
                    while True:
                        i = pop(st)
                        block.append(i)
                        if i == pe[v]:
                            break
                    blocks.append(block)
    return blocks
--cpp
// edges[i] = {u, v}. Returns the blocks, each a list of edge ids.
vector<vector<int>> biconnected(int n, const vector<pair<int,int>>& edges) {
    vector<vector<pair<int,int>>> adj(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < (int)edges.size(); i++) {
        adj[edges[i].first].push_back({edges[i].second, i});
        adj[edges[i].second].push_back({edges[i].first, i});
    }
    vector<int> tin(n, -1), low(n, 0), pe(n, -1), it(n, 0);
    stack<int> st;                                         // stack of edge ids
    vector<vector<int>> blocks;
    int timer = 0;
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        if (tin[s] != -1) continue;
        tin[s] = low[s] = timer++;
        vector<int> call = {s};
        while (!call.empty()) {
            int v = call.back();
            if (it[v] < (int)adj[v].size()) {
                auto [w, id] = adj[v][it[v]++];
                if (id == pe[v]) continue;
                if (tin[w] == -1) {                        // tree edge
                    pe[w] = id; tin[w] = low[w] = timer++;
                    push(st, id); call.push_back(w);
                } else if (tin[w] < tin[v]) {              // edge up to an ancestor
                    push(st, id);
                    low[v] = min(low[v], tin[w]);
                }
            } else {
                call.pop_back();
                if (call.empty()) continue;
                int p = call.back();
                low[p] = min(low[p], low[v]);
                if (low[v] >= tin[p]) {                    // p closes a block
                    vector<int> block;
                    int id;
                    do { id = pop(st); block.push_back(id); } while (id != pe[v]);
                    blocks.push_back(block);
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return blocks;
}
--go
// edges[i] = {u, v}. Returns the blocks, each a list of edge ids.
func biconnected(n int, edges [][2]int) [][]int {
    type arc struct{ to, id int }
    adj := make([][]arc, n)
    for i, e := range edges {
        adj[e[0]] = append(adj[e[0]], arc{e[1], i})
        adj[e[1]] = append(adj[e[1]], arc{e[0], i})
    }
    tin, low, pe, it := make([]int, n), make([]int, n), make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
    for i := range tin {
        tin[i], pe[i] = -1, -1
    }
    var st []int // stack of edge ids
    var blocks [][]int
    timer := 0
    for s := 0; s < n; s++ {
        if tin[s] != -1 {
            continue
        }
        tin[s], low[s] = timer, timer
        timer++
        for call := []int{s}; len(call) > 0; {
            v := call[len(call)-1]
            if it[v] < len(adj[v]) {
                e := adj[v][it[v]]
                it[v]++
                if e.id == pe[v] {
                    continue
                }
                if tin[e.to] == -1 { // tree edge
                    pe[e.to], tin[e.to], low[e.to] = e.id, timer, timer
                    timer++
                    push(&st, e.id) // piece
                    call = append(call, e.to)
                } else if tin[e.to] < tin[v] { // edge up to an ancestor
                    push(&st, e.id)
                    low[v] = min(low[v], tin[e.to])
                }
            } else {
                call = call[:len(call)-1]
                if len(call) == 0 {
                    continue
                }
                p := call[len(call)-1]
                low[p] = min(low[p], low[v])
                if low[v] >= tin[p] { // p closes a block
                    var block []int
                    for id := -1; id != pe[v]; {
                        id = pop(&st) // piece
                        block = append(block, id)
                    }
                    blocks = append(blocks, block)
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return blocks
}
### twosat
--idea
2-SAT asks: given yes-or-no variables and a list of conditions that each involve two of them ("`a` or `b`", "not `a` or `c`", and so on), can every condition be true at once? It is the easy cousin of general satisfiability: with two choices per clause there is a fast exact algorithm, while with three it becomes NP-hard.

The key idea is that a clause `(a OR b)` is the same as two implications: if `a` is false then `b` must be true, and if `b` is false then `a` must be true. Draw a graph with one node for every literal (a variable `x`, and its negation not-`x`), and an arrow for each implication. Choosing a literal forces everything it points to. A contradiction appears exactly when `x` and not-`x` can each reach the other, which is when they sit in the same strongly connected component (SCC, see `scc`).
--build
1. Number literals: variable `x` gives node `2x` for "x is true" and `2x + 1` for "x is false". The negation of literal `l` is `l xor 1`.
2. For each clause `(a OR b)` (two literals), add the arrows `not a -> b` and `not b -> a`. A clause with one literal `a` is `(a OR a)`: arrow `not a -> a`.
3. Compute the SCCs of this graph of `2n` nodes with `kosaraju` (standard form from that lesson). Its ids are in topological order: every arrow goes from a lower id to a higher or equal id.
4. If some variable has `comp[2x] == comp[2x + 1]`, then `x` implies not-`x` and not-`x` implies `x`: the formula has no solution. Return none.
5. Otherwise build an assignment: `x` is true if `comp[2x] > comp[2x + 1]`, that is, if "x true" comes later in the topological order than "x false".
6. Why this assignment satisfies everything: suppose an arrow `p -> q` exists with `p` true. If we choose literals that come later in the order, then `q` comes at or after `p`, so `q` is also chosen. Together with the symmetric arrow `not q -> not p`, no implication can go from a chosen literal to an unchosen one, so every clause holds.
7. Why unsatisfiable when `x` and not-`x` share an SCC: each implies the other through arrows, so whatever value `x` has, it forces its opposite.
--think
- Signal: pairwise constraints on yes-or-no choices: "either A or B must be chosen", "A and B cannot both be chosen" (that is `not A or not B`), "if A then B" (`not A or B`), scheduling with two slots per item, "each pair of friends must go to different teams" (colouring with two colours, also `bipartite`), assigning each item one of two options where only conflicts between pairs matter.
- Typical modelling: each item has two options, so one variable; each conflict between two options of two items becomes a clause `not (option_i and option_j)` = `(not option_i or not option_j)`.
- Compare: if constraints involve three variables at once (3-SAT), it is NP-hard and this method does not apply. If there are no "or" mixing and everything is "same" or "different", plain two-colouring is enough.
- To count solutions, 2-SAT does not help (counting is hard). To get the smallest or lexicographically first solution you need a more careful search with unit propagation.
- With Tarjan's algorithm (`tarjan`) the ids come in reverse topological order, so flip the comparison to `comp[2x] < comp[2x + 1]`.
- Pitfalls: forgetting the second implication of each clause, mixing literal numbering (`2x` and `2x + 1`), and reading the comparison the wrong way round for your SCC algorithm's ordering.
--cost
Time O(n + m) for `n` variables and `m` clauses, space O(n + m).
--java
// n variables; literal 2x = "x is true", 2x+1 = "x is false". clauses[i] = {a, b} means (a OR b).
// Uses kosaraju from the kosaraju lesson. Returns the assignment, or null if unsatisfiable.
static boolean[] twoSat(int n, int[][] clauses) {
    List<List<Integer>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < 2 * n; i++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int[] c : clauses) {
        adj.get(c[0] ^ 1).add(c[1]);                       // not a -> b
        adj.get(c[1] ^ 1).add(c[0]);                       // not b -> a
    }
    int[] comp = kosaraju(adj);                            // ids in topological order
    boolean[] value = new boolean[n];
    for (int x = 0; x < n; x++) {
        if (comp[2 * x] == comp[2 * x + 1]) return null;   // x and not-x imply each other
        value[x] = comp[2 * x] > comp[2 * x + 1];          // pick the literal that comes later
    }
    return value;
}
--py
def two_sat(n, clauses):
    """n variables; literal 2x = 'x is true', 2x+1 = 'x is false'. clauses is a list of (a, b) meaning (a OR b).
    Uses kosaraju from the kosaraju lesson. Returns the assignment, or None if unsatisfiable."""
    adj = [[] for _ in range(2 * n)]
    for a, b in clauses:
        adj[a ^ 1].append(b)                      # not a -> b
        adj[b ^ 1].append(a)                      # not b -> a
    comp, _ = kosaraju(adj)                       # ids in topological order
    value = []
    for x in range(n):
        if comp[2 * x] == comp[2 * x + 1]:
            return None                           # x and not-x imply each other
        value.append(comp[2 * x] > comp[2 * x + 1])    # pick the literal that comes later
    return value
--cpp
// n variables; literal 2x = "x is true", 2x+1 = "x is false". clauses[i] = {a, b} means (a OR b).
// Uses kosaraju from the kosaraju lesson. Returns the assignment, or an empty vector if unsatisfiable.
vector<char> twoSat(int n, const vector<pair<int,int>>& clauses) {
    vector<vector<int>> adj(2 * n);
    for (auto [a, b] : clauses) {
        adj[a ^ 1].push_back(b);                           // not a -> b
        adj[b ^ 1].push_back(a);                           // not b -> a
    }
    vector<int> comp = kosaraju(adj);                      // ids in topological order
    vector<char> value(n);
    for (int x = 0; x < n; x++) {
        if (comp[2 * x] == comp[2 * x + 1]) return {};     // x and not-x imply each other
        value[x] = comp[2 * x] > comp[2 * x + 1];          // pick the literal that comes later
    }
    return value;
}
--go
// n variables; literal 2x = "x is true", 2x+1 = "x is false". clauses[i] = {a, b} means (a OR b).
// Uses kosaraju from the kosaraju lesson. Returns the assignment, or nil if unsatisfiable.
func twoSat(n int, clauses [][2]int) []bool {
    adj := make([][]int, 2*n)
    for _, c := range clauses {
        adj[c[0]^1] = append(adj[c[0]^1], c[1]) // not a -> b
        adj[c[1]^1] = append(adj[c[1]^1], c[0]) // not b -> a
    }
    comp := kosaraju(adj) // ids in topological order
    value := make([]bool, n)
    for x := 0; x < n; x++ {
        if comp[2*x] == comp[2*x+1] { // x and not-x imply each other
            return nil
        }
        value[x] = comp[2*x] > comp[2*x+1] // pick the literal that comes later
    }
    return value
}
### dinic
--idea
Dinic's algorithm computes maximum flow (see `flow`) much faster than finding one path at a time. It works in phases. In each phase it first runs a BFS from the source over edges that still have spare capacity and labels every node with its level, the number of edges from the source. Then it sends flow only along edges that go from level `d` to level `d + 1`, which is called the level graph. It keeps pushing along such paths until no more can be pushed in this level graph (a blocking flow), then starts a new phase.

Each phase makes the shortest source-to-sink distance in the residual graph strictly larger, and that distance can be at most `V`, so there are at most `V` phases. Within a phase, a pointer per node remembers which outgoing edge to try next, so a dead-end edge is never examined twice.
--build
1. Store the graph as paired edges: `addEdge(u, v, c)` appends an edge `u -> v` with capacity `c` at an even index `e`, and its reverse edge `v -> u` with capacity 0 at index `e + 1` (so the reverse of `e` is `e xor 1`). Each node keeps a list of its edge indexes.
2. Phase: run BFS from `s` over edges with `cap[e] > 0`, filling `level[v]`. If `t` was not reached, stop: the total pushed so far is the maximum flow.
3. Set `it[v] = 0` for every node (the current-edge pointer).
4. `dfs(v, f)` tries to push up to `f` units from `v` to `t`. If `v == t`, return `f`. Otherwise look at edges from `it[v]` on: take edge `e` to `w` only if `cap[e] > 0` and `level[w] == level[v] + 1`. Ask `d = dfs(w, min(f, cap[e]))`. If `d > 0`: `cap[e] -= d`, `cap[e xor 1] += d`, return `d`. If the edge fails, advance `it[v]` past it for good. If no edge works, return 0.
5. Call `dfs(s, infinity)` repeatedly and add what it returns to the total, until it returns 0. That ends the phase; go back to step 2.
6. Invariant: the pointer only moves forward, and an edge is skipped only when it cannot carry flow in this phase (zero capacity, wrong level, or its far end is a dead end). Cap changes within a phase only lower forward edges and raise reverse edges that point back a level, which are not usable in the level graph, so a skipped edge never becomes useful again in the same phase.
7. The answer is the sum of everything pushed. The min cut (`mincut`) is the set reachable from `s` in the last BFS.
--think
- Signal: max flow or bipartite matching on thousands of nodes and tens of thousands of edges, where the simple augmenting path method is too slow.
- Dinic is the default choice for contests. On unit-capacity graphs (such as bipartite matching) it runs in O(E times the square root of V), the same as Hopcroft-Karp (`hopcroft`).
- Push-relabel (`pushrelabel`) is the alternative for very dense graphs. Edmonds-Karp (`flow`) is the simplest to write but slower.
- Modelling is the real work: split nodes to limit flow through a node, add a super-source and super-sink to join many sources and sinks, give edges capacity 1 for "use at most once". See `flow` for those patterns. For minimum cost, see `mcmf`. For edges that must carry at least some flow, see `lowerflow`.
- Python recursion depth equals the path length in the level graph, which is at most `V`. Raise the limit for large graphs, or write the depth-first search with an explicit stack.
- Pitfalls: forgetting the reverse edge (index `e xor 1` needs paired storage), resetting the pointers `it` only once rather than per phase, and using `int` capacities when the total can exceed 2 billion.
--cost
Time O(V squared times E) in general, O(E times the square root of V) for unit-capacity bipartite graphs, and fast in practice. Space O(V + E).
--java
static class Dinic {
    int n, cnt = 0;
    int[] to, level, it;
    long[] cap;
    List<List<Integer>> g = new ArrayList<>();

    Dinic(int n, int maxEdges) {                        // room for maxEdges edges plus their reverses
        this.n = n;
        to = new int[2 * maxEdges]; cap = new long[2 * maxEdges];
        level = new int[n]; it = new int[n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) g.add(new ArrayList<>());
    }

    void addEdge(int u, int v, long c) {
        g.get(u).add(cnt); to[cnt] = v; cap[cnt++] = c;         // forward edge, even index
        g.get(v).add(cnt); to[cnt] = u; cap[cnt++] = 0;         // reverse edge, index xor 1
    }

    boolean bfs(int s, int t) {                         // levels over edges with spare capacity
        Arrays.fill(level, -1);
        level[s] = 0;
        ArrayDeque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
        q.add(s);
        while (!q.isEmpty()) {
            int v = q.poll();
            for (int e : g.get(v))
                if (cap[e] > 0 && level[to[e]] < 0) { level[to[e]] = level[v] + 1; q.add(to[e]); }
        }
        return level[t] >= 0;
    }

    long dfs(int v, int t, long f) {                    // push one path along the level graph
        if (v == t) return f;
        for (; it[v] < g.get(v).size(); it[v]++) {      // current-edge pointer: never retry a dead edge
            int e = g.get(v).get(it[v]), w = to[e];
            if (cap[e] > 0 && level[w] == level[v] + 1) {
                long d = dfs(w, t, Math.min(f, cap[e]));
                if (d > 0) { cap[e] -= d; cap[e ^ 1] += d; return d; }
            }
        }
        return 0;
    }

    long maxFlow(int s, int t) {
        if (s == t) return 0;                           // otherwise the first path is endless
        long total = 0;
        while (bfs(s, t)) {                             // one phase per BFS
            Arrays.fill(it, 0);
            long f;
            while ((f = dfs(s, t, Long.MAX_VALUE)) > 0) total += f;
        }
        return total;
    }
}
--py
import sys

class Dinic:
    def __init__(self, n):
        self.n = n
        self.g = [[] for _ in range(n)]                 # edge indexes per node
        self.to = []
        self.cap = []
        sys.setrecursionlimit(10000 + 4 * n)

    def add_edge(self, u, v, c):
        self.g[u].append(len(self.to)); self.to.append(v); self.cap.append(c)    # forward edge, even index
        self.g[v].append(len(self.to)); self.to.append(u); self.cap.append(0)    # reverse edge, index xor 1

    def _bfs(self, s, t):                               # levels over edges with spare capacity
        self.level = [-1] * self.n
        self.level[s] = 0
        q = deque([s])
        while q:
            v = q.popleft()
            for e in self.g[v]:
                if self.cap[e] > 0 and self.level[self.to[e]] < 0:
                    self.level[self.to[e]] = self.level[v] + 1
                    q.append(self.to[e])
        return self.level[t] >= 0

    def _dfs(self, v, t, f):                            # push one path along the level graph
        if v == t:
            return f
        while self.it[v] < len(self.g[v]):              # current-edge pointer: never retry a dead edge
            e = self.g[v][self.it[v]]
            w = self.to[e]
            if self.cap[e] > 0 and self.level[w] == self.level[v] + 1:
                d = self._dfs(w, t, min(f, self.cap[e]))
                if d > 0:
                    self.cap[e] -= d
                    self.cap[e ^ 1] += d
                    return d
            self.it[v] += 1
        return 0

    def max_flow(self, s, t):
        total = 0
        while self._bfs(s, t):                          # one phase per BFS
            self.it = [0] * self.n
            f = self._dfs(s, t, float("inf"))
            while f > 0:
                total += f
                f = self._dfs(s, t, float("inf"))
        return total
--cpp
struct Dinic {
    int n;
    vector<vector<int>> g;                              // edge indexes per node
    vector<int> to, level, it;
    vector<long long> cap;

    Dinic(int n) : n(n), g(n), level(n), it(n) {}

    void addEdge(int u, int v, long long c) {
        g[u].push_back(to.size()); to.push_back(v); cap.push_back(c);   // forward edge, even index
        g[v].push_back(to.size()); to.push_back(u); cap.push_back(0);   // reverse edge, index xor 1
    }

    bool bfs(int s, int t) {                            // levels over edges with spare capacity
        fill(level.begin(), level.end(), -1);
        level[s] = 0;
        queue<int> q;
        q.push(s);
        while (!q.empty()) {
            int v = q.front(); q.pop();
            for (int e : g[v])
                if (cap[e] > 0 && level[to[e]] < 0) { level[to[e]] = level[v] + 1; q.push(to[e]); }
        }
        return level[t] >= 0;
    }

    long long dfs(int v, int t, long long f) {          // push one path along the level graph
        if (v == t) return f;
        for (int& i = it[v]; i < (int)g[v].size(); i++) {   // current-edge pointer: never retry a dead edge
            int e = g[v][i], w = to[e];
            if (cap[e] > 0 && level[w] == level[v] + 1) {
                long long d = dfs(w, t, min(f, cap[e]));
                if (d > 0) { cap[e] -= d; cap[e ^ 1] += d; return d; }
            }
        }
        return 0;
    }

    long long maxFlow(int s, int t) {
        long long total = 0;
        while (bfs(s, t)) {                             // one phase per BFS
            fill(it.begin(), it.end(), 0);
            while (long long f = dfs(s, t, LLONG_MAX)) total += f;
        }
        return total;
    }
};
--go
type Dinic struct {
    n, cnt int
    to     []int
    capa   []int
    level  []int
    it     []int
    g      [][]int // g[v]: ids of edges leaving v
}

func newDinic(n, maxEdges int) *Dinic { // room for maxEdges edges plus their reverses
    return &Dinic{n: n, to: make([]int, 2*maxEdges), capa: make([]int, 2*maxEdges),
        level: make([]int, n), it: make([]int, n), g: make([][]int, n)}
}

func (d *Dinic) addEdge(u, v, c int) {
    d.g[u] = append(d.g[u], d.cnt) // forward edge, even index
    d.to[d.cnt], d.capa[d.cnt] = v, c
    d.cnt++
    d.g[v] = append(d.g[v], d.cnt) // reverse edge, index xor 1
    d.to[d.cnt], d.capa[d.cnt] = u, 0
    d.cnt++
}

func (d *Dinic) bfs(s, t int) bool { // levels over edges with spare capacity
    for i := range d.level {
        d.level[i] = -1
    }
    d.level[s] = 0
    for q := []int{s}; len(q) > 0; q = q[1:] {
        v := q[0]
        for _, e := range d.g[v] {
            if d.capa[e] > 0 && d.level[d.to[e]] < 0 {
                d.level[d.to[e]] = d.level[v] + 1
                q = append(q, d.to[e])
            }
        }
    }
    return d.level[t] >= 0
}

func (d *Dinic) dfs(v, t, f int) int { // push one path along the level graph
    if v == t {
        return f
    }
    for ; d.it[v] < len(d.g[v]); d.it[v]++ { // current-edge pointer: never retry a dead edge
        e := d.g[v][d.it[v]]
        w := d.to[e]
        if d.capa[e] > 0 && d.level[w] == d.level[v]+1 {
            if x := d.dfs(w, t, min(f, d.capa[e])); x > 0 {
                d.capa[e] -= x
                d.capa[e^1] += x
                return x
            }
        }
    }
    return 0
}

func (d *Dinic) maxFlow(s, t int) int {
    if s == t { // otherwise the first path is endless
        return 0
    }
    total := 0
    for d.bfs(s, t) { // one phase per BFS
        for i := range d.it {
            d.it[i] = 0
        }
        for f := d.dfs(s, t, 1<<62); f > 0; f = d.dfs(s, t, 1<<62) {
            total += f
        }
    }
    return total
}
### pushrelabel
--idea
Push-relabel computes maximum flow in a different style from augmenting paths. Instead of finding a whole path from the source to the sink, it works locally. It first floods: every edge out of the source is filled to capacity, so the neighbours of the source hold extra material, called excess. Then nodes with excess try to pass it on, one edge at a time, and the material gradually moves toward the sink.

Each node has a height. Material may only be pushed downhill, along a residual edge to a node exactly one lower. The source starts at height `n` (above everything), the sink at 0, everything else at 0. If a node holds excess but has no downhill residual edge, it is stuck, so it is relabelled: raised to just above its lowest residual neighbour. When no node except the source and the sink holds excess, material that could not reach the sink has been sent back to the source, and the amount in the sink is the maximum flow.
--build
1. Keep a residual capacity table `res[u][v]` (a copy of the capacities), the height `h[v]`, and the excess `ex[v]`. Set `h[s] = n`, everything else 0.
2. Preflow: for every neighbour `v` of the source, push the whole capacity: `res[s][v] -= c`, `res[v][s] += c`, `ex[v] += c`. Every such `v` (except the sink) goes on a queue of active nodes (`enqueue`, code piece `u-queueop`).
3. Take an active node: `u = dequeue(q)`.
4. While `ex[u] > 0`: scan every `v`. If `res[u][v] > 0` and `h[u] == h[v] + 1`, push `d = min(ex[u], res[u][v])`: `res[u][v] -= d`, `res[v][u] += d`, `ex[u] -= d`, `ex[v] += d`. If `v` is neither the source nor the sink and was not active, `enqueue(q, v)`.
5. If after the scan `ex[u]` is still positive, relabel: `h[u] = 1 + min(h[v])` over all `v` with `res[u][v] > 0`. Then scan again.
6. When the queue is empty, `ex[t]` is the maximum flow.
7. Invariant: for every residual edge `(u, v)`, `h[u] <= h[v] + 1`. A push moves material only to a node exactly one lower, and a relabel raises `h[u]` only as far as that rule permits. Because the source stays at height `n` and a simple path has fewer than `n` edges, no residual path from source to sink exists at the end, so the flow is maximum.
8. This FIFO version (first in, first out queue of active nodes) runs in O(V cubed). Better orders (highest label first) and gap heuristics make it faster.
--think
- Signal: max flow on dense graphs, or when graphs are too big for path-based methods. In contests Dinic (`dinic`) is usually enough; push-relabel with the highest-label rule and the gap heuristic is among the fastest known in practice.
- It only keeps a preflow (flow that may hold excess at nodes) during the run, and it ends with a valid flow only for the sink side. To get the actual flow values per edge, run a second phase that returns the stuck excess to the source, or read it as `capacity - res` for edges where flow reached the sink. For just the value, `ex[t]` suffices.
- The local nature makes it good for parallel and hardware implementations.
- Think of heights as a guess of the distance to the sink. Relabelling is an estimate update, like in A* (`astar`).
- Pitfalls: queueing the source or sink as active, forgetting to re-queue after relabel (the loop above stays on `u` until its excess is gone), heights that never stop growing when the code mishandles the source, and `res` mutated in the caller's table (copy it).
--cost
Time O(V cubed) for this FIFO version with a matrix; O(V squared times the square root of E) with the highest-label rule. Space O(V squared).
--uses
u-queueop
--java
// cap[u][v]: capacity. Returns the maximum flow from s to t. The table is not modified.
static long pushRelabel(long[][] cap, int s, int t) {
    int n = cap.length;
    if (s == t) return 0;
    long[][] res = new long[n][];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) res[i] = cap[i].clone();
    int[] h = new int[n];
    long[] ex = new long[n];
    boolean[] active = new boolean[n];
    Deque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    h[s] = n;
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {                       // preflow: saturate every edge out of s
        long c = res[s][v];
        if (c <= 0) continue;
        res[s][v] -= c; res[v][s] += c; ex[v] += c;
        if (v != t && v != s && !active[v]) { enqueue(q, v); active[v] = true; }
    }
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int u = dequeue(q);
        active[u] = false;
        while (ex[u] > 0) {
            for (int v = 0; v < n && ex[u] > 0; v++) {
                if (res[u][v] > 0 && h[u] == h[v] + 1) {          // push downhill
                    long d = Math.min(ex[u], res[u][v]);
                    res[u][v] -= d; res[v][u] += d; ex[u] -= d; ex[v] += d;
                    if (v != s && v != t && !active[v]) { enqueue(q, v); active[v] = true; }
                }
            }
            if (ex[u] > 0) {                                      // stuck: relabel
                int low = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
                for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (res[u][v] > 0) low = Math.min(low, h[v]);
                h[u] = low + 1;
            }
        }
    }
    return ex[t];
}
--py
def push_relabel(cap, s, t):
    """cap[u][v]: capacity. Returns the maximum flow from s to t. The table is not modified."""
    n = len(cap)
    if s == t:
        return 0
    res = [row[:] for row in cap]
    h = [0] * n
    ex = [0] * n
    active = [False] * n
    q = deque()
    h[s] = n
    for v in range(n):                                  # preflow: saturate every edge out of s
        c = res[s][v]
        if c <= 0:
            continue
        res[s][v] -= c
        res[v][s] += c
        ex[v] += c
        if v != t and v != s and not active[v]:
            enqueue(q, v)
            active[v] = True
    while q:
        u = dequeue(q)
        active[u] = False
        while ex[u] > 0:
            for v in range(n):
                if ex[u] == 0:
                    break
                if res[u][v] > 0 and h[u] == h[v] + 1:           # push downhill
                    d = min(ex[u], res[u][v])
                    res[u][v] -= d
                    res[v][u] += d
                    ex[u] -= d
                    ex[v] += d
                    if v != s and v != t and not active[v]:
                        enqueue(q, v)
                        active[v] = True
            if ex[u] > 0:                                        # stuck: relabel
                h[u] = 1 + min(h[v] for v in range(n) if res[u][v] > 0)
    return ex[t]
--cpp
// cap[u][v]: capacity. Returns the maximum flow from s to t. The table is not modified.
long long pushRelabel(vector<vector<long long>> res, int s, int t) {
    int n = res.size();
    if (s == t) return 0;
    vector<int> h(n, 0);
    vector<long long> ex(n, 0);
    vector<char> active(n, 0);
    queue<int> q;
    h[s] = n;
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {                       // preflow: saturate every edge out of s
        long long c = res[s][v];
        if (c <= 0) continue;
        res[s][v] -= c; res[v][s] += c; ex[v] += c;
        if (v != t && v != s && !active[v]) { enqueue(q, v); active[v] = 1; }
    }
    while (!q.empty()) {
        int u = dequeue(q);
        active[u] = 0;
        while (ex[u] > 0) {
            for (int v = 0; v < n && ex[u] > 0; v++) {
                if (res[u][v] > 0 && h[u] == h[v] + 1) {         // push downhill
                    long long d = min(ex[u], res[u][v]);
                    res[u][v] -= d; res[v][u] += d; ex[u] -= d; ex[v] += d;
                    if (v != s && v != t && !active[v]) { enqueue(q, v); active[v] = 1; }
                }
            }
            if (ex[u] > 0) {                                     // stuck: relabel
                int low = INT_MAX;
                for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (res[u][v] > 0) low = min(low, h[v]);
                h[u] = low + 1;
            }
        }
    }
    return ex[t];
}
--go
// capa[u][v]: capacity. Returns the maximum flow from s to t. The table is not modified.
func pushRelabel(capa [][]int, s, t int) int {
    n := len(capa)
    if s == t {
        return 0
    }
    res := make([][]int, n)
    for i := range res {
        res[i] = append([]int(nil), capa[i]...)
    }
    h, ex := make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
    active := make([]bool, n)
    var q []int
    h[s] = n
    for v := 0; v < n; v++ { // preflow: saturate every edge out of s
        c := res[s][v]
        if c <= 0 {
            continue
        }
        res[s][v] -= c
        res[v][s] += c
        ex[v] += c
        if v != t && v != s && !active[v] {
            enqueue(&q, v) // piece
            active[v] = true
        }
    }
    for len(q) > 0 {
        u := dequeue(&q) // piece
        active[u] = false
        for ex[u] > 0 {
            for v := 0; v < n && ex[u] > 0; v++ {
                if res[u][v] > 0 && h[u] == h[v]+1 { // push downhill
                    d := min(ex[u], res[u][v])
                    res[u][v] -= d
                    res[v][u] += d
                    ex[u] -= d
                    ex[v] += d
                    if v != s && v != t && !active[v] {
                        enqueue(&q, v)
                        active[v] = true
                    }
                }
            }
            if ex[u] > 0 { // stuck: relabel
                low := 1 << 62
                for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
                    if res[u][v] > 0 {
                        low = min(low, h[v])
                    }
                }
                h[u] = low + 1
            }
        }
    }
    return ex[t]
}
### mcmf
--idea
Min-cost max-flow sends the largest possible amount of flow from a source to a sink, and among all ways to send that amount picks the cheapest. Every edge now has a capacity and a cost per unit of flow. Think of shipping goods: roads have a limit on how many trucks fit and a price per truck, and you want everything delivered at the lowest total price.

The method is successive shortest paths. Repeatedly find the cheapest path from the source to the sink in the residual graph (see `flow`) and push as much as fits along it. A reverse edge carries the negative of the cost, because undoing flow refunds the money. Since costs can be negative there, the path search must be Bellman-Ford style (or SPFA), not plain Dijkstra. Always taking the cheapest augmenting path keeps the flow cheapest for its current size, and when no path is left the flow is maximum.
--build
1. Store paired edges as in Dinic: `addEdge(u, v, cap, cost)` adds `u -> v` with the capacity and cost at an even index `e`, and the reverse `v -> u` with capacity 0 and cost `-cost` at `e xor 1`.
2. Repeat: set `dist[v] = INF` for all `v` and `dist[s] = 0`. Find the cheapest path to every node over edges with `cap > 0`, using a queue of changed nodes (SPFA, see `spfa`). For each edge `e` from `u` to `w`, call `relax(dist, parent, u, w, cost[e])` (code piece `u-relax`). If it returns true, also remember `pe[w] = e`, the edge used to reach `w`.
3. If `dist[t]` is still `INF`, no augmenting path exists: stop.
4. Otherwise walk back from `t` along `pe` to find the bottleneck, the smallest `cap` on the path. Push that much: for each edge `e` on the path `cap[e] -= f` and `cap[e xor 1] += f`.
5. Add `f` to the total flow and `f * dist[t]` to the total cost.
6. Repeat from step 2 until no path is left. Return `(flow, cost)`.
7. Invariant: at every moment the current flow has the least cost among all flows of its size. That holds because the residual graph has no negative cycle (a negative cycle would mean you could lower cost by rerouting without changing the flow size), and pushing along a shortest path cannot create one.
8. To stop at a given amount of flow instead of the maximum, cap the pushed amount so that the total never exceeds the target.
--think
- Signal: "cheapest way to send `k` units", "assign workers to jobs at minimum total cost" (this contains the assignment problem, see `hungarian`), "transportation problem", "minimum cost circulation", "k edge-disjoint paths with minimum total length". Anything where max flow fits but each edge also has a price.
- Matching variant: source to workers with capacity 1 and cost 0, workers to jobs with capacity 1 and the job cost, jobs to sink with capacity 1. The result is the minimum cost perfect matching.
- With negative edge costs in the original graph (but no negative cycle), the same method works because the search tolerates negative edges.
- For large graphs, use Dijkstra with potentials (see `johnson`) instead of SPFA, so each round costs O(E log V). The reweighting keeps costs non-negative.
- Cost with unlimited flow of negative total: stop when `dist[t] >= 0` if you want minimum cost over any flow size, not necessarily the maximum.
- Pitfalls: forgetting to give the reverse edge a negative cost, mixing up the cost of an edge with the cost of a path, `int` overflow (costs times flows), and negative cycles in the input (not allowed).
--cost
Time O(F times (V times E)) in the worst case for SPFA, where F is the total flow, since each augmentation takes one search. Space O(V + E).
--uses
u-relax
--java
static class MinCostFlow {
    int n, cnt = 0;
    int[] to, pe;
    long[] cap, cost;
    List<List<Integer>> g = new ArrayList<>();

    MinCostFlow(int n, int maxEdges) {
        this.n = n;
        to = new int[2 * maxEdges]; cap = new long[2 * maxEdges]; cost = new long[2 * maxEdges];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) g.add(new ArrayList<>());
    }

    void addEdge(int u, int v, long c, long w) {
        g.get(u).add(cnt); to[cnt] = v; cap[cnt] = c; cost[cnt++] = w;       // forward edge
        g.get(v).add(cnt); to[cnt] = u; cap[cnt] = 0; cost[cnt++] = -w;      // reverse edge refunds
    }

    // returns {max flow, min cost}
    long[] run(int s, int t) {
        final long INF = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4;
        long flow = 0, total = 0;
        while (true) {
            long[] dist = new long[n];
            int[] parent = new int[n];
            pe = new int[n];
            Arrays.fill(dist, INF); Arrays.fill(parent, -1); Arrays.fill(pe, -1);
            boolean[] inQ = new boolean[n];
            ArrayDeque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
            dist[s] = 0; q.add(s); inQ[s] = true;
            while (!q.isEmpty()) {                                          // SPFA over the residual graph
                int u = q.poll();
                inQ[u] = false;
                for (int e : g.get(u)) {
                    if (cap[e] > 0 && relax(dist, parent, u, to[e], cost[e])) {   // piece
                        pe[to[e]] = e;
                        if (!inQ[to[e]]) { q.add(to[e]); inQ[to[e]] = true; }
                    }
                }
            }
            if (dist[t] >= INF) return new long[]{flow, total};             // no augmenting path left
            long f = Long.MAX_VALUE;
            for (int v = t; v != s; v = parent[v]) f = Math.min(f, cap[pe[v]]);
            for (int v = t; v != s; v = parent[v]) { cap[pe[v]] -= f; cap[pe[v] ^ 1] += f; }
            flow += f;
            total += f * dist[t];
        }
    }
}
--py
class MinCostFlow:
    def __init__(self, n):
        self.n = n
        self.g = [[] for _ in range(n)]
        self.to, self.cap, self.cost = [], [], []

    def add_edge(self, u, v, c, w):
        self.g[u].append(len(self.to)); self.to.append(v); self.cap.append(c); self.cost.append(w)     # forward edge
        self.g[v].append(len(self.to)); self.to.append(u); self.cap.append(0); self.cost.append(-w)    # reverse edge refunds

    def run(self, s, t):
        """Returns (max flow, min cost)."""
        INF = float("inf")
        flow = total = 0
        while True:
            dist = [INF] * self.n
            parent = [-1] * self.n
            pe = [-1] * self.n
            in_q = [False] * self.n
            dist[s] = 0
            q = deque([s])
            in_q[s] = True
            while q:                                                  # SPFA over the residual graph
                u = q.popleft()
                in_q[u] = False
                for e in self.g[u]:
                    w = self.to[e]
                    if self.cap[e] > 0 and relax(dist, parent, u, w, self.cost[e]):    # piece
                        pe[w] = e
                        if not in_q[w]:
                            q.append(w)
                            in_q[w] = True
            if dist[t] == INF:
                return flow, total                                    # no augmenting path left
            f = INF
            v = t
            while v != s:
                f = min(f, self.cap[pe[v]])
                v = parent[v]
            v = t
            while v != s:
                self.cap[pe[v]] -= f
                self.cap[pe[v] ^ 1] += f
                v = parent[v]
            flow += f
            total += f * dist[t]
--cpp
struct MinCostFlow {
    int n;
    vector<vector<int>> g;
    vector<int> to;
    vector<long long> cap, cost;

    MinCostFlow(int n) : n(n), g(n) {}

    void addEdge(int u, int v, long long c, long long w) {
        g[u].push_back(to.size()); to.push_back(v); cap.push_back(c); cost.push_back(w);     // forward edge
        g[v].push_back(to.size()); to.push_back(u); cap.push_back(0); cost.push_back(-w);    // reverse edge refunds
    }

    // returns {max flow, min cost}
    pair<long long, long long> run(int s, int t) {
        const long long INF = LLONG_MAX / 4;
        long long flow = 0, total = 0;
        while (true) {
            vector<long long> dist(n, INF);
            vector<int> parent(n, -1), pe(n, -1);
            vector<char> inQ(n, 0);
            queue<int> q;
            dist[s] = 0; q.push(s); inQ[s] = 1;
            while (!q.empty()) {                                        // SPFA over the residual graph
                int u = q.front(); q.pop();
                inQ[u] = 0;
                for (int e : g[u]) {
                    if (cap[e] > 0 && relax(dist, parent, u, to[e], cost[e])) {   // piece
                        pe[to[e]] = e;
                        if (!inQ[to[e]]) { q.push(to[e]); inQ[to[e]] = 1; }
                    }
                }
            }
            if (dist[t] >= INF) return {flow, total};                   // no augmenting path left
            long long f = LLONG_MAX;
            for (int v = t; v != s; v = parent[v]) f = min(f, cap[pe[v]]);
            for (int v = t; v != s; v = parent[v]) { cap[pe[v]] -= f; cap[pe[v] ^ 1] += f; }
            flow += f;
            total += f * dist[t];
        }
    }
};
--go
type MinCostFlow struct {
    n, cnt     int
    to         []int
    capa, cost []int
    g          [][]int // g[v]: ids of edges leaving v
}

func newMinCostFlow(n, maxEdges int) *MinCostFlow {
    return &MinCostFlow{n: n, to: make([]int, 2*maxEdges), capa: make([]int, 2*maxEdges),
        cost: make([]int, 2*maxEdges), g: make([][]int, n)}
}

func (m *MinCostFlow) addEdge(u, v, c, w int) {
    m.g[u] = append(m.g[u], m.cnt) // forward edge
    m.to[m.cnt], m.capa[m.cnt], m.cost[m.cnt] = v, c, w
    m.cnt++
    m.g[v] = append(m.g[v], m.cnt) // reverse edge refunds
    m.to[m.cnt], m.capa[m.cnt], m.cost[m.cnt] = u, 0, -w
    m.cnt++
}

// returns max flow and min cost
func (m *MinCostFlow) run(s, t int) (flow, total int) {
    const inf = 1 << 60
    for {
        dist, parent, pe := make([]int, m.n), make([]int, m.n), make([]int, m.n)
        for i := range dist {
            dist[i], parent[i], pe[i] = inf, -1, -1
        }
        inQ := make([]bool, m.n)
        dist[s], inQ[s] = 0, true
        for q := []int{s}; len(q) > 0; { // SPFA over the residual graph
            u := q[0]
            q = q[1:]
            inQ[u] = false
            for _, e := range m.g[u] {
                if m.capa[e] > 0 && relax(dist, parent, u, m.to[e], m.cost[e]) { // piece
                    pe[m.to[e]] = e
                    if !inQ[m.to[e]] {
                        q = append(q, m.to[e])
                        inQ[m.to[e]] = true
                    }
                }
            }
        }
        if dist[t] >= inf { // no augmenting path left
            return
        }
        f := inf
        for v := t; v != s; v = parent[v] {
            f = min(f, m.capa[pe[v]])
        }
        for v := t; v != s; v = parent[v] {
            m.capa[pe[v]] -= f
            m.capa[pe[v]^1] += f
        }
        flow += f
        total += f * dist[t]
    }
}
### lowerflow
--idea
In an ordinary flow, an edge can carry anywhere from 0 up to its capacity. With lower bounds, every edge `(u, v)` has a minimum `lo` and a maximum `hi`: it must carry at least `lo`.
