Examples: a road that must be used by at least 10 trucks, a machine that must run at least 3 shifts, a pipe with a minimum flow so it does not freeze. The question is whether any valid flow exists at all (feasibility), and then you can optimise inside it.

The trick removes the lower bounds. Force `lo` units onto each edge up front. That leaves the edge a capacity of `hi - lo` for extra flow, but it unbalances the endpoints: node `v` received `lo` units it did not pass on, and node `u` sent `lo` units it did not receive. Fix those imbalances with a new super-source `SS` and super-sink `TT`: `SS` supplies the nodes with surplus, and the nodes with a deficit send their need to `TT`. If a max flow from `SS` to `TT` saturates every supply edge, the imbalances can be balanced and a valid flow exists.
--build
1. Input: edges `(u, v, lo, hi)` with `lo <= hi`, and optionally a source `s` and sink `t` of an s-t flow. With no `s` and `t` you are asking for a circulation (flow around in cycles, conservation at every node).
2. For each edge add `u -> v` with capacity `hi - lo` to a new flow network, and record `excess[v] += lo`, `excess[u] -= lo`.
3. For an s-t flow (any amount, from `s` to `t`), add an edge `t -> s` with infinite capacity. This turns it into a circulation: whatever leaves `s` comes back through `t`.
4. Add two new nodes, `SS` and `TT`. For each node `v` with `excess[v] > 0`, add `SS -> v` with capacity `excess[v]`. For `excess[v] < 0`, add `v -> TT` with capacity `-excess[v]`. Let `need` be the sum of the positive excess values.
5. Run Dinic's algorithm (`dinic`, standard form) from `SS` to `TT`.
6. A valid flow exists exactly when the max flow equals `need`.
7. To recover the actual flow on an edge: its flow in the reduced network plus its `lo`.
8. To maximise the s-t flow value after feasibility is established: remove the `t -> s` edge (keeping its flow as the current value) and run an ordinary max flow from `s` to `t` on what remains. For the minimum, run it from `t` to `s`.
9. Invariant: a flow on the reduced network that saturates all the supply edges corresponds one to one with a valid flow in the original, because adding `lo` back to every edge restores conservation at each node exactly where the `SS` and `TT` edges had compensated for it.
--think
- Signal: "each edge must be used at least `lo` times", "each worker must get at least k tasks", "demands" at nodes (a node that must consume or produce a fixed amount is a lower bound on its connecting edge), circulations, "can the schedule satisfy all the minimum requirements".
- Node demands: a node that needs `d` units net can be modelled by an edge with lower bound `d`, or directly as an excess in step 4.
- Combined with costs (`mcmf`), it gives minimum-cost flows that satisfy lower bounds: add the forced `lo * cost` to the total, and run min-cost max-flow from `SS` to `TT`.
- Bipartite matching with every node covered at least once and at most `k` times is the same pattern: edges from the source with `lo = 1`, `hi = k`.
- Pitfalls: giving the `t -> s` edge a finite capacity that is too small, forgetting that the answer checks saturation (compare with `need`, not just a non-zero flow), `lo > hi` (immediately infeasible), and mixing up the sign of the excess.
--cost
One max flow on a network with `V + 2` nodes and `E + V + 1` edges. With Dinic that is the cost of `dinic`.
--java
// edges[i] = {u, v, lo, hi}. s < 0 asks for a circulation, otherwise for any s-t flow. Uses Dinic from the dinic lesson.
static boolean feasibleFlow(int n, int[][] edges, int s, int t) {
    final long INF = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4;
    int SS = n, TT = n + 1;
    Dinic d = new Dinic(n + 2, edges.length + n + 1);
    long[] excess = new long[n];
    for (int[] e : edges) {
        d.addEdge(e[0], e[1], e[3] - e[2]);            // what is left after the forced lo
        excess[e[1]] += e[2];
        excess[e[0]] -= e[2];
    }
    if (s >= 0) d.addEdge(t, s, INF);                  // turn the s-t flow into a circulation
    long need = 0;
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
        if (excess[v] > 0) { d.addEdge(SS, v, excess[v]); need += excess[v]; }
        else if (excess[v] < 0) d.addEdge(v, TT, -excess[v]);
    }
    return d.maxFlow(SS, TT) == need;                  // every supply edge saturated
}
--py
def feasible_flow(n, edges, s=-1, t=-1):
    """edges is a list of (u, v, lo, hi). s < 0 asks for a circulation, otherwise for any s-t flow.
    Uses Dinic from the dinic lesson."""
    INF = 10 ** 18
    SS, TT = n, n + 1
    d = Dinic(n + 2)
    excess = [0] * n
    for u, v, lo, hi in edges:
        d.add_edge(u, v, hi - lo)                      # what is left after the forced lo
        excess[v] += lo
        excess[u] -= lo
    if s >= 0:
        d.add_edge(t, s, INF)                          # turn the s-t flow into a circulation
    need = 0
    for v in range(n):
        if excess[v] > 0:
            d.add_edge(SS, v, excess[v])
            need += excess[v]
        elif excess[v] < 0:
            d.add_edge(v, TT, -excess[v])
    return d.max_flow(SS, TT) == need                  # every supply edge saturated
--cpp
// edges[i] = {u, v, lo, hi}. s < 0 asks for a circulation, otherwise for any s-t flow. Uses Dinic from the dinic lesson.
bool feasibleFlow(int n, const vector<array<int,4>>& edges, int s, int t) {
    const long long INF = LLONG_MAX / 4;
    int SS = n, TT = n + 1;
    Dinic d(n + 2);
    vector<long long> excess(n, 0);
    for (auto& [u, v, lo, hi] : edges) {
        d.addEdge(u, v, hi - lo);                      // what is left after the forced lo
        excess[v] += lo;
        excess[u] -= lo;
    }
    if (s >= 0) d.addEdge(t, s, INF);                  // turn the s-t flow into a circulation
    long long need = 0;
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
        if (excess[v] > 0) { d.addEdge(SS, v, excess[v]); need += excess[v]; }
        else if (excess[v] < 0) d.addEdge(v, TT, -excess[v]);
    }
    return d.maxFlow(SS, TT) == need;                  // every supply edge saturated
}
--go
// edges[i] = {u, v, lo, hi}. s < 0 asks for a circulation, otherwise for any s-t flow. Uses Dinic from the dinic lesson.
func feasibleFlow(n int, edges [][4]int, s, t int) bool {
    const inf = 1 << 60
    SS, TT := n, n+1
    d := newDinic(n+2, len(edges)+n+1)
    excess := make([]int, n)
    for _, e := range edges {
        d.addEdge(e[0], e[1], e[3]-e[2]) // what is left after the forced lo
        excess[e[1]] += e[2]
        excess[e[0]] -= e[2]
    }
    if s >= 0 {
        d.addEdge(t, s, inf) // turn the s-t flow into a circulation
    }
    need := 0
    for v, x := range excess {
        if x > 0 {
            d.addEdge(SS, v, x)
            need += x
        } else if x < 0 {
            d.addEdge(v, TT, -x)
        }
    }
    return d.maxFlow(SS, TT) == need // every supply edge saturated
}
### hopcroft
--idea
Hopcroft-Karp finds a maximum matching in a bipartite graph (left nodes and right nodes, edges only between the two sides) faster than Kuhn's algorithm (`matching`). Kuhn finds one augmenting path at a time with a fresh search. Hopcroft-Karp finds a whole batch of shortest augmenting paths in one round, all disjoint from each other, which reduces the number of rounds to about the square root of the number of nodes.

An augmenting path starts at an unmatched left node, alternates between a non-matching edge and a matching edge, and ends at an unmatched right node; flipping it adds one to the matching. Each round has two steps. A BFS from all unmatched left nodes assigns layers: layer 0 is the free left nodes, layer 1 the left nodes reached through a matching edge, and so on until a free right node is seen. Then a DFS searches only along layer-increasing steps, from each free left node, and flips each augmenting path it finds.
--build
1. Left nodes `0 .. L-1`, right nodes `0 .. R-1`, `adj[u]` as in `matching`. Keep `matchL[u]` (the right partner of left `u`, or -1) and `matchR[r]`.
2. BFS phase: set `dist[u] = 0` for each free left node and `dist[u] = -1` for the others, and queue the free ones. Pop `u`; for each right neighbour `r`, let `w = matchR[r]`. If `w == -1`, a free right node is reachable: remember `found = true`. Otherwise, if `dist[w] == -1`, set `dist[w] = dist[u] + 1` and queue `w`.
3. If `found` is false, no augmenting path exists: stop. The matching is maximum.
4. DFS phase: for each free left node `u`, call `dfs(u)`. For each right neighbour `r` of `u` with `w = matchR[r]`: if `w == -1`, or (`dist[w] == dist[u] + 1` and `dfs(w)` succeeds), then match them: `matchL[u] = r`, `matchR[r] = u`, return true. If no neighbour works, set `dist[u] = -1` (this node is a dead end for the rest of the phase) and return false.
5. Go back to step 2.
6. Invariant: after each phase, the length of the shortest augmenting path is strictly larger. A matching is maximum exactly when no augmenting path exists (Berge's lemma). After about `sqrt(V)` phases the paths are so long that at most `sqrt(V)` more can exist, because they are node-disjoint within a phase.
7. The code returns `matchR` as in the Kuhn lesson (`matchR[r]` is the left node paired with `r`, or -1).
--think
- Signal: bipartite matching with 10^4 to 10^5 nodes and up to a few hundred thousand edges, where Kuhn's O(V times E) is too slow. For small inputs, Kuhn is simpler.
- It is equivalent to Dinic (`dinic`) on the unit-capacity flow network of the matching, which has the same bound of O(E times the square root of V). Either can be used; Hopcroft-Karp is specialised and has less overhead.
- For minimum vertex cover and maximum independent set in a bipartite graph (König's theorem), take the maximum matching, then walk alternating paths from free left nodes: the cover is the left nodes not reached plus the right nodes reached.
- For weighted assignment (cheapest perfect matching) see `hungarian`; for general graphs with odd cycles see `blossom`.
- Pitfalls: not resetting `dist` each phase, treating the final layer carefully (the DFS here also accepts any free right node, which is still correct and finds valid vertex-disjoint paths), and recursion depth on long alternating paths (use an explicit stack for very large inputs).
--cost
Time O(E times the square root of V), space O(V + E).
--java
// adj.get(u): right nodes that left node u may take; R = number of right nodes.
// Returns matchR, where matchR[r] is the left node paired with r (or -1).
static int[] hopcroftKarp(List<List<Integer>> adj, int R) {
    int L = adj.size();
    int[] matchL = new int[L], matchR = new int[R], dist = new int[L];
    Arrays.fill(matchL, -1); Arrays.fill(matchR, -1);
    while (hkBfs(adj, matchL, matchR, dist))              // one round per BFS
        for (int u = 0; u < L; u++)
            if (matchL[u] == -1) hkDfs(adj, u, matchL, matchR, dist);
    return matchR;
}

static boolean hkBfs(List<List<Integer>> adj, int[] matchL, int[] matchR, int[] dist) {
    ArrayDeque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int u = 0; u < adj.size(); u++) {
        dist[u] = matchL[u] == -1 ? 0 : -1;               // layer 0: free left nodes
        if (matchL[u] == -1) q.add(u);
    }
    boolean found = false;
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int u = q.poll();
        for (int r : adj.get(u)) {
            int w = matchR[r];
            if (w == -1) found = true;                    // a free right node is reachable
            else if (dist[w] == -1) { dist[w] = dist[u] + 1; q.add(w); }
        }
    }
    return found;
}

static boolean hkDfs(List<List<Integer>> adj, int u, int[] matchL, int[] matchR, int[] dist) {
    for (int r : adj.get(u)) {
        int w = matchR[r];
        if (w == -1 || (dist[w] == dist[u] + 1 && hkDfs(adj, w, matchL, matchR, dist))) {
            matchL[u] = r; matchR[r] = u;                 // flip the path
            return true;
        }
    }
    dist[u] = -1;                                         // dead end for the rest of this round
    return false;
}
--py
import sys

def hopcroft_karp(adj, R):
    """adj[u]: right nodes that left node u may take; R = number of right nodes.
    Returns match_r, where match_r[r] is the left node paired with r (or -1)."""
    sys.setrecursionlimit(10000 + 4 * len(adj))
    L = len(adj)
    match_l = [-1] * L
    match_r = [-1] * R
    dist = [-1] * L

    def bfs():
        q = deque()
        for u in range(L):
            dist[u] = 0 if match_l[u] == -1 else -1       # layer 0: free left nodes
            if match_l[u] == -1:
                q.append(u)
        found = False
        while q:
            u = q.popleft()
            for r in adj[u]:
                w = match_r[r]
                if w == -1:
                    found = True                          # a free right node is reachable
                elif dist[w] == -1:
                    dist[w] = dist[u] + 1
                    q.append(w)
        return found

    def dfs(u):
        for r in adj[u]:
            w = match_r[r]
            if w == -1 or (dist[w] == dist[u] + 1 and dfs(w)):
                match_l[u], match_r[r] = r, u             # flip the path
                return True
        dist[u] = -1                                      # dead end for the rest of this round
        return False

    while bfs():                                          # one round per BFS
        for u in range(L):
            if match_l[u] == -1:
                dfs(u)
    return match_r
--cpp
bool hkBfs(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, const vector<int>& matchL, const vector<int>& matchR, vector<int>& dist) {
    queue<int> q;
    for (int u = 0; u < (int)adj.size(); u++) {
        dist[u] = matchL[u] == -1 ? 0 : -1;               // layer 0: free left nodes
        if (matchL[u] == -1) q.push(u);
    }
    bool found = false;
    while (!q.empty()) {
        int u = q.front(); q.pop();
        for (int r : adj[u]) {
            int w = matchR[r];
            if (w == -1) found = true;                    // a free right node is reachable
            else if (dist[w] == -1) { dist[w] = dist[u] + 1; q.push(w); }
        }
    }
    return found;
}

bool hkDfs(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int u, vector<int>& matchL, vector<int>& matchR, vector<int>& dist) {
    for (int r : adj[u]) {
        int w = matchR[r];
        if (w == -1 || (dist[w] == dist[u] + 1 && hkDfs(adj, w, matchL, matchR, dist))) {
            matchL[u] = r; matchR[r] = u;                 // flip the path
            return true;
        }
    }
    dist[u] = -1;                                         // dead end for the rest of this round
    return false;
}

// adj[u]: right nodes that left node u may take; R = number of right nodes.
// Returns matchR, where matchR[r] is the left node paired with r (or -1).
vector<int> hopcroftKarp(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int R) {
    int L = adj.size();
    vector<int> matchL(L, -1), matchR(R, -1), dist(L, -1);
    while (hkBfs(adj, matchL, matchR, dist))              // one round per BFS
        for (int u = 0; u < L; u++)
            if (matchL[u] == -1) hkDfs(adj, u, matchL, matchR, dist);
    return matchR;
}
--go
// adj[u]: right nodes that left node u may take; R = number of right nodes.
// Returns matchR, where matchR[r] is the left node paired with r (or -1).
func hopcroftKarp(adj [][]int, R int) []int {
    L := len(adj)
    matchL, matchR, dist := make([]int, L), make([]int, R), make([]int, L)
    for i := range matchL {
        matchL[i] = -1
    }
    for i := range matchR {
        matchR[i] = -1
    }
    for hkBfs(adj, matchL, matchR, dist) { // one round per BFS
        for u := 0; u < L; u++ {
            if matchL[u] == -1 {
                hkDfs(adj, u, matchL, matchR, dist)
            }
        }
    }
    return matchR
}

func hkBfs(adj [][]int, matchL, matchR, dist []int) bool {
    var q []int
    for u := range adj {
        dist[u] = -1
        if matchL[u] == -1 { // layer 0: free left nodes
            dist[u] = 0
            q = append(q, u)
        }
    }
    found := false
    for ; len(q) > 0; q = q[1:] {
        u := q[0]
        for _, r := range adj[u] {
            if w := matchR[r]; w == -1 { // a free right node is reachable
                found = true
            } else if dist[w] == -1 {
                dist[w] = dist[u] + 1
                q = append(q, w)
            }
        }
    }
    return found
}

func hkDfs(adj [][]int, u int, matchL, matchR, dist []int) bool {
    for _, r := range adj[u] {
        w := matchR[r]
        if w == -1 || (dist[w] == dist[u]+1 && hkDfs(adj, w, matchL, matchR, dist)) {
            matchL[u], matchR[r] = r, u // flip the path
            return true
        }
    }
    dist[u] = -1 // dead end for the rest of this round
    return false
}
### hungarian
--idea
The assignment problem: there are `n` workers and `m` jobs (`n <= m`), and a table `cost[i][j]` says what it costs to give job `j` to worker `i`. Give every worker exactly one different job so that the total cost is as small as possible. Trying all assignments takes `n!` steps, hopeless beyond about ten workers. The Hungarian algorithm solves it in `n` cubed steps.

It uses prices, the same idea as shadow prices in economics. Give each worker a price `u[i]` and each job a price `v[j]`, kept so that `u[i] + v[j] <= cost[i][j]` for every pair. A pair is tight when this holds with equality. Any assignment made only of tight pairs is optimal, because its total cost equals the sum of all prices, and no assignment can cost less than that sum. The algorithm adds workers one at a time. If the new worker has no tight route to a free job, it raises prices by the smallest amount that creates a new tight pair, and tries again.
--build
1. Number workers `1..n` and jobs `1..m` (index 0 is a helper). Keep worker prices `u`, job prices `v`, `p[j]` (the worker currently holding job `j`, or 0), and `way[j]` (the previous job on the alternating path to `j`).
2. For each worker `i` in turn: set `p[0] = i`, `j0 = 0`, `minv[j] = INF` for all jobs, `used[j] = false`.
3. Repeat: mark `used[j0]`. Let `i0 = p[j0]` be the worker at the current end of the path. For each job `j` not yet used: compute `cur = cost[i0][j] - u[i0] - v[j]` (the slack: how far from tight). If `cur < minv[j]`, set `minv[j] = cur` and `way[j] = j0`. Track the unused job `j1` with the smallest `minv`, and `delta = minv[j1]`.
4. Update prices: for every job `j`, if it is used, add `delta` to `u[p[j]]` and subtract `delta` from `v[j]`; otherwise subtract `delta` from `minv[j]`. This makes the pair to `j1` tight while keeping all used pairs tight.
5. Set `j0 = j1`. If `p[j0] == 0`, job `j0` is free: stop repeating. Otherwise its holder joins the search, so go back to step 3.
6. Augment along the path: while `j0 != 0`: let `j1 = way[j0]`, set `p[j0] = p[j1]`, `j0 = j1`. This shifts workers along the alternating path so that the new worker gets a job.
7. After all workers are added, `p[j]` gives the assignment, and the minimum total cost is `-v[0]`.
8. Invariant: `u[i] + v[j] <= cost[i][j]` always holds, and every assigned pair is tight. So the sum of prices equals the cost of the current assignment and is a lower bound for any assignment of the workers so far.
--think
- Signal: "assign each person one job at minimum total cost", "minimum cost perfect matching in a complete bipartite graph", "match points to points with least total distance", "maximum total profit" (negate the costs, or subtract each from the largest).
- Compare: plain matching (`matching`, `hopcroft`) ignores costs. Min-cost max-flow (`mcmf`) solves the same problem and more general ones, but is slower for dense tables. Gale-Shapley (`stablematch`) is about preferences, not total cost.
- If there are more workers than jobs, transpose the table (the algorithm needs `n <= m`). To forbid a pair, use a very large cost, not infinity (infinity breaks the price arithmetic).
- Rectangular tables work directly: the `n` workers each get a job and `m - n` jobs stay free.
- Negative costs are fine.
- Pitfalls: mixing 0-indexed input with the 1-indexed helper arrays, using `int` where costs times `n` overflow, and reading the answer from `-v[0]` (not from summing prices).
--cost
Time O(n squared times m), which is O(n cubed) for a square table. Space O(n + m) beyond the table.
--java
// cost[i][j] for n workers and m jobs, n <= m. Returns {total cost, job of worker 0, job of worker 1, ...}.
static long[] hungarian(long[][] cost) {
    final long INF = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4;
    int n = cost.length, m = cost[0].length;
    long[] u = new long[n + 1], v = new long[m + 1];
    int[] p = new int[m + 1], way = new int[m + 1];
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        p[0] = i;
        int j0 = 0;
        long[] minv = new long[m + 1];
        boolean[] used = new boolean[m + 1];
        Arrays.fill(minv, INF);
        do {
            used[j0] = true;
            int i0 = p[j0], j1 = 0;
            long delta = INF;
            for (int j = 1; j <= m; j++) {
                if (used[j]) continue;
                long cur = cost[i0 - 1][j - 1] - u[i0] - v[j];          // slack of the pair
                if (cur < minv[j]) { minv[j] = cur; way[j] = j0; }
                if (minv[j] < delta) { delta = minv[j]; j1 = j; }
            }
            for (int j = 0; j <= m; j++) {                              // raise prices
                if (used[j]) { u[p[j]] += delta; v[j] -= delta; }
                else minv[j] -= delta;
            }
            j0 = j1;
        } while (p[j0] != 0);
        do { int j1 = way[j0]; p[j0] = p[j1]; j0 = j1; } while (j0 != 0);   // augment along the path
    }
    long[] res = new long[n + 1];
    res[0] = -v[0];
    for (int j = 1; j <= m; j++) if (p[j] != 0) res[p[j]] = j - 1;
    return res;
}
--py
def hungarian(cost):
    """cost[i][j] for n workers and m jobs, n <= m. Returns (total cost, job of each worker)."""
    INF = float("inf")
    n, m = len(cost), len(cost[0])
    u = [0] * (n + 1)
    v = [0] * (m + 1)
    p = [0] * (m + 1)                         # p[j]: worker holding job j (1-indexed), 0 if free
    way = [0] * (m + 1)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        p[0] = i
        j0 = 0
        minv = [INF] * (m + 1)
        used = [False] * (m + 1)
        while True:
            used[j0] = True
            i0, delta, j1 = p[j0], INF, 0
            for j in range(1, m + 1):
                if used[j]:
                    continue
                cur = cost[i0 - 1][j - 1] - u[i0] - v[j]        # slack of the pair
                if cur < minv[j]:
                    minv[j], way[j] = cur, j0
                if minv[j] < delta:
                    delta, j1 = minv[j], j
            for j in range(m + 1):                              # raise prices
                if used[j]:
                    u[p[j]] += delta
                    v[j] -= delta
                else:
                    minv[j] -= delta
            j0 = j1
            if p[j0] == 0:
                break
        while j0:                                               # augment along the path
            j1 = way[j0]
            p[j0] = p[j1]
            j0 = j1
    assign = [0] * n
    for j in range(1, m + 1):
        if p[j]:
            assign[p[j] - 1] = j - 1
    return -v[0], assign
--cpp
// cost[i][j] for n workers and m jobs, n <= m. Returns {total cost, job of each worker}.
pair<long long, vector<int>> hungarian(const vector<vector<long long>>& cost) {
    const long long INF = LLONG_MAX / 4;
    int n = cost.size(), m = cost[0].size();
    vector<long long> u(n + 1, 0), v(m + 1, 0);
    vector<int> p(m + 1, 0), way(m + 1, 0);
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        p[0] = i;
        int j0 = 0;
        vector<long long> minv(m + 1, INF);
        vector<char> used(m + 1, 0);
        do {
            used[j0] = 1;
            int i0 = p[j0], j1 = 0;
            long long delta = INF;
            for (int j = 1; j <= m; j++) {
                if (used[j]) continue;
                long long cur = cost[i0 - 1][j - 1] - u[i0] - v[j];     // slack of the pair
                if (cur < minv[j]) { minv[j] = cur; way[j] = j0; }
                if (minv[j] < delta) { delta = minv[j]; j1 = j; }
            }
            for (int j = 0; j <= m; j++) {                              // raise prices
                if (used[j]) { u[p[j]] += delta; v[j] -= delta; }
                else minv[j] -= delta;
            }
            j0 = j1;
        } while (p[j0] != 0);
        do { int j1 = way[j0]; p[j0] = p[j1]; j0 = j1; } while (j0 != 0);   // augment along the path
    }
    vector<int> assign(n);
    for (int j = 1; j <= m; j++) if (p[j]) assign[p[j] - 1] = j - 1;
    return {-v[0], assign};
}
--go
// cost[i][j] for n workers and m jobs, n <= m. Returns {total cost, job of worker 0, job of worker 1, ...}.
func hungarian(cost [][]int) []int {
    const inf = 1 << 60
    n, m := len(cost), len(cost[0])
    u, v := make([]int, n+1), make([]int, m+1)
    p, way := make([]int, m+1), make([]int, m+1)
    for i := 1; i <= n; i++ {
        p[0] = i
        j0 := 0
        minv := make([]int, m+1)
        used := make([]bool, m+1)
        for j := range minv {
            minv[j] = inf
        }
        for ok := true; ok; ok = p[j0] != 0 {
            used[j0] = true
            i0, j1, delta := p[j0], 0, inf
            for j := 1; j <= m; j++ {
                if used[j] {
                    continue
                }
                if cur := cost[i0-1][j-1] - u[i0] - v[j]; cur < minv[j] { // slack of the pair
                    minv[j], way[j] = cur, j0
                }
                if minv[j] < delta {
                    delta, j1 = minv[j], j
                }
            }
            for j := 0; j <= m; j++ { // raise prices
                if used[j] {
                    u[p[j]] += delta
                    v[j] -= delta
                } else {
                    minv[j] -= delta
                }
            }
            j0 = j1
        }
        for ok := true; ok; ok = j0 != 0 { // augment along the path
            j1 := way[j0]
            p[j0] = p[j1]
            j0 = j1
        }
    }
    res := make([]int, n+1)
    res[0] = -v[0]
    for j := 1; j <= m; j++ {
        if p[j] != 0 {
            res[p[j]] = j - 1
        }
    }
    return res
}
### blossom
--idea
Bipartite matching (`matching`) assumes the nodes split into two sides. In a general graph (people who can pair with anyone, such as chess opponents or roommates) this fails, because the graph can have odd cycles: loops with an odd number of edges, such as a triangle or a pentagon. The augmenting path search of Kuhn's algorithm gets confused there: going around an odd cycle makes the alternating pattern clash.

Edmonds' blossom algorithm fixes this. An odd cycle where all but one edge pair up is called a blossom. When the search discovers one, it contracts the whole blossom into one super-node and continues. Inside a blossom, any node can be the entry point to continue an alternating path in either direction, so shrinking it loses nothing. If an augmenting path is found in the shrunk graph, it expands back to a real path in the original graph.
--build
1. Keep `match[v]` (partner or -1), `p[v]` (the parent of `v` in the search tree), `base[v]` (the base, or representative, of the blossom `v` currently belongs to; starts as `v` itself), and `used[v]` (reached in the search).
2. For each unmatched node `root`, call `findPath(root)`. It grows an alternating search tree from `root`: nodes at even depth (reached through a matching edge, or the root) are "outer" and are placed in the queue (`enqueue`, code piece `u-queueop`).
3. Pop an outer node `v`. For each neighbour `to`: skip it if `base[v] == base[to]` (same blossom) or `match[v] == to` (the matching edge itself).
4. If `to` is outer (it is the root, or it has a matched partner whose parent is set): an odd cycle is found. Compute `cb = lca(v, to)`, the lowest common base of the two paths up the tree. Mark the cycle by calling `markPath(v, cb, to)` and `markPath(to, cb, v)`, which sets parent pointers so a path through the blossom can go either way. Then for every node `i` whose base is in the marked set, set `base[i] = cb`, and queue it if not yet reached.
5. Else if `to` has no parent: set `p[to] = v`. If `to` is unmatched, an augmenting path ends here: return `to`. Otherwise its partner `match[to]` becomes an outer node: mark it used and queue it.
6. If the queue empties without finding a free node, return -1: no augmenting path from `root`.
7. Augment: with the returned end `v`, walk back: `pv = p[v]`, `ppv = match[pv]`, set `match[v] = pv`, `match[pv] = v`, and move on with `v = ppv`, until `v == -1`.
8. Invariant: the search tree's parent pointers always describe a valid alternating route from the root to every reached node, and contracting a blossom preserves the existence of augmenting paths (Edmonds' key lemma). Berge's lemma then says that when no root has an augmenting path, the matching is maximum.
--think
- Signal: pairing within one group where anyone may pair with anyone allowed: "match students into project pairs", "maximum number of disjoint pairs among compatible people", "domino-like tilings on general graphs", "maximum set of edges with no shared endpoint" on a non-bipartite graph.
- First check whether the graph is bipartite (`bipartite`). If yes, use `matching` or `hopcroft`: simpler and faster. Blossom is the tool only when odd cycles are possible.
- This implementation is O(V cubed) and fine up to about a thousand nodes. Faster variants (Micali-Vazirani, or Gabow's) exist and are rarely needed.
- Weighted general matching (maximum total weight) is a significantly harder algorithm, the weighted blossom algorithm. Min-cost flow does not apply to non-bipartite graphs.
- The matching size is `(number of matched nodes) / 2`. The number of unmatched nodes is the deficiency.
- Tutte's theorem gives the existence test for perfect matchings, but the algorithm is the practical tool.
- Pitfalls: forgetting to reset `blossom` marks before each contraction, not resetting `base` and `parent` for each root, self-loops (they are skipped by the same-base test), and mixing up "outer" nodes with matched nodes.
--cost
Time O(V cubed) as written, space O(V + E).
--uses
u-queueop
--java
static class Blossom {
    int n;
    List<List<Integer>> adj;
    int[] match, p, base;
    boolean[] used, blossom;

    Blossom(List<List<Integer>> adj) {
        this.adj = adj; n = adj.size();
        match = new int[n]; p = new int[n]; base = new int[n];
        used = new boolean[n]; blossom = new boolean[n];
        Arrays.fill(match, -1);
    }

    int lca(int a, int b) {                                  // lowest common base of two outer nodes
        boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
        while (true) {
            a = base[a]; seen[a] = true;
            if (match[a] == -1) break;
            a = p[match[a]];
        }
        while (true) {
            b = base[b];
            if (seen[b]) return b;
            b = p[match[b]];
        }
    }

    void markPath(int v, int b, int child) {                 // mark the cycle from v up to base b
        while (base[v] != b) {
            blossom[base[v]] = blossom[base[match[v]]] = true;
            p[v] = child;
            child = match[v];
            v = p[match[v]];
        }
    }

    int findPath(int root) {
        Arrays.fill(used, false); Arrays.fill(p, -1);
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) base[i] = i;
        used[root] = true;
        Deque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
        enqueue(q, root);                                    // piece: queue of outer nodes
        while (!q.isEmpty()) {
            int v = dequeue(q);
            for (int to : adj.get(v)) {
                if (base[v] == base[to] || match[v] == to) continue;
                if (to == root || (match[to] != -1 && p[match[to]] != -1)) {   // odd cycle: contract
                    int cb = lca(v, to);
                    Arrays.fill(blossom, false);
                    markPath(v, cb, to); markPath(to, cb, v);
                    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
                        if (blossom[base[i]]) {
                            base[i] = cb;
                            if (!used[i]) { used[i] = true; enqueue(q, i); }
                        }
                } else if (p[to] == -1) {
                    p[to] = v;
                    if (match[to] == -1) return to;          // augmenting path found
                    used[match[to]] = true;
                    enqueue(q, match[to]);
                }
            }
        }
        return -1;
    }

    int[] solve() {                                          // match[v] = partner or -1
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            if (match[i] != -1) continue;
            int v = findPath(i);
            while (v != -1) {                                // flip the augmenting path
                int pv = p[v], ppv = match[pv];
                match[v] = pv; match[pv] = v;
                v = ppv;
            }
        }
        return match;
    }
}
--py
class Blossom:
    def __init__(self, adj):
        self.adj = adj
        self.n = len(adj)
        self.match = [-1] * self.n

    def _lca(self, a, b):                               # lowest common base of two outer nodes
        match, p, base = self.match, self.p, self.base
        seen = [False] * self.n
        while True:
            a = base[a]
            seen[a] = True
            if match[a] == -1:
                break
            a = p[match[a]]
        while True:
            b = base[b]
            if seen[b]:
                return b
            b = p[match[b]]

    def _mark_path(self, v, b, child):                  # mark the cycle from v up to base b
        match, p, base, blossom = self.match, self.p, self.base, self.blossom
        while base[v] != b:
            blossom[base[v]] = blossom[base[match[v]]] = True
            p[v] = child
            child = match[v]
            v = p[match[v]]

    def _find_path(self, root):
        n, adj, match = self.n, self.adj, self.match
        self.used = used = [False] * n
        self.p = p = [-1] * n
        self.base = base = list(range(n))
        self.blossom = blossom = [False] * n
        used[root] = True
        q = deque()
        enqueue(q, root)                                # piece: queue of outer nodes
        while q:
            v = dequeue(q)
            for to in adj[v]:
                if base[v] == base[to] or match[v] == to:
                    continue
                if to == root or (match[to] != -1 and p[match[to]] != -1):   # odd cycle: contract
                    cb = self._lca(v, to)
                    for i in range(n):
                        blossom[i] = False
                    self._mark_path(v, cb, to)
                    self._mark_path(to, cb, v)
                    for i in range(n):
                        if blossom[base[i]]:
                            base[i] = cb
                            if not used[i]:
                                used[i] = True
                                enqueue(q, i)
                elif p[to] == -1:
                    p[to] = v
                    if match[to] == -1:
                        return to                       # augmenting path found
                    used[match[to]] = True
                    enqueue(q, match[to])
        return -1

    def solve(self):
        """Returns match, where match[v] is the partner of v or -1."""
        match = self.match
        for i in range(self.n):
            if match[i] != -1:
                continue
            v = self._find_path(i)
            while v != -1:                              # flip the augmenting path
                pv = self.p[v]
                ppv = match[pv]
                match[v], match[pv] = pv, v
                v = ppv
        return match
--cpp
struct Blossom {
    int n;
    const vector<vector<int>>& adj;
    vector<int> match, p, base;
    vector<char> used, blossom;

    Blossom(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) : n(adj.size()), adj(adj), match(n, -1), p(n), base(n), used(n), blossom(n) {}

    int lca(int a, int b) {                                  // lowest common base of two outer nodes
        vector<char> seen(n, 0);
        while (true) {
            a = base[a]; seen[a] = 1;
            if (match[a] == -1) break;
            a = p[match[a]];
        }
        while (true) {
            b = base[b];
            if (seen[b]) return b;
            b = p[match[b]];
        }
    }

    void markPath(int v, int b, int child) {                 // mark the cycle from v up to base b
        while (base[v] != b) {
            blossom[base[v]] = blossom[base[match[v]]] = 1;
            p[v] = child;
            child = match[v];
            v = p[match[v]];
        }
    }

    int findPath(int root) {
        fill(used.begin(), used.end(), 0); fill(p.begin(), p.end(), -1);
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) base[i] = i;
        used[root] = 1;
        queue<int> q;
        enqueue(q, root);                                    // piece: queue of outer nodes
        while (!q.empty()) {
            int v = dequeue(q);
            for (int to : adj[v]) {
                if (base[v] == base[to] || match[v] == to) continue;
                if (to == root || (match[to] != -1 && p[match[to]] != -1)) {   // odd cycle: contract
                    int cb = lca(v, to);
                    fill(blossom.begin(), blossom.end(), 0);
                    markPath(v, cb, to); markPath(to, cb, v);
                    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
                        if (blossom[base[i]]) {
                            base[i] = cb;
                            if (!used[i]) { used[i] = 1; enqueue(q, i); }
                        }
                } else if (p[to] == -1) {
                    p[to] = v;
                    if (match[to] == -1) return to;          // augmenting path found
                    used[match[to]] = 1;
                    enqueue(q, match[to]);
                }
            }
        }
        return -1;
    }

    vector<int> solve() {                                    // match[v] = partner or -1
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            if (match[i] != -1) continue;
            int v = findPath(i);
            while (v != -1) {                                // flip the augmenting path
                int pv = p[v], ppv = match[pv];
                match[v] = pv; match[pv] = v;
                v = ppv;
            }
        }
        return match;
    }
};
--go
type Blossom struct {
    n                int
    adj              [][]int
    match, p, base   []int
    used, blossom    []bool
}

func newBlossom(adj [][]int) *Blossom {
    n := len(adj)
    b := &Blossom{n: n, adj: adj, match: make([]int, n), p: make([]int, n), base: make([]int, n),
        used: make([]bool, n), blossom: make([]bool, n)}
    for i := range b.match {
        b.match[i] = -1
    }
    return b
}

func (b *Blossom) lca(x, y int) int { // lowest common base of two outer nodes
    seen := make([]bool, b.n)
    for {
        x = b.base[x]
        seen[x] = true
        if b.match[x] == -1 {
            break
        }
        x = b.p[b.match[x]]
    }
    for {
        y = b.base[y]
        if seen[y] {
            return y
        }
        y = b.p[b.match[y]]
    }
}

func (b *Blossom) markPath(v, bs, child int) { // mark the cycle from v up to base bs
    for b.base[v] != bs {
        b.blossom[b.base[v]], b.blossom[b.base[b.match[v]]] = true, true
        b.p[v] = child
        child = b.match[v]
        v = b.p[b.match[v]]
    }
}

func (b *Blossom) findPath(root int) int {
    for i := 0; i < b.n; i++ {
        b.used[i], b.p[i], b.base[i] = false, -1, i
    }
    b.used[root] = true
    var q []int
    enqueue(&q, root) // piece: queue of outer nodes
    for len(q) > 0 {
        v := dequeue(&q)
        for _, to := range b.adj[v] {
            if b.base[v] == b.base[to] || b.match[v] == to {
                continue
            }
            if to == root || (b.match[to] != -1 && b.p[b.match[to]] != -1) { // odd cycle: contract
                cb := b.lca(v, to)
                for i := range b.blossom {
                    b.blossom[i] = false
                }
                b.markPath(v, cb, to)
                b.markPath(to, cb, v)
                for i := 0; i < b.n; i++ {
                    if b.blossom[b.base[i]] {
                        b.base[i] = cb
                        if !b.used[i] {
                            b.used[i] = true
                            enqueue(&q, i)
                        }
                    }
                }
            } else if b.p[to] == -1 {
                b.p[to] = v
                if b.match[to] == -1 { // augmenting path found
                    return to
                }
                b.used[b.match[to]] = true
                enqueue(&q, b.match[to])
            }
        }
    }
    return -1
}

func (b *Blossom) solve() []int { // match[v] = partner or -1
    for i := 0; i < b.n; i++ {
        if b.match[i] != -1 {
            continue
        }
        for v := b.findPath(i); v != -1; { // flip the augmenting path
            pv := b.p[v]
            ppv := b.match[pv]
            b.match[v], b.match[pv] = pv, v
            v = ppv
        }
    }
    return b.match
}
### dominator
--idea
In a directed graph with a start node `root` (think of the entry of a program), a node `d` dominates a node `v` if every path from the root to `v` passes through `d`. Every node dominates itself. If you imagine the root as the entrance to a building and `v` as a room, the dominators of `v` are the doors you cannot avoid on the way. Compilers use this to find loops and to place code.

Among the dominators of `v` (other than `v`), the one closest to `v` is the immediate dominator, `idom(v)`. Linking each node to its immediate dominator gives a tree rooted at `root`, the dominator tree. Then `d` dominates `v` exactly when `d` is an ancestor of `v` in that tree. The algorithm here is the simple iterative one of Cooper, Harvey and Kennedy: it improves its guess for each node until nothing changes.
--build
1. Run a DFS from `root` and record the order in which nodes finish: `post[v]`, the postorder number (the root finishes last, so it has the largest). Unreachable nodes are ignored. Also build the predecessor lists `pred[v]`: the nodes with an edge into `v`.
2. Set `idom[root] = root` and `idom[v] = -1` (unknown) for the others.
3. Process the reachable nodes (except the root) in reverse postorder, that is from the highest `post` to the lowest. This order lets most predecessors be processed before the node.
4. For node `v`: look at its predecessors `q` that already have an `idom`. Start with the first as `cand`. For each further such `q`, set `cand = intersect(q, cand)`.
5. `intersect(a, b)`: while `a != b`: while `post[a] < post[b]`, move `a = idom[a]`; while `post[b] < post[a]`, move `b = idom[b]`. It walks both up the current dominator tree until they meet at the nearest common ancestor.
6. If `idom[v] != cand`, store it and remember that something changed.
7. Repeat steps 3 to 6 until a full pass changes nothing. Then `idom` is final. Set `idom[root] = -1` for the answer.
8. Invariant: at all times `idom[v]` is an ancestor-or-equal in the true dominator tree of the answer being built, and each pass can only move values up. On the final pass, for every node, the common dominator of all its predecessors is exactly its immediate dominator.
--think
- Signal: "which nodes must be passed to get to `v`", "single points of failure on every route from the entrance", "remove a node, which others become unreachable" (they are the nodes it dominates, its subtree in the tree), "loop detection in control-flow graphs".
- Not the same as bridges and cut points (`bridges`), which are about undirected graphs and splitting. Dominators are directed and measured from one root.
- Applications: the size of the subtree of `d` in the dominator tree is the number of nodes that become unreachable if `d` is deleted.
- Faster algorithms exist: Lengauer-Tarjan (nearly linear) for very large graphs. The iterative method here is simple, short, and quick on real graphs (worst case quadratic).
- For DAGs there is a neat shortcut: process in topological order (`topo`) and `idom[v]` is the LCA in the dominator tree built so far (`lca`) of all predecessors of `v`.
- Pitfalls: nodes not reachable from the root have no dominators, so skip them; iterating in plain order rather than reverse postorder is still correct but needs more passes; and forgetting that the root is its own immediate dominator in the algorithm.
--cost
Time O(V times E) worst case, usually near linear in practice. Space O(V + E).
--java
// adj is a directed graph; returns idom[v] (-1 for the root and for unreachable nodes)
static int[] dominators(List<List<Integer>> adj, int root) {
    int n = adj.size();
    int[] post = new int[n], it = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(post, -1);
    List<Integer> order = new ArrayList<>();               // nodes in finish order
    boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
    ArrayDeque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
    seen[root] = true; st.push(root);
    while (!st.isEmpty()) {
        int v = st.peek();
        if (it[v] < adj.get(v).size()) {
            int w = adj.get(v).get(it[v]++);
            if (!seen[w]) { seen[w] = true; st.push(w); }
        } else { post[v] = order.size(); order.add(v); st.pop(); }
    }
    List<List<Integer>> pred = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) pred.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) for (int w : adj.get(v)) pred.get(w).add(v);
    int[] idom = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(idom, -1);
    idom[root] = root;
    boolean changed = true;
    while (changed) {
        changed = false;
        for (int i = order.size() - 2; i >= 0; i--) {      // reverse postorder, skipping the root
            int v = order.get(i), cand = -1;
            for (int q : pred.get(v)) {
                if (idom[q] == -1) continue;               // predecessor not processed yet
                if (cand == -1) { cand = q; continue; }
                int a = q, b = cand;                       // intersect: climb to the common ancestor
                while (a != b) {
                    while (post[a] < post[b]) a = idom[a];
                    while (post[b] < post[a]) b = idom[b];
                }
                cand = a;
            }
            if (idom[v] != cand) { idom[v] = cand; changed = true; }
        }
    }
    idom[root] = -1;
    return idom;
}
--py
def dominators(adj, root=0):
    """adj is a directed graph. Returns idom, where idom[v] is the immediate dominator of v
    (-1 for the root and for unreachable nodes)."""
    n = len(adj)
    post = [-1] * n
    order = []                                          # nodes in finish order
    seen = [False] * n
    it = [0] * n
    seen[root] = True
    stack = [root]
    while stack:
        v = stack[-1]
        if it[v] < len(adj[v]):
            w = adj[v][it[v]]
            it[v] += 1
            if not seen[w]:
                seen[w] = True
                stack.append(w)
        else:
            post[v] = len(order)
            order.append(v)
            stack.pop()
    pred = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    for v in range(n):
        for w in adj[v]:
            pred[w].append(v)
    idom = [-1] * n
    idom[root] = root
    changed = True
    while changed:
        changed = False
        for v in reversed(order[:-1]):                  # reverse postorder, skipping the root
            cand = -1
            for q in pred[v]:
                if idom[q] == -1:
                    continue                            # predecessor not processed yet
                if cand == -1:
                    cand = q
                    continue
                a, b = q, cand                          # intersect: climb to the common ancestor
                while a != b:
                    while post[a] < post[b]:
                        a = idom[a]
                    while post[b] < post[a]:
                        b = idom[b]
                cand = a
            if idom[v] != cand:
                idom[v] = cand
                changed = True
    idom[root] = -1
    return idom
--cpp
// adj is a directed graph; returns idom[v] (-1 for the root and for unreachable nodes)
vector<int> dominators(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int root) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<int> post(n, -1), it(n, 0), order, st = {root};   // order: nodes in finish order
    vector<char> seen(n, 0);
    seen[root] = 1;
    while (!st.empty()) {
        int v = st.back();
        if (it[v] < (int)adj[v].size()) {
            int w = adj[v][it[v]++];
            if (!seen[w]) { seen[w] = 1; st.push_back(w); }
        } else { post[v] = order.size(); order.push_back(v); st.pop_back(); }
    }
    vector<vector<int>> pred(n);
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) for (int w : adj[v]) pred[w].push_back(v);
    vector<int> idom(n, -1);
    idom[root] = root;
    bool changed = true;
    while (changed) {
        changed = false;
        for (int i = (int)order.size() - 2; i >= 0; i--) {   // reverse postorder, skipping the root
            int v = order[i], cand = -1;
            for (int q : pred[v]) {
                if (idom[q] == -1) continue;                 // predecessor not processed yet
                if (cand == -1) { cand = q; continue; }
                int a = q, b = cand;                         // intersect: climb to the common ancestor
                while (a != b) {
                    while (post[a] < post[b]) a = idom[a];
                    while (post[b] < post[a]) b = idom[b];
                }
                cand = a;
            }
            if (idom[v] != cand) { idom[v] = cand; changed = true; }
        }
    }
    idom[root] = -1;
    return idom;
}
--go
// adj is a directed graph; returns idom[v] (-1 for the root and for unreachable nodes)
func dominators(adj [][]int, root int) []int {
    n := len(adj)
    post, it := make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
    var order []int // nodes in finish order
    seen := make([]bool, n)
    seen[root] = true
    for st := []int{root}; len(st) > 0; {
        v := st[len(st)-1]
        if it[v] < len(adj[v]) {
            w := adj[v][it[v]]
            it[v]++
            if !seen[w] {
                seen[w] = true
                st = append(st, w)
            }
        } else {
            post[v] = len(order)
            order = append(order, v)
            st = st[:len(st)-1]
        }
    }
    pred := make([][]int, n)
    for v := range adj {
        for _, w := range adj[v] {
            pred[w] = append(pred[w], v)
        }
    }
    idom := make([]int, n)
    for i := range idom {
        idom[i] = -1
    }
    idom[root] = root
    for changed := true; changed; {
        changed = false
        for i := len(order) - 2; i >= 0; i-- { // reverse postorder, skipping the root
            v, cand := order[i], -1
            for _, q := range pred[v] {
                if idom[q] == -1 { // predecessor not processed yet
                    continue
                }
                if cand == -1 {
                    cand = q
                    continue
                }
                a, b := q, cand // intersect: climb to the common ancestor
                for a != b {
                    for post[a] < post[b] {
                        a = idom[a]
                    }
                    for post[b] < post[a] {
                        b = idom[b]
                    }
                }
                cand = a
            }
            if idom[v] != cand {
                idom[v], changed = cand, true
            }
        }
    }
    idom[root] = -1
    return idom
}
### dynconn
--idea
Dynamic connectivity asks "are `u` and `v` connected?" while edges are added and removed over time. A disjoint set union (DSU, see `dsu`) handles additions beautifully but cannot undo a union in a way that splits correctly after path compression. The offline trick: if you know all operations in advance, you can avoid deletions altogether.

Each edge exists during a time interval, from the moment it is added to the moment it is removed. Build a segment tree over time and store each edge in the `O(log T)` tree nodes that together cover its interval. Now walk the tree depth first. On entering a node, union the endpoints of all edges stored there; on leaving, undo those unions. At a leaf (one point in time), exactly the edges alive at that time have been united, so a connectivity query is a single `find`. Undoing needs a DSU with rollback: union by size without path compression, with a history stack.
--build
1. Events are numbered by time `0 .. T-1`. Each is an add of edge `(u, v)`, a delete of edge `(u, v)`, or a query "are `u` and `v` connected".
2. Turn adds and deletes into intervals. Keep a map from each edge to the list of times it was added and not yet deleted. A delete at time `t` pairs with an earlier add at time `s` and creates the interval `[s, t)`. Edges never deleted get the interval `[s, T)`.
3. Insert each interval `[l, r)` with its edge into a segment tree over times `[0, T)`: store the edge in the nodes whose range lies inside the interval, `O(log T)` of them.
4. DSU with rollback: `parent[x]`, `size[x]`, and a history stack of the roots that were attached. `find` just follows parents (no compression, so depth stays `O(log V)` thanks to union by size). `unite(a, b)` finds the roots, attaches the smaller under the larger, adds the child root to the history, and does nothing if they are already joined.
5. `undo(mark)`: while the history is longer than `mark`, pop a child root `b`, let `a = parent[b]`, subtract `size[b]` from `size[a]`, and set `parent[b] = b`.
6. `go(node, l, r)`: remember `mark = history length`. Unite the endpoints of every edge stored at the node. If the range is a single time `l`, and event `l` is a query, answer `find(u) == find(v)`. Otherwise recurse into the two halves. Finally `undo(mark)`.
7. Collect the answers of the queries in order.
8. Invariant: while the DFS is inside node `[l, r)`, the DSU contains exactly the edges whose interval covers the whole of `[l, r)`. At a leaf that is every edge alive at that time, since the intervals were split into nodes that contain the leaf.
--think
- Signal: "edges are added and removed", "connectivity after each operation", "is there a path between u and v at time t", offline queries with temporary edges. If only additions, plain DSU is enough. If only deletions, reverse time and treat them as additions.
- Divide and conquer on time is the general pattern: use it whenever an operation is easy to do but hard to undo, and you can see all operations up front. Rollback data structures (a DSU with history, a stack-based structure) are the partner.
- Online dynamic connectivity (answering before seeing the future) needs much heavier structures: link-cut trees for forests, or Holm-de Lichtenberg-Thorup with polylogarithmic time.
- Extra information stays possible: store component counts, sizes, or bipartiteness (DSU with parity) and roll them back too.
- Without path compression, `find` costs `O(log V)`: that is the price of undo. Do not add compression.
- Pitfalls: handling repeated adds of the same edge (keep a list of start times per edge, not a single value), deleting an edge that was never added, using path compression with rollback, and recursion depth (only `log T`, which is fine).
--cost
Time O((T + E) log T log V) for `T` events and `E` edge intervals, space O(V + T log T).
--java
// events[i] = {kind, u, v}: kind 0 = add edge, 1 = delete edge, 2 = query. Returns the answers to the queries in order.
static List<Boolean> offlineConnectivity(int n, int[][] events) {
    int T = events.length;
    List<List<int[]>> seg = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < 4 * Math.max(T, 1); i++) seg.add(new ArrayList<>());
    Map<Long, ArrayDeque<Integer>> open = new HashMap<>();       // edge -> start times still alive
    for (int t = 0; t < T; t++) {
        int u = Math.min(events[t][1], events[t][2]), v = Math.max(events[t][1], events[t][2]);
        long key = (long) u * n + v;
        if (events[t][0] == 0) open.computeIfAbsent(key, k -> new ArrayDeque<>()).add(t);
        else if (events[t][0] == 1) addInterval(seg, 1, 0, T, open.get(key).poll(), t, new int[]{u, v});
    }
    for (Map.Entry<Long, ArrayDeque<Integer>> en : open.entrySet())
        for (int s : en.getValue()) addInterval(seg, 1, 0, T, s, T, new int[]{(int) (en.getKey() / n), (int) (en.getKey() % n)});
    int[] parent = new int[n], size = new int[n];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { parent[i] = i; size[i] = 1; }
    List<Boolean> ans = new ArrayList<>();
    walkTime(seg, events, 1, 0, T, parent, size, new ArrayDeque<>(), ans);
    return ans;
}

static void addInterval(List<List<int[]>> seg, int node, int l, int r, int ql, int qr, int[] edge) {
    if (qr <= l || r <= ql) return;
    if (ql <= l && r <= qr) { seg.get(node).add(edge); return; }   // node range fully inside the interval
    int mid = (l + r) / 2;
    addInterval(seg, 2 * node, l, mid, ql, qr, edge);
    addInterval(seg, 2 * node + 1, mid, r, ql, qr, edge);
}

static int findRb(int[] parent, int x) { while (parent[x] != x) x = parent[x]; return x; }   // no compression

static void walkTime(List<List<int[]>> seg, int[][] events, int node, int l, int r,
                     int[] parent, int[] size, ArrayDeque<Integer> hist, List<Boolean> ans) {
    if (l >= r) return;
    int mark = hist.size();
    for (int[] e : seg.get(node)) {                                 // unite, remembering how to undo
        int a = findRb(parent, e[0]), b = findRb(parent, e[1]);
        if (a == b) continue;
        if (size[a] < size[b]) { int t = a; a = b; b = t; }
        parent[b] = a; size[a] += size[b]; hist.push(b);
    }
    if (r - l == 1) {
        if (events[l][0] == 2) ans.add(findRb(parent, events[l][1]) == findRb(parent, events[l][2]));
    } else {
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        walkTime(seg, events, 2 * node, l, mid, parent, size, hist, ans);
        walkTime(seg, events, 2 * node + 1, mid, r, parent, size, hist, ans);
    }
    while (hist.size() > mark) {                                    // undo this node's unions
        int b = hist.pop(), a = parent[b];
        size[a] -= size[b]; parent[b] = b;
    }
}
--py
import sys

def offline_connectivity(n, events):
    """events is a list of (kind, u, v): kind 0 = add edge, 1 = delete edge, 2 = query.
    Returns the answers to the queries in order."""
    sys.setrecursionlimit(10000)
    T = len(events)
    seg = [[] for _ in range(4 * max(T, 1))]
    open_edges = {}                                     # edge -> start times still alive
    intervals = []
    for t, (kind, u, v) in enumerate(events):
        key = (min(u, v), max(u, v))
        if kind == 0:
            open_edges.setdefault(key, []).append(t)
        elif kind == 1:
            intervals.append((open_edges[key].pop(0), t, key))
    for key, starts in open_edges.items():
        for s in starts:
            intervals.append((s, T, key))

    def add(node, l, r, ql, qr, edge):
        if qr <= l or r <= ql:
            return
        if ql <= l and r <= qr:
            seg[node].append(edge)                      # node range fully inside the interval
            return
        mid = (l + r) // 2
        add(2 * node, l, mid, ql, qr, edge)
        add(2 * node + 1, mid, r, ql, qr, edge)

    for s, e, key in intervals:
        add(1, 0, T, s, e, key)

    parent = list(range(n))
    size = [1] * n
    hist = []
    ans = []

    def find(x):
        while parent[x] != x:                           # no path compression: it could not be undone
            x = parent[x]
        return x

    def walk(node, l, r):
        if l >= r:
            return
        mark = len(hist)
        for u, v in seg[node]:                          # unite, remembering how to undo
            a, b = find(u), find(v)
            if a == b:
                continue
            if size[a] < size[b]:
                a, b = b, a
            parent[b] = a
            size[a] += size[b]
            hist.append(b)
        if r - l == 1:
            if events[l][0] == 2:
                ans.append(find(events[l][1]) == find(events[l][2]))
        else:
            mid = (l + r) // 2
            walk(2 * node, l, mid)
            walk(2 * node + 1, mid, r)
        while len(hist) > mark:                         # undo this node's unions
            b = hist.pop()
            a = parent[b]
            size[a] -= size[b]
            parent[b] = b

    walk(1, 0, T)
    return ans
--cpp
struct OfflineConn {
    int n, T;
    vector<vector<pair<int,int>>> seg;
    vector<array<int,3>> events;
    vector<int> parent, size, hist;
    vector<char> ans;

    int find(int x) { while (parent[x] != x) x = parent[x]; return x; }   // no path compression: it could not be undone

    void add(int node, int l, int r, int ql, int qr, pair<int,int> e) {
        if (qr <= l || r <= ql) return;
        if (ql <= l && r <= qr) { seg[node].push_back(e); return; }       // node range fully inside the interval
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        add(2 * node, l, mid, ql, qr, e);
        add(2 * node + 1, mid, r, ql, qr, e);
    }

    void walk(int node, int l, int r) {
        if (l >= r) return;
        size_t mark = hist.size();
        for (auto [u, v] : seg[node]) {                                   // unite, remembering how to undo
            int a = find(u), b = find(v);
            if (a == b) continue;
            if (size[a] < size[b]) swap(a, b);
            parent[b] = a; size[a] += size[b]; hist.push_back(b);
        }
        if (r - l == 1) {
            if (events[l][0] == 2) ans.push_back(find(events[l][1]) == find(events[l][2]));
        } else {
            int mid = (l + r) / 2;
            walk(2 * node, l, mid);
            walk(2 * node + 1, mid, r);
        }
        while (hist.size() > mark) {                                      // undo this node's unions
            int b = hist.back(); hist.pop_back();
            int a = parent[b];
            size[a] -= size[b]; parent[b] = b;
        }
    }

    // events[i] = {kind, u, v}: kind 0 = add edge, 1 = delete edge, 2 = query. Returns the query answers in order.
    vector<char> run(int n_, const vector<array<int,3>>& ev) {
        n = n_; events = ev; T = ev.size();
        seg.assign(4 * max(T, 1), {});
        map<pair<int,int>, vector<int>> open;                             // edge -> start times still alive
        vector<array<int,4>> intervals;
        for (int t = 0; t < T; t++) {
            pair<int,int> key = {min(ev[t][1], ev[t][2]), max(ev[t][1], ev[t][2])};
            if (ev[t][0] == 0) open[key].push_back(t);
            else if (ev[t][0] == 1) {
                intervals.push_back({open[key].front(), t, key.first, key.second});
                open[key].erase(open[key].begin());
            }
        }
        for (auto& [key, starts] : open) for (int s : starts) intervals.push_back({s, T, key.first, key.second});
        for (auto& iv : intervals) add(1, 0, T, iv[0], iv[1], {iv[2], iv[3]});
        parent.resize(n); iota(parent.begin(), parent.end(), 0);
        size.assign(n, 1); hist.clear(); ans.clear();
        walk(1, 0, T);
        return ans;
    }
};
--go
// events[i] = {kind, u, v}: kind 0 = add edge, 1 = delete edge, 2 = query. Returns the answers to the queries in order.
func offlineConnectivity(n int, events [][3]int) []bool {
    T := len(events)
    seg := make([][][2]int, 4*max(T, 1))
    open := map[int][]int{} // edge -> start times still alive
    for t, e := range events {
        u, v := min(e[1], e[2]), max(e[1], e[2])
        key := u*n + v
        if e[0] == 0 {
            open[key] = append(open[key], t)
        } else if e[0] == 1 {
            addInterval(seg, 1, 0, T, open[key][0], t, [2]int{u, v})
            open[key] = open[key][1:]
        }
    }
    for key, starts := range open {
        for _, s := range starts {
            addInterval(seg, 1, 0, T, s, T, [2]int{key / n, key % n})
        }
    }
    parent, size := make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
    for i := range parent {
        parent[i], size[i] = i, 1
    }
    var ans []bool
    var hist []int
    var walk func(node, l, r int)
    walk = func(node, l, r int) {
        if l >= r {
            return
        }
        mark := len(hist)
        for _, e := range seg[node] { // unite, remembering how to undo
            a, b := findRb(parent, e[0]), findRb(parent, e[1])
            if a == b {
                continue
            }
            if size[a] < size[b] {
                a, b = b, a
            }
            parent[b] = a
            size[a] += size[b]
            hist = append(hist, b)
        }
        if r-l == 1 {
            if events[l][0] == 2 {
                ans = append(ans, findRb(parent, events[l][1]) == findRb(parent, events[l][2]))
            }
        } else {
            mid := (l + r) / 2
            walk(2*node, l, mid)
            walk(2*node+1, mid, r)
        }
        for len(hist) > mark { // undo this node's unions
            b := hist[len(hist)-1]
            hist = hist[:len(hist)-1]
            a := parent[b]
            size[a] -= size[b]
            parent[b] = b
        }
    }
    walk(1, 0, T)
    return ans
}

func addInterval(seg [][][2]int, node, l, r, ql, qr int, edge [2]int) {
    if qr <= l || r <= ql {
        return
    }
    if ql <= l && r <= qr { // node range fully inside the interval
        seg[node] = append(seg[node], edge)
        return
    }
    mid := (l + r) / 2
    addInterval(seg, 2*node, l, mid, ql, qr, edge)
    addInterval(seg, 2*node+1, mid, r, ql, qr, edge)
}

func findRb(parent []int, x int) int { // no compression
    for parent[x] != x {
        x = parent[x]
    }
    return x
}
### arbor
--idea
A spanning tree of an undirected graph connects all nodes with no cycle. In a directed graph, the matching idea is a minimum arborescence: a tree rooted at a chosen node `root` in which every other node can be reached from the root by following the arrows, so every non-root node has exactly one incoming edge. The cheapest one answers questions like "what is the cheapest way to broadcast from a headquarters when links are one-way". Kruskal's greedy method does not work here, because direction matters.

The Chu-Liu/Edmonds method has a simple first step: for every non-root node, pick its cheapest incoming edge. If these edges form no cycle, they already are the answer. If they form cycles, contract each cycle into one super-node and repeat. The trick that makes the costs right: when an edge enters the contracted cycle at node `v`, replace its weight `w` by `w - inw[v]`, where `inw[v]` is the cost of the cycle edge that entered `v`. Choosing the new edge means dropping that cycle edge, so the difference is the true extra cost.
--build
1. Keep a list of edges `(u, v, w)` meaning `u -> v`, the node count `n`, the `root`, and a total `res = 0`.
2. For each node `v`, find the cheapest incoming edge: `inw[v]` is its weight and `pre[v]` its start. Ignore self-loops. If some non-root node has no incoming edge, no arborescence exists: return -1.
3. Set `inw[root] = 0`. Add every `inw[v]` to `res`.
4. Find cycles in the chosen edges: for each `v`, follow `pre` backwards from `v`, marking the nodes with `v` as a visit stamp, until you reach the root, a node already numbered, or a node stamped in this walk. If the walk stops on a node stamped in this walk (and it is not the root), a new cycle was found. Give every node of that cycle the next component number `cnt`.
5. If no cycle was found (`cnt == 0`), stop: `res` is the answer.
6. Otherwise every node not on a cycle gets its own new number, so each cycle and each lone node is now one node of a smaller graph. The root's new number is `id[root]`.
7. Build the new edge list: for every edge `(u, v, w)` with `id[u] != id[v]`, add `(id[u], id[v], w - inw[v])`. Edges inside a contracted cycle vanish.
8. Repeat from step 2 with the smaller graph.
9. Invariant: `res` plus the cost of the best arborescence of the contracted graph equals the cost of the best arborescence of the graph before the contraction. The reduced weights `w - inw[v]` are never negative, and an edge entering a cycle at `v` replaces exactly the cycle edge `pre[v] -> v` that it displaces.
--think
- Signal: "directed spanning tree from a root", "cheapest way to reach every node from the source along one-way links", "minimum cost branching", "every node except the root picks one parent".
- Compare: undirected spanning trees use Kruskal or Prim (`mst`, `prim`). In directed graphs, taking the cheapest incoming edge per node is the right first guess, and cycles repair it.
- Choice of root matters: if no fixed root is given, add a virtual root with an edge of a large cost `X` to every node, solve, and subtract `X` (it is used once if a single root suffices, more times if several roots are needed).
- The simple version here is O(V times E), enough for a few thousand edges. Tarjan's version with mergeable heaps runs in O(E log V) for big graphs.
- To reconstruct the actual edges you must record the contractions and expand them back, which takes extra bookkeeping. Most problems only ask for the total cost.
- Pitfalls: forgetting to reduce the weight by `inw[v]` for edges entering a cycle, using an infinity so small that a real cost looks like missing, ignoring self-loops (they never help), and the root's incoming edges: they must be ignored (`inw[root] = 0`).
--cost
Time O(V times E), space O(V + E).
--java
// edges[i] = {u, v, w} means u -> v. Returns the minimum cost of an arborescence rooted at root, or -1.
static long minArborescence(int n, int[][] edges, int root) {
    final long INF = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4;
    List<long[]> es = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int[] e : edges) es.add(new long[]{e[0], e[1], e[2]});
    long res = 0;
    while (true) {
        long[] inw = new long[n];
        int[] pre = new int[n];
        Arrays.fill(inw, INF);
        for (long[] e : es)                                           // cheapest incoming edge per node
            if (e[0] != e[1] && e[2] < inw[(int) e[1]]) { inw[(int) e[1]] = e[2]; pre[(int) e[1]] = (int) e[0]; }
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (v != root && inw[v] == INF) return -1;
        int cnt = 0;
        int[] id = new int[n], vis = new int[n];
        Arrays.fill(id, -1); Arrays.fill(vis, -1);
        inw[root] = 0;
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
            res += inw[v];
            int x = v;
            while (x != root && vis[x] != v && id[x] == -1) { vis[x] = v; x = pre[x]; }
            if (x != root && id[x] == -1) {                           // found a new cycle through x
                for (int y = pre[x]; y != x; y = pre[y]) id[y] = cnt;
                id[x] = cnt++;
            }
        }
        if (cnt == 0) break;                                          // no cycles: done
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (id[v] == -1) id[v] = cnt++;   // lone nodes keep their own number
        List<long[]> next = new ArrayList<>();
        for (long[] e : es) {
            int nu = id[(int) e[0]], nv = id[(int) e[1]];
            if (nu != nv) next.add(new long[]{nu, nv, e[2] - inw[(int) e[1]]});   // reduced weight
        }
        es = next; n = cnt; root = id[root];
    }
    return res;
}
--py
def min_arborescence(n, edges, root):
    """edges is a list of (u, v, w) meaning u -> v. Returns the minimum cost of an arborescence
    rooted at root, or -1 if none exists."""
    INF = float("inf")
    res = 0
    while True:
        inw = [INF] * n
        pre = [-1] * n
        for u, v, w in edges:                                  # cheapest incoming edge per node
            if u != v and w < inw[v]:
                inw[v], pre[v] = w, u
        if any(v != root and inw[v] == INF for v in range(n)):
            return -1
        cnt = 0
        ident = [-1] * n
        vis = [-1] * n
        inw[root] = 0
        for v in range(n):
            res += inw[v]
            x = v
            while x != root and vis[x] != v and ident[x] == -1:
                vis[x] = v
                x = pre[x]
            if x != root and ident[x] == -1:                   # found a new cycle through x
                y = pre[x]
                while y != x:
                    ident[y] = cnt
                    y = pre[y]
                ident[x] = cnt
                cnt += 1
        if cnt == 0:
            break                                              # no cycles: done
        for v in range(n):
            if ident[v] == -1:                                 # lone nodes keep their own number
                ident[v] = cnt
                cnt += 1
        edges = [(ident[u], ident[v], w - inw[v]) for u, v, w in edges if ident[u] != ident[v]]   # reduced weight
        n, root = cnt, ident[root]
    return res
--cpp
// edges[i] = {u, v, w} means u -> v. Returns the minimum cost of an arborescence rooted at root, or -1.
long long minArborescence(int n, vector<array<long long,3>> edges, int root) {
    const long long INF = LLONG_MAX / 4;
    long long res = 0;
    while (true) {
        vector<long long> inw(n, INF);
        vector<int> pre(n, -1);
        for (auto& [u, v, w] : edges)                                    // cheapest incoming edge per node
            if (u != v && w < inw[v]) { inw[v] = w; pre[v] = u; }
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (v != root && inw[v] == INF) return -1;
        int cnt = 0;
        vector<int> id(n, -1), vis(n, -1);
        inw[root] = 0;
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
            res += inw[v];
            int x = v;
            while (x != root && vis[x] != v && id[x] == -1) { vis[x] = v; x = pre[x]; }
            if (x != root && id[x] == -1) {                              // found a new cycle through x
                for (int y = pre[x]; y != x; y = pre[y]) id[y] = cnt;
                id[x] = cnt++;
            }
        }
        if (cnt == 0) break;                                             // no cycles: done
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (id[v] == -1) id[v] = cnt++;      // lone nodes keep their own number
        vector<array<long long,3>> next;
        for (auto& [u, v, w] : edges)
            if (id[u] != id[v]) next.push_back({id[u], id[v], w - inw[v]});   // reduced weight
        edges = next; n = cnt; root = id[root];
    }
    return res;
}
--go
// edges[i] = {u, v, w} means u -> v. Returns the minimum cost of an arborescence rooted at root, or -1.
func minArborescence(n int, edges [][3]int, root int) int {
    const inf = 1 << 60
    es := append([][3]int(nil), edges...)
    res := 0
    for {
        inw, pre := make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
        for i := range inw {
            inw[i] = inf
        }
        for _, e := range es { // cheapest incoming edge per node
            if e[0] != e[1] && e[2] < inw[e[1]] {
                inw[e[1]], pre[e[1]] = e[2], e[0]
            }
        }
        for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
            if v != root && inw[v] == inf {
                return -1
            }
        }
        cnt := 0
        id, vis := make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
        for i := range id {
            id[i], vis[i] = -1, -1
        }
        inw[root] = 0
        for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
            res += inw[v]
            x := v
            for x != root && vis[x] != v && id[x] == -1 {
                vis[x] = v
                x = pre[x]
            }
            if x != root && id[x] == -1 { // found a new cycle through x
                for y := pre[x]; y != x; y = pre[y] {
                    id[y] = cnt
                }
                id[x] = cnt
                cnt++
            }
        }
        if cnt == 0 { // no cycles: done
            break
        }
        for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
            if id[v] == -1 { // lone nodes keep their own number
                id[v] = cnt
                cnt++
            }
        }
        var next [][3]int
        for _, e := range es {
            if nu, nv := id[e[0]], id[e[1]]; nu != nv {
                next = append(next, [3]int{nu, nv, e[2] - inw[e[1]]}) // reduced weight
            }
        }
        es, n, root = next, cnt, id[root]
    }
    return res
}
### gomory
--idea
Between every pair of nodes in an undirected network there is a minimum cut (the cheapest set of edges whose removal separates the two, see `mincut`). With `n` nodes there are about `n squared / 2` pairs, and computing each by its own max flow would be slow. The Gomory-Hu tree stores all of them in a tree with only `n - 1` edges: the minimum cut between any two nodes `u` and `v` equals the smallest edge weight on the tree path between them.

The version here is Gusfield's, which needs just `n - 1` max flow computations and no graph contraction. Start with all nodes hanging below node 0. Process nodes `1, 2, ...` in order. For node `i`, compute the min cut between `i` and its current parent. The flow value becomes the weight of the tree edge `(i, parent)`. Then every later node that sits on `i`'s side of that cut and has the same parent is moved to hang under `i`.
--build
1. Input: an undirected capacity table `cap[u][v] = cap[v][u]`. Start with `parent[i] = 0` for every node.
2. For each `i` from 1 to `n - 1`: make a copy `res` of `cap`, and compute `f = maxFlow(res, i, parent[i])` (the standard form from the `flow` lesson). It leaves `res` as the residual graph.
3. Find the side of `i` in the minimum cut: build adjacency lists of the residual edges (`res[u][v] > 0`) and run `bfs(adj, i)` (code piece `u-bfsloop`). Nodes with distance not `-1` are on the source side, as in `mincut`.
4. Set `weight[i] = f`: the tree edge `(i, parent[i])` has weight `f`.
5. For every `j > i`: if `j` is on `i`'s side and `parent[j] == parent[i]`, set `parent[j] = i`.
6. After the loop, the tree edges are `(i, parent[i])` with weight `weight[i]` for `i = 1 .. n - 1`.
7. To answer a query for `u` and `v`: walk up from the deeper one to the other (depths from `parent`, where `parent[j] < j` always), keeping the minimum `weight` seen. That minimum is the value of the minimum cut between `u` and `v`.
8. Invariant: after processing `i`, for every pair among the nodes `0 .. i`, the minimum edge on the tree path equals their true min cut value. Each step splits one group into two groups separated by a true minimum cut between `i` and its parent, and cuts of a smaller value never get crossed by pairs on the same side.
--think
- Signal: "minimum cut between every pair", many queries of the form "how many edges must be removed to separate `u` from `v`", "the weakest link between two nodes", all-pairs bottleneck in an undirected network.
- This computes an equivalent flow tree: the values for every pair are right. The classic Gomory-Hu tree additionally has every tree edge be an actual min cut of the whole graph (the tree edge weight splits the nodes into the right two groups). Gusfield's full version adds a swap step to get that; for values only, this is enough.
- Only works for undirected graphs. For directed graphs, min cuts between pairs have no such tree.
- The cost is `n - 1` max flows, so use Dinic (`dinic`) instead of the matrix version when the graph is large. For a single `s` and `t`, just run `mincut`.
- Variants: the global minimum cut of a graph is the smallest edge of the Gomory-Hu tree. (Stoer-Wagner finds it directly.)
