- Pitfalls: mixing up the direction (use symmetric capacities), forgetting to copy `cap` before each flow (the flow modifies it), and updating parents of nodes with index below `i`.
--cost
`n - 1` max flow computations, plus O(V squared) per step for the reachability. Space O(V squared).
--uses
u-bfsloop
--java
// cap[u][v] = cap[v][u]: undirected capacities. Uses maxFlow from the flow lesson.
// Returns {parent, weight}: tree edge (i, parent[i]) has weight[i], for i >= 1.
static int[][] gomoryHu(long[][] cap) {
    int n = cap.length;
    int[] parent = new int[n];
    int[] weight = new int[n];
    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        long[][] res = new long[n][];
        for (int u = 0; u < n; u++) res[u] = cap[u].clone();
        long f = maxFlow(res, i, parent[i]);               // min cut between i and its parent
        List<List<Integer>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int u = 0; u < n; u++) {
            adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (res[u][v] > 0) adj.get(u).add(v);
        }
        int[] dist = bfs(adj, i);                          // piece: dist >= 0 means on i's side
        weight[i] = (int) f;
        for (int j = i + 1; j < n; j++)
            if (dist[j] >= 0 && parent[j] == parent[i]) parent[j] = i;
    }
    return new int[][]{parent, weight};
}

// minimum cut between u and v, read off the tree
static int pairMinCut(int[] parent, int[] weight, int u, int v) {
    int n = parent.length;
    int[] depth = new int[n];
    for (int j = 1; j < n; j++) depth[j] = depth[parent[j]] + 1;   // parent[j] < j
    int best = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
    while (u != v) {
        if (depth[u] < depth[v]) { int t = u; u = v; v = t; }
        best = Math.min(best, weight[u]);
        u = parent[u];
    }
    return best;
}
--py
def gomory_hu(cap):
    """cap[u][v] = cap[v][u]: undirected capacities. Uses max_flow from the flow lesson.
    Returns (parent, weight): tree edge (i, parent[i]) has weight[i], for i >= 1."""
    n = len(cap)
    parent = [0] * n
    weight = [0] * n
    for i in range(1, n):
        res = [row[:] for row in cap]
        f = max_flow(res, i, parent[i])                    # min cut between i and its parent
        adj = [[v for v in range(n) if res[u][v] > 0] for u in range(n)]
        dist = bfs(adj, i)                                 # piece: dist >= 0 means on i's side
        weight[i] = f
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            if dist[j] >= 0 and parent[j] == parent[i]:
                parent[j] = i
    return parent, weight

def pair_min_cut(parent, weight, u, v):
    """Minimum cut between u and v, read off the tree."""
    n = len(parent)
    depth = [0] * n
    for j in range(1, n):
        depth[j] = depth[parent[j]] + 1                    # parent[j] < j
    best = float("inf")
    while u != v:
        if depth[u] < depth[v]:
            u, v = v, u
        best = min(best, weight[u])
        u = parent[u]
    return best
--cpp
// cap[u][v] = cap[v][u]: undirected capacities. Uses maxFlow from the flow lesson.
// Fills parent and weight: tree edge (i, parent[i]) has weight[i], for i >= 1.
void gomoryHu(const vector<vector<long long>>& cap, vector<int>& parent, vector<long long>& weight) {
    int n = cap.size();
    parent.assign(n, 0);
    weight.assign(n, 0);
    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        vector<vector<long long>> res = cap;
        long long f = maxFlow(res, i, parent[i]);          // min cut between i and its parent
        vector<vector<int>> adj(n);
        for (int u = 0; u < n; u++)
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (res[u][v] > 0) adj[u].push_back(v);
        vector<int> dist = bfs(adj, i);                    // piece: dist >= 0 means on i's side
        weight[i] = f;
        for (int j = i + 1; j < n; j++)
            if (dist[j] >= 0 && parent[j] == parent[i]) parent[j] = i;
    }
}

// minimum cut between u and v, read off the tree
long long pairMinCut(const vector<int>& parent, const vector<long long>& weight, int u, int v) {
    int n = parent.size();
    vector<int> depth(n, 0);
    for (int j = 1; j < n; j++) depth[j] = depth[parent[j]] + 1;   // parent[j] < j
    long long best = LLONG_MAX;
    while (u != v) {
        if (depth[u] < depth[v]) swap(u, v);
        best = min(best, weight[u]);
        u = parent[u];
    }
    return best;
}
--go
// capa[u][v] = capa[v][u]: undirected capacities. Uses maxFlow from the flow lesson.
// Returns parent and weight: tree edge (i, parent[i]) has weight[i], for i >= 1.
func gomoryHu(capa [][]int) (parent, weight []int) {
    n := len(capa)
    parent, weight = make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
    for i := 1; i < n; i++ {
        res := make([][]int, n)
        for u := range res {
            res[u] = append([]int(nil), capa[u]...)
        }
        weight[i] = maxFlow(res, i, parent[i]) // min cut between i and its parent
        adj := make([][]int, n)
        for u := range adj {
            for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
                if res[u][v] > 0 {
                    adj[u] = append(adj[u], v)
                }
            }
        }
        dist := bfs(adj, i) // piece: dist >= 0 means on i's side
        for j := i + 1; j < n; j++ {
            if dist[j] >= 0 && parent[j] == parent[i] {
                parent[j] = i
            }
        }
    }
    return
}

// minimum cut between u and v, read off the tree
func pairMinCut(parent, weight []int, u, v int) int {
    depth := make([]int, len(parent))
    for j := 1; j < len(parent); j++ {
        depth[j] = depth[parent[j]] + 1 // parent[j] < j
    }
    best := 1 << 60
    for u != v {
        if depth[u] < depth[v] {
            u, v = v, u
        }
        best = min(best, weight[u])
        u = parent[u]
    }
    return best
}
### clique
--idea
A clique is a group of nodes in which every pair is joined by an edge: everybody knows everybody. An independent set is the opposite, a group where no pair is joined. They are two sides of one problem: an independent set in a graph is a clique in its complement (the graph that has an edge exactly where the original has none). Finding the largest of either is NP-hard, so no fast general algorithm is known, but for graphs with up to about 40 to 60 nodes, branching with strong pruning is quick.

The search keeps a growing clique and a set `P` of candidates: nodes adjacent to everyone in the clique so far. Take a candidate `v`, add it to the clique, and shrink `P` to the candidates that are also neighbours of `v`. When `P` is empty the clique cannot grow, so compare its size with the best. Pruning: if the clique size plus the number of remaining candidates cannot beat the best, stop that branch. Storing `P` as a bitmask makes "intersect with the neighbours of `v`" a single AND.
--build
1. Number nodes `0 .. n-1` with `n` at most 30 (so a mask fits in an `int`). For each node build `nb[v]`, the mask of its neighbours, with `setBit(nb[v], w)` for each neighbour `w` (code piece `u-bit`).
2. Keep `best`, the size of the largest clique found so far, and `bestMask`, its members.
3. `expand(size, members, P)`: if `P` is empty, the clique is maximal: if `size > best`, record it. Then return.
4. If `size + popcount(P) <= best`, return: even taking every candidate cannot win.
5. Otherwise loop over the nodes `v` in `P` (test with `getBit(P, v)`). For each one, again check the bound `size + popcount(P) <= best` and stop if it fails. Else call `expand(size + 1, setBit(members, v), P & nb[v])`, then remove `v` from `P` with `clearBit(P, v)` so that cliques containing `v` are not found again.
6. Start with `expand(0, 0, all nodes)`. At the end `best` is the maximum clique size and `bestMask` is one maximum clique.
7. For a maximum independent set, build the complement graph first (an edge between `u` and `v` exactly when the original has none) and run the same search.
8. Invariant: `P` holds exactly the nodes adjacent to every member of the current clique, and not yet tried at this level. Removing each tried `v` from `P` means a clique containing `v` is only searched in the branch where `v` was chosen first, so no clique is generated twice.
--think
- Signal: "largest group where everyone is compatible", "largest set of mutually conflicting items" (a clique in the conflict graph, which is also a lower bound on the colours needed, see `graphcolor`), "largest set of items with no conflicts" (independent set), "does a group of `k` mutual friends exist", small `n` (up to about 40).
- Relations: the maximum independent set is `n` minus the minimum vertex cover. In a bipartite graph that cover is easy by matching (`matching`, `treematch` for trees); in a general graph it is hard. For perfect graphs and special classes (interval graphs, trees, chordal graphs) clique and independent set have fast exact algorithms.
- Faster for `n` up to 60 or more: Bron-Kerbosch with pivoting and bitmasks, or meet-in-the-middle splitting the nodes into two halves of about `n / 2` each: solve each half by subset enumeration (`2^(n/2)` masks) and combine.
- Counting maximal cliques or listing all of them is a different task; Bron-Kerbosch does that.
- Pitfalls: `n` larger than 30 with an `int` mask (use `long` for up to 64 nodes), forgetting to remove `v` from `P` after trying it (the search repeats work), and building the complement with self-loops.
--cost
Exponential in the worst case, roughly O(2^(n/2)) in practice with the pruning for moderate graphs; space O(n) for the recursion.
--uses
u-bit
--java
// adj.get(v) lists neighbours; n <= 30. Returns the nodes of a maximum clique.
static List<Integer> maxClique(List<List<Integer>> adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    int[] nb = new int[n];
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) for (int w : adj.get(v)) nb[v] = setBit(nb[v], w);   // piece: neighbour masks
    int[] best = {0, 0};                                  // {size, members mask}
    expand(nb, n, 0, 0, (int) ((1L << n) - 1), best);
    List<Integer> res = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (getBit(best[1], v)) res.add(v);
    return res;
}

static void expand(int[] nb, int n, int size, int members, int P, int[] best) {
    if (P == 0) {                                         // maximal clique
        if (size > best[0]) { best[0] = size; best[1] = members; }
        return;
    }
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
        if (!getBit(P, v)) continue;
        if (size + Integer.bitCount(P) <= best[0]) return;          // cannot beat the best
        expand(nb, n, size + 1, setBit(members, v), P & nb[v], best);
        P = clearBit(P, v);                                          // do not try v again
    }
    if (size > best[0]) { best[0] = size; best[1] = members; }
}
--py
def max_clique(adj):
    """adj[v] lists neighbours; n <= 30 keeps masks small, but Python ints have no limit. Returns the nodes of a maximum clique."""
    n = len(adj)
    nb = [0] * n
    for v in range(n):
        for w in adj[v]:
            nb[v] = set_bit(nb[v], w)                  # piece: neighbour masks
    best = [0, 0]                                      # [size, members mask]

    def expand(size, members, P):
        if P == 0:                                     # maximal clique
            if size > best[0]:
                best[0], best[1] = size, members
            return
        for v in range(n):
            if not get_bit(P, v):
                continue
            if size + bin(P).count("1") <= best[0]:
                return                                 # cannot beat the best
            expand(size + 1, set_bit(members, v), P & nb[v])
            P = clear_bit(P, v)                        # do not try v again
        if size > best[0]:
            best[0], best[1] = size, members

    expand(0, 0, (1 << n) - 1)
    return [v for v in range(n) if get_bit(best[1], v)]
--cpp
void expandClique(const vector<int>& nb, int n, int size, int members, int P, int best[2]) {
    if (P == 0) {                                         // maximal clique
        if (size > best[0]) { best[0] = size; best[1] = members; }
        return;
    }
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
        if (!getBit(P, v)) continue;
        if (size + __builtin_popcount(P) <= best[0]) return;        // cannot beat the best
        expandClique(nb, n, size + 1, setBit(members, v), P & nb[v], best);
        P = clearBit(P, v);                                          // do not try v again
    }
    if (size > best[0]) { best[0] = size; best[1] = members; }
}

// adj[v] lists neighbours; n <= 30. Returns the nodes of a maximum clique.
vector<int> maxClique(const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
    int n = adj.size();
    vector<int> nb(n, 0);
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) for (int w : adj[v]) nb[v] = setBit(nb[v], w);   // piece: neighbour masks
    int best[2] = {0, 0};                                 // {size, members mask}
    expandClique(nb, n, 0, 0, (int)((1LL << n) - 1), best);
    vector<int> res;
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (getBit(best[1], v)) res.push_back(v);
    return res;
}
--go
// adj[v] lists neighbours; n <= 30. Returns the nodes of a maximum clique.
func maxClique(adj [][]int) []int {
    n := len(adj)
    nb := make([]int, n)
    for v := range adj {
        for _, w := range adj[v] {
            nb[v] = setBit(nb[v], w) // piece: neighbour masks
        }
    }
    best := [2]int{0, 0} // {size, members mask}
    expand(nb, n, 0, 0, 1<<n-1, &best)
    var res []int
    for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
        if getBit(best[1], v) {
            res = append(res, v)
        }
    }
    return res
}

func expand(nb []int, n, size, members, P int, best *[2]int) {
    if P == 0 { // maximal clique
        if size > best[0] {
            *best = [2]int{size, members}
        }
        return
    }
    for v := 0; v < n; v++ {
        if !getBit(P, v) {
            continue
        }
        if size+bits.OnesCount(uint(P)) <= best[0] { // cannot beat the best
            return
        }
        expand(nb, n, size+1, setBit(members, v), P&nb[v], best)
        P = clearBit(P, v) // do not try v again
    }
    if size > best[0] {
        *best = [2]int{size, members}
    }
}
### planar
--idea
A graph is planar if it can be drawn on a flat sheet with no two edges crossing. A drawing divides the sheet into regions called faces, including the unbounded outer face. Planarity matters for maps, circuit boards and road networks, and planar graphs have special properties: few edges, and easy colouring and many fast algorithms.

Euler's formula ties the three counts together. For a connected planar graph with `V` nodes, `E` edges and `F` faces, `V - E + F = 2`. It leads to quick tests: a simple planar graph with `V >= 3` has at most `3V - 6` edges, and if it has no triangles (for example if it is bipartite) at most `2V - 4`. A graph that breaks these bounds, like the complete graph on 5 nodes (10 edges, bound 9) or the complete bipartite graph with 3 and 3 nodes (9 edges, bound 8), cannot be planar. Kuratowski's theorem says these two graphs are the only obstacles: a graph is planar exactly when it contains no subdivision of them.

Duality: every planar drawing has a dual graph, with one node per face and one edge crossing each original edge, joining the faces on its two sides. Cuts in the original are cycles in the dual, and a shortest cycle in the dual gives a minimum cut, so some flow problems on planar graphs reduce to shortest paths. The code below does the practical part: given an embedding as a rotation system (for each node, its neighbours in clockwise order), it traces the faces, checks Euler's formula, and builds the dual.
--build
1. Describe an embedding by `rot[v]`, the neighbours of `v` in cyclic order around `v` (all clockwise, or all counter-clockwise).
2. A dart is one direction of an edge: the pair `(v, i)` meaning the edge from `v` to `rot[v][i]`. Number the darts consecutively with `start[v]` as the first dart number of `v`, so dart `(v, i)` has number `start[v] + i`.
3. Precompute `pos[w][v]`, the index of `v` in `rot[w]`.
4. Trace the face that a dart `(v, i)` borders. Let `w = rot[v][i]`. The next dart is `(w, j)` with `j = (pos[w][v] + 1) mod deg(w)`: arrive at `w` along the edge from `v`, then turn to the next edge around `w`. Keep going until you come back to the first dart. All darts on the way form one face.
5. Give each unvisited dart a new face id and trace its face, marking the darts. The number of face ids is `F`.
6. For a connected graph, check `V - E + F == 2`. If it holds, the rotation system is a drawing on the plane. If it gives a smaller value, the system describes a drawing on a surface with handles (a torus, for example), not the plane.
7. Dual graph: for each edge, with darts `d` and its reverse `d'`, add the dual edge `(face[d], face[d'])`. The dual has `F` nodes and `E` edges.
8. Quick rejection without an embedding: if `E > 3V - 6` for `V >= 3`, the graph is not planar.
9. Invariant: tracing visits every dart exactly once, because "next dart" is a one-to-one rule on darts. So the faces partition the darts and each edge borders exactly two face sides (or the same face twice).
--think
- Signal: "can this be drawn without crossings", "map colouring", "number of regions", "minimum cut in a planar graph as a shortest cycle", "the graph is planar" given as a hint (use `E <= 3V - 6` to bound the work: a planar graph has `O(V)` edges, so algorithms with `E` in their cost are fast).
- A full planarity test (given only the graph, no embedding) exists in linear time (Hopcroft-Tarjan, or the left-right planarity test used by graph libraries). It is long; use a library when you need it. Here the embedding is given.
- Four colour theorem: every planar graph can be coloured with four colours (`graphcolor`). Five colours always suffice with a short proof, and six with an even shorter one (some node has degree at most 5).
- Planar graphs are sparse: `E <= 3V - 6`, so the average degree is under 6. That bounds many things: for example some node always has degree 5 or less.
- Grids and maps drawn from coordinates are naturally planar: sort the neighbours of each node by angle to get the rotation system.
- Pitfalls: mixing clockwise and counter-clockwise orders between nodes (all must agree), applying Euler's formula to a disconnected graph (each piece adds its own outer face when traced separately, so the faces counted this way satisfy `V - E + F = 2` times the number of pieces), and isolated nodes (no darts, so no faces).
--cost
Tracing faces and building the dual take O(V + E) time and space. The edge-bound test is O(1).
--java
// rot.get(v): neighbours of v in cyclic order. Graph must be simple and connected.
// Returns {F, then the dual edges as pairs}: res.get(0) = {F}, the rest are {faceA, faceB} per edge.
static List<int[]> facesAndDual(List<List<Integer>> rot) {
    int n = rot.size();
    int[] start = new int[n + 1];
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) start[v + 1] = start[v] + rot.get(v).size();
    int darts = start[n];
    List<Map<Integer, Integer>> pos = new ArrayList<>();             // pos[w][v]: index of v in rot[w]
    for (int w = 0; w < n; w++) {
        Map<Integer, Integer> m = new HashMap<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < rot.get(w).size(); i++) m.put(rot.get(w).get(i), i);
        pos.add(m);
    }
    int[] face = new int[darts];
    Arrays.fill(face, -1);
    int F = 0;
    for (int v0 = 0; v0 < n; v0++)
        for (int i0 = 0; i0 < rot.get(v0).size(); i0++) {
            if (face[start[v0] + i0] != -1) continue;
            int v = v0, i = i0;                                       // trace one face
            while (face[start[v] + i] == -1) {
                face[start[v] + i] = F;
                int w = rot.get(v).get(i);
                int j = (pos.get(w).get(v) + 1) % rot.get(w).size();  // turn to the next edge around w
                v = w; i = j;
            }
            F++;
        }
    List<int[]> res = new ArrayList<>();
    res.add(new int[]{F});
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++)
        for (int i = 0; i < rot.get(v).size(); i++) {
            int w = rot.get(v).get(i);
            if (v < w) res.add(new int[]{face[start[v] + i], face[start[w] + pos.get(w).get(v)]});   // one per edge
        }
    return res;
}
--py
def faces_and_dual(rot):
    """rot[v]: neighbours of v in cyclic order. Graph must be simple and connected.
    Returns (F, dual_edges) with one dual edge (faceA, faceB) per original edge."""
    n = len(rot)
    start = [0] * (n + 1)
    for v in range(n):
        start[v + 1] = start[v] + len(rot[v])
    pos = [{w: i for i, w in enumerate(rot[v])} for v in range(n)]    # pos[w][v]: index of v in rot[w]
    face = [-1] * start[n]
    F = 0
    for v0 in range(n):
        for i0 in range(len(rot[v0])):
            if face[start[v0] + i0] != -1:
                continue
            v, i = v0, i0                                              # trace one face
            while face[start[v] + i] == -1:
                face[start[v] + i] = F
                w = rot[v][i]
                j = (pos[w][v] + 1) % len(rot[w])                      # turn to the next edge around w
                v, i = w, j
            F += 1
    dual = []
    for v in range(n):
        for i, w in enumerate(rot[v]):
            if v < w:
                dual.append((face[start[v] + i], face[start[w] + pos[w][v]]))   # one per edge
    return F, dual
--cpp
// rot[v]: neighbours of v in cyclic order. Graph must be simple and connected.
// Returns F and fills dual with one {faceA, faceB} per original edge.
int facesAndDual(const vector<vector<int>>& rot, vector<pair<int,int>>& dual) {
    int n = rot.size();
    vector<int> start(n + 1, 0);
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) start[v + 1] = start[v] + rot[v].size();
    vector<map<int,int>> pos(n);                                  // pos[w][v]: index of v in rot[w]
    for (int w = 0; w < n; w++) for (int i = 0; i < (int)rot[w].size(); i++) pos[w][rot[w][i]] = i;
    vector<int> face(start[n], -1);
    int F = 0;
    for (int v0 = 0; v0 < n; v0++)
        for (int i0 = 0; i0 < (int)rot[v0].size(); i0++) {
            if (face[start[v0] + i0] != -1) continue;
            int v = v0, i = i0;                                   // trace one face
            while (face[start[v] + i] == -1) {
                face[start[v] + i] = F;
                int w = rot[v][i];
                int j = (pos[w][v] + 1) % rot[w].size();          // turn to the next edge around w
                v = w; i = j;
            }
            F++;
        }
    dual.clear();
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++)
        for (int i = 0; i < (int)rot[v].size(); i++) {
            int w = rot[v][i];
            if (v < w) dual.push_back({face[start[v] + i], face[start[w] + pos[w][v]]});   // one per edge
        }
    return F;
}
--go
// rot[v]: neighbours of v in cyclic order. Graph must be simple and connected.
// Returns F and the dual edges {faceA, faceB}, one per edge.
func facesAndDual(rot [][]int) (F int, dual [][2]int) {
    n := len(rot)
    start := make([]int, n+1)
    for v := range rot {
        start[v+1] = start[v] + len(rot[v])
    }
    pos := make([]map[int]int, n) // pos[w][v]: index of v in rot[w]
    for w := range rot {
        pos[w] = map[int]int{}
        for i, x := range rot[w] {
            pos[w][x] = i
        }
    }
    face := make([]int, start[n])
    for i := range face {
        face[i] = -1
    }
    for v0 := range rot {
        for i0 := range rot[v0] {
            if face[start[v0]+i0] != -1 {
                continue
            }
            v, i := v0, i0 // trace one face
            for face[start[v]+i] == -1 {
                face[start[v]+i] = F
                w := rot[v][i]
                v, i = w, (pos[w][v]+1)%len(rot[w]) // turn to the next edge around w
            }
            F++
        }
    }
    for v := range rot {
        for i, w := range rot[v] {
            if v < w { // one per edge
                dual = append(dual, [2]int{face[start[v]+i], face[start[w]+pos[w][v]]})
            }
        }
    }
    return
}
